================================================================================
  STAGE 9: UNDERGRADUATE — YEAR 1 (Bachelor's) — QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS
  Rigour and foundations
================================================================================

How to use this file (note for the parent / teacher)
----------------------------------------------------
- There are 25 topics (9.1 to 9.25, the same as in topic.txt), in six
  strands: Foundations and proof (9.1-9.4), Analysis and calculus
  (9.5-9.12), Linear algebra (9.13-9.18), Differential equations (9.19),
  Discrete mathematics, graphs, number theory and probability (9.20-9.23)
  and Tools of the working mathematician (9.24-9.25).
  Each topic has:
    * WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN   the checklist of definitions, theorems
                                  and methods in that topic
    * PART A — DESCRIPTIVE        12 questions (D1-D12): proofs,
                                  explanations, derivations and worked
                                  numerical problems, each with the answer
                                  and "Teacher explains" (the method step
                                  by step as a teacher shows it on the
                                  board, common mistakes, and a "Now you
                                  try" question)
    * PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY  12 questions (M1-M12): pure drills with
                                  answers. Most have many parts (a, b,
                                  c ...) so that EVERY case of the topic is
                                  practised — every convergence test, every
                                  kind of integral and coordinate change,
                                  every matrix size and method, every type
                                  of differential equation and recurrence —
                                  not just one example.
    * TEACHING TIPS               what to do if the student struggles, and
                                  how to make it harder
- In total: 300 descriptive + 300 mathematical questions, then a mixed
  revision test (equal descriptive and mathematical parts) and a readiness
  checklist for Stage 10.
- Level: first-year university mathematics (a Bachelor's in mathematics,
  physics, engineering or computer science): "Introduction to proof",
  Calculus I-III / Analysis I, Linear Algebra I, first ODE course,
  Discrete Mathematics, Probability I.
- What is new in Stage 9: RIGOUR. Definitions are exact, every claim needs
  a proof, and "it looks true" is not enough. The student learns to write
  proofs (direct, contrapositive, contradiction, induction, construction,
  counterexample), meets infinity properly (countable and uncountable
  sets), builds the number systems, proves limit statements with ε and δ,
  studies convergence of sequences and series, extends calculus to several
  variables and vector fields, and learns linear algebra as the study of
  vector spaces and linear maps rather than only of matrices.
- Conventions: N = {1, 2, 3, ...} unless 0 is stated; log means the
  natural logarithm ln; vectors in Rⁿ are columns unless written as rows;
  ⟨u, v⟩ is an inner product; "iff" means "if and only if"; □ ends a
  proof; angles are in radians; exact answers first, decimals with ≈.
- Method: read the definition, write out an example and a non-example,
  then prove the theorem yourself before reading the proof. For every
  computation, know which theorem justifies it and check its hypotheses.
  Use Python/SageMath (topic 9.24) to CHECK calculations and to explore,
  and write solutions in LaTeX (topic 9.25) from the first week.
- Do 90-120 minutes a day: 2 descriptive questions (write the full proof
  or solution) and 2 drills per session, plus weekly mixed revision.
- Revise Stage 8 (A8.txt) whenever the student finds something difficult;
  especially 8.1-8.6 (sets, functions, logic, induction) before 9.1-9.4,
  and 8.31-8.50 (calculus) before 9.5-9.12.


================================================================================
TOPIC 9.1 — PROOF WRITING AND MATHEMATICAL LANGUAGE (QUANTIFIERS, NEGATION,
SETS, FUNCTIONS)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A STATEMENT (proposition) is a sentence that is either true or false.
    Connectives: ¬P (not), P ∧ Q (and), P ∨ Q (inclusive or), P ⇒ Q (if P then
    Q), P ⇔ Q (if and only if). P ⇒ Q is FALSE only when P is true and Q is
    false; a false hypothesis makes the implication (vacuously) true. Two
    formulas are EQUIVALENT (≡) when they have the same truth table; a
    TAUTOLOGY is true in every row.
  - Key equivalences: P ⇒ Q ≡ ¬P ∨ Q ≡ ¬Q ⇒ ¬P (CONTRAPOSITIVE). The CONVERSE
    Q ⇒ P and the INVERSE ¬P ⇒ ¬Q are NOT equivalent to P ⇒ Q. De Morgan:
    ¬(P ∧ Q) ≡ ¬P ∨ ¬Q, ¬(P ∨ Q) ≡ ¬P ∧ ¬Q. Also ¬(P ⇒ Q) ≡ P ∧ ¬Q and
    P ⇒ (Q ⇒ R) ≡ (P ∧ Q) ⇒ R. 'P is sufficient for Q', 'Q is necessary for P'
    and 'P only if Q' all mean P ⇒ Q.
  - QUANTIFIERS: ∀x ∈ A (for every x in A), ∃x ∈ A (there exists at least one
    x in A), ∃!x (exactly one). ORDER MATTERS: ∀x ∃y (y may depend on x) is
    weaker than ∃y ∀x (one y works for all x). Quantifiers of the same kind
    commute: ∀x ∀y ≡ ∀y ∀x.
  - NEGATION: ¬(∀x P(x)) ≡ ∃x ¬P(x) and ¬(∃x P(x)) ≡ ∀x ¬P(x). To negate a
    long statement flip every quantifier from left to right and negate the
    inner part: ¬(∀ε > 0 ∃δ > 0 ∀x (A ⇒ B)) ≡ ∃ε > 0 ∀δ > 0 ∃x (A ∧ ¬B). The
    restriction 'ε > 0' is NOT negated.
  - PROOF METHODS: DIRECT (assume P, deduce Q); CONTRAPOSITIVE (assume ¬Q,
    deduce ¬P); CONTRADICTION (assume the statement false, reach an
    impossibility); CASES (split into exhaustive cases); INDUCTION (base case +
    step P(n) ⇒ P(n + 1); STRONG induction uses P(1), ..., P(n)); EXISTENCE
    (constructive: exhibit it; non-constructive: show it must exist);
    UNIQUENESS (suppose two, show they are equal); COUNTEREXAMPLE (one example
    disproves a ∀-statement). An example never proves a ∀-statement.
  - SETS in proofs: A ⊆ B means ∀x (x ∈ A ⇒ x ∈ B), proved by 'let x ∈ A ...
    so x ∈ B'. A = B is proved by DOUBLE INCLUSION. Indexed families:
    x ∈ ⋃ Aᵢ ⇔ ∃i x ∈ Aᵢ, and x ∈ ⋂ Aᵢ ⇔ ∀i x ∈ Aᵢ. The Archimedean property
    (for every ε > 0 there is n ∈ N with 1/n < ε) settles most interval
    examples such as ⋂ (0, 1/n) = ∅.
  - FUNCTIONS: f : A → B assigns to every a ∈ A exactly one f(a) ∈ B; A is the
    DOMAIN, B the CODOMAIN, f(A) the RANGE (image). f is INJECTIVE if
    f(x) = f(y) ⇒ x = y, SURJECTIVE if ∀b ∈ B ∃a ∈ A with f(a) = b, BIJECTIVE
    if both. Changing the domain or codomain changes these properties
    (x ↦ x² is neither on R → R but bijective on [0, ∞) → [0, ∞)).
  - COMPOSITION (g ∘ f)(x) = g(f(x)) is associative, not commutative. f has a
    two-sided INVERSE f⁻¹ exactly when f is bijective, and
    (g ∘ f)⁻¹ = f⁻¹ ∘ g⁻¹. If g ∘ f is injective then f is injective; if g ∘ f
    is surjective then g is surjective. A one-sided inverse (g ∘ f = id) makes
    f injective and g surjective, but need not be two-sided.
  - IMAGES AND PREIMAGES: f(S) = {f(s) : s ∈ S}; f⁻¹(T) = {a : f(a) ∈ T} makes
    sense even when f has no inverse. Preimages respect everything:
    f⁻¹(T ∪ U) = f⁻¹(T) ∪ f⁻¹(U), f⁻¹(T ∩ U) = f⁻¹(T) ∩ f⁻¹(U),
    f⁻¹(B − T) = A − f⁻¹(T). Images respect unions, but only
    f(S ∩ S′) ⊆ f(S) ∩ f(S′) (equality for injective f). Always
    S ⊆ f⁻¹(f(S)) and f(f⁻¹(T)) ⊆ T.
  - COUNTING for |A| = m, |B| = n: there are nᵐ functions A → B,
    n(n − 1)...(n − m + 1) injections (none if m > n), n! bijections when
    m = n, and ∑ₖ (−1)ᵏ C(n, k)(n − k)ᵐ surjections (inclusion-exclusion).
  - WRITING: say what each letter is ('Let n be an odd integer'), state the
    method ('Suppose, for a contradiction, ...'), give reasons for every step,
    finish with a conclusion and ∎. Traps: proving the converse instead,
    assuming what is to be proved, 'let x = 2' in a ∀-proof, dividing by
    something that may be 0, and using the same letter for two different
    objects (m, n odd: write m = 2k + 1, n = 2l + 1, not both 2k + 1).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Draw up one truth table for P ⇒ Q, ¬P ∨ Q, ¬Q ⇒ ¬P and Q ⇒ P. Which of
    them are equivalent? Then decide whether (P ⇒ Q) ⇒ R and P ⇒ (Q ⇒ R) are
    logically equivalent.
    Answer: P ⇒ Q, ¬P ∨ Q and ¬Q ⇒ ¬P are equivalent; the converse Q ⇒ P is
    not. (P ⇒ Q) ⇒ R and P ⇒ (Q ⇒ R) are NOT equivalent: they differ when P
    and R are false (whatever Q is).
    Teacher explains: List the four rows TT, TF, FT, FF and work column by
    column. An implication is false only in the row 'true ⇒ false':
       P  Q  |  P ⇒ Q  |  ¬P ∨ Q  |  ¬Q ⇒ ¬P  |  Q ⇒ P
       T  T  |    T    |    T     |     T     |    T
       T  F  |    F    |    F     |     F     |    T
       F  T  |    T    |    T     |     T     |    F
       F  F  |    T    |    T     |     T     |    T
    The first three columns agree in every row, so they are equivalent. The
    converse differs in rows 2 and 3. For the second question you do not need
    all 8 rows: look for a row where one side is false. P ⇒ (Q ⇒ R) is false
    only when P is true and Q ⇒ R is false, i.e. P = T, Q = T, R = F. Now try
    P = F, R = F:
       (P ⇒ Q) ⇒ R  =  T ⇒ F  =  F        P ⇒ (Q ⇒ R)  =  F ⇒ (...)  =  T
    One row of disagreement is enough to show the formulas are not
    equivalent. Why the row 'F ⇒ anything is T' makes sense: 'If you score
    100, I will buy you a bicycle' is not broken when you score 60, whatever
    I do. Common mistakes: treating ⇒ as 'and', and thinking a statement and
    its converse say the same thing. Now you try: show that P ⇒ (Q ⇒ R) and
    (P ∧ Q) ⇒ R are equivalent. (Both are false exactly in the one row
    P = T, Q = T, R = F, and true in the other seven.)

D2. For integers n consider S: 'If n² is even, then n is even.' Write its
    converse, inverse and contrapositive and decide which are true. Do the
    same for T: 'If x > 2 then x² > 4' (x real), and rewrite T using
    'sufficient', 'necessary' and 'only if'.
    Answer: For S all four statements are true. For T: statement true,
    converse 'x² > 4 ⇒ x > 2' false, inverse 'x ≤ 2 ⇒ x² ≤ 4' false,
    contrapositive 'x² ≤ 4 ⇒ x ≤ 2' true. 'x > 2 is sufficient for x² > 4';
    'x² > 4 is necessary for x > 2'; 'x > 2 only if x² > 4'.
    Teacher explains: For P ⇒ Q write the four forms side by side:
       statement        P ⇒ Q          if n² even then n even
       converse         Q ⇒ P          if n even then n² even
       inverse          ¬P ⇒ ¬Q        if n² odd then n odd
       contrapositive   ¬Q ⇒ ¬P        if n odd then n² odd
    The statement and its contrapositive always have the same truth value;
    the converse and the inverse always have the same truth value (the
    inverse is the contrapositive of the converse). For S, 'n odd ⇒ n² odd'
    is easy: n = 2k + 1 gives n² = 4k² + 4k + 1 = 2(2k² + 2k) + 1; that proves
    S. The converse 'n even ⇒ n² even' holds as well, so S is really an 'if
    and only if'. For T the converse fails with ONE counterexample: x = −3
    has x² = 9 > 4 but x < 2. The language: in P ⇒ Q, P is SUFFICIENT (enough)
    for Q, and Q is NECESSARY (cannot be avoided) for P. 'P only if Q' means
    P cannot happen without Q, which is again P ⇒ Q, NOT Q ⇒ P. Common
    mistake: reading 'only if' as 'if'. Now you try: is the converse of 'if
    f is differentiable at a then f is continuous at a' true? (No: f(x) = |x|
    is continuous but not differentiable at 0.)

D3. Decide whether each statement is true, and explain. (N = {1, 2, 3, ...})
    (a) ∀x ∈ R ∃y ∈ R : y > x          (b) ∃y ∈ R ∀x ∈ R : y > x
    (c) ∀x ∈ Z ∃y ∈ Z : x + y = 0      (d) ∀x ∈ N ∃y ∈ N : x + y = 0
    (e) ∃x ∈ R ∀y ∈ R : xy = 0         (f) ∀x ∈ R − {0} ∃y ∈ R : xy = 1
    (g) ∃!x ∈ R : x² = 0               (h) ∃!x ∈ R : x² = 1
    Answer: (a) T (b) F (c) T (d) F (e) T (f) T (g) T (h) F.
    Teacher explains: Read a quantified statement as a GAME. For ∀x you must
    win against every x your opponent chooses; for ∃y you choose y, and you
    may use the x already chosen.
       (a) opponent picks x, you answer y = x + 1 > x.          T
       (b) you must pick y FIRST; opponent answers x = y + 1.    F
       (c) given x, answer y = −x ∈ Z.                          T
       (d) x + y ≥ 2 for x, y ∈ N, so x + y = 0 is impossible.  F
       (e) pick x = 0; then 0·y = 0 for every y.                T
       (f) given x ≠ 0, answer y = 1/x.                         T
       (g) x² = 0 ⇔ x = 0: exists and unique.                   T
       (h) x = 1 and x = −1 both work: not unique.              F
    (a) and (b) contain the same words in a different order and have
    opposite truth values: in (a) y may depend on x, in (b) one y must beat
    every x. ∃!x P(x) means ∃x (P(x) ∧ ∀y (P(y) ⇒ y = x)). Common mistakes:
    reading (b) as (a); forgetting that N has no 0 or negatives. Now you try:
    ∀x ∈ Q ∃y ∈ Q : x < y < x + 1, and ∃x ∈ N ∀y ∈ N : x ≤ y. (True: take
    y = x + 1/2; true: x = 1.)

D4. Write the negation of each statement in positive form (no ¬ in front of a
    quantifier or bracket).
    (a) Every continuous function on [0, 1] is bounded.
    (b) lim (x→a) f(x) = L: ∀ε > 0 ∃δ > 0 ∀x (0 < |x − a| < δ ⇒
        |f(x) − L| < ε).
    (c) f : R → R is bounded: ∃M > 0 ∀x ∈ R : |f(x)| ≤ M.
    (d) The sequence (aₙ) is increasing: ∀n ∈ N : aₙ ≤ aₙ₊₁.
    (e) If it rains, then the match is cancelled.
    Answer: (a) There is a continuous function on [0, 1] that is not bounded.
    (b) ∃ε > 0 ∀δ > 0 ∃x : 0 < |x − a| < δ and |f(x) − L| ≥ ε.
    (c) ∀M > 0 ∃x ∈ R : |f(x)| > M. (d) ∃n ∈ N : aₙ > aₙ₊₁. (e) It rains
    and the match is not cancelled.
    Teacher explains: The rules are only three:
       ¬∀x P(x)  ≡  ∃x ¬P(x)
       ¬∃x P(x)  ≡  ∀x ¬P(x)
       ¬(P ⇒ Q)  ≡  P ∧ ¬Q
    Push the ¬ from left to right, flipping each quantifier as it passes:
       ¬ ∀ε>0 ∃δ>0 ∀x (A ⇒ B)
       ≡ ∃ε>0 ¬ ∃δ>0 ∀x (A ⇒ B)
       ≡ ∃ε>0 ∀δ>0 ¬ ∀x (A ⇒ B)
       ≡ ∃ε>0 ∀δ>0 ∃x ¬(A ⇒ B)
       ≡ ∃ε>0 ∀δ>0 ∃x (A ∧ ¬B)
    with A: 0 < |x − a| < δ and ¬B: |f(x) − L| ≥ ε. The restriction 'ε > 0'
    stays as it is: we are still talking about positive ε. In words: there
    is a tolerance ε that no δ can guarantee. The negation of an implication
    is NOT an implication: 'it rains and the match goes on' is what breaks
    the promise in (e). For (d), 'not increasing' does NOT mean 'decreasing';
    it means one step goes down somewhere. Common mistakes: negating
    'ε > 0' to 'ε ≤ 0'; writing the negation of (e) as 'if it rains the
    match is not cancelled'. Now you try: negate 'every student has read
    some book'. (There is a student who has read no book:
    ∃s ∀b, s has not read b.)

D5. Prove: (a) if m and n are odd integers then m + n is even and mn is odd;
    (b) if n² is even then n is even (by contrapositive); (c) for every integer
    n, n² + n is even (by cases); (d) for all real x, y,
    max(x, y) = (x + y + |x − y|)/2.
    Answer: Proofs below.
    Teacher explains: (a) DIRECT. Use different letters for the two numbers:
       m = 2k + 1,  n = 2l + 1        (k, l ∈ Z)
       m + n = 2k + 2l + 2 = 2(k + l + 1)                 even
       mn = 4kl + 2k + 2l + 1 = 2(2kl + k + l) + 1        odd
    Writing m = n = 2k + 1 would prove the result only for m = n.
    (b) CONTRAPOSITIVE: the direct route (n² = 2j, so n = √(2j) ...) goes
    nowhere. Prove instead 'n odd ⇒ n² odd': n = 2k + 1 gives
    n² = 2(2k² + 2k) + 1, odd. Since the contrapositive is equivalent, the
    statement is proved. (c) CASES: every integer is even or odd.
       n = 2k:      n² + n = 4k² + 2k = 2(2k² + k)             even
       n = 2k + 1:  n² + n = (2k + 1)(2k + 2) = 2(2k + 1)(k + 1) even
    (Quicker: n² + n = n(n + 1), a product of two consecutive integers, one
    of which is even.) (d) CASES on the sign of x − y:
       x ≥ y:  |x − y| = x − y,  (x + y + x − y)/2 = x = max(x, y)
       x < y:  |x − y| = y − x,  (x + y + y − x)/2 = y = max(x, y)
    Common mistakes: checking a few values of n instead of all; forgetting a
    case (for example x = y). Now you try: prove that if 3 does not divide n,
    then 3 divides n² − 1. (n = 3k ± 1 gives n² − 1 = 9k² ± 6k = 3(3k² ± 2k))

D6. Prove by contradiction: (a) √2 is irrational; (b) there are infinitely
    many primes; (c) log₂ 3 is irrational.
    Answer: Proofs below.
    Teacher explains: Contradiction: assume the statement is FALSE and derive
    something impossible. (a) Suppose √2 = p/q with p, q ∈ N and the fraction
    in lowest terms (gcd(p, q) = 1). Then
       2q² = p²   ⇒  p² even  ⇒  p even (D5(b)),  p = 2r
       2q² = 4r²  ⇒  q² = 2r² ⇒  q even
    So 2 divides both p and q, contradicting lowest terms. Hence √2 ∉ Q.
    (b) Suppose there are only finitely many primes p₁, p₂, ..., pₖ. Put
       N = p₁p₂...pₖ + 1.
    N > 1, so N has a prime factor p, which must be one of the pᵢ. But pᵢ
    divides p₁...pₖ and divides N, so it divides their difference 1:
    impossible. (Note: N itself need not be prime: 2·3·5·7·11·13 + 1 = 30031
    = 59 × 509.) (c) log₂ 3 > 0. Suppose log₂ 3 = p/q with p, q ∈ N. Then
    2^(p/q) = 3, so 2ᵖ = 3^q. The left side is even and the right side is odd:
    contradiction. Structure of a good write-up: 'Suppose, for a
    contradiction, that ...', then the argument, then 'This contradicts ...,
    so ...' ∎. Common mistakes: forgetting to assume lowest terms in (a);
    claiming in (b) that p₁...pₖ + 1 is always prime. Now you try: prove √3
    is irrational. (p² = 3q² ⇒ 3 | p, p = 3r ⇒ q² = 3r² ⇒ 3 | q, contradiction;
    it uses: 3 | p² ⇒ 3 | p, true because 3 is prime.)

D7. Prove by induction: (a) 1² + 2² + ... + n² = n(n + 1)(2n + 1)/6 for all
    n ∈ N; (b) 2ⁿ > n² for all n ≥ 5; (c) (strong induction) every integer
    n ≥ 12 can be written as 4a + 5b with integers a, b ≥ 0.
    Answer: Proofs below.
    Teacher explains: Induction has two parts, both compulsory: the BASE case
    and the STEP P(n) ⇒ P(n + 1). (a) Base n = 1: 1 = 1·2·3/6 ✓. Step: assume
    the formula for n (the induction hypothesis) and add (n + 1)²:
       n(n + 1)(2n + 1)/6 + (n + 1)²
         = (n + 1)[n(2n + 1) + 6(n + 1)]/6
         = (n + 1)(2n² + 7n + 6)/6
         = (n + 1)(n + 2)(2n + 3)/6
    which is the formula with n + 1 in place of n ✓. (b) Base n = 5:
    32 > 25 ✓. (For n = 4: 16 = 16, so the base must be 5.) Step: assume
    2ⁿ > n² with n ≥ 5. Then
       2ⁿ⁺¹ = 2·2ⁿ > 2n² = n² + n² ≥ n² + 3n > n² + 2n + 1 = (n + 1)²
    using n² ≥ 3n (as n ≥ 3) and 3n > 2n + 1 (as n > 1). (c) STRONG
    induction: check four bases, then step back by 4:
       12 = 4·3,  13 = 4·2 + 5,  14 = 4·1 + 5·2,  15 = 5·3
    For n ≥ 16, n − 4 ≥ 12, so by hypothesis n − 4 = 4a + 5b, and then
    n = 4(a + 1) + 5b ✓. Four base cases are needed because the step reaches
    back 4. Common mistakes: omitting the base (then 'n = n + 1' can be
    'proved'); using P(n + 1) inside its own proof; in strong induction
    checking too few base cases. Now you try: prove
    1/(1·2) + 1/(2·3) + ... + 1/(n(n + 1)) = n/(n + 1).
    (Base 1/2 ✓; step: n/(n + 1) + 1/((n + 1)(n + 2))
    = (n² + 2n + 1)/((n + 1)(n + 2)) = (n + 1)/(n + 2))

D8. (a) Prove that x³ + x − 1 = 0 has exactly one real solution. (b) Prove that
    there exist irrational a, b with aᵇ rational. (c) Disprove: 'n² + n + 41 is
    prime for every n ∈ N.' (d) Disprove: 'for all reals a, b,
    (a + b)² = a² + b².'
    Answer: (a) Existence by the intermediate value theorem on [0, 1],
    uniqueness since the function is strictly increasing. (b) Consider √2^√2
    (proof below). (c) n = 40: 1681 = 41². (d) a = b = 1: 4 ≠ 2.
    Teacher explains: 'Exactly one' needs TWO proofs. (a) EXISTENCE: let
    f(x) = x³ + x − 1, continuous, with
       f(0) = −1 < 0,   f(1) = 1 > 0
    so by the IVT f(c) = 0 for some c ∈ (0, 1). UNIQUENESS: if a < b then
    a³ < b³ and a < b, so f(a) < f(b): f is strictly increasing and cannot
    vanish twice. (b) A NON-CONSTRUCTIVE proof. Look at x = √2^√2. Either x
    is rational, and then a = b = √2 works; or x is irrational, and then take
    a = x, b = √2:
       aᵇ = (√2^√2)^√2 = √2^(√2·√2) = √2² = 2    rational
    Either way such a, b exist, although we have not said which case holds.
    (c) A single counterexample disproves a ∀-statement. The formula gives
    primes for n = 1, ..., 39 (43, 47, 53, ...), which proves nothing; at
    n = 40:
       40² + 40 + 41 = 40·41 + 41 = 41·41 = 1681
    (d) a = b = 1: (1 + 1)² = 4 but 1² + 1² = 2. Common mistakes: in (a)
    proving existence only; in (c) believing a pattern because it held 39
    times. Now you try: Fermat thought 2^(2ⁿ) + 1 is prime for all n ≥ 0.
    Disprove it. (n = 5: 2³² + 1 = 4294967297 = 641 × 6700417, found by
    Euler.)

D9. Prove: (a) A × (B ∪ C) = (A × B) ∪ (A × C); (b) A ⊆ B if and only if
    P(A) ⊆ P(B); (c) ⋃ (n ≥ 1) [1/n, 2] = (0, 2]; (d) ⋂ (n ≥ 1) (−1/n, 1/n) =
    {0}; (e) ⋂ (n ≥ 1) (0, 1/n) = ∅.
    Answer: Proofs below.
    Teacher explains: To prove X = Y, show X ⊆ Y and Y ⊆ X by chasing an
    arbitrary element. (a) For ordered pairs:
       (x, y) ∈ A × (B ∪ C) ⇔ x ∈ A and (y ∈ B or y ∈ C)
                            ⇔ (x ∈ A and y ∈ B) or (x ∈ A and y ∈ C)
                            ⇔ (x, y) ∈ (A × B) ∪ (A × C)
    Every element of a product is a pair, so this covers everything. (b) An
    'if and only if' needs both directions. (⇒) Let A ⊆ B and X ∈ P(A); then
    X ⊆ A ⊆ B, so X ∈ P(B). (⇐) Let P(A) ⊆ P(B). Since A ∈ P(A), A ∈ P(B),
    i.e. A ⊆ B. (c) Each [1/n, 2] ⊆ (0, 2], so the union is ⊆ (0, 2].
    Conversely let x ∈ (0, 2]. By the Archimedean property pick n with
    1/n < x; then x ∈ [1/n, 2], so x is in the union. Note 0 is in NO set of
    the family, so 0 is not in the union, although the sets creep towards 0.
    (d) 0 ∈ (−1/n, 1/n) for every n, so 0 is in the intersection. If x ≠ 0,
    pick n with 1/n < |x|; then x ∉ (−1/n, 1/n), so x is not in the
    intersection. (e) If x ≤ 0 then x ∉ (0, 1); if x > 0 pick n with
    1/n < x, so x ∉ (0, 1/n). No real number is in every set:
       ⋂ (0, 1/n) = ∅   although every set in the family is non-empty
                        and each contains the next.
    Common mistakes: 'the intersection is (0, 0) = {0}' in (e); treating ∞ as
    a value of n. Now you try: find ⋃ (n ≥ 1) [0, 1 − 1/n] and
    ⋂ (n ≥ 1) [0, 1 + 1/n). ([0, 1); [0, 1])

D10. Decide which of these functions are injective, surjective or bijective,
     and find the inverse of each bijection:
     (a) f : R → R, f(x) = 3x − 7      (b) g : R → R, g(x) = x²
     (c) h : Z → Z, h(n) = 2n          (d) k : R → [0, ∞), k(x) = x²
     (e) φ : R → (−1, 1), φ(x) = x/(1 + |x|)
     Answer: (a) bijective, f⁻¹(y) = (y + 7)/3. (b) neither. (c) injective,
     not surjective. (d) surjective, not injective. (e) bijective,
     φ⁻¹(y) = y/(1 − |y|).
     Teacher explains: INJECTIVE: start from f(x) = f(y) and deduce x = y.
     SURJECTIVE: start from an arbitrary b in the CODOMAIN and solve f(a) = b
     inside the domain. (a) 3x − 7 = 3y − 7 ⇒ x = y; and 3x − 7 = b has the
     solution x = (b + 7)/3 ∈ R. (b) g(−1) = g(1) = 1, not injective; −1 is
     never a value of x², not surjective. (c) 2m = 2n ⇒ m = n; 1 = 2n has no
     integer solution. (d) Same rule as (b) but codomain [0, ∞): every b ≥ 0
     equals (√b)², so surjective; still −1 and 1 collide. (e) φ(x) has the
     same sign as x and |φ(x)| = |x|/(1 + |x|) < 1, so φ maps into (−1, 1).
       injective:  if φ(x) = φ(y), x and y have the same sign and
                   |x|/(1 + |x|) = |y|/(1 + |y|) ⇒ |x| + |x||y| = |y| + |x||y|
                   ⇒ |x| = |y| ⇒ x = y
       surjective: given y ∈ (−1, 1), put x = y/(1 − |y|). Then
                   1 + |x| = 1/(1 − |y|), so φ(x) = x(1 − |y|) = y ✓
     Lesson: a formula alone is not a function; the properties depend on the
     domain and the codomain. Common mistakes: testing surjectivity against
     the domain instead of the codomain; 'proving' injectivity from
     x = y ⇒ f(x) = f(y) (that is true for every function). Now you try: is
     f : N → N, f(n) = n + 1, injective? surjective? (Injective; not
     surjective, since 1 is not a value.)

D11. (a) For f(x) = 2x + 1 and g(x) = x² on R find g ∘ f and f ∘ g. (b) Prove:
     if f : A → B and g : B → C are injective then g ∘ f is injective. (c)
     Prove: if g ∘ f is injective then f is injective; if g ∘ f is surjective
     then g is surjective. (d) With f, g : N → N, f(n) = n + 1, g(1) = 1 and
     g(n) = n − 1 for n ≥ 2, find g ∘ f and f ∘ g, and comment.
     Answer: (a) (g ∘ f)(x) = 4x² + 4x + 1, (f ∘ g)(x) = 2x² + 1. (b), (c)
     below. (d) g ∘ f = id_N but f ∘ g ≠ id_N (f(g(1)) = 2).
     Teacher explains: (a) g ∘ f means 'first f, then g':
        (g ∘ f)(x) = g(2x + 1) = (2x + 1)² = 4x² + 4x + 1
        (f ∘ g)(x) = f(x²) = 2x² + 1
     These differ (at x = 1: 9 and 3), so composition is not commutative.
     (b) Let g(f(x)) = g(f(y)). g injective gives f(x) = f(y); f injective
     gives x = y. (c) If f(x) = f(y) then g(f(x)) = g(f(y)), and injectivity
     of g ∘ f gives x = y. For the second part let c ∈ C; g ∘ f surjective
     gives a with g(f(a)) = c, so b = f(a) satisfies g(b) = c. (d) g(f(n)) =
     g(n + 1) = n for all n, so g ∘ f = id. But
        f(g(1)) = f(1) = 2 ≠ 1
     so g is only a LEFT inverse of f. Consistent with (c): f is injective
     (not surjective), g is surjective (not injective, since g(1) = g(2) = 1).
     A TWO-sided inverse exists exactly when f is bijective. Common mistakes:
     computing g ∘ f as 'g first'; assuming g ∘ f = id forces f ∘ g = id (true
     for finite sets of equal size, false in general). Now you try: check
     that f(x) = (x − 1)/2 and g(x) = 2x + 1 are inverse bijections of R.
     (f(g(x)) = (2x + 1 − 1)/2 = x and g(f(x)) = (x − 1) + 1 = x)

D12. Let f : R → R, f(x) = x². Find f([−1, 2]), f⁻¹([1, 4]), f⁻¹([−3, −1]),
     f⁻¹((−1, 4)) and f⁻¹(f([0, 1])). With A = [−2, −1] and B = [1, 2]
     compare f(A ∩ B) with f(A) ∩ f(B). Prove that f(S ∩ T) ⊆ f(S) ∩ f(T) for
     every function, with equality when f is injective. Finally count the
     functions, injections and surjections from a 3-element set to a
     4-element set, and from the 4-element set to the 3-element set.
     Answer: f([−1, 2]) = [0, 4]; f⁻¹([1, 4]) = [−2, −1] ∪ [1, 2];
     f⁻¹([−3, −1]) = ∅; f⁻¹((−1, 4)) = (−2, 2); f⁻¹(f([0, 1])) = [−1, 1].
     f(A ∩ B) = ∅ but f(A) ∩ f(B) = [1, 4]. 3 → 4: 64 functions, 24
     injections, 0 surjections. 4 → 3: 81 functions, 0 injections, 36
     surjections.
     Teacher explains: f(S) is 'where S goes'; f⁻¹(T) is 'what lands in T'.
     No inverse function is needed for f⁻¹(T).
        f([−1, 2]):  x² runs over [0, 1] on [−1, 0] and [0, 4] on [0, 2]
                     → [0, 4]     (NOT [1, 4]: 0 is a value)
        f⁻¹([1, 4]): 1 ≤ x² ≤ 4 ⇔ 1 ≤ |x| ≤ 2 → [−2, −1] ∪ [1, 2]
        f⁻¹((−1, 4)): x² < 4 (x² > −1 always) → (−2, 2)
        f⁻¹(f([0, 1])) = f⁻¹([0, 1]) = [−1, 1] ⊋ [0, 1]
     For the proof: if y ∈ f(S ∩ T) then y = f(x) with x ∈ S and x ∈ T, so
     y ∈ f(S) and y ∈ f(T). The reverse fails because y may come from an x in
     S and a DIFFERENT x′ in T, exactly as −1 and 1 do above. If f is
     injective then x = x′ and equality holds. Counting (|A| = 3, |B| = 4):
        functions      4³ = 64          (each of 3 inputs: 4 choices)
        injections     4·3·2 = 24
        surjections    0                (3 inputs cannot cover 4 outputs)
     and from the 4-set to the 3-set: 3⁴ = 81 functions, no injections, and
     by inclusion-exclusion (remove the maps missing some value)
        3⁴ − C(3, 1)·2⁴ + C(3, 2)·1⁴ = 81 − 48 + 3 = 36 surjections.
     Common mistakes: f⁻¹([1, 4]) = [1, 2] (forgetting negatives); counting
     surjections as 3⁴ − 3·2⁴ = 33 (the maps missing TWO values were removed
     twice). Now you try: how many surjections are there from a 4-element set
     onto a 2-element set? (2⁴ − 2 = 14)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. P and Q are true, R is false. Find the truth value (T or F) of:
    a) P ∧ Q                    b) P ∧ R
    c) Q ∨ R                    d) ¬P ∨ R
    e) P ⇒ R                    f) R ⇒ P
    g) R ⇒ ¬Q                   h) P ⇔ R
    i) ¬(P ∧ R)                 j) (P ∨ R) ⇒ Q
    k) (P ⇒ Q) ⇒ R              l) P ⇒ (Q ⇒ R)
    m) (R ⇒ P) ∧ (P ⇒ R)        n) ¬P ⇔ R
    o) (P ∧ ¬Q) ∨ R             p) (¬R ⇒ Q) ∧ ¬(Q ⇒ R)
    q) (P ∨ Q) ⇒ (P ∧ R)        r) (R ∧ P) ⇒ ¬Q
    s) (P ⇒ R) ⇒ R              t) (P ⇔ Q) ⇔ R
    Answers: a) T   b) F   c) T   d) F   e) F   f) T   g) T   h) F   i) T   j) T
             k) F   l) F   m) F   n) T   o) F   p) T   q) F   r) T   s) T   t) F

M2. Are the two formulas logically equivalent? Answer E (equivalent) or N (not);
    for N a row of disagreement is given.
    a) P ⇒ Q;  ¬P ∨ Q                      b) P ⇒ Q;  Q ⇒ P
    c) P ⇒ Q;  ¬Q ⇒ ¬P                     d) ¬(P ∧ Q);  ¬P ∨ ¬Q
    e) ¬(P ∨ Q);  ¬P ∨ ¬Q                  f) ¬(P ⇒ Q);  P ∧ ¬Q
    g) P ⇔ Q;  (P ⇒ Q) ∧ (Q ⇒ P)           h) P ⇒ (Q ⇒ R);  (P ∧ Q) ⇒ R
    i) (P ⇒ Q) ⇒ R;  P ⇒ (Q ⇒ R)           j) P ∧ (Q ∨ R);  (P ∧ Q) ∨ (P ∧ R)
    k) P ∨ (Q ∧ R);  (P ∨ Q) ∧ R           l) (P ⇒ R) ∧ (Q ⇒ R);  (P ∨ Q) ⇒ R
    m) (P ⇒ Q) ∨ (P ⇒ R);  P ⇒ (Q ∨ R)     n) (P ⇒ R) ∨ (Q ⇒ R);  (P ∧ Q) ⇒ R
    o) (P ⇒ Q) ∧ (P ⇒ R);  P ⇒ (Q ∧ R)     p) ¬(P ⇔ Q);  P ⇔ ¬Q
    q) (P ⇒ Q) ∧ (Q ⇒ R);  P ⇒ R           r) P ⇔ Q;  (P ∧ Q) ∨ (¬P ∧ ¬Q)
    s) (P ∨ Q) ⇒ R;  (P ⇒ R) ∨ (Q ⇒ R)     t) P ∧ (P ∨ Q);  P
    Answers:
      a) E   b) N (P = T, Q = F)   c) E   d) E   e) N (P = T, Q = F)   f) E
      g) E   h) E   i) N (P = F, R = F)   j) E   k) N (P = T, R = F)   l) E
      m) E   n) E   o) E   p) E   q) N (P = F, Q = T, R = F: left F, right T)
      r) E   s) N (P = T, Q = F, R = F: left F, right T)   t) E (absorption)

M3. True (T) or false (F)? (N = {1, 2, 3, ...})
    a) ∀x ∈ R : x² ≥ 0                     b) ∀x ∈ R : x² > 0
    c) ∃x ∈ R : x² = 2                     d) ∃x ∈ Q : x² = 2
    e) ∀x ∈ R ∃y ∈ R : y² = x              f) ∀x ∈ R ∃y ∈ R : y³ = x
    g) ∀x ∈ N ∃y ∈ N : y > x               h) ∃y ∈ N ∀x ∈ N : y > x
    i) ∃x ∈ N ∀y ∈ N : x ≤ y               j) ∃x ∈ Z ∀y ∈ Z : x ≤ y
    k) ∀x ∈ Z ∃y ∈ Z : x + y = 1           l) ∃y ∈ Z ∀x ∈ Z : x + y = x
    m) ∀x ∈ R ∃y ∈ R : xy = 1              n) ∀x < y in R ∃z ∈ Q : x < z < y
    o) ∃!x ∈ R : x³ = 8                    p) ∃!x ∈ R : x² = 4
    q) ∃!x ∈ N : x² − 5x + 6 = 0           r) ∀ε > 0 ∃n ∈ N : 1/n < ε
    s) ∃n ∈ N ∀ε > 0 : 1/n < ε             t) ∀x ∈ R ∃n ∈ Z : n ≤ x < n + 1
    u) ∀x ∈ R : (x > 1 ⇒ x² > 1)           v) ∀x ∈ R : (x² > 1 ⇒ x > 1)
    w) ∀x ∈ ∅ : x² < 0                     x) ∃x ∈ ∅ : x = x
    Answers: a) T   b) F   c) T   d) F   e) F   f) T   g) T   h) F   i) T   j) F
             k) T   l) T   m) F   n) T   o) T   p) F   q) F   r) T   s) F   t) T
             u) T   v) F   w) T (vacuously)   x) F

M4. Write the negation in positive form (no ¬ before a quantifier or bracket).
    a) ∀x ∈ R : x² ≥ 0
    b) ∃x ∈ Q : x² = 2
    c) ∀x ∃y : x + y = 0
    d) ∃y ∀x : y > x
    e) P ∧ Q
    f) P ⇒ Q
    g) P ⇔ Q
    h) ∀x (P(x) ⇒ Q(x))
    i) ∃x (P(x) ∧ Q(x))
    j) ∀x ∈ A : f(x) ≤ M
    k) ∃M ∀x : |f(x)| ≤ M                  (f is bounded)
    l) ∀n : aₙ ≤ aₙ₊₁                       (increasing)
    m) ∀x, y : (f(x) = f(y) ⇒ x = y)       (injective)
    n) ∀y ∈ B ∃x ∈ A : f(x) = y            (surjective)
    o) ∀ε > 0 ∃N ∀n ≥ N : |aₙ − L| < ε     (aₙ → L)
    p) ∀ε > 0 ∃δ > 0 ∀x : (|x − a| < δ ⇒ |f(x) − f(a)| < ε)
    q) ∀ε > 0 ∃δ > 0 ∀x, y : (|x − y| < δ ⇒ |f(x) − f(y)| < ε)
    r) ∀M ∃N ∀n ≥ N : aₙ > M               (aₙ → ∞)
    s) Every prime greater than 2 is odd.
    t) Some integer is both even and odd.
    u) x ≤ 3 or y > 5
    v) ∃!x : P(x)
    Answers:
      a) ∃x ∈ R : x² < 0
      b) ∀x ∈ Q : x² ≠ 2
      c) ∃x ∀y : x + y ≠ 0
      d) ∀y ∃x : y ≤ x
      e) ¬P ∨ ¬Q
      f) P ∧ ¬Q
      g) P ⇔ ¬Q   (equivalently (P ∧ ¬Q) ∨ (¬P ∧ Q))
      h) ∃x (P(x) ∧ ¬Q(x))
      i) ∀x (P(x) ⇒ ¬Q(x))
      j) ∃x ∈ A : f(x) > M
      k) ∀M ∃x : |f(x)| > M
      l) ∃n : aₙ > aₙ₊₁
      m) ∃x, y : f(x) = f(y) and x ≠ y
      n) ∃y ∈ B ∀x ∈ A : f(x) ≠ y
      o) ∃ε > 0 ∀N ∃n ≥ N : |aₙ − L| ≥ ε
      p) ∃ε > 0 ∀δ > 0 ∃x : |x − a| < δ and |f(x) − f(a)| ≥ ε
      q) ∃ε > 0 ∀δ > 0 ∃x, y : |x − y| < δ and |f(x) − f(y)| ≥ ε
      r) ∃M ∀N ∃n ≥ N : aₙ ≤ M
      s) There is a prime greater than 2 that is even.
      t) No integer is both even and odd (every integer is not even or not
         odd).
      u) x > 3 and y ≤ 5
      v) ∀x ¬P(x), or ∃x ∃y (x ≠ y ∧ P(x) ∧ P(y))  (none, or at least two)

M5. For each statement give the truth value of (S) the statement, (C) its
    converse, (P) its contrapositive. x, y are real, n is an integer.
    a) If x = 2 then x² = 4.
    b) If 6 divides n then 3 divides n.
    c) If x > 0 then x³ > 0.
    d) If n² is odd then n is odd.
    e) If x² < 1 then x < 1.
    f) If a triangle is equilateral then it is isosceles.
    g) If xy = 0 then x = 0.
    h) If n is prime then n is odd.
    i) If x is rational then x² is rational.
    j) If x + y is irrational then x or y is irrational.
    k) If f is differentiable at a then f is continuous at a.
    l) If a sequence converges then it is bounded.
    m) If n is divisible by 4 then n is even.
    n) If x² = x then x = 1.
    Answers (S, C, P):
      a) T, F (x = −2), T      b) T, F (n = 3), T      c) T, T, T
      d) T, T, T               e) T, F (x = −2), T     f) T, F, T
      g) F (x = 1, y = 0), T, F                       h) F (n = 2), F (n = 9), F
      i) T, F (x = √2), T      j) T, F (√2 + (−√2) = 0), T
      k) T, F (|x| at 0), T    l) T, F ((−1)ⁿ), T      m) T, F (n = 2), T
      n) F (x = 0), T, F
      (S and P always agree: a statement and its contrapositive are
      equivalent.)

M6. Prove (proof sketches given).
    a) The product of two odd integers is odd.
    b) If n is even then 4 divides n².
    c) If 3n + 2 is odd then n is odd.
    d) If 3 divides n² then 3 divides n.
    e) √3 is irrational.
    f) For every integer n, 6 divides n³ − n.
    g) For all real x, x² − 6x + 10 > 0.
    h) If x > 0 then x + 1/x ≥ 2.
    i) There is no smallest positive rational number.
    j) For integers a, b, a² − 4b ≠ 2.
    k) For every integer n, n² leaves remainder 0 or 1 on division by 4.
    l) If a | b and b | c then a | c.
    m) The sum of a rational and an irrational number is irrational.
    n) min(x, y) = (x + y − |x − y|)/2.
    o) log₂ 5 is irrational.
    p) Among any three integers, two have an even sum.
    Answers:
      a) (2k + 1)(2l + 1) = 2(2kl + k + l) + 1.
      b) n = 2k ⇒ n² = 4k².
      c) Contrapositive: n = 2k ⇒ 3n + 2 = 2(3k + 1) is even.
      d) Contrapositive: n = 3k ± 1 ⇒ n² = 3(3k² ± 2k) + 1, not divisible by 3.
      e) p² = 3q² in lowest terms ⇒ 3 | p (by d), p = 3r ⇒ q² = 3r² ⇒ 3 | q,
         contradiction.
      f) n³ − n = (n − 1)n(n + 1): among three consecutive integers one is
         even and one is a multiple of 3.
      g) x² − 6x + 10 = (x − 3)² + 1 ≥ 1 > 0.
      h) x + 1/x − 2 = (x − 1)²/x ≥ 0 for x > 0.
      i) If r > 0 were the smallest, r/2 is a smaller positive rational.
      j) a² ≡ 0 or 1 (mod 4) and a² − 4b ≡ a² (mod 4), never 2.
      k) Cases n = 2k: n² = 4k²; n = 2k + 1: n² = 4(k² + k) + 1.
      l) b = ak, c = bl ⇒ c = a(kl).
      m) If r + x = s with r, s ∈ Q then x = s − r ∈ Q, contradiction.
      n) Cases x ≤ y: right side = (x + y − (y − x))/2 = x; x > y gives y.
      o) log₂ 5 = p/q ⇒ 2ᵖ = 5^q, even = odd (p, q ≥ 1), contradiction.
      p) Only two parities exist, so two of the three share a parity
         (pigeonhole); same parity ⇒ even sum.

M7. Prove by induction for n ∈ N (base and key step given).
    a) 1 + 3 + 5 + ... + (2n − 1) = n²
    b) 1³ + 2³ + ... + n³ = (n(n + 1)/2)²
    c) 1·1! + 2·2! + ... + n·n! = (n + 1)! − 1
    d) 1 + 2 + 4 + ... + 2ⁿ⁻¹ = 2ⁿ − 1
    e) 1·2 + 2·3 + ... + n(n + 1) = n(n + 1)(n + 2)/3
    f) 3 divides 4ⁿ − 1
    g) 5 divides n⁵ − n
    h) 7 divides 3²ⁿ − 2ⁿ
    i) n! > 2ⁿ for n ≥ 4
    j) (1 + x)ⁿ ≥ 1 + nx for x ≥ −1 (Bernoulli)
    k) F₁ + F₂ + ... + Fₙ = Fₙ₊₂ − 1 (Fibonacci, F₁ = F₂ = 1)
    l) Fₙ < 2ⁿ (strong induction)
    m) Every n ≥ 8 is 3a + 5b with a, b ≥ 0 (strong induction)
    n) d/dx xⁿ = nxⁿ⁻¹ (using the product rule)
    o) Find the error: 'all horses have the same colour' (step from a set of
       n horses to n + 1 by removing one horse at a time).
    Answers:
      a) Base 1 = 1. Step: n² + (2n + 1) = (n + 1)².
      b) Base 1 = 1. Step: n²(n + 1)²/4 + (n + 1)³ = (n + 1)²(n² + 4n + 4)/4
         = ((n + 1)(n + 2)/2)².
      c) Base 1 = 2! − 1. Step: (n + 1)! − 1 + (n + 1)(n + 1)!
         = (n + 2)(n + 1)! − 1 = (n + 2)! − 1.
      d) Base 1 = 2 − 1. Step: 2ⁿ − 1 + 2ⁿ = 2ⁿ⁺¹ − 1.
      e) Base 2 = 1·2·3/3. Step: n(n + 1)(n + 2)/3 + (n + 1)(n + 2)
         = (n + 1)(n + 2)(n + 3)/3.
      f) Base 3. Step: 4ⁿ⁺¹ − 1 = 4(4ⁿ − 1) + 3.
      g) Base 0. Step: (n + 1)⁵ − (n + 1) = (n⁵ − n) + 5(n⁴ + 2n³ + 2n² + n).
      h) Base 9 − 2 = 7. Step: 9ⁿ⁺¹ − 2ⁿ⁺¹ = 9(9ⁿ − 2ⁿ) + 7·2ⁿ.
      i) Base 24 > 16. Step: (n + 1)! = (n + 1)n! > 2·2ⁿ.
      j) Base 1 + x ≥ 1 + x. Step: (1 + x)ⁿ⁺¹ ≥ (1 + nx)(1 + x)
         = 1 + (n + 1)x + nx² ≥ 1 + (n + 1)x (uses 1 + x ≥ 0).
      k) Base F₁ = 1 = F₃ − 1. Step: Fₙ₊₂ − 1 + Fₙ₊₁ = Fₙ₊₃ − 1.
      l) Bases F₁ = 1 < 2, F₂ = 1 < 4. Step: Fₙ₊₁ = Fₙ + Fₙ₋₁ < 2ⁿ + 2ⁿ⁻¹ < 2ⁿ⁺¹.
      m) Bases 8 = 3 + 5, 9 = 3·3, 10 = 5·2. Step: n ≥ 11, n − 3 ≥ 8 is
         3a + 5b, so n = 3(a + 1) + 5b.
      n) Base d/dx x = 1. Step: xⁿ⁺¹ = x·xⁿ ⇒ derivative
         xⁿ + x·nxⁿ⁻¹ = (n + 1)xⁿ.
      o) The step fails from n = 1 to n = 2: the two sets of n = 1 horses
         (all but the first, all but the last) do not overlap, so nothing
         links the colours.

M8. Find each union or intersection (n runs over N unless stated).
    a) ⋃ [1/n, 1]                  b) ⋂ [1/n, 1]
    c) ⋃ (−n, n)                   d) ⋂ (−n, n)
    e) ⋂ (−1/n, 1/n)               f) ⋂ (0, 1/n)
    g) ⋂ [0, 1/n]                  h) ⋃ [0, 1 − 1/n]
    i) ⋂ [0, 1 + 1/n)              j) ⋃ (n ∈ Z) [n, n + 1)
    k) ⋂ [n, ∞)                    l) ⋃ (1/n, 2 − 1/n)
    m) ⋂ (1 − 1/n, 2 + 1/n)        n) ⋃ (n = 1 to 5) {n, n + 1}
    o) ⋂ (n = 1 to 5) {k ∈ N : k ≥ n}
    p) ⋂ nZ                        q) ⋃ (n ≥ 2) nZ
    r) ⋂ (−1/n, 1]                 s) ⋃ (x ∈ [0, 1]) [x, x + 1]
    t) ⋂ (r ∈ R, r > 0) (−r, r)
    Answers:
      a) (0, 1]        b) {1}           c) R             d) (−1, 1)
      e) {0}           f) ∅             g) {0}           h) [0, 1)
      i) [0, 1]        j) R             k) ∅             l) (0, 2)
      m) [1, 2]        n) {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}             o) {5, 6, 7, ...}
      p) {0}           q) Z − {−1, 1}   r) [0, 1]        s) [0, 2]
      t) {0}

M9. Prove (T, with a sketch) or disprove (F, with a counterexample), for all
    sets A, B, C, D.
    a) A − (B ∪ C) = (A − B) ∩ (A − C)
    b) A − (B ∩ C) = (A − B) ∪ (A − C)
    c) A − (B − C) = (A − B) ∪ (A ∩ C)
    d) (A − B) − C = A − (B ∪ C)
    e) A ∪ (B − C) = (A ∪ B) − C
    f) A × (B ∩ C) = (A × B) ∩ (A × C)
    g) (A × B) ∪ (C × D) = (A ∪ C) × (B ∪ D)
    h) (A × B) ∩ (C × D) = (A ∩ C) × (B ∩ D)
    i) P(A ∩ B) = P(A) ∩ P(B)
    j) P(A ∪ B) = P(A) ∪ P(B)
    k) If A ∪ B = A ∪ C then B = C.
    l) If A ∪ B = A ∪ C and A ∩ B = A ∩ C then B = C.
    m) If A ⊆ B then B′ ⊆ A′.
    n) A ⊆ B if and only if A ∪ B = B.
    o) (A Δ B) Δ B = A
    p) If A × B = B × A then A = B.
    Answers:
      a) T: x ∈ A, x ∉ B and x ∉ C (De Morgan).
      b) T: x ∈ A and (x ∉ B or x ∉ C).
      c) T: x ∈ A and not (x ∈ B and x ∉ C) ⇔ x ∈ A and (x ∉ B or x ∈ C).
      d) T: both say x ∈ A, x ∉ B, x ∉ C.
      e) F: A = C = {1}, B = ∅: left {1}, right ∅.
      f) T: (x, y) with x ∈ A, y ∈ B and y ∈ C.
      g) F: A = B = {1}, C = D = {2}: (1, 2) is on the right only.
      h) T: (x, y) with x ∈ A ∩ C and y ∈ B ∩ D.
      i) T: X ⊆ A ∩ B ⇔ X ⊆ A and X ⊆ B.
      j) F: A = {1}, B = {2}: {1, 2} ∈ P(A ∪ B) only.
      k) F: A = {1}, B = {1}, C = ∅.
      l) T: if x ∈ B, either x ∈ A (then x ∈ A ∩ B = A ∩ C) or x ∉ A (then
         x ∈ A ∪ C, so x ∈ C); so B ⊆ C, and symmetrically.
      m) T: x ∉ B ⇒ x ∉ A (contrapositive of A ⊆ B).
      n) T: (⇒) A ∪ B ⊆ B ∪ B = B; (⇐) A ⊆ A ∪ B = B.
      o) T: Δ is associative and B Δ B = ∅, A Δ ∅ = A.
      p) F: A = ∅, B = {1} (both products are ∅); true if A, B ≠ ∅.

M10. Is the function injective (I only), surjective (S only), bijective (B) or
     neither (N)? For B give the inverse.
     a) R → R, x ↦ 5x + 2               b) R → R, x ↦ x²
     c) [0, ∞) → [0, ∞), x ↦ x²         d) R → [0, ∞), x ↦ x²
     e) [0, ∞) → R, x ↦ x²              f) R → R, x ↦ x³
     g) R → R, x ↦ x³ − x               h) R → R, x ↦ eˣ
     i) R → (0, ∞), x ↦ eˣ              j) (0, ∞) → R, x ↦ ln x
     k) R → R, x ↦ sin x                l) [−π/2, π/2] → [−1, 1], x ↦ sin x
     m) Z → Z, n ↦ n + 3                n) Z → Z, n ↦ 2n
     o) Z → Z, n ↦ ⌊n/2⌋                p) N → N, n ↦ n + 1
     q) R → R, x ↦ ⌊x⌋                  r) R − {1} → R − {2},
                                           x ↦ (2x + 1)/(x − 1)
     s) R → (−1, 1), x ↦ x/(1 + |x|)    t) Z × Z → Z, (m, n) ↦ m + n
     u) Z → Z × Z, n ↦ (n, n)           v) R² → R², (x, y) ↦ (x + y, x − y)
     w) N → Z, n ↦ n/2 (n even), −(n − 1)/2 (n odd)
     x) P({1, 2, 3}) → {0, 1, 2, 3}, X ↦ |X|
     Answers:
       a) B, (x − 2)/5        b) N                   c) B, √x
       d) S                   e) I                   f) B, ∛x
       g) S (0 ↦ 0 three times: x = −1, 0, 1)        h) I
       i) B, ln x             j) B, eˣ               k) N
       l) B, arcsin x         m) B, n − 3            n) I
       o) S (0 and 1 ↦ 0)     p) I                   q) N
       r) B, (x + 1)/(x − 2)  s) B, x/(1 − |x|)      t) S
       u) I                   v) B, (u, v) ↦ ((u + v)/2, (u − v)/2)
       w) B, m ↦ 2m (m > 0), 1 − 2m (m ≤ 0)          x) S

M11. Images and preimages. f(x) = x² (R → R), g(x) = sin x (R → R),
     h(n) = 2n (Z → Z), ⌊x⌋ the floor function, e(x) = eˣ.
     a) f([0, 3])               b) f([−2, 1])
     c) f((−1, 1))              d) f({−2, −1, 0, 1, 2})
     e) f⁻¹([0, 9])             f) f⁻¹([4, 9])
     g) f⁻¹((−∞, 0))            h) f⁻¹({1})
     i) f⁻¹((1, 4))             j) f(f⁻¹([−4, 4]))
     k) f⁻¹(f([1, 2]))          l) g([0, π])
     m) g([0, 2π])              n) g⁻¹({0})
     o) g⁻¹({1})                p) g⁻¹([2, 3])
     q) h(Z)                    r) h⁻¹({1, 2, 3, 4})
     s) h⁻¹(h({1, 2, 3}))       t) ⌊·⌋⁻¹({2})
     u) ⌊·⌋([0, 2.5])           v) ⌊·⌋⁻¹({0, 1})
     w) e((−∞, 0])              x) e⁻¹([1, e²])
     Answers:
       a) [0, 9]                    b) [0, 4]
       c) [0, 1)                    d) {0, 1, 4}
       e) [−3, 3]                   f) [−3, −2] ∪ [2, 3]
       g) ∅                         h) {−1, 1}
       i) (−2, −1) ∪ (1, 2)         j) [0, 4]
       k) [−2, −1] ∪ [1, 2]         l) [0, 1]
       m) [−1, 1]                   n) {kπ : k ∈ Z}
       o) {π/2 + 2kπ : k ∈ Z}       p) ∅
       q) 2Z (the even integers)    r) {1, 2}
       s) {1, 2, 3}                 t) [2, 3)
       u) {0, 1, 2}                 v) [0, 2)
       w) (0, 1]                    x) [0, 2]

M12. Composition, inverses and counting. For a)–e): f(x) = 2x + 3, g(x) = x².
     a) (g ∘ f)(x)                      b) (f ∘ g)(x)
     c) (f ∘ f)(x)                      d) (g ∘ f)(1)
     e) (f ∘ g)(−2)                     f) f⁻¹(x) for f(x) = 2x + 3
     g) (f ∘ f)⁻¹(x) for f(x) = 2x + 3  h) f⁻¹ for f(x) = (x + 1)/(x − 2),
                                           R − {2} → R − {1}
     i) f⁻¹ for f(x) = x³ + 1           j) f⁻¹ for f(x) = e^(2x − 1), R → (0, ∞)
     k) functions from a 3-set to a 5-set
     l) injections from a 3-set to a 5-set
     m) functions from a 5-set to a 3-set
     n) surjections from a 5-set onto a 3-set
     o) surjections from a 4-set onto a 3-set
     p) surjections from a 4-set onto a 2-set
     q) bijections from a 5-set to itself
     r) functions {1, ..., 10} → {0, 1}
     s) strictly increasing functions {1, 2, 3} → {1, ..., 6}
     t) non-decreasing functions {1, 2, 3} → {1, 2, 3, 4}
     u) functions f : {1, 2, 3} → {1, 2, 3} with f ∘ f = id
     v) bijections of {1, 2, 3, 4} with no fixed point
     w) injections from a 2-set to a 6-set
     x) surjections from a 6-set onto a 3-set
     Answers:
       a) 4x² + 12x + 9    b) 2x² + 3    c) 4x + 9    d) 25    e) 11
       f) (x − 3)/2        g) (x − 9)/4    h) (2x + 1)/(x − 1)
       i) ∛(x − 1)         j) (1 + ln x)/2
       k) 125   l) 60   m) 243   n) 150   o) 36   p) 14   q) 120   r) 1024
       s) 20   t) 20   u) 4   v) 9   w) 30   x) 540

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: insist on a truth table whenever an implication feels odd, and
  read every quantified statement as a game ('you pick, I answer'); negate by
  the three rules only, one symbol at a time, writing each line; before any
  proof write 'Assume ...' and 'Want: ...' on two lines, and for functions
  always write the domain and codomain before deciding anything. Draw the
  graph of x² to answer image and preimage questions. Harder: prove that
  f : A → B has a left inverse iff it is injective (A ≠ ∅) and a right
  inverse iff it is surjective (this uses the axiom of choice); show that
  f(f⁻¹(T)) = T for all T iff f is surjective and f⁻¹(f(S)) = S for all S
  iff f is injective; prove that ∀x ∃y P ⇒ ∃y ∀x P fails but the converse
  holds; show that the number of surjections from an n-set onto a k-set is
  k!·S(n, k) and check it for n = 5, k = 3 (150 = 6 × 25); and explain why
  'for every ε > 0 there is δ > 0 ...' with δ allowed to depend on x as well
  is continuity, while δ independent of x is uniform continuity.


================================================================================
TOPIC 9.2 — NAIVE SET THEORY: CARDINALITY, COUNTABLE/UNCOUNTABLE SETS,
CANTOR'S DIAGONAL
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Two sets have the SAME CARDINALITY, |A| = |B| (A and B are EQUINUMEROUS),
    when there is a BIJECTION A → B. |A| ≤ |B| when there is an INJECTION
    A → B (equivalently, for A ≠ ∅, a surjection B → A). |A| < |B| means
    |A| ≤ |B| but |A| ≠ |B|. For finite sets this is ordinary counting; the
    PIGEONHOLE principle says there is no injection from an (n + 1)-set into
    an n-set.
  - A set is FINITE if it is equinumerous with {1, ..., n} for some n ≥ 0,
    COUNTABLY INFINITE if equinumerous with N (|A| = ℵ₀, 'aleph-nought'),
    COUNTABLE if finite or countably infinite (its elements can be LISTED
    a₁, a₂, a₃, ...), and UNCOUNTABLE otherwise. An infinite set can be
    equinumerous with a proper subset (N ~ 2N via n ↦ 2n): HILBERT'S HOTEL.
    A finite set never can.
  - Standard countable sets: N, Z (0, 1, −1, 2, −2, ...), N × N (Cantor's
    pairing π(m, n) = (m + n)(m + n + 1)/2 + n is a bijection N₀ × N₀ → N₀,
    N₀ = {0, 1, 2, ...}), Q, Zᵏ, Qᵏ, finite strings over a countable
    alphabet, polynomials with integer or rational coefficients, ALGEBRAIC
    numbers.
  - Closure rules: a subset of a countable set is countable; the image of a
    countable set under any function is countable; a finite product of
    countable sets is countable; a COUNTABLE UNION of countable sets is
    countable (list them in a grid and use the pairing; this uses the axiom
    of choice to pick the listings). To prove A countable it is enough to
    find an injection A → N or a surjection N → A.
  - CANTOR'S DIAGONAL ARGUMENT: given any list r₁, r₂, ... of numbers in
    (0, 1), build x whose k-th decimal digit differs from the k-th digit of
    rₖ (use digits 4 and 5 only, to avoid the double expansions such as
    0.0999... = 0.1000...). Then x ≠ rₖ for every k, so no list contains all
    of (0, 1): (0, 1), R, R − Q, {0, 1}^N and P(N) are UNCOUNTABLE.
  - |R| = |(0, 1)| = |any interval with more than one point| = |P(N)| =
    |{0, 1}^N| = 2^ℵ₀ = c (the CONTINUUM). Useful bijections: x ↦ a + (b − a)x
    from (0, 1) to (a, b); tan from (−π/2, π/2) to R; x ↦ x/(1 − |x|) from
    (−1, 1) to R; eˣ from R to (0, ∞); a 'Hilbert hotel shift' along
    1/2, 1/3, 1/4, ... to swallow extra end points ([0, 1] ~ (0, 1)).
  - CANTOR'S THEOREM: for every set A, |A| < |P(A)|. Proof: for any
    f : A → P(A) the DIAGONAL SET D = {a ∈ A : a ∉ f(a)} is not a value of f,
    so f is not surjective; and a ↦ {a} is an injection. Hence
    ℵ₀ < 2^ℵ₀ < 2^(2^ℵ₀) < ... : there is no largest cardinal.
  - SCHRÖDER–BERNSTEIN: if there are injections A → B and B → A, then there
    is a bijection A → B. So to prove |A| = |B| it suffices to inject both
    ways (for example [0, 1] and (0, 1); P(N) and R; R and R²).
  - CARDINAL ARITHMETIC: ℵ₀ + ℵ₀ = ℵ₀·ℵ₀ = ℵ₀; c + c = c·c = c^ℵ₀ = ℵ₀^ℵ₀ = c;
    |set of functions A → B| = |B|^|A|, so |R^R| = c^c = 2^c = |P(R)| > c. For
    finite sets |P(A)| = 2ⁿ and |A × B| = |A||B|.
  - Transcendental numbers exist and are uncountable (R is uncountable,
    algebraic numbers are countable); 'almost every' real is transcendental.
    The CONTINUUM HYPOTHESIS (no cardinal strictly between ℵ₀ and c) can be
    neither proved nor disproved from the usual axioms ZFC (Gödel 1940,
    Cohen 1963).
  - RUSSELL'S PARADOX: {x : x ∉ x} cannot be a set (it would contain itself
    exactly when it does not), so 'every property defines a set' is false;
    ZFC allows only {x ∈ A : P(x)} for an existing set A. There is no set of
    all sets. Traps: 'Q is dense, so it is as big as R' (false); 'a list
    needs a formula' (any bijection with N will do); 'infinite + 1 is
    bigger' (ℵ₀ + 1 = ℵ₀); forgetting 0.4999... = 0.5 in a diagonal proof.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Give explicit bijections, with inverses, from N = {1, 2, 3, ...} onto
    (a) the even positive integers 2N; (b) N₀ = {0, 1, 2, ...}; (c) Z.
    Deduce that all three sets have cardinality ℵ₀.
    Answer: (a) n ↦ 2n, inverse m ↦ m/2. (b) n ↦ n − 1, inverse m ↦ m + 1.
    (c) f(n) = n/2 for n even, f(n) = −(n − 1)/2 for n odd; inverse
    f⁻¹(m) = 2m for m > 0, f⁻¹(m) = 1 − 2m for m ≤ 0.
    Teacher explains: 'Same size' for infinite sets means 'can be paired off
    exactly', which is what a bijection does. For Z, zigzag outwards from 0:
       n      1    2    3    4    5    6    7   ...
       f(n)   0    1   −1    2   −2    3   −3   ...
    Evens go to the positive integers, odds to 0 and the negatives. Check
    the inverse: f⁻¹(3) = 6 and f(6) = 3 ✓; f⁻¹(−2) = 1 + 4 = 5 and
    f(5) = −(5 − 1)/2 = −2 ✓. To prove bijectivity, check both compositions
    are the identity, or check injective and surjective directly. Note that
    2N is a PROPER subset of N yet has the same cardinality: this is what
    makes a set infinite. Common mistakes: 'Z is twice as big as N'; the map
    n ↦ n − 1 from N to Z (not surjective). Now you try: give bijections from
    N onto {5, 10, 15, ...} and onto {n ∈ N : n ≥ 10}. (n ↦ 5n; n ↦ n + 9)

D2. Hilbert's Hotel has rooms 1, 2, 3, ..., all occupied. Show how to
    accommodate (a) one new guest; (b) 50 new guests; (c) a coach with
    guests g₁, g₂, g₃, ...; (d) infinitely many coaches C₁, C₂, ..., each with
    infinitely many guests. Then explain why no finite hotel can do (a).
    Answer: (a) n → n + 1, new guest in room 1. (b) n → n + 50, new guests in
    1–50. (c) n → 2n, gₖ → 2k − 1. (d) old guest n → 2n (or 2ⁿ); guest j of
    coach i → 3^i·5^j (any injection into free rooms), or use the pairing
    function. A finite hotel: an injection {1, ..., n + 1} → {1, ..., n}
    does not exist (pigeonhole).
    Teacher explains: Each instruction is an INJECTION from 'old guests ∪ new
    guests' into N:
       (a) old n ↦ n + 1              new ↦ 1
       (b) old n ↦ n + 50             new k ↦ k   (k = 1, ..., 50)
       (c) old n ↦ 2n   (evens)       gₖ ↦ 2k − 1  (odds)
       (d) old n ↦ 2ⁿ                 guest j of coach i ↦ 3^i·5^j
    In (d) the rooms 3^i·5^j are all different (unique factorisation) and
    never a power of 2, so nobody shares. Empty rooms are allowed (6, 7, 10,
    ... stay empty); if we want a bijection, Schröder–Bernstein (or a more
    careful rule) provides one. This shows N ∪ N ∪ ... ∪ N (countably many
    copies) is countable: ℵ₀·ℵ₀ = ℵ₀. A finite hotel with n rooms cannot take
    n + 1 guests one to a room: that is exactly why 'equinumerous with a
    proper subset' characterises infinite sets. Common mistake: in (c),
    moving everyone to n + 1 'infinitely many times': the process never ends,
    so it describes no assignment. Now you try: where do the old guests go
    to make room for 3 new guests, and which rooms do the newcomers get?
    (n → n + 3; rooms 1, 2, 3)

D3. Cantor's pairing function on N₀ × N₀ is π(m, n) = (m + n)(m + n + 1)/2 + n.
    Compute π(0, 0), π(2, 3), π(4, 1) and find the pair with π(m, n) = 25.
    Explain why π is a bijection and deduce that N × N is countable. Give
    also a simpler injection N × N → N.
    Answer: π(0, 0) = 0, π(2, 3) = 18, π(4, 1) = 16; π⁻¹(25) = (2, 4). An
    injection: (m, n) ↦ 2^m·3^n.
    Teacher explains: π walks through the grid along the anti-diagonals
    m + n = w = 0, 1, 2, ...:
          n=0 n=1 n=2 n=3
    m=0    0   2   5   9
    m=1    1   4   8
    m=2    3   7
    m=3    6
    The diagonals with w = 0, 1, ..., w − 1 contain 1 + 2 + ... + w =
    w(w + 1)/2 points, so the point (m, n) on diagonal w is number
    w(w + 1)/2 + n (counting from 0). Examples:
       π(2, 3):  w = 5,  15 + 3 = 18        π(4, 1):  w = 5,  15 + 1 = 16
    To invert z = 25: find the largest w with w(w + 1)/2 ≤ 25: 21 ≤ 25 < 28,
    so w = 6. Then n = 25 − 21 = 4 and m = w − n = 2. Check π(2, 4) =
    6·7/2 + 4 = 25 ✓. Every z has exactly one such w and n, which is why π is
    a bijection. The map (m, n) ↦ 2^m 3^n is injective by unique
    factorisation (not surjective: 5 is missed), which is also enough: an
    injection into N proves countability. Common mistake: listing row by row
    (0,0), (0,1), (0,2), ...: you never reach row 1. Now you try: π(3, 3)
    and π⁻¹(10). (24; (4, 0))

D4. Prove that Q is countable. List the positive rationals p/q (in lowest
    terms) of 'height' p + q ≤ 5, and count those of height 7.
    Answer: Heights 2 to 5 give 1/1; 1/2, 2/1; 1/3, 3/1; 1/4, 2/3, 3/2, 4/1
    (9 numbers). Height 7 gives 6 numbers.
    Teacher explains: Method 1 (injection): write each rational uniquely as
    p/q in lowest terms with q > 0 and send it to the pair (p, q) ∈ Z × N.
    This is injective and Z × N is countable (Z and N are countable, a
    product of two countable sets is countable), so Q is countable. Method 2
    (listing): for each height h = |p| + q there are only finitely many
    rationals, so list height 2, then height 3, and so on; every rational
    appears at some finite stage:
       h = 2:  1/1                         h = 3:  1/2, 2/1
       h = 4:  1/3, 3/1   (2/2 = 1/1 skip) h = 5:  1/4, 2/3, 3/2, 4/1
    For height h, p/q with p + q = h is in lowest terms exactly when
    gcd(p, h) = 1, so there are φ(h) of them; φ(7) = 6: 1/6, 2/5, 3/4, 4/3,
    5/2, 6/1. Putting 0 first and each −r after r gives a list of all of Q.
    The surprise: Q is dense (between any two reals there is a rational) but
    no bigger than N. Common mistake: listing 1/1, 1/2, 1/3, ... first and
    'then' 2/1, 2/2, ...: the first row never ends. Now you try: how many
    positive rationals in lowest terms have height 10? (φ(10) = 4: 1/9, 3/7,
    7/3, 9/1)

D5. Prove that a countable union of countable sets is countable. Deduce that
    (a) the set S of all finite strings over the alphabet {a, b} is
    countable, and count the non-empty strings of length at most 3; (b) the
    set of polynomials with integer coefficients is countable; (c) the set of
    algebraic numbers is countable.
    Answer: Proof below; 2 + 4 + 8 = 14 non-empty strings of length ≤ 3.
    Teacher explains: Let A₁, A₂, ... be countable. List each one (this is
    where the axiom of choice quietly picks a listing for every i):
       A₁:  a₁₁  a₁₂  a₁₃  ...
       A₂:  a₂₁  a₂₂  a₂₃  ...
       A₃:  a₃₁  a₃₂  a₃₃  ...
    The map N × N → ⋃ Aᵢ, (i, j) ↦ aᵢⱼ is a SURJECTION from a countable set,
    so the union is countable (repeats and finite sets do no harm: skip
    repeats). (a) S = ⋃ Sₙ with Sₙ = strings of length n, and |Sₙ| = 2ⁿ is
    finite; a countable union of finite sets is countable. Length ≤ 3:
    2 + 4 + 8 = 14 (15 with the empty string). (b) Polynomials of degree
    ≤ n with integer coefficients correspond to (a₀, ..., aₙ) ∈ Zⁿ⁺¹, a
    countable set; take the union over n. (c) Each non-zero polynomial has
    finitely many roots; algebraic numbers = ⋃ (over countably many
    polynomials) (finite root sets): countable. Consequence: since R is
    uncountable (D6), most real numbers are TRANSCENDENTAL. Common mistakes:
    'each Sₙ is finite so S is finite'; forgetting that a countable union of
    FINITE sets can be infinite. Now you try: how many strings of length
    exactly 4 are there over the 26-letter alphabet? (26⁴ = 456976)

D6. Prove that (0, 1) is uncountable by Cantor's diagonal argument. Carry out
    the construction for a list beginning
       r₁ = 0.314159...    r₂ = 0.555555...    r₃ = 0.123456...
       r₄ = 0.718281...    r₅ = 0.000050...    r₆ = 0.999995...
    using the rule 'new digit 5, unless the diagonal digit is 5, then 4'.
    Answer: x = 0.545544... differs from every rₖ; (0, 1) and hence R are
    uncountable.
    Teacher explains: Suppose (0, 1) were countable, listed as r₁, r₂, ....
    Write the decimal expansions and look down the diagonal:
       r₁ = 0.[3] 1  4  1  5  9          d₁₁ = 3  →  5
       r₂ = 0. 5 [5] 5  5  5  5          d₂₂ = 5  →  4
       r₃ = 0. 1  2 [3] 4  5  6          d₃₃ = 3  →  5
       r₄ = 0. 7  1  8 [2] 8  1          d₄₄ = 2  →  5
       r₅ = 0. 0  0  0  0 [5] 0          d₅₅ = 5  →  4
       r₆ = 0. 9  9  9  9  9 [5]         d₆₆ = 5  →  4
    x = 0.545544... differs from rₖ in the k-th digit for EVERY k (the rule
    is applied for all k, not just six). Since x uses only the digits 4 and
    5, its decimal expansion is unique, so 'different digit' really means
    'different number'. (With the careless rule 'add 1, 9 → 0' one could
    produce 0.1000... against a listed 0.0999..., which is the same number.)
    Hence x ∈ (0, 1) is missing from the list: no list is complete, so (0, 1)
    is uncountable. Since tan(π(x − 1/2)) maps (0, 1) bijectively onto R,
    R is uncountable too. Common mistake: 'add x to the list as r₀': the
    new list gets a new diagonal; the argument works against EVERY list.
    Now you try: the diagonal digits of a list are 1, 5, 5, 0, 9, ...; write
    the first five digits of x. (0.54455...)

D7. Prove that {0, 1}^N (all infinite 0–1 sequences) is uncountable, deduce
    that P(N) is uncountable, and prove that R − Q and the set T of
    transcendental numbers are uncountable.
    Answer: Diagonal argument; P(N) ↔ {0, 1}^N by indicator sequences;
    R − Q and T are uncountable because R is uncountable and Q, algebraic
    numbers are countable.
    Teacher explains: Given a list s₁, s₂, ... of 0–1 sequences, FLIP the
    diagonal: t(k) = 1 − sₖ(k). Then t ≠ sₖ at place k, for every k. For
    example
       s₁ = [0] 1  1  0 ...
       s₂ =  1 [1] 0  1 ...
       s₃ =  0  0 [0] 0 ...      t = 1 0 1 0 ...
       s₄ =  1  1  1 [1] ...
    (no double-expansion issue here: sequences are compared entry by entry).
    A subset A ⊆ N corresponds to its INDICATOR sequence (1 at n if n ∈ A,
    0 otherwise); this is a bijection P(N) → {0, 1}^N, so P(N) is uncountable.
    For the irrationals: if R − Q were countable then R = Q ∪ (R − Q) would be
    a union of two countable sets, hence countable: contradiction. The same
    argument with the countable set of algebraic numbers shows T is
    uncountable; in particular transcendental numbers EXIST, a proof that
    names none of them (Liouville, Hermite and Lindemann found explicit ones:
    e and π). Common mistake: thinking 'uncountable' means 'we cannot write
    down any element'. Now you try: is the set of all functions N → {0, 1, 2}
    countable? (No: it contains {0, 1}^N.)

D8. Prove Cantor's theorem: for every set A there is no surjection
    f : A → P(A), so |A| < |P(A)|. Find the diagonal set D for A = {1, 2, 3},
    f(1) = {1, 2}, f(2) = ∅, f(3) = {1, 3}, and explain the link with
    Russell's paradox.
    Answer: D = {a ∈ A : a ∉ f(a)} is never a value of f. In the example
    D = {2}, which is not among {1, 2}, ∅, {1, 3}.
    Teacher explains: Let f : A → P(A) be any function and put
       D = {a ∈ A : a ∉ f(a)}  ⊆ A.
    If D = f(d) for some d ∈ A, ask whether d ∈ D:
       d ∈ D  ⇒  d ∉ f(d) = D        contradiction
       d ∉ D  ⇒  d ∈ f(d) = D        contradiction
    So D is not a value: f is not surjective. And a ↦ {a} is an injection
    A → P(A), so |A| ≤ |P(A)| but not equal: |A| < |P(A)|. In the example:
       1 ∈ f(1) = {1, 2}      so 1 ∉ D
       2 ∉ f(2) = ∅           so 2 ∈ D
       3 ∈ f(3) = {1, 3}      so 3 ∉ D
    D = {2}, and indeed {2} ≠ f(1), f(2), f(3) ✓. D differs from f(a) 'at a',
    exactly like the diagonal number differs from rₖ at digit k. Taking A to
    be 'the set of all sets' (with f(x) = x when x is a set) gives
    D = {x : x ∉ x}, Russell's set, which is why there is no set of all
    sets. Consequences: ℵ₀ < 2^ℵ₀ = c < 2^c < ...; infinitely many infinite
    sizes. For finite A this is 2ⁿ > n. Common mistake: testing only a few
    functions f; the proof works against every f at once. Now you try:
    A = {a, b, c}, f(a) = {b}, f(b) = {a, b, c}, f(c) = {a, c}. Find D.
    ({a})

D9. Give explicit bijections: (a) (0, 1) → (2, 7); (b) (−1, 1) → R;
    (c) (0, 1) → (0, ∞); (d) [0, 1) → (0, 1); (e) [0, 1] → (0, 1). Conclude
    that all intervals with more than one point have the same cardinality.
    Answer: (a) x ↦ 2 + 5x. (b) x ↦ x/(1 − |x|) (or tan(πx/2)).
    (c) x ↦ x/(1 − x). (d) 0 ↦ 1/2, 1/n ↦ 1/(n + 1) for n ≥ 2, other x
    fixed. (e) 0 ↦ 1/2, 1 ↦ 1/3, 1/n ↦ 1/(n + 2) for n ≥ 2, other x fixed.
    Teacher explains: Open to open intervals: stretch and shift. In (a) the
    line through (0, 2) and (1, 7) is y = 2 + 5x, inverse x = (y − 2)/5. For
    (b), as x → ±1 the denominator → 0, so values run over all of R; inverse
    y ↦ y/(1 + |y|). (c) inverse y ↦ y/(1 + y). The end points need a TRICK,
    since no continuous bijection [0, 1) → (0, 1) exists. Pick the sequence
    1/2, 1/3, 1/4, ... inside the interval and shift it along, Hilbert-hotel
    style, to make room for the extra point:
       (d)  0 ↦ 1/2,  1/2 ↦ 1/3,  1/3 ↦ 1/4,  ...,  every other x ↦ x
       (e)  0 ↦ 1/2,  1 ↦ 1/3,  1/2 ↦ 1/4,  1/3 ↦ 1/5,  ...,  other x ↦ x
    In (e) the set {0, 1, 1/2, 1/3, ...} is mapped bijectively onto
    {1/2, 1/3, 1/4, ...} and everything else stays put, so the whole map is a
    bijection [0, 1] → (0, 1). Combining (a)–(e) with compositions (and
    eˣ, ln for rays): every interval of positive length has cardinality
    |R| = c. Common mistakes: x ↦ x/2 + 1/4 as a 'bijection' [0, 1] → (0, 1)
    (it is only an injection, onto [1/4, 3/4]); forgetting to say what
    happens to points not in the sequence. Now you try: a bijection from
    (0, 1) onto (1, ∞). (x ↦ 1/x)

D10. State the Schröder–Bernstein theorem and use it to show (a)
     |[0, 1]| = |(0, 1)|; (b) |P(N)| = |R|; (c) |(0, 1) × (0, 1)| = |(0, 1)|.
     Answer: Injections both ways give a bijection. (a) inclusion (0, 1) →
     [0, 1] and x ↦ x/2 + 1/4. (b) A ↦ ∑ (n ∈ A) 10⁻ⁿ and
     x ↦ {q ∈ Q : q < x} (with Q ~ N). (c) inclusion-type map x ↦ (x, 1/2)
     and interleaving of decimal digits.
     Teacher explains: Schröder–Bernstein: if f : A → B and g : B → A are
     both injective, then there is a bijection A → B. Its value is that
     injections are easy to find even when bijections are messy. (a) The
     inclusion (0, 1) ⊆ [0, 1] is injective, and x ↦ x/2 + 1/4 maps [0, 1]
     injectively into [1/4, 3/4] ⊆ (0, 1). Done, without the trick of D9.
     (b) P(N) → R: send A to the decimal 0.d₁d₂d₃... with dₙ = 1 if n ∈ A and
     0 otherwise. Such decimals (digits 0 and 1 only) have a unique
     expansion, so different sets give different numbers. R → P(Q): send x
     to its 'cut' {q ∈ Q : q < x}; if x < y there is a rational between
     them, so the cuts differ. Since |Q| = |N|, P(Q) ~ P(N). (c) (0, 1) →
     (0, 1)²: x ↦ (x, 1/2). Back: write x = 0.x₁x₂x₃..., y = 0.y₁y₂y₃...
     (never ending in 9s) and send (x, y) to 0.x₁y₁x₂y₂x₃y₃...; the pair can
     be read back from the digits, so the map is injective. Hence
     |R²| = |R|: a line has as many points as a plane (Cantor wrote 'I see it,
     but I don't believe it'). Common mistake: claiming the digit-interleaving
     map is a bijection (0.1909090... is not in its range), which is exactly
     why Schröder–Bernstein is used. Now you try: show |N × R| = |R|.
     (x ↦ (1, x) one way; (n, x) ↦ n + g(x) with g : R → (0, 1) a bijection
     the other way)

D11. Arrange in order of cardinality, grouping equal ones: ∅, {1, ..., 10},
     N, Z, Q, the algebraic numbers, R, R − Q, C, the set F of all functions
     R → R, P(R), R^N (all real sequences). Justify, and compute ℵ₀ + ℵ₀,
     ℵ₀·ℵ₀, c + ℵ₀, c·c, ℵ₀^ℵ₀.
     Answer: 0 < 10 < ℵ₀ (N, Z, Q, algebraic numbers) < c (R, R − Q, C, R^N)
     < 2^c (F and P(R)). ℵ₀ + ℵ₀ = ℵ₀·ℵ₀ = ℵ₀; c + ℵ₀ = c·c = ℵ₀^ℵ₀ = c.
     Teacher explains: Collect the facts:
        N, Z, Q, algebraic numbers    countably infinite         ℵ₀
        R, R − Q                      diagonal argument           c
        C ~ R²                        interleaving (D10)          c
        R^N                           c^ℵ₀ = (2^ℵ₀)^ℵ₀ = 2^(ℵ₀·ℵ₀) = c
        P(R)                          Cantor's theorem          2^c
        F = R^R                       c^c = 2^(ℵ₀·c) = 2^c
     Cardinal arithmetic is defined through sets: κ + λ = |A ∪ B| (A, B
     disjoint), κ·λ = |A × B|, λ^κ = |functions A → B| with |A| = κ, |B| = λ.
     So ℵ₀ + ℵ₀ = |evens ∪ odds| = ℵ₀; ℵ₀·ℵ₀ = |N × N| = ℵ₀ (D3);
     c + ℵ₀ = |(0, 1) ∪ N| ≤ |R| = c; c·c = |R²| = c (D10). For ℵ₀^ℵ₀ squeeze:
        2^ℵ₀ ≤ ℵ₀^ℵ₀ ≤ (2^ℵ₀)^ℵ₀ = 2^(ℵ₀·ℵ₀) = 2^ℵ₀
     so ℵ₀^ℵ₀ = c. Is there a set strictly between N and R in size? That is
     the CONTINUUM HYPOTHESIS, independent of ZFC. Common mistakes: 'C is
     two-dimensional so it is bigger than R'; 'c·c = c²' treated as bigger.
     Now you try: what is the cardinality of the set of continuous functions
     R → R? (c: such a function is determined by its values on Q, so there
     are at most c^ℵ₀ = c, and the constants give at least c)

D12. Countable or uncountable? Prove each. (a) The set of finite subsets of
     N. (b) The set of infinite subsets of N. (c) Integer sequences that are
     eventually 0. (d) All integer sequences. (e) Circles in the plane with
     rational centre and rational radius. (f) Any collection of pairwise
     disjoint open intervals in R. (g) The set of points where a monotone
     function f : R → R is discontinuous.
     Answer: (a) countable (b) uncountable (c) countable (d) uncountable
     (e) countable (f) countable (g) countable.
     Teacher explains: For 'countable', inject into a known countable set or
     write as a countable union of countable sets; for 'uncountable', inject
     a known uncountable set, or remove a countable part from one.
       (a) finite subsets of size n ↔ ⊆ Nⁿ; union over n: countable
       (b) P(N) = (finite) ∪ (infinite); if both were countable P(N) would
           be: so the infinite subsets are uncountable
       (c) sequences zero after place n ↔ Zⁿ; union over n: countable
       (d) {0, 1}^N ⊆ Z^N, and {0, 1}^N is uncountable
       (e) circle ↦ (a, b, r) ∈ Q³, injective: countable
       (f) choose a rational qᵢ in each interval Iᵢ; disjoint intervals give
           distinct rationals: an injection into Q
       (g) at a discontinuity x of an increasing f the one-sided limits
           satisfy f(x−) < f(x+); the open 'jump intervals' (f(x−), f(x+))
           are pairwise disjoint, so by (f) there are countably many
     (f) and (g) show a typical use of density of Q: 'pick a rational'.
     Common mistakes: thinking (a) is uncountable because P(N) is; in (f),
     picking an irrational in each interval (no injection into a countable
     set then). Now you try: the set of lines through the origin with rational
     slope, and the set of all lines in the plane. (Countable; uncountable,
     since y = mx for m ∈ R already gives c of them)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Give an explicit bijection and its inverse (N = {1, 2, ...},
    N₀ = {0, 1, 2, ...}).
    a) N → 2N (even positive integers)     b) N → odd positive integers
    c) N → N₀                              d) N → {n ∈ N : n ≥ 5}
    e) N → Z                               f) N₀ → Z
    g) Z → 2Z                              h) N → {1, 4, 9, 16, ...}
    i) N → N − {3}                         j) (0, 1) → (0, 5)
    k) (0, 1) → (−3, 4)                    l) [0, 1] → [2, 8]
    m) (1, ∞) → (0, 1)                     n) (0, 1) → (0, ∞)
    o) R → (0, ∞)                          p) (−π/2, π/2) → R
    q) (0, 1) → R                          r) (−1, 1) → R
    s) [0, 1) → (0, 1]                     t) P({1, ..., n}) → {0, 1}ⁿ
    Answers:
      a) n ↦ 2n; m ↦ m/2
      b) n ↦ 2n − 1; m ↦ (m + 1)/2
      c) n ↦ n − 1; m ↦ m + 1
      d) n ↦ n + 4; m ↦ m − 4
      e) n ↦ n/2 (n even), −(n − 1)/2 (n odd); m ↦ 2m (m > 0), 1 − 2m (m ≤ 0)
      f) n ↦ n/2 (n even), −(n + 1)/2 (n odd); m ↦ 2m (m ≥ 0), −2m − 1 (m < 0)
      g) n ↦ 2n; m ↦ m/2
      h) n ↦ n²; m ↦ √m
      i) n ↦ n (n ≤ 2), n + 1 (n ≥ 3); m ↦ m (m ≤ 2), m − 1 (m ≥ 4)
      j) x ↦ 5x; y ↦ y/5
      k) x ↦ 7x − 3; y ↦ (y + 3)/7
      l) x ↦ 6x + 2; y ↦ (y − 2)/6
      m) x ↦ 1/x; y ↦ 1/y
      n) x ↦ x/(1 − x); y ↦ y/(1 + y)
      o) x ↦ eˣ; y ↦ ln y
      p) x ↦ tan x; y ↦ arctan y
      q) x ↦ ln(x/(1 − x)); y ↦ 1/(1 + e⁻ʸ)
      r) x ↦ x/(1 − |x|); y ↦ y/(1 + |y|)
      s) x ↦ 1 − x; y ↦ 1 − y
      t) S ↦ (s₁, ..., sₙ) with sₖ = 1 if k ∈ S, else 0; back: (s) ↦ {k : sₖ = 1}

M2. Countable (C) or uncountable (U)?
    a) Z                                   b) Q
    c) R − Q                               d) Q × Q
    e) Z³                                  f) [0, 0.001]
    g) {0, 1}^N                            h) finite subsets of N
    i) P(N)                                j) infinite subsets of N
    k) polynomials with rational coefficients
    l) algebraic numbers                   m) transcendental numbers
    n) finite strings over {a, b, ..., z}
    o) 0–1 sequences that are eventually 0
    p) all functions N → N                 q) all bijections N → N
    r) all functions {0, 1} → N            s) points of the plane with both
                                              coordinates rational
    t) all circles in the plane            u) complex roots of unity
    v) numbers in (0, 1) whose decimals use only the digits 3 and 7
    w) the Cantor set                      x) the primes
    Answers: a) C   b) C   c) U   d) C   e) C   f) U   g) U   h) C   i) U   j) U
             k) C   l) C   m) U   n) C   o) C   p) U   q) U   r) C   s) C   t) U
             u) C   v) U   w) U   x) C

M3. Cantor's pairing π(m, n) = (m + n)(m + n + 1)/2 + n on N₀ × N₀, and the
    bijection β(m, n) = 2^m(2n + 1) − 1 from N₀ × N₀ onto N₀.
    a) π(1, 0)       b) π(0, 1)       c) π(1, 1)       d) π(2, 0)
    e) π(0, 3)       f) π(2, 3)       g) π(3, 2)       h) π(4, 1)
    i) π(5, 5)       j) π(10, 0)      k) π(0, 10)      l) π(7, 3)
    m) π(20, 30)     n) π⁻¹(5)        o) π⁻¹(12)       p) π⁻¹(20)
    q) π⁻¹(36)       r) π⁻¹(50)       s) π⁻¹(100)      t) π⁻¹(1000)
    u) β(3, 2)       v) β(1, 5)       w) β⁻¹(47)       x) β⁻¹(95)
    Answers: a) 1   b) 2   c) 4   d) 3   e) 9   f) 18   g) 17   h) 16   i) 60
             j) 55   k) 65   l) 58   m) 1305   n) (0, 2)   o) (2, 2)   p) (0, 5)
             q) (8, 0)   r) (4, 5)   s) (4, 9)   t) (34, 10)   u) 39   v) 21
             w) (4, 1)   x) (5, 1)

M4. Diagonal constructions. Rule A: new k-th digit 5, unless the k-th digit
    of rₖ is 5, then 4. Rule B: new digit = diagonal digit + 1 if it is 0–7,
    and 1 if it is 8 or 9. For 0–1 sequences: flip the diagonal. The list
    r₁, r₂, ... begins as shown (only the first digits after '0.' are given);
    write the first digits of the diagonal number.
    a) Rule A: 1234, 5678, 9012, 3456
    b) Rule A: 5000, 0500, 0050, 0005
    c) Rule A: 14159, 71828, 41421, 73205, 23606
    d) Rule A: 55555, 45454, 05050, 12345, 99995
    e) Rule B: 31415, 27182, 14142, 17320, 22360
    f) Rule B: 9999, 8888, 7777, 0000
    g) Rule B: 123456, 234567, 345678, 456789, 567890, 678901
    h) Rule B: 500000, 050000, 005000, 000500, 000050, 000005
    i) Flip: 0000, 1111, 0101, 1010
    j) Flip: 10110, 01101, 11100, 00011, 10101
    k) Flip: 000000, 010101, 101010, 111000, 000111, 110011
    l) Flip: 1001, 0110, 1111, 0000
    Answers:
      a) 0.5555...     b) 0.4444...     c) 0.55555...    d) 0.44554...
      e) 0.48231...    f) 0.1181...     g) 0.246812...   h) 0.666666...
      i) 1011...       j) 00000...      k) 100100...     l) 0001...

M5. Cantor's theorem. For each f : A → P(A) find D = {a ∈ A : a ∉ f(a)} (D is
    never a value of f). In i)–p), A = N.
    a) A = {1, 2, 3}: f(1) = {1, 2}, f(2) = ∅, f(3) = {1, 3}
    b) A = {a, b, c}: f(a) = {b}, f(b) = {a, b, c}, f(c) = {a, c}
    c) A = {1, 2, 3}: f(1) = f(2) = f(3) = ∅
    d) A = {1, 2, 3}: f(1) = f(2) = f(3) = {1, 2, 3}
    e) A = {1, 2, 3}: f(1) = {2, 3}, f(2) = {1, 3}, f(3) = {1, 2}
    f) A = {1, 2, 3, 4}: f(1) = {1}, f(2) = {1, 2}, f(3) = {4}, f(4) = {1, 2, 3}
    g) A = {1, 2, 3, 4}: f(n) = {k ∈ A : k ≥ n}
    h) A = {1, 2, 3, 4}: f(n) = {k ∈ A : k > n}
    i) f(n) = {n}                          j) f(n) = {1, ..., n − 1} (f(1) = ∅)
    k) f(n) = {multiples of n}             l) f(n) = {primes}
    m) f(n) = {2n}                         n) f(n) = {even numbers}
    o) f(n) = {k : k ≥ n²}                 p) f(n) = {divisors of 12}
    Answers:
      a) {2}   b) {a}   c) {1, 2, 3}   d) ∅   e) {1, 2, 3}   f) {3, 4}   g) ∅
      h) {1, 2, 3, 4}   i) ∅   j) N   k) ∅   l) N − {primes} = {1, 4, 6, 8, 9, ...}
      m) N   n) the odd numbers   o) N − {1}   p) N − {1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12}

M6. Prove countable (sketches).
    a) the odd integers
    b) N × N × N
    c) Q × Z
    d) the set of all finite subsets of N
    e) the polynomials with integer coefficients
    f) the algebraic numbers
    g) the finite strings over a countable alphabet
    h) any collection of pairwise disjoint open intervals in R
    i) the set of discontinuities of a monotone f : R → R
    j) the lines y = mx + c with m, c ∈ Q
    k) any set S ⊆ R all of whose points are isolated (each s ∈ S has an
       open interval containing no other point of S)
    l) a countable union of finite sets
    m) the eventually constant sequences of natural numbers
    n) the set of all open intervals with rational end points
    Answers:
      a) n ↦ 2n − 1 from Z (a bijection onto the odd integers) and Z is
         countable.
      b) (a, b, c) ↦ 2ᵃ3ᵇ5ᶜ is injective into N.
      c) Q and Z are countable; a finite product of countable sets is.
      d) Finite subsets of size n inject into Nⁿ; countable union over n.
      e) Degree ≤ n ↔ Zⁿ⁺¹; countable union over n.
      f) Each of countably many non-zero polynomials has finitely many roots.
      g) Strings of length n ↔ Σⁿ (countable); union over n.
      h) Pick a rational in each interval; disjointness makes this injective.
      i) Jump intervals (f(x−), f(x+)) are disjoint; apply h).
      j) (m, c) ↦ line is a bijection from Q × Q.
      k) Pick an interval (p, q) with p, q ∈ Q around each s containing no
         other point of S; s ↦ (p, q) is injective into Q × Q.
      l) List A₁ in full, then A₂, ... (each finite); or surject N × N.
      m) Sequence ↦ (first n terms, constant value) with n the place from
         which it is constant: an injection into ⋃ Nⁿ⁺¹, countable.
      n) (a, b) ↦ (a, b) ∈ Q × Q, injective.

M7. Prove uncountable (sketches).
    a) (0, 1)
    b) R − Q
    c) {0, 1}^N
    d) P(N)
    e) the infinite subsets of N
    f) all functions N → N
    g) the transcendental numbers
    h) the Cantor set (ternary expansions using digits 0 and 2 only)
    i) any interval [a, b] with a < b
    j) the strictly increasing sequences of natural numbers
    k) the bijections N → N
    l) the unit circle {(x, y) : x² + y² = 1}
    m) the reals in (0, 1) with no digit 7 in their decimal expansion
    n) any S ⊆ R whose complement R − S is countable
    Answers:
      a) Diagonal argument with digits 4 and 5.
      b) R = Q ∪ (R − Q); if both were countable, R would be.
      c) Flip the diagonal of any list.
      d) Indicator sequences: P(N) ~ {0, 1}^N.
      e) P(N) minus the countable set of finite subsets.
      f) Contains {1, 2}^N ~ {0, 1}^N.
      g) R minus the countable set of algebraic numbers.
      h) (a₁, a₂, ...) ∈ {0, 1}^N ↦ ∑ 2aₖ/3ᵏ is injective into the Cantor set.
      i) x ↦ a + (b − a)x is a bijection [0, 1] → [a, b], and [0, 1] ⊇ (0, 1).
      j) Sequence ↦ its set of values is a bijection onto infinite subsets.
      k) For s ∈ {0, 1}^N swap 2k − 1 and 2k exactly when sₖ = 1: an
         injection {0, 1}^N → bijections.
      l) θ ↦ (cos θ, sin θ) is injective on [0, 2π).
      m) Contains the reals whose digits are all 4 or 5 (a diagonal argument
         on those, or injection from {0, 1}^N).
      n) R = S ∪ (R − S); if S were countable R would be.

M8. Finite cardinalities. A = {1, 2, 3}, B = {3, 4}. Find:
    a) |A × B|                     b) |P(A)|
    c) |P(A × B)|                  d) number of functions A → B
    e) number of functions B → A   f) |P(P(B))|
    g) number of functions A → P(B)
    h) |P(A) × P(B)|               i) number of relations on A
    j) number of bijections A → A  k) number of injections B → A
    l) number of surjections A → B m) |P(A) − {∅}|
    n) number of subsets of A of even size
    o) number of pairs (X, Y) with X ⊆ Y ⊆ A
    p) number of pairs (X, Y) of subsets of A with X ∩ Y = ∅
    q) |P(A) ∩ P(B)|              r) |P(A) ∪ P(B)|
    s) |P(A ∪ B)|                 t) number of partial functions A → B
    u) |A ∪ B|                    v) |A Δ B|
    w) |P(A Δ B)|                 x) number of functions P(B) → B
    Answers: a) 6   b) 8   c) 64   d) 8   e) 9   f) 16   g) 64   h) 32   i) 512
             j) 6   k) 6   l) 6   m) 7   n) 4   o) 27   p) 27   q) 2   r) 10
             s) 16   t) 27   u) 4   v) 3   w) 8   x) 16

M9. Explicit bijections that absorb end points (x not mentioned is fixed).
    a) [0, 1) → (0, 1)            b) [0, 1] → (0, 1)
    c) [0, 1] → [0, 1)            d) (0, 1) → (0, 1]
    e) [0, ∞) → (0, ∞)            f) R → R − {0}
    g) (0, 2) − {1} → (0, 2)      h) N ∪ {0} → N
    i) R → (0, 1)                 j) [0, 1] → R
    Answers:
      a) 0 ↦ 1/2, 1/n ↦ 1/(n + 1) (n ≥ 2)
      b) 0 ↦ 1/2, 1 ↦ 1/3, 1/n ↦ 1/(n + 2) (n ≥ 2)
      c) 1 ↦ 1/2, 1/n ↦ 1/(n + 1) (n ≥ 2)
      d) 1/n ↦ 1/(n − 1) (n ≥ 2)
      e) n ↦ n + 1 for n = 0, 1, 2, ...
      f) n ↦ n + 1 for n = 0, 1, 2, ...
      g) 1/2 ↦ 1, 1/n ↦ 1/(n − 1) (n ≥ 3)
      h) n ↦ n + 1
      i) x ↦ 1/(1 + e⁻ˣ)
      j) compose b) with (0, 1) → R, x ↦ ln(x/(1 − x))

M10. Use Schröder–Bernstein: give an injection each way.
     a) |[0, 1]| = |(0, 1)|
     b) |[0, 1]| = |R|
     c) |N × N| = |N|
     d) |Q| = |N|
     e) |P(N)| = |R|
     f) |{0, 1}^N| = |[0, 1]|
     g) |R²| = |R|
     h) |N^N| = |{0, 1}^N|
     i) |C| = |R|
