     j) |{continuous f : R → R}| = |R|
     Answers:
       a) inclusion; x ↦ x/2 + 1/4
       b) inclusion; x ↦ 1/(1 + e⁻ˣ) into (0, 1) ⊆ [0, 1]
       c) (m, n) ↦ 2^m 3^n; n ↦ (n, 1)
       d) p/q (lowest terms, q > 0) ↦ 2^|p| 3^q 5^s (s = 1 if p < 0, else 0);
          inclusion N ⊆ Q
       e) A ↦ ∑ (n ∈ A) 10⁻ⁿ; x ↦ {q ∈ Q : q < x} ∈ P(Q) ~ P(N)
       f) (aₖ) ↦ ∑ 2aₖ/3ᵏ; x ↦ its binary digits (not ending in all 1s,
          except x = 1)
       g) x ↦ (x, 0); interleave the decimal digits of (x, y) (on (0, 1)²)
       h) (n₁, n₂, ...) ↦ 1…1 0 1…1 0 ... (n₁ ones, 0, n₂ ones, 0, ...);
          (aₖ) ↦ (aₖ + 1)
       i) x ↦ x + 0i; a + bi ↦ (a, b) ∈ R² ~ R (by g)
       j) f ↦ (f(q)) (q ∈ Q) ∈ R^Q, and |R^Q| = c^ℵ₀ = c; c ↦ constant c

M11. Cardinal arithmetic (c = 2^ℵ₀ = |R|). Find:
     a) ℵ₀ + 1                   b) ℵ₀ + ℵ₀
     c) ℵ₀·ℵ₀                    d) ℵ₀⁵
     e) 2^ℵ₀                     f) c + ℵ₀
     g) c + c                    h) c·c
     i) c·ℵ₀                     j) ℵ₀^ℵ₀
     k) c^ℵ₀                     l) 10^ℵ₀
     m) c^c                      n) |Q^N|
     o) |finite sequences of reals|
     p) |R − Q|                  q) |functions R → {0, 1}|
     r) |functions R → R|        s) |continuous functions R → R|
     t) |finite subsets of N|    u) |open intervals with rational end points|
     v) |P(P(N))|                w) |countable subsets of R|
     x) |P(R) − {countable subsets}|
     Answers: a) ℵ₀   b) ℵ₀   c) ℵ₀   d) ℵ₀   e) c   f) c   g) c   h) c   i) c
              j) c   k) c   l) c   m) 2^c   n) c   o) c   p) c   q) 2^c   r) 2^c
              s) c   t) ℵ₀   u) ℵ₀   v) 2^c   w) c   x) 2^c

M12. True (T) or false (F)?
     a) Every subset of a countable set is countable.
     b) Every infinite set has a countably infinite subset.
     c) The union of two uncountable sets is uncountable.
     d) The intersection of two uncountable sets is uncountable.
     e) If A is uncountable and B is countable then A − B is uncountable.
     f) There is a surjection N → R.
     g) There is an injection Q → N.
     h) There is a bijection R → P(N).
     i) For some non-empty set A there is a surjection A → P(A).
     j) Q is dense in R, so |Q| = |R|.
     k) |(0, 1)| = |R|
     l) The set of irrational numbers is countable.
     m) If there is a surjection A → B then |B| ≤ |A|.
     n) A set equinumerous with one of its proper subsets is infinite.
     o) Every infinite set is equinumerous with a proper subset of itself.
     p) The set of all finite subsets of R is countable.
     q) The Cantor set has the same cardinality as R.
     r) The continuum hypothesis has been proved from the ZFC axioms.
     s) |N × N| = |N|
     t) There is a largest cardinal number.
     u) There is a set of all sets.
     v) Some set is equinumerous with its power set.
     w) A countable union of countable sets is countable (with choice).
     x) ℵ₀ < c
     Answers: a) T   b) T   c) T   d) F ((0, 1) ∩ (2, 3) = ∅)   e) T   f) F
              g) T   h) T   i) F   j) F   k) T   l) F   m) T   n) T   o) T
              p) F (it has cardinality c)   q) T   r) F (independent)   s) T
              t) F   u) F (Russell)   v) F (Cantor)   w) T   x) T

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep coming back to one picture, pairing off two sets with
  arrows; say 'same size = there is a bijection' until it is automatic; act
  out Hilbert's Hotel with room numbers; draw the grid for N × N and walk the
  diagonals; for the diagonal argument write out a list of five decimals and
  build the new number digit by digit in a different colour. Emphasise that
  to show 'countable' it is enough to INJECT into N. Harder: prove
  Schröder–Bernstein (chase the chains a → f(a) → g(f(a)) → ...); show
  |R^N| = c but |R^R| = 2^c; show that every uncountable closed set in R has
  cardinality c (Cantor–Bendixson); prove that the set of all subsets of N
  that are either finite or cofinite is countable while the set of
  'eventually periodic' 0–1 sequences is countable; show that any countable
  dense linear order without end points is order-isomorphic to Q (Cantor's
  back-and-forth argument); and read about Cohen's proof that the continuum
  hypothesis cannot be proved.


================================================================================
TOPIC 9.3 — EQUIVALENCE RELATIONS, PARTITIONS, ORDERS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A RELATION on a set A is a subset R ⊆ A × A; write a R b for (a, b) ∈ R.
    R is REFLEXIVE if a R a for all a ∈ A; IRREFLEXIVE if a R a for no a;
    SYMMETRIC if a R b ⇒ b R a; ANTISYMMETRIC if (a R b and b R a) ⇒ a = b;
    ASYMMETRIC if a R b ⇒ not b R a; TRANSITIVE if (a R b and b R c) ⇒ a R c.
    'Antisymmetric' is NOT the negation of 'symmetric' (∅ and the identity
    relation are both). A relation can be drawn as a directed graph or a 0–1
    matrix.
  - An EQUIVALENCE RELATION (~) is reflexive, symmetric and transitive. The
    EQUIVALENCE CLASS of a is [a] = {x ∈ A : x ~ a}; the set of classes is the
    QUOTIENT A/~. Two classes are either EQUAL or DISJOINT, and a ~ b ⇔
    [a] = [b]. Trap: 'symmetric + transitive ⇒ reflexive' is false (a R b and
    b R a give a R a only if a is related to SOMETHING).
  - A PARTITION of A is a collection of non-empty, pairwise disjoint subsets
    (BLOCKS) whose union is A. FUNDAMENTAL THEOREM: the classes of an
    equivalence relation form a partition, and every partition comes from
    exactly one equivalence relation (x ~ y ⇔ same block). An equivalence
    relation with blocks of sizes n₁, ..., nₖ contains n₁² + ... + nₖ² pairs.
  - Standard examples: congruence mod n on Z (n classes [0], ..., [n − 1],
    quotient Z/nZ); the KERNEL of a function, x ~ y ⇔ f(x) = f(y) (classes are
    the fibres f⁻¹(c), and every equivalence arises this way via a ↦ [a]);
    x − y ∈ Z on R (the circle R/Z); (a, b) ~ (c, d) ⇔ a + d = b + c on N × N
    (builds Z); (a, b) ~ (c, d) ⇔ ad = bc on Z × (Z − {0}) (builds Q);
    parallel lines; similar triangles.
  - WELL-DEFINEDNESS: a rule on classes, [a] ↦ F(a), is a function only if
    a ~ a′ ⇒ F(a) = F(a′). Addition and multiplication of classes mod n are
    well defined; [a] ↦ [a] from Z/6 to Z/4 is not (0 ≡ 6 mod 6 but not
    mod 4); a/b ↦ a + b on Q is not (1/2 = 2/4 but 3 ≠ 6).
  - COUNTING on an n-element set: 2^(n²) relations; 2^(n² − n) reflexive;
    2^(n(n + 1)/2) symmetric; 2^(n(n − 1)/2) reflexive and symmetric;
    2ⁿ·3^(n(n − 1)/2) antisymmetric. Equivalence relations = partitions =
    BELL number Bₙ: 1, 1, 2, 5, 15, 52, 203, 877, 4140 (n = 0 to 8), with
    Bₙ₊₁ = ∑ₖ C(n, k)Bₖ. Partitions into k blocks = STIRLING number S(n, k),
    S(n, k) = kS(n − 1, k) + S(n − 1, k − 1), S(n, 2) = 2ⁿ⁻¹ − 1; surjections
    n → k number k!·S(n, k).
  - A PARTIAL ORDER (≤, a POSET) is reflexive, ANTISYMMETRIC and transitive:
    ≤ on R, ⊆ on P(X), divisibility | on N (NOT on Z: 2 | −2 and −2 | 2). A
    STRICT order < is irreflexive and transitive. a and b are COMPARABLE if
    a ≤ b or b ≤ a; a TOTAL (linear) order makes every pair comparable. The
    HASSE DIAGRAM draws only the covering relations (a < b with nothing in
    between), larger elements higher.
  - SPECIAL ELEMENTS of a poset: m is MAXIMAL if nothing is strictly above it,
    GREATEST (maximum) if it is above everything; a greatest element is
    unique and is the only maximal one, but maximal elements need not be
    greatest (there may be several, or none). Similarly minimal/least. For
    S ⊆ P: upper and lower bounds; SUPREMUM (least upper bound, lub) and
    INFIMUM (glb). In (N, |): sup{a, b} = lcm(a, b), inf = gcd(a, b); in
    (P(X), ⊆): A ∪ B and A ∩ B. A poset in which every pair has a sup and an
    inf is a LATTICE.
  - A WELL-ORDER is a total order in which every non-empty subset has a least
    element: N is well ordered, Z, Q ∩ [0, 1] and (0, 1] are not. The
    well-ordering of N is equivalent to induction. The LEXICOGRAPHIC order
    on N × N ((a, b) < (c, d) if a < c, or a = c and b < d) is a well-order;
    the PRODUCT order (a ≤ c and b ≤ d) is only partial.
  - CHAINS (totally ordered subsets), ANTICHAINS (pairwise incomparable) and
    LINEAR EXTENSIONS (total orders containing ≤; 'topological sorting'):
    every finite poset has one. In P({1, ..., n}) the largest antichain has
    C(n, ⌊n/2⌋) elements (Sperner).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. On A = {1, 2, 3, 4} decide whether each relation is reflexive (R),
    symmetric (S), antisymmetric (A) or transitive (T). Which are equivalence
    relations, which partial orders? Give the classes of any equivalence.
      R₁ = {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (4,4), (1,2), (2,1)}
      R₂ = {(1,2), (2,3), (1,3)}
      R₃ = {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (4,4), (1,2), (2,3)}
      R₄ = {(1,2), (2,1), (1,1)}
      R₅ = ∅
    Answer: R₁: R, S, T (equivalence; classes {1, 2}, {3}, {4}). R₂: A, T
    (a strict order, not reflexive). R₃: R, A, not T. R₄: S only. R₅: S, A, T
    (not R).
    Teacher explains: Check each property against its definition, hunting
    for ONE failure:
       reflexive:     are all four loops (1,1), ..., (4,4) present?
       symmetric:     for each (a, b), is (b, a) there?
       antisymmetric: is there a pair (a, b), (b, a) with a ≠ b?
       transitive:    for each 'path' (a, b), (b, c), is (a, c) there?
    R₃ fails transitivity: (1, 2) and (2, 3) are present but (1, 3) is not.
    R₄: (2, 1) and (1, 2) force (2, 2) for transitivity, and (2, 2) is
    missing; it is not reflexive either. R₂ has no pair to contradict
    antisymmetry, and its only path 1 → 2 → 3 is closed by (1, 3). R₅ = ∅
    satisfies every 'if ... then' property VACUOUSLY (there is no pair to
    test) but fails reflexivity, which demands loops. A relation that is
    both symmetric and antisymmetric can only contain loops. Common
    mistakes: calling R₄ antisymmetric because (1,1) is there; thinking a
    relation must be either symmetric or antisymmetric. Now you try: the
    identity relation {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (4,4)}. (R, S, A, T: it is both
    an equivalence relation, with classes {1}, {2}, {3}, {4}, and a partial
    order)

D2. Decide which properties hold and identify the equivalence relations and
    partial orders: (a) on Z, a ~ b iff a − b is even; (b) on N, a | b; (c) on
    Z, a | b; (d) on R, x R y iff |x − y| ≤ 1; (e) on R, x R y iff xy > 0;
    (f) on R, x ~ y iff x − y ∈ Q.
    Answer: (a) equivalence (2 classes: evens, odds). (b) partial order.
    (c) reflexive, transitive, not symmetric, not antisymmetric. (d)
    reflexive, symmetric, not transitive. (e) symmetric, transitive, not
    reflexive (it is an equivalence on R − {0}, classes (0, ∞) and (−∞, 0)).
    (f) equivalence (uncountably many classes x + Q).
    Teacher explains: (a) a − a = 0 is even; if a − b = 2k then
    b − a = 2(−k); if a − b = 2k and b − c = 2l then a − c = 2(k + l). (b)
    a | a; a | b and b | a with a, b ≥ 1 force a = b; a | b, b | c give
    c = b·l = a·k·l. (c) On Z antisymmetry fails: 2 | −2 and −2 | 2 but
    2 ≠ −2; symmetry fails: 2 | 4 but not 4 | 2. (d) The 'close to' relation:
       0 R 1  (|0 − 1| = 1)   and   1 R 2   but   |0 − 2| = 2 > 1
    so not transitive: 'close to close to' need not be 'close to'. (e) 0·0 =
    0 is not > 0, so 0 is related to nothing and reflexivity fails; drop 0
    and the relation becomes 'same sign', an equivalence with two classes.
    (f) x − x = 0 ∈ Q; x − y ∈ Q ⇒ y − x ∈ Q; sums of rationals are rational.
    Each class x + Q is countable, so there must be uncountably many classes
    (choosing one element from each gives the famous non-measurable Vitali
    set). Common mistake: calling (d) an equivalence because it 'looks like'
    equality. Now you try: on R, x ~ y iff x² = y². (Equivalence;
    [x] = {x, −x}, [0] = {0})

D3. Prove that congruence modulo n (a ≡ b iff n | a − b) is an equivalence
    relation on Z, describe the classes for n = 5, and show that
    [a] + [b] = [a + b] and [a]·[b] = [ab] are well defined. Compute
    [3] + [4], [3]·[4], the class of −17 and the class of 2024 in Z/5.
    Answer: Five classes [0], [1], [2], [3], [4] with [r] = {5k + r : k ∈ Z}.
    [3] + [4] = [2], [3]·[4] = [2], −17 ∈ [3], 2024 ∈ [4].
    Teacher explains: The three properties: n | 0; n | a − b ⇒ n | b − a;
    n | a − b and n | b − c ⇒ n | (a − b) + (b − c) = a − c. By the division
    algorithm every a is 5q + r with r ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3, 4}, so the classes are
       [0] = {..., −5, 0, 5, 10, ...}     [1] = {..., −4, 1, 6, 11, ...}
       [2] = {..., −3, 2, 7, 12, ...}     [3] = {..., −2, 3, 8, 13, ...}
       [4] = {..., −1, 4, 9, 14, ...}
    WELL DEFINED means the answer does not depend on the representatives
    chosen. If a′ = a + 5s and b′ = b + 5t then
       a′ + b′ = (a + b) + 5(s + t)        ≡ a + b
       a′b′   = ab + 5(at + bs + 5st)      ≡ ab
    Now compute: [3] + [4] = [7] = [2]; [3][4] = [12] = [2]. For −17 use a
    remainder in 0..4: −17 = 5·(−4) + 3, so −17 ∈ [3] (not [−2] 'as a
    remainder'; [−2] = [3] anyway). 2024 = 5·404 + 4 ∈ [4]. Common mistakes:
    thinking −17 mod 5 is −2 and stopping; forgetting to check well
    definedness for division, which is NOT always possible ([2]·[x] = [1]
    has no solution mod 4). Now you try: in Z/7 find [5]·[6] and the class
    of −10. ([30] = [2]; [4])

D4. Prove that the equivalence classes of ~ on A form a partition of A, and
    conversely that a partition defines an equivalence relation. For the
    partition {1, 4, 7}, {2, 5, 8}, {3, 6} of {1, ..., 8}, name the relation
    and count its ordered pairs.
    Answer: Proofs below; it is congruence mod 3 restricted to {1, ..., 8};
    it contains 3² + 3² + 2² = 22 pairs.
    Teacher explains: Three things make the classes a partition:
       non-empty:  a ∈ [a] by reflexivity
       cover A:    every a lies in its own class [a]
       disjoint or equal: suppose c ∈ [a] ∩ [b]. Then c ~ a and c ~ b, so
                   a ~ c (symmetry) and a ~ b (transitivity). If x ∈ [a],
                   x ~ a ~ b, so x ∈ [b]: [a] ⊆ [b], and similarly
                   [b] ⊆ [a]. So two classes that meet are equal.
    Conversely, given a partition, define x ~ y iff x and y are in the same
    block. Every x is in some block (reflexive); 'same block' is symmetric;
    if x, y share block B and y, z share block B′, then y ∈ B ∩ B′, so
    B = B′ (blocks are disjoint) and x, z share it (transitive). The two
    constructions are inverse to each other. Counting pairs: inside a block
    of size k every ordered pair (x, y) is related, k² pairs, and there are
    no pairs across blocks:
       {1, 4, 7}: 9     {2, 5, 8}: 9     {3, 6}: 4      total 22
    Common mistakes: counting k(k − 1)/2 (unordered, without loops) instead of
    k²; forgetting to prove 'non-empty'. Now you try: how many ordered pairs
    are in the equivalence relation with blocks {a, b, c, d}, {e}, {f, g}?
    (16 + 1 + 4 = 21)

D5. For f : A → B define x ~ y iff f(x) = f(y). Prove that ~ is an
    equivalence relation and describe its classes for (a) f : R² → R,
    f(x, y) = x² + y²; (b) g : R² → R, g(x, y) = y − 2x; (c) h : R → R,
    h(x) = sin x. Show that every equivalence relation arises in this way.
    Answer: Classes: (a) circles centred at the origin and {(0, 0)};
    (b) the lines y = 2x + c; (c) [0] = {kπ : k ∈ Z},
    [π/6] = {π/6 + 2kπ} ∪ {5π/6 + 2kπ}, and in general {x + 2kπ} ∪
    {π − x + 2kπ}. Every ~ is the kernel of a ↦ [a].
    Teacher explains: The properties are inherited from '=':
    f(x) = f(x); f(x) = f(y) ⇒ f(y) = f(x); f(x) = f(y) = f(z) ⇒ f(x) = f(z).
    The class of x is the FIBRE f⁻¹(f(x)), all points with the same value:
       (a) x² + y² = r²          circle of radius r (r = 0: a point)
       (b) y − 2x = c            line of slope 2, intercept c
       (c) sin t = sin x         t = x + 2kπ or t = π − x + 2kπ
    So A/~ is in bijection with the RANGE f(A): circles ↔ [0, ∞), lines ↔ R,
    classes of (c) ↔ [−1, 1]. This is the pattern 'quotient ≅ image' that
    recurs in group theory and linear algebra. Conversely, given any
    equivalence ~ on A, the QUOTIENT MAP q : A → A/~, q(a) = [a], satisfies
    q(x) = q(y) ⇔ [x] = [y] ⇔ x ~ y, so ~ is the kernel of q. Common mistake
    in (c): forgetting the second family π − x + 2kπ. Now you try: describe
    the classes of x ~ y ⇔ ⌊x⌋ = ⌊y⌋ on R. (The intervals [n, n + 1), n ∈ Z)

D6. On N × N define (a, b) ~ (c, d) iff a + d = b + c. Prove that ~ is an
    equivalence relation, describe the classes of (5, 3) and (3, 5), and show
    that [(a, b)] + [(c, d)] = [(a + c, b + d)] is well defined. Then on
    Z × (Z − {0}) define (a, b) ≈ (c, d) iff ad = bc and prove transitivity.
    Answer: [(5, 3)] = {(n + 2, n) : n ∈ N} (this class 'is' the integer 2);
    [(3, 5)] = {(n, n + 2)} ('is' −2). Proofs below.
    Teacher explains: Think of (a, b) as 'a − b', but we may only use
    addition in N. Reflexive: a + b = b + a. Symmetric: a + d = b + c ⇒
    c + b = d + a. Transitive: from a + d = b + c and c + f = d + e add:
       a + d + c + f = b + c + d + e   ⇒   a + f = b + e   (cancel c + d)
    so (a, b) ~ (e, f). Classes: (5, 3) ~ (c, d) iff 5 + d = 3 + c iff
    c = d + 2: the pairs (3, 1), (4, 2), (5, 3), ... Well defined: if
    (a, b) ~ (a′, b′) and (c, d) ~ (c′, d′) then
       (a + c) + (b′ + d′) = (a + b′) + (c + d′) = (b + a′) + (d + c′)
                           = (b + d) + (a′ + c′)
    so (a + c, b + d) ~ (a′ + c′, b′ + d′). That is how Z is BUILT from N.
    For Q: (a, b) means a/b. Transitivity: ad = bc and cf = de. Multiply:
       adf = bcf = bde  ⇒  d(af − be) = 0  ⇒  af = be   (d ≠ 0)
    The condition 'second entry ≠ 0' is exactly what makes the cancellation
    legal. Common mistakes: using subtraction or division in the proofs
    (they do not exist yet in N and Z); in the Q case forgetting that c may
    be 0 (the proof above does not divide by c). Now you try: is
    (6, −9) ≈ (−4, 6)? (Yes: 6·6 = 36 = (−9)(−4); both are −2/3)

D7. Decide whether each rule is well defined, and prove or disprove it:
    (a) Z/6 → Z/3, [a] ↦ [a]; (b) Z/6 → Z/4, [a] ↦ [a]; (c) Z/6 → Z/12,
    [a] ↦ [2a]; (d) Q → Z, a/b ↦ a + b; (e) R/Z → R, [x] ↦ cos 2πx;
    (f) R/Z → R, [x] ↦ x².
    Answer: (a) yes (b) no (c) yes (d) no (e) yes (f) no.
    Teacher explains: A rule on classes is given through a REPRESENTATIVE, so
    we must check that equivalent representatives give the same answer:
       a ~ a′  ⇒  F(a) = F(a′)
    (a) a′ = a + 6k ⇒ a′ − a = 6k = 3(2k), so [a′] = [a] in Z/3 ✓. In general
    Z/m → Z/n, [a] ↦ [a], is well defined exactly when n | m.
    (b) [0] = [6] in Z/6, but 0 and 6 differ by 6, not a multiple of 4, so
    they give [0] ≠ [2] in Z/4 ✗.
    (c) a′ = a + 6k ⇒ 2a′ = 2a + 12k ≡ 2a (mod 12) ✓ (doubling the modulus is
    matched by doubling the element).
    (d) 1/2 = 2/4 but 1 + 2 = 3 ≠ 6 = 2 + 4 ✗. (By contrast a/b ↦ (a + b)/b
    is well defined: it is just x ↦ x + 1.)
    (e) x′ = x + k with k ∈ Z: cos(2πx + 2πk) = cos 2πx ✓ (period 1).
    (f) [0] = [1] but 0² ≠ 1² ✗.
    The failure is always shown by ONE pair of equivalent representatives
    with different outputs; success needs a general argument. Common
    mistake: 'defining' a function on a quotient without this check; much
    of the construction of Z, Q and R consists of these checks. Now you try:
    is [a] ↦ [a²] from Z/8 to Z/4 well defined? (Yes:
    (a + 8k)² = a² + 16ak + 64k² ≡ a² (mod 4))

D8. List all partitions of {1, 2, 3}. Define the Stirling number S(n, k) and
    the Bell number Bₙ, prove S(n, k) = kS(n − 1, k) + S(n − 1, k − 1), and
    compute S(4, 2), B₄, S(5, 3) and B₅. How many equivalence relations does
    a 5-element set carry, and how many surjections are there from a 5-set
    onto a 3-set?
    Answer: 5 partitions: {1,2,3}; {1,2}{3}; {1,3}{2}; {2,3}{1}; {1}{2}{3}.
    S(4, 2) = 7, B₄ = 15, S(5, 3) = 25, B₅ = 52. 52 equivalence relations;
    3!·S(5, 3) = 150 surjections.
    Teacher explains: S(n, k) = number of partitions of an n-set into k
    non-empty blocks; Bₙ = ∑ₖ S(n, k) = number of equivalence relations. For
    the recurrence, look at the element n:
       n alone in a block:  partition the other n − 1 into k − 1 blocks:
                            S(n − 1, k − 1) ways
       n with others:       partition the other n − 1 into k blocks and put
                            n into one of them: k·S(n − 1, k) ways
    Build the triangle row by row (S(n, 1) = S(n, n) = 1):
       n = 1:   1                               B₁ = 1
       n = 2:   1   1                           B₂ = 2
       n = 3:   1   3   1                       B₃ = 5
       n = 4:   1   7   6   1                   B₄ = 15
       n = 5:   1  15  25  10   1               B₅ = 52
    e.g. S(4, 2) = 2·S(3, 2) + S(3, 1) = 2·3 + 1 = 7 and S(5, 3) =
    3·S(4, 3) + S(4, 2) = 18 + 7 = 25. Check: S(n, 2) = 2ⁿ⁻¹ − 1 (split into
    a set and its non-empty complement, unordered): S(4, 2) = 7 ✓. A
    surjection 5 → 3 is a partition into 3 blocks (the fibres) with the
    blocks labelled by the 3 values in 3! ways: 6·25 = 150, which agrees with
    inclusion-exclusion 3⁵ − 3·2⁵ + 3 = 150 ✓. Common mistakes: counting
    labelled blocks (3ⁿ-type answers) as partitions; forgetting that blocks
    are non-empty. Now you try: S(6, 2) and B₆. (31; 203)

D9. On a set with n elements count the relations that are (a) arbitrary;
    (b) reflexive; (c) symmetric; (d) reflexive and symmetric;
    (e) antisymmetric. Evaluate for n = 3.
    Answer: (a) 2^(n²) = 512; (b) 2^(n² − n) = 64; (c) 2^(n(n + 1)/2) = 64;
    (d) 2^(n(n − 1)/2) = 8; (e) 2ⁿ·3^(n(n − 1)/2) = 216.
    Teacher explains: A relation is a choice, for each of the n² cells of the
    n × n matrix, of 'in' or 'out'. Split the cells into the n DIAGONAL cells
    (a, a) and the n(n − 1)/2 OFF-DIAGONAL PAIRS {(a, b), (b, a)}:
       (a) every cell free                       2^(n²)       = 2⁹ = 512
       (b) diagonal forced in, rest free         2^(n² − n)   = 2⁶ = 64
       (c) diagonal free (2ⁿ), each pair both
           in or both out (2 per pair)           2^(n + n(n − 1)/2) = 2⁶ = 64
       (d) diagonal forced, pairs as in (c)      2^(n(n − 1)/2) = 2³ = 8
       (e) diagonal free, each pair: neither,
           only (a, b), only (b, a): 3 ways      2³·3³ = 216
    Transitivity has no such simple formula: there are 13 transitive
    relations on 2 elements and 171 on 3, and the counts of partial orders
    are 3, 19, 219 for n = 2, 3, 4; only computer searches give large n.
    Common mistake in (e): allowing 4 choices per pair (both in is
    forbidden). Now you try: for n = 4, how many reflexive and how many
    symmetric relations are there? (2¹² = 4096; 2¹⁰ = 1024)

D10. Draw the Hasse diagram of the divisors of 12 under divisibility. In the
     poset P = {2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12} under | find the minimal, maximal, least
     and greatest elements. In the divisors of 12 find the upper bounds and
     the least upper bound of {2, 3}, and the greatest lower bound of {4, 6}.
     Answer: Hasse diagram below. In P: minimal 2 and 3, maximal 8 and 12,
     no least and no greatest element. Upper bounds of {2, 3}: 6 and 12,
     lub 6 = lcm(2, 3); glb{4, 6} = 2 = gcd(4, 6).
     Teacher explains: Draw an edge from a up to b only when b COVERS a (a | b
     and nothing lies strictly between):
                  12
                /    \
               4      6
                \   /   \
                  2      3
                    \   /
                      1
     The covers are 1–2, 1–3, 2–4, 2–6, 3–6, 4–12, 6–12 (7 edges); 1–4 is
     NOT an edge because 2 lies between. Transitivity is read off as upward
     paths. In P = {2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12} (no 1, and 8 added): nothing in P
     divides 2 or 3 except themselves, so both are minimal, and since neither
     divides the other there is no least element. Nothing in P is a multiple of
     8 or of 12 except themselves: both maximal, no greatest. In (N, |) sup =
     lcm and inf = gcd, so the divisors of 12 form a LATTICE. Common mistakes:
     calling 12 the greatest element of P (8 does not divide 12); drawing all
     comparabilities instead of covers. Now you try: for the divisors of 30,
     describe the Hasse diagram and find lub and glb of {6, 10}. (A cube with 8
     vertices and 12 edges; lub 30, glb 2)

D11. (a) Show that the lexicographic order on R² ((a, b) < (c, d) iff a < c,
     or a = c and b < d) is a total order, and compare (1, 5) with (2, −3)
     and (2, 1) with (2, 4). (b) Show that the product order ((a, b) ≤ (c, d)
     iff a ≤ c and b ≤ d) is a partial order but not total. (c) Which of N,
     Z, Q ∩ [0, 1], (0, 1] and S = {1 − 1/n : n ∈ N} ∪ {1} are well ordered
     by the usual ≤? (d) Prove that N × N with the lexicographic order is well
     ordered. (e) Prove that the well-ordering of N implies induction.
     Answer: (a) (1, 5) < (2, −3); (2, 1) < (2, 4). (b) (1, 5) and (2, −3)
     are incomparable. (c) N and S are well ordered; Z, Q ∩ [0, 1] and
     (0, 1] are not. (d), (e) below.
     Teacher explains: (a) LEXICOGRAPHIC = dictionary order: compare first
     letters, and only on a tie compare second letters. Given (a, b), (c, d):
     either a < c, or c < a, or a = c and then b < d, d < b or b = d. So any
     two are comparable: TOTAL. (1, 5) < (2, −3) because 1 < 2; the second
     coordinates are never looked at. (b) Reflexive, antisymmetric and
     transitive follow coordinatewise from ≤ on R, but 1 < 2 while 5 > −3, so
     neither (1, 5) ≤ (2, −3) nor the reverse. (c) WELL ORDERED: every
     non-empty subset has a LEAST element.
        N              yes (the well-ordering principle)
        Z              no: Z itself has no least element
        Q ∩ [0, 1]     no: {x ∈ Q : 0 < x ≤ 1} has no least element
        (0, 1]         no: (0, 1] itself has no least element
        S              yes: 0, 1/2, 2/3, ..., then 1 at the top
     S looks like N with one extra point on top (order type ω + 1). (d) Let
     T ⊆ N × N be non-empty. Let a be the least first coordinate occurring in
     T (N is well ordered), then b the least n with (a, n) ∈ T. Then (a, b) is
     the least element of T. (e) Suppose P(1) holds and P(n) ⇒ P(n + 1), but
     P fails somewhere. The set of failures has a least element m; m ≠ 1, so
     m − 1 ∈ N and P(m − 1) holds (m is the LEAST failure), hence P(m) holds:
     contradiction. Common mistakes: thinking 'has a least element' is
     enough for the set itself only (it is needed for every subset); calling
     the product order total. Now you try: is {1/n : n ∈ N} well ordered by
     ≤? By ≥? (No: no least element; yes: under ≥ it is 1, 1/2, 1/3, ... like
     N)

D12. (a) In Q = P({1, 2, 3}) − {∅, {1, 2, 3}} ordered by ⊆, find the minimal,
     maximal, least and greatest elements, a longest chain and a largest
     antichain. (b) Tasks a, b, c, d must be done one at a time, with a and
     b both before c (d is free). In how many orders can they be done? And
     if instead a comes before b and c, which both come before d? (c) Find
     sup and inf in R of S = {1 − 1/n : n ∈ N} and T = {(−1)ⁿ + 1/n : n ∈ N},
     and say whether they are attained.
     Answer: (a) Minimal: {1}, {2}, {3}; maximal: {1, 2}, {1, 3}, {2, 3}; no
     least, no greatest; longest chain e.g. {1} ⊂ {1, 2} (2 elements);
     largest antichain e.g. the three singletons (3 elements). (b) 8 orders;
     2 orders. (c) sup S = 1 (not attained), inf S = 0 = min S; sup T = 3/2
     = max T, inf T = −1 (not attained).
     Teacher explains: (a) Draw the Hasse diagram: three singletons on the
     bottom row, three pairs on the top row, each singleton joined to the two
     pairs containing it (a hexagon). Nothing lies below a singleton inside
     Q (∅ was removed), so all three are minimal; since they are pairwise
     incomparable, none is LEAST. Likewise the pairs are maximal but none is
     greatest. Several maximal elements ⇒ no greatest element. (b) A valid
     order of tasks is a LINEAR EXTENSION of the poset. First case: order a,
     b, c with c last among them: 2 ways (a b c, b a c); then insert d into
     any of the 4 gaps: 2 × 4 = 8. Second case (a 'diamond'): a first, d
     last, b and c in either order: a b c d, a c b d: 2. (c) The supremum is
     the LEAST upper bound, which need not belong to the set:
        S:  0, 1/2, 2/3, 3/4, ...    increasing to 1      sup 1, inf 0
        T:  n = 1: 0,  n = 2: 3/2,  n = 3: −2/3,  n = 4: 5/4, ...
            odd n:  −1 + 1/n > −1, approaching −1       inf −1
            even n: 1 + 1/n ≤ 3/2                       sup 3/2 at n = 2
     To prove sup S = 1: 1 is an upper bound, and for any c < 1 some
     1 − 1/n > c (Archimedes), so no smaller upper bound exists. Common
     mistakes: calling a maximal element 'the maximum'; claiming sup S = 1
     'is in S'. Now you try: sup and inf of {n/(n + 1) : n ∈ N}. (1, not
     attained; 1/2, attained at n = 1)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Relations on A = {1, 2, 3, 4}; Δ = {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (4,4)}. List which
    of R (reflexive), S (symmetric), T (transitive), A (antisymmetric) hold.
    a) Δ                                   b) A × A
    c) ∅                                   d) {(1,2), (2,1)}
    e) Δ ∪ {(1,2), (2,1)}                  f) Δ ∪ {(1,2), (2,3)}
    g) Δ ∪ {(1,2), (2,3), (1,3)}           h) {(1,2), (2,3), (1,3)}
    i) {(1,1), (2,2), (1,2), (2,1)}        j) Δ ∪ {(1,2), (2,1), (3,4), (4,3)}
    k) Δ ∪ {(1,2), (2,1), (2,3), (3,2)}    l) {(a, b) : a < b}
    m) {(a, b) : a ≤ b}                    n) {(a, b) : a divides b}
    o) {(a, b) : a + b even}               p) {(a, b) : a + b odd}
    q) {(a, b) : |a − b| ≤ 1}              r) {(a, b) : a = b + 1}
    s) {(1,2), (2,1), (1,1)}               t) {(1,1)}
    Answers:
      a) R S T A    b) R S T    c) S T A    d) S    e) R S T    f) R A
      g) R T A      h) T A      i) S T      j) R S T    k) R S    l) T A
      m) R T A      n) R T A    o) R S T    p) S        q) R S    r) A
      s) S          t) S T A

M2. Which of R, S, T, A hold? Say E if it is an equivalence relation and P if
    it is a partial order.
    a) on Z: 3 | a − b                     b) on Z: a | b
    c) on N: a | b                         d) on R: x < y
    e) on R: x ≤ y                         f) on R: |x − y| ≤ 1
    g) on R: xy > 0                        h) on R: xy ≥ 0
    i) on R: x − y ∈ Q                     j) on R: x − y ∈ Z
    k) on R: x² = y²                       l) on R²: x₁² + y₁² = x₂² + y₂²
    m) lines in the plane: parallel (or equal)
    n) lines in the plane: perpendicular
    o) on P(X): A ⊆ B                      p) on P(X), |X| ≥ 2: A ∩ B ≠ ∅
    q) on N: same number of decimal digits r) on Z: a + b even
    s) on Z: a + b odd                     t) on N: gcd(a, b) > 1
    u) on R: ⌊x⌋ = ⌊y⌋                     v) on Z: a ≤ b and b − a even
    w) on functions R → R: f(x) ≤ g(x) for all x
    x) on N × N: lexicographic ≤
    Answers:
      a) R S T, E       b) R T            c) R T A, P       d) T A (strict)
      e) R T A, P       f) R S            g) S T            h) R S
      i) R S T, E       j) R S T, E       k) R S T, E       l) R S T, E
      m) R S T, E       n) S              o) R T A, P       p) S
      q) R S T, E       r) R S T, E       s) S              t) S
      u) R S T, E       v) R T A, P       w) R T A, P       x) R T A, P (total)

M3. Describe the equivalence classes (or count them).
    a) Z, a ≡ b (mod 4): the classes
    b) Z, a ≡ b (mod 4): the class of 7
    c) R, x − y ∈ Z: the class of 0.3
    d) R, x² = y²: the class of −5
    e) R², same distance from the origin: the class of (3, 4)
    f) R², y₁ − 2x₁ = y₂ − 2x₂: the class of (1, 1)
    g) N, same number of decimal digits: size of the class of 57
    h) {1, ..., 20}, same remainder mod 6: number and sizes of the classes
    i) Z, a² ≡ b² (mod 5): the classes
    j) R − {0}, xy > 0: the classes
    k) Z × (Z − {0}), ad = bc: the class of (1, 2)
    l) N × N, a + d = b + c: the class of (5, 3)
    m) functions {1, 2, 3} → {0, 1}, f ~ g iff f(1) = g(1): classes and sizes
    n) permutations of {1, 2, 3}, σ ~ τ iff σ(1) = τ(1): classes and sizes
    o) 2 × 2 real matrices, A ~ B iff det A = det B: the classes
    p) R, sin x = sin y: the class of π/2
    Answers:
      a) [0] = 4Z, [1] = 4Z + 1, [2] = 4Z + 2, [3] = 4Z + 3
      b) {..., −5, −1, 3, 7, 11, ...} = 4Z + 3
      c) {0.3 + k : k ∈ Z}
      d) {−5, 5}
      e) the circle x² + y² = 25
      f) the line y = 2x − 1
      g) 90 (the numbers 10 to 99)
      h) 6 classes: remainders 1, 2 have 4 elements; 0, 3, 4, 5 have 3
      i) 3 classes: 5Z; {a ≡ ±1}; {a ≡ ±2} (squares ≡ 0, 1, 4)
      j) (0, ∞) and (−∞, 0)
      k) {(k, 2k) : k ∈ Z − {0}}
      l) {(n + 2, n) : n ∈ N}
      m) 2 classes (f(1) = 0 or 1), each of size 4
      n) 3 classes (σ(1) = 1, 2, 3), each of size 2
      o) one class for each real number d: {A : det A = d}
      p) {π/2 + 2kπ : k ∈ Z}

M4. Arithmetic of classes in Z/n (answers as the least non-negative
    representative).
    a) [3] + [4] in Z/5                  b) [3]·[4] in Z/5
    c) class of −17 in Z/5               d) class of 2024 in Z/7
    e) [5]·[6] in Z/7                    f) [2]⁻¹ in Z/7
    g) [3]⁻¹ in Z/11                     h) [7]⁻¹ in Z/26
    i) does [4] have an inverse in Z/6?  j) 3¹⁰⁰ in Z/7
    k) 2¹⁰ in Z/11                       l) solve [3][x] = [5] in Z/7
    m) solve 4x ≡ 6 (mod 10)             n) the units of Z/12
    o) the non-zero zero divisors of Z/12
    p) [0] + [1] + ... + [7] in Z/8      q) [6]·[10] in Z/15
    r) 17·23 in Z/9                      s) 10ⁿ in Z/9
    t) last digit of 7²⁰²⁶
    Answers: a) 2   b) 2   c) 3   d) 1   e) 2   f) 4   g) 4   h) 15
             i) no (gcd(4, 6) = 2)   j) 4   k) 1   l) x = 4   m) x ≡ 4 or 9
             (mod 10)   n) 1, 5, 7, 11   o) 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 9, 10   p) 4   q) 0
             r) 4   s) 1   t) 9

M5. Partitions and equivalence relations.
    a) List the pairs of the relation on {1, 2, 3, 4} with blocks {1, 2},
       {3}, {4}.
    b) Number of ordered pairs in the relation with blocks of sizes 3, 3, 2.
    c) Number of pairs for blocks {a, b, c, d}, {e}, {f, g}.
    d) Number of pairs of congruence mod 3 on {1, ..., 10}.
    e) The partition of {1, ..., 5} for Δ ∪ {(1,3), (3,1), (2,4), (4,2)}.
    f) The partition of {1, ..., 5} for Δ ∪ {(1,2), (2,1), (2,3), (3,2),
       (1,3), (3,1)}.
    g) Is {{1, 2}, {2, 3}, {4}} a partition of {1, 2, 3, 4}?
    h) Is {{1}, {2, 3}, ∅, {4}} a partition of {1, 2, 3, 4}?
    i) Is {{1, 3}, {2}} a partition of {1, 2, 3, 4}?
    j) The smallest equivalence relation on {1, ..., 6} containing (1, 2),
       (2, 3), (4, 5): its blocks and number of pairs.
    k) An equivalence relation on a 5-set has 13 pairs: the block sizes.
    l) An equivalence relation on a 7-set has 3 classes and 19 pairs: the
       block sizes.
    Answers:
      a) (1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (4,4), (1,2), (2,1)   (6 pairs)
      b) 22   c) 21   d) 34 (blocks of sizes 4, 3, 3)
      e) {1, 3}, {2, 4}, {5}
      f) {1, 2, 3}, {4}, {5}
      g) No (the blocks overlap in 2)
      h) No (a block is empty)
      i) No (4 is not covered)
      j) {1, 2, 3}, {4, 5}, {6}; 9 + 4 + 1 = 14 pairs
      k) 3 and 2
      l) 3, 3 and 1

M6. Well defined (W) or not (N)? For N a failing pair is given.
    a) Z/6 → Z/3, [a] ↦ [a]              b) Z/6 → Z/4, [a] ↦ [a]
    c) Z/6 → Z/12, [a] ↦ [2a]            d) Z/8 → Z/4, [a] ↦ [a²]
    e) Z/4 → Z/6, [a] ↦ [3a]             f) Z/5 → Z/10, [a] ↦ [a]
    g) Z/n × Z/n → Z/n, ([a], [b]) ↦ [ab]
    h) Z/5 × Z/5 → Z/5, ([a], [b]) ↦ [aᵇ] (b ≥ 0)
    i) Q → Z, a/b ↦ a + b                j) Q → Q, a/b ↦ (a + b)/b
    k) Q → Z, a/b ↦ b                    l) Q → Q, a/b ↦ a²/b²
    m) R/Z → R, [x] ↦ cos 2πx            n) R/Z → R, [x] ↦ x²
    o) R/Z → R/Z, [x] ↦ [2x]             p) R/Z → R/Z, [x] ↦ [x/2]
    q) Q → Q, a/b ↦ a/(b + 1) (b ≠ −1)
    r) on classes of N × N under a + d = b + c:
       [(a, b)]·[(c, d)] = [(ac + bd, ad + bc)]
    Answers:
      a) W   b) N (0 ~ 6 but [0] ≠ [2])   c) W   d) W   e) W
      f) N (0 ~ 5 but [0] ≠ [5] in Z/10)   g) W
      h) N ([2]^[1] = [2] but [2]^[6] = [64] = [4])   i) N (1/2 = 2/4: 3 ≠ 6)
      j) W (it is x ↦ x + 1)   k) N (1/2 = 2/4: 2 ≠ 4)   l) W   m) W
      n) N (0 ~ 1: 0 ≠ 1)   o) W   p) N (0 ~ 1: [0] ≠ [1/2])
      q) N (1/1 = 2/2: 1/2 ≠ 2/3)   r) W (it is the product of integers
      a − b and c − d)

M7. Stirling numbers S(n, k) (partitions of an n-set into k blocks) and Bell
    numbers Bₙ.
    a) S(4, 2)       b) S(4, 3)       c) S(5, 2)       d) S(5, 3)
    e) S(5, 4)       f) S(6, 2)       g) S(6, 3)       h) S(6, 4)
    i) S(6, 5)       j) S(7, 2)       k) S(7, 3)       l) S(10, 9)
    m) S(10, 2)      n) B₃            o) B₄            p) B₅
    q) B₆            r) B₇            s) B₈
    t) partitions of a 6-set into three blocks of size 2
    u) partitions of a 6-set into two blocks of size 3
    v) partitions of a 4-set into two blocks of size 2
    w) equivalence relations on a 5-set with exactly 2 classes
    x) surjections from a 6-set onto a 3-set
    Answers: a) 7   b) 6   c) 15   d) 25   e) 10   f) 31   g) 90   h) 65   i) 15
             j) 63   k) 301   l) 45   m) 511   n) 5   o) 15   p) 52   q) 203
             r) 877   s) 4140   t) 15   u) 10   v) 3   w) 15   x) 540

M8. Count the relations on an n-element set with the given property.
    a) all, n = 2                        b) all, n = 3
    c) all, n = 4                        d) reflexive, n = 2
    e) reflexive, n = 4                  f) symmetric, n = 2
    g) symmetric, n = 3                  h) symmetric, n = 4
    i) reflexive and symmetric, n = 4    j) antisymmetric, n = 2
    k) antisymmetric, n = 3              l) asymmetric, n = 3
    m) irreflexive, n = 3                n) symmetric and antisymmetric, n = 3
    o) reflexive and antisymmetric, n = 3
    p) transitive, n = 2                 q) transitive, n = 3
    r) partial orders, n = 3             s) total orders, n = 4
    t) equivalence relations, n = 4      u) relations from a 2-set to a 3-set
    v) reflexive, symmetric and antisymmetric, n = 5

             i) 64   j) 12   k) 216   l) 27   m) 64   n) 8   o) 27   p) 13
             q) 171   r) 19   s) 24   t) 15   u) 64   v) 1 (only Δ)

M9. Find the minimal, maximal, least and greatest elements ('none' if there
    are none).
    a) {2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12} under |
    b) {1, 2, 3, 6} under |
    c) {2, 3, 5, 7} under |
    d) {1, 2, ..., 10} under |
    e) P({a, b, c}) − {∅} under ⊆
    f) P({a, b, c}) − {∅, {a, b, c}} under ⊆
    g) (0, 1] under ≤
    h) N under |
    i) N − {1} under |
    j) {(1, 3), (2, 2), (3, 1), (3, 3)} under the product order
    k) Z under ≤
    l) {1/n : n ∈ N} under ≤
    m) the divisors of 30 other than 1 and 30, under |
    n) {4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 36} under |
    o) the strings {a, ab, abc, b, ba} under 'is a prefix of'
    p) {2, 4, 8, 16} under |
    Answers (minimal; maximal; least; greatest):
      a) 2, 3; 8, 12; none; none
      b) 1; 6; 1; 6
      c) 2, 3, 5, 7; 2, 3, 5, 7; none; none

      e) {a}, {b}, {c}; {a, b, c}; none; {a, b, c}
      f) {a}, {b}, {c}; {a, b}, {a, c}, {b, c}; none; none
      g) none; 1; none; 1
      h) 1; none; 1; none
      i) the primes; none; none; none
      j) (1, 3), (2, 2), (3, 1); (3, 3); none; (3, 3)
      k) none; none; none; none
      l) none; 1; none; 1
      m) 2, 3, 5; 6, 10, 15; none; none
      n) 4, 6, 9; 36; none; 36
      o) a, b; abc, ba; none; none
      p) 2; 16; 2; 16

M10. Bounds, suprema and infima.
     a) sup, inf of {1 − 1/n : n ∈ N} in R
     b) sup, inf of {(−1)ⁿ + 1/n : n ∈ N}
     c) sup, inf of (0, 1]
     d) sup, inf of {x ∈ Q : x² < 2} in R
     e) sup, inf of {1/m + 1/n : m, n ∈ N}
     f) sup, inf of {x ∈ R : x² − 3x + 2 < 0}
     g) sup, inf of {n/(n + 1) : n ∈ N}
     h) sup, inf of {2⁻ⁿ + 3⁻ᵐ : m, n ∈ N}
     i) lub and glb of {4, 6} in (N, |)
     j) lub and glb of {12, 18} in (N, |)
     k) lub and glb of {6, 10, 15} in (N, |)
     l) lub and glb of {{1, 2}, {2, 3}} in (P({1, 2, 3}), ⊆)
     m) upper bounds of {2, 3} in the divisors of 12 under |
     n) upper bounds of {2, 3} in {1, 2, 3, 4} under |
     o) upper bounds, lub, lower bounds and glb of {4, 6} in the divisors
        of 24 under |
     p) sup of {x ∈ Q : x² < 2} in Q
     q) inf of {x > 0 : x² > 2}
     r) sup of {x ∈ R : x³ < 8}
     s) sup ∅ in the poset [0, 1] with ≤
     t) max and sup of [0, 1)
