       a) 1, 0            b) 3/2, −1           c) 1, 0            d) √2, −√2
       e) 2, 0            f) 2, 1              g) 1, 1/2          h) 5/6, 0
       i) 12, 2           j) 36, 6             k) 30, 1           l) {1, 2, 3}, {2}
       m) 6, 12           n) none (no lub)     o) 12, 24; 12; 1, 2; 2
       p) does not exist  q) √2                r) 2               s) 0
       t) no maximum; sup 1

M11. Hasse diagrams, chains, antichains and linear extensions.
     a) edges in the Hasse diagram of the divisors of 12
     b) edges in the Hasse diagram of P({a, b, c})
     c) edges in the Hasse diagram of the divisors of 30
     d) edges in the Hasse diagram of {1, ..., 6} under |
     e) elements in a longest chain of the divisors of 60
     f) size of a largest antichain in P({1, 2, 3, 4})
     g) size of a largest antichain in {1, ..., 10} under |
     h) linear extensions of a 3-element antichain
     i) linear extensions of a 4-element chain
     j) linear extensions of the diamond a < b, a < c, b < d, c < d
     k) linear extensions of a < c, b < c with d incomparable to all
     l) linear extensions of P({1, 2})
     m) linear extensions of the divisors of 12
     n) linear extensions of two disjoint chains a < b and c < d
     o) linear extensions of P({1, 2, 3})
     p) elements of {1, ..., 20} (under |) that cover 1
     q) maximal chains ∅ ⊂ ... ⊂ {1, 2, 3} in P({1, 2, 3})
     r) number of elements of the divisor lattice of 360
     Answers: a) 7   b) 12   c) 12   d) 6   e) 5 (1 | 2 | 4 | 12 | 60)   f) 6
              g) 5 (e.g. {6, 7, 8, 9, 10})   h) 6   i) 1   j) 2   k) 8   l) 2
              m) 5   n) 6   o) 48   p) 8 (the primes 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19)
              q) 6   r) 24

M12. Prove (sketches).
     a) Congruence mod n is an equivalence relation on Z.
     b) Two equivalence classes are equal or disjoint.
     c) A partition defines an equivalence relation.
     d) (a, b) ≈ (c, d) iff ad = bc is transitive on Z × (Z − {0}).
     e) Addition on Z/n is well defined.
     f) x ~ y iff f(x) = f(y) is an equivalence relation.
     g) 'Symmetric and transitive ⇒ reflexive' is false.
     h) Divisibility is a partial order on N but not on Z.
     i) The intersection of two equivalence relations on A is an equivalence
        relation; the union need not be.
     j) A non-empty finite poset has a minimal element.
     k) A greatest element, if it exists, is unique and is the only maximal
        element.
     l) Every finite poset has a linear extension.
     m) The lexicographic order on N × N is a well-order.
     n) If ≤ is a partial order then ≥ (a ≥ b iff b ≤ a) is a partial order,
        and a < b iff (a ≤ b and a ≠ b) is irreflexive and transitive.
     Answers:
       a) n | 0; n | a − b ⇒ n | b − a; n | a − b, n | b − c ⇒ n | a − c.
       b) c ∈ [a] ∩ [b] ⇒ a ~ c ~ b ⇒ a ~ b ⇒ [a] = [b].
       c) Same block: every x is in a block; symmetric clearly; x, y ∈ B,
          y, z ∈ B′ ⇒ y ∈ B ∩ B′ ⇒ B = B′.
       d) ad = bc, cf = de ⇒ adf = bcf = bde ⇒ d(af − be) = 0 ⇒ af = be.
       e) a′ = a + nk, b′ = b + nl ⇒ a′ + b′ = a + b + n(k + l).
       f) Inherited from reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity of '='.
       g) {(1, 1)} on {1, 2} is symmetric and transitive, not reflexive (2 is
          related to nothing).
       h) On N: a | b, b | a ⇒ b = ak, a = bl ⇒ kl = 1 ⇒ a = b. On Z:
          2 | −2 and −2 | 2 but 2 ≠ −2.
       i) Each property survives intersection. Union: mod 2 ∪ mod 3 relates
          0 ~ 2 and 2 ~ 5 (2 ≡ 5 mod 3) but not 0 ~ 5: not transitive.
       j) Start anywhere and step down while possible; no infinite descent in
          a finite set (or take an element with fewest elements below it).
       k) g, g′ greatest ⇒ g ≤ g′ and g′ ≤ g ⇒ g = g′; if m is maximal,
          m ≤ g forces m = g.
       l) Induction on |P|: remove a minimal element m (j), extend the rest,
          put m first.
       m) Least first coordinate a in S, then least b with (a, b) ∈ S.
       n) Reflexivity, antisymmetry, transitivity read backwards;
          a < b < c ⇒ a ≤ c, and a = c would give a ≤ b ≤ a, so a = b,
          contradiction.

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw every small relation as arrows between dots (loops for
  reflexive, two-way arrows for symmetric, 'shortcuts' for transitive) and
  look for the missing arrow; colour the blocks of a partition and point
  out that an equivalence relation is just 'same colour'; practise
  well-definedness with fractions (1/2 = 2/4) before quotient groups; for
  posets always draw the Hasse diagram before naming maximal or greatest
  elements. Harder: prove that the equivalence relations on A, ordered by
  inclusion, form a lattice isomorphic (reversed) to the partitions ordered
  by refinement; show Bₙ = (1/e) ∑ (k ≥ 0) kⁿ/k! (Dobiński); prove
  Dilworth's theorem for finite posets (the least number of chains covering
  P equals the largest antichain) and Sperner's theorem; show that every
  partial order on a finite set is the intersection of its linear
  extensions; and prove that a total order is a well-order iff it has no
  strictly decreasing infinite sequence.


================================================================================
TOPIC 9.4 — CONSTRUCTION OF NUMBER SYSTEMS: N, Z, Q, R (DEDEKIND CUTS / CAUCHY
SEQUENCES)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - PEANO AXIOMS for N = {0, 1, 2, ...}: there is an element 0 and a SUCCESSOR
    map S : N → N such that (i) S is one-one, (ii) 0 is not a successor, (iii)
    INDUCTION: any subset of N containing 0 and closed under S is all of N.
    Write 1 = S(0), 2 = S(1), .... Addition and multiplication are defined by
    RECURSION: a + 0 = a, a + S(b) = S(a + b); a·0 = 0, a·S(b) = a·b + a. Every
    law (associative, commutative, distributive, cancellation) is then PROVED by
    induction. Order: a ≤ b means b = a + c for some c ∈ N.
  - INTEGERS: Z = (N × N)/~ where (a, b) ~ (c, d) ⇔ a + d = b + c; the class
    [(a, b)] stands for 'a − b'. Operations: [(a, b)] + [(c, d)] =
    [(a + c, b + d)], [(a, b)]·[(c, d)] = [(ac + bd, ad + bc)],
    −[(a, b)] = [(b, a)]. N sits inside Z through n ↦ [(n, 0)]. Every class has
    exactly one representative of the form (n, 0) or (0, n).
  - RATIONALS: Q = (Z × (Z − {0}))/~ where (a, b) ~ (c, d) ⇔ ad = bc; [(a, b)]
    stands for a/b. [(a, b)] + [(c, d)] = [(ad + bc, bd)], [(a, b)]·[(c, d)] =
    [(ac, bd)], [(a, b)]⁻¹ = [(b, a)] for a ≠ 0. Q is an ORDERED FIELD: a/b > 0
    (with b > 0) exactly when a > 0. Z sits inside Q through n ↦ [(n, 1)].
  - WELL-DEFINEDNESS: an operation on classes is defined through
    representatives, so you MUST prove the answer does not depend on the
    representatives chosen. Transitivity of ~ and well-definedness use
    cancellation in the previous system (in N for Z; in Z, with b ≠ 0, for Q).
  - Q HAS GAPS: √2 is irrational, and A = {x ∈ Q : x ≤ 0 or x² < 2} is
    non-empty and bounded above in Q but has NO least upper bound in Q (for any
    rational p > 0, q = (2p + 2)/(p + 2) lies strictly between p and √2 and has
    q² − 2 = 2(p² − 2)/(p + 2)², the same sign as p² − 2).
  - SUPREMUM: s = sup A when s is an upper bound of A and no smaller number is;
    equivalently, s is an upper bound and ∀ε > 0 ∃a ∈ A with a > s − ε.
    COMPLETENESS (least-upper-bound property): every non-empty subset of R that
    is bounded above has a supremum in R. R is the unique complete ordered field
    (up to isomorphism). Infima: inf A = −sup(−A).
  - DEDEKIND CUTS: a cut is α ⊆ Q with (i) α ≠ ∅ and α ≠ Q, (ii) downward
    closed (p ∈ α, q < p ⇒ q ∈ α), (iii) no greatest element. R = the set of
    all cuts; α ≤ β means α ⊆ β; α + β = {p + q : p ∈ α, q ∈ β}; the rational r
    is the cut r* = {p ∈ Q : p < r}; −α = {p : −p − r ∉ α for some rational
    r > 0}. The supremum of a bounded set of cuts is their UNION, which is
    exactly why R is complete.
  - CAUCHY SEQUENCES: (xₙ) is Cauchy when ∀ε > 0 ∃N such that
    |xₘ − xₙ| < ε for all m, n ≥ N. Cauchy sequences of rationals need not
    converge in Q (1, 1.4, 1.41, 1.414, ...). R = (Cauchy sequences in Q)/~
    where (xₙ) ~ (yₙ) ⇔ xₙ − yₙ → 0; add and multiply termwise; (xₙ) is
    POSITIVE when xₙ ≥ c for all large n, for some rational c > 0. Every
    Cauchy sequence is bounded; that is what makes the product well defined.
  - CONSEQUENCES of completeness: ARCHIMEDEAN property (∀x > 0, y ∃n ∈ N with
    nx > y; so 1/n → 0); DENSITY (between any two reals lies a rational: take
    1/n < y − x and m = ⌊nx⌋ + 1; and an irrational: m/n + √2/N for large N);
    NESTED INTERVALS; every real has a decimal expansion, with 0.999... = 1
    because the sequences (0.9, 0.99, ...) and (1, 1, ...) are equivalent.
  - TRAPS: {x ∈ Q : x ≤ 3} is NOT a cut (it has a greatest element), which is
    why each rational has exactly one cut; {x ∈ Q : x² < 2} is not a cut (not
    downward closed); an intersection of cuts need not be a cut; 'Cauchy' and
    'convergent' agree in R but not in Q; N and Z do have the least-upper-bound
    property (a bounded set of integers has a maximum) but are not fields.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Using only the Peano axioms and the recursive definitions a + 0 = a,
    a + S(b) = S(a + b), a·0 = 0, a·S(b) = a·b + a, prove that 2 + 2 = 4 and
    that 2·2 = 4. Why can there be no natural number n with S(n) = 0?
    Answer: 2 + 2 = S(S(2)) = S(3) = 4 and 2·2 = 2·1 + 2 = 2 + 2 = 4. No n has
    S(n) = 0 because axiom (ii) says 0 is not a successor.
    Teacher explains: The numbers are NAMES: 1 = S(0), 2 = S(1), 3 = S(2),
    4 = S(3). Addition is defined by recursion on the SECOND argument, so we
    peel one S off the right each step:
       2 + 2 = 2 + S(1)      = S(2 + 1)
             = S(2 + S(0))   = S(S(2 + 0))
             = S(S(2))       = S(3) = 4
    For multiplication first get 2·1 = 2·S(0) = 2·0 + 2 = 0 + 2. Careful: 0 + 2
    = 2 is NOT a definition (the definition is a + 0 = a); it needs its own
    little induction (0 + 0 = 0; if 0 + b = b then 0 + S(b) = S(0 + b) = S(b)).
    Then
       2·2 = 2·S(1) = 2·1 + 2 = 2 + 2 = 4
    The point of the construction: nothing about numbers is assumed; every fact
    comes from the axioms. Common mistakes: using 'a + 1 = S(a)' before proving
    it (it is a + S(0) = S(a + 0) = S(a), one line, but it must be said); using
    commutativity before it has been proved. Now you try: show 3 + 1 = 4 and
    2·3 = 6. (3 + S(0) = S(3 + 0) = S(3) = 4; 2·3 = 2·2 + 2 = 4 + 2 = 6)

D2. Prove, by induction on c, that addition on N is ASSOCIATIVE:
    (a + b) + c = a + (b + c). Then prove that a + b = a + c implies b = c
    (cancellation), assuming commutativity.
    Answer: Proofs below: induction on c using a + S(c) = S(a + c), and
    cancellation by induction on a using that S is one-one.
    Teacher explains: Fix a and b, and let T = {c ∈ N : (a + b) + c =
    a + (b + c)}. Base: (a + b) + 0 = a + b = a + (b + 0), so 0 ∈ T. Step:
    suppose c ∈ T. Then
       (a + b) + S(c) = S((a + b) + c)      definition
                      = S(a + (b + c))      c ∈ T
                      = a + S(b + c)        definition, read backwards
                      = a + (b + S(c))      definition, inside
    so S(c) ∈ T. By the induction axiom T = N. For cancellation, induct on a:
    if 0 + b = 0 + c then b = c (because 0 + x = x). If the statement holds for
    a and S(a) + b = S(a) + c, then by commutativity and the definition
    S(a + b) = S(a + c); since S is ONE-ONE, a + b = a + c, and the hypothesis
    gives b = c. Notice which axiom does which job: induction runs the argument,
    injectivity of S gives cancellation. Common mistake: inducting on the wrong
    variable; the recursion is on the second argument, so induct on c. Now you
    try: prove a·(b + c) = a·b + a·c by induction on c. (c = 0: a·b = a·b + 0;
    step: a·(b + S(c)) = a·S(b + c) = a·(b + c) + a = a·b + a·c + a =
    a·b + a·S(c))

D3. On N × N define (a, b) ~ (c, d) ⇔ a + d = b + c. Prove that ~ is an
    equivalence relation. Which integer does each class represent: [(7, 3)],
    [(3, 7)], [(5, 5)], [(0, 4)]? Find the representative of [(15, 22)] having
    a zero entry.
    Answer: ~ is reflexive, symmetric and transitive (proof below). The classes
    are 4, −4, 0, −4; [(15, 22)] = [(0, 7)], which is −7.
    Teacher explains: Think of (a, b) as 'a − b' before negative numbers exist;
    the relation says a − b = c − d but is written with + only.
       reflexive:   a + b = b + a                      (commutativity)
       symmetric:   a + d = b + c ⇒ c + b = d + a      (commutativity)
       transitive:  a + d = b + c and c + f = d + e
                    ⇒ a + d + c + f = b + c + d + e    (add)
                    ⇒ a + f = b + e                    (cancel c + d)
    Transitivity is the only step with content: it needs CANCELLATION in N.
    Classes: 7 − 3 = 4; 3 − 7 = −4; 5 − 5 = 0; 0 − 4 = −4, and indeed
    (3, 7) ~ (0, 4) since 3 + 4 = 7 + 0. For (15, 22): subtract 15 from both
    entries, (15, 22) ~ (0, 7) because 15 + 7 = 22 + 0. Each class looks like a
    diagonal line of lattice points in the (a, b) plane. Common mistake: trying
    to write a − b inside N; the whole point is to avoid subtraction. Now you
    try: show (9, 2) ~ (12, 5) and name the integer. (9 + 5 = 14 = 2 + 12; 7)

D4. Integers are multiplied by [(a, b)]·[(c, d)] = [(ac + bd, ad + bc)]. Explain
    where the formula comes from, prove it is WELL DEFINED in the first factor,
    and compute [(2, 5)]·[(4, 1)] and [(2, 5)] + [(4, 1)].
    Answer: (a − b)(c − d) = (ac + bd) − (ad + bc). Product [(13, 22)] = −9;
    sum [(6, 6)] = 0.
    Teacher explains: Expand the intended meaning:
       (a − b)(c − d) = ac − ad − bc + bd = (ac + bd) − (ad + bc)
    so the positive part is ac + bd and the negative part ad + bc. Well
    defined: suppose (a, b) ~ (a′, b′), that is a + b′ = b + a′. We need
       (ac + bd, ad + bc) ~ (a′c + b′d, a′d + b′c), that is
       ac + bd + a′d + b′c = ad + bc + a′c + b′d
    Group the left side as (a + b′)c + (b + a′)d and the right side as
    (b + a′)c + (a + b′)d. Since a + b′ = b + a′, both equal (a + b′)(c + d) ✓.
    (By symmetry the second factor works the same way.) Now compute:
       [(2, 5)]·[(4, 1)] = [(2·4 + 5·1, 2·1 + 5·4)] = [(13, 22)] = −9
       check: (2 − 5)(4 − 1) = (−3)(3) = −9 ✓
       [(2, 5)] + [(4, 1)] = [(6, 6)] = 0,  check −3 + 3 = 0 ✓
    Why well-definedness matters: a class has infinitely many names, and a
    'definition' that gave different answers for (2, 5) and (0, 3) would not
    define anything. Common mistake: multiplying entrywise, [(ac, bd)], which
    would give [(8, 5)] = 3, not −9. Now you try: compute [(1, 4)]·[(1, 4)] and
    [(7, 2)]·[(2, 5)]. ([(17, 8)] = 9; [(24, 39)] = −15)

D5. Build Q from pairs (a, b) with a, b ∈ Z, b ≠ 0, and (a, b) ~ (c, d) ⇔
    ad = bc. Prove transitivity, show that addition [(a, b)] + [(c, d)] =
    [(ad + bc, bd)] is well defined, and compute [(1, 2)] + [(1, 3)],
    [(2, 3)]·[(3, 4)] and the inverse of [(−3, 5)]. Why must b ≠ 0?
    Answer: Proofs below; [(5, 6)] = 5/6, [(6, 12)] = [(1, 2)] = 1/2, and
    [(5, −3)] = −5/3. With b = 0 every pair would be equivalent to (0, 0).
    Teacher explains: Transitivity: ad = bc and cf = de, with b, d, f ≠ 0.
    Multiply the two equations: (ad)(cf) = (bc)(de), that is
       cd·(af) = cd·(be)
    If c ≠ 0, cancel the nonzero factor cd to get af = be ✓. If c = 0, then
    ad = 0 with d ≠ 0 gives a = 0, and de = 0 gives e = 0, so af = 0 = be ✓.
    Cancellation of a NONZERO
    factor in Z is the tool, which is why denominators must be nonzero; if
    (a, 0) were allowed, (1, 0) ~ (0, 0) ~ (5, 7) and everything would collapse.
    Well-defined addition: let ab′ = a′b. Then
       (ad + bc)(b′d) = ab′d² + bb′cd = a′bd² + bb′cd = (a′d + b′c)(bd)
    so (ad + bc, bd) ~ (a′d + b′c, b′d) ✓. Computations:
       [(1, 2)] + [(1, 3)] = [(1·3 + 2·1, 6)] = [(5, 6)]
       [(2, 3)]·[(3, 4)] = [(6, 12)] = [(1, 2)]      since 6·2 = 12·1
       [(−3, 5)]⁻¹ = [(5, −3)] = [(−5, 3)]           since 5·3 = (−3)(−5)
    Common mistake: 'adding' as [(a + c, b + d)] (that is the mediant, which
    gives 2/5, not 5/6). Now you try: compute [(2, 3)] + [(−3, 4)] and decide
    whether (−3, 4) ~ (6, −8). ([(−1, 12)]; yes, (−3)(−8) = 24 = 4·6)

D6. Prove that √2 is irrational. Then prove that A = {x ∈ Q : x ≤ 0 or x² < 2}
    has no least upper bound in Q, using q = (2p + 2)/(p + 2). Compute q for
    p = 3/2 and for p = 4/3.
    Answer: Proofs below; p = 3/2 gives q = 10/7 (a smaller upper bound), and
    p = 4/3 gives q = 7/5 (a larger element of A).
    Teacher explains: Irrationality: suppose √2 = m/n in lowest terms. Then
    m² = 2n², so m² is even, so m is even, m = 2k; then 4k² = 2n², n² = 2k², so
    n is even too, contradicting lowest terms. Now take any rational p > 0 and
    put q = p − (p² − 2)/(p + 2) = (2p + 2)/(p + 2). Algebra gives
       q² − 2 = [(2p + 2)² − 2(p + 2)²]/(p + 2)² = 2(p² − 2)/(p + 2)²
    Two cases (p² = 2 is impossible):
       p² < 2:  q > p and q² < 2, so q ∈ A is bigger than p:
                p is NOT an upper bound.
       p² > 2:  q < p and q² > 2, so q is a SMALLER upper bound:
                p is NOT the least one.
    (Negative p or p = 0 are not upper bounds since 1 ∈ A.) So no rational is
    the least upper bound: Q has a hole at √2. Numbers:
       p = 3/2: q = (3 + 2)/(3/2 + 2) = 5/(7/2) = 10/7,  (10/7)² = 100/49 > 2
       p = 4/3: q = (8/3 + 2)/(4/3 + 2) = (14/3)/(10/3) = 7/5,  (7/5)² = 49/25
                < 2
    and 10/7 ≈ 1.4286 < 1.5, 7/5 = 1.4 > 1.333 ✓. Common mistake: saying 'the
    supremum is √2' inside Q; in Q there is no such number, and that is the
    whole point. Now you try: for B = {x ∈ Q : x ≤ 0 or x² < 3}, which q works?
    (q = p − (p² − 3)/(p + 3) = (3p + 3)/(p + 3), q² − 3 = 6(p² − 3)/(p + 3)²)

D7. Find sup and inf (saying whether they are attained) of
    A = {1 − 1/n : n ∈ N, n ≥ 1} and B = {(−1)ⁿ + 1/n : n ≥ 1}. Prove
    sup A = 1 using the ε-characterisation, and deduce the Archimedean property
    from the least-upper-bound property.
    Answer: sup A = 1 (not attained), inf A = 0 = min A; sup B = 3/2 = max B
    (n = 2), inf B = −1 (not attained).
    Teacher explains: List terms: A = {0, 1/2, 2/3, 3/4, ...}, increasing
    towards 1. Every term is < 1, so 1 is an upper bound. Given ε > 0 choose n
    with 1/n < ε; then 1 − 1/n > 1 − ε, so no number 1 − ε is an upper bound:
       s = sup A  ⇔  (a ≤ s ∀a ∈ A)  and  (∀ε > 0 ∃a ∈ A : a > s − ε)
    For B, split by parity:
       n even: 1 + 1/n = 3/2, 5/4, 7/6, ...  (decreasing to 1)
       n odd:  −1 + 1/n = 0, −2/3, −4/5, ... (decreasing to −1)
    Largest term 3/2 at n = 2; the odd terms approach −1 but all are > −1.
    Archimedean property: suppose N were bounded above in R. By completeness
    s = sup N exists; s − 1 is not an upper bound, so some n > s − 1; then
    n + 1 > s with n + 1 ∈ N, a contradiction. Hence for x > 0 and any y there
    is n > y/x, i.e. nx > y. Common mistake: using 'choose n with 1/n < ε'
    before proving the Archimedean property; in a first course this step IS the
    Archimedean property. Now you try: sup and inf of {n/(n + 1) : n ≥ 1}.
    (sup 1 not attained; inf = min = 1/2)

D8. Recall the definition of a Dedekind cut. Decide which of these subsets of Q
    are cuts, and name the real number each cut represents:
    α₁ = {x : x < 3}, α₂ = {x : x ≤ 3}, α₃ = {x : x < 0 or x² < 2},
    α₄ = {x : x² < 2}, α₅ = {x : x ≤ 0 or x² ≤ 2}, α₆ = {x : x³ < 5}.
    Answer: α₁ yes (3), α₂ no, α₃ yes (√2), α₄ no, α₅ yes (√2, the same cut as
    α₃), α₆ yes (∛5).
    Teacher explains: Check the three conditions every time:
       (i) not ∅, not Q    (ii) closed downwards    (iii) no largest element
    α₁: 0 ∈ α₁, 3 ∉ α₁; if x < 3 and y < x then y < 3; and for x < 3 the
    rational (x + 3)/2 is bigger than x and still < 3 ✓: this is 3*.
    α₂: fails (iii): 3 is its largest element. We insist on (iii) so that each
    rational gives ONE cut, not two.
    α₃: downward closed because negatives are in and x² < 2 is monotone on
    x ≥ 0; no largest element by the q of D6 ✓.
    α₄: fails (ii): 1 ∈ α₄ but −5 < 1 and 25 > 2, so −5 ∉ α₄.
    α₅: x² = 2 has no rational solution, so α₅ = α₃ as SETS of rationals ✓.
    α₆: x ↦ x³ is increasing on all of Q, so (ii) holds, and (iii) because ∛5
    is irrational, so no rational has x³ = 5 and there is always room above.
    Picture: a cut is the LEFT half of Q when you slice the line at a point:
       <=============================)  · · · · · · · · · · ·
                  the cut α          ↑ the 'number' α
    Common mistake: forgetting (iii) or writing a cut with x² < 2 alone. Now
    you try: is {x ∈ Q : x < 1/n for some n ≥ 1} a cut? {x : x < 1/n for all
    n ≥ 1}? (first: it equals {x < 1}, yes; second: it is {x ≤ 0}, no, it has
    largest element 0)

D9. Define α + β = {p + q : p ∈ α, q ∈ β}. Prove that α + β is a cut, that
    α + 0* = α, and compute (1/2)* + (1/3)*.
    Answer: Proofs below; (1/2)* + (1/3)* = (5/6)*.
    Teacher explains: (i) α, β non-empty so α + β is non-empty. Pick r ∉ α,
    s ∉ β; then every p ∈ α has p < r (otherwise r would be in α by (ii)),
    similarly q < s, so p + q < r + s and r + s ∉ α + β ✓. (ii) If t < p + q,
    then t − q < p, so t − q ∈ α and t = (t − q) + q ∈ α + β ✓. (iii) If
    p + q ∈ α + β, pick p′ ∈ α with p′ > p; then p′ + q is bigger ✓.
    α + 0* = α: if p ∈ α and q < 0 then p + q < p so p + q ∈ α: α + 0* ⊆ α.
    Conversely, for p ∈ α pick p′ ∈ α with p′ > p; then
       p = p′ + (p − p′)   with   p − p′ < 0,  i.e.  p − p′ ∈ 0*
    so α ⊆ α + 0* ✓. (Condition (iii) was essential here.) Example: p < 1/2,
    q < 1/3 give p + q < 5/6; conversely any t < 5/6 is (1/2 − d) + (1/3 − d′)
    for suitable small positive d, d′ adding to 5/6 − t. So
       (1/2)* + (1/3)* = (5/6)*
    Common mistake: defining multiplication the same way, {pq}; with negative
    rationals in both cuts that set is all of Q. Products are first defined for
    α, β > 0* as {pq : p ∈ α, q ∈ β, p > 0, q > 0} ∪ {x ∈ Q : x ≤ 0}. Now you
    try: show (2*)·(3*) = 6* with that definition. (0 < p < 2, 0 < q < 3 gives
    0 < pq < 6; conversely for 0 < t < 6 choose a rational p with
    max(t/3, 0) < p < 2 and put q = t/p, then 0 < q < 3 and t = pq)

D10. Prove that R, constructed as the set of Dedekind cuts ordered by
     inclusion, has the least-upper-bound property: if 𝒜 is a non-empty set of
     cuts bounded above by a cut β, then γ = ∪𝒜 is a cut and γ = sup 𝒜.
     Illustrate with the cuts (1 − 1/n)*, n ≥ 1.
     Answer: γ is a cut and is the least upper bound (proof below); for the
     example γ = 1*.
     Teacher explains: γ = union of all α in 𝒜.
        (i)   γ ⊇ α ≠ ∅; and γ ⊆ β ≠ Q since each α ⊆ β, so γ ≠ Q
        (ii)  p ∈ γ ⇒ p ∈ α for some α ∈ 𝒜; if q < p then q ∈ α ⊆ γ
        (iii) p ∈ α ⇒ there is p′ ∈ α ⊆ γ with p′ > p
     Upper bound: α ⊆ γ for every α ∈ 𝒜, i.e. α ≤ γ. Least: if δ is any upper
     bound, every α ⊆ δ, so the union γ ⊆ δ, i.e. γ ≤ δ ✓. That is the whole
     proof: completeness is 'the union of the left halves is a left half'. The
     example: (1 − 1/n)* = {x < 1 − 1/n}; a rational x < 1 satisfies
     x < 1 − 1/n as soon as 1/n < 1 − x, so
        ∪ₙ (1 − 1/n)* = {x ∈ Q : x < 1} = 1*
     even though no single cut in the family equals 1*: the supremum is not
     attained. Contrast with Q itself, where D6 shows sup can fail to exist.
     Common mistake: taking the INTERSECTION for the infimum: ∩ₙ (1/n)* =
     {x ≤ 0}, which is not a cut; the infimum is 0*, the interior of the
     intersection. Now you try: what is ∪ₙ αₙ where αₙ = {x : x < 0 or
     x² < 2 − 1/n}? (the √2 cut)

D11. The Babylonian (Newton) sequence x₁ = 1, xₙ₊₁ = (xₙ + 2/xₙ)/2 consists of
     rationals. Compute x₂, x₃, x₄, show that xₙ₊₁² − 2 = (xₙ² − 2)²/(4xₙ²),
     and explain why (xₙ) is a Cauchy sequence in Q with no limit in Q.
     Answer: x₂ = 3/2, x₃ = 17/12, x₄ = 577/408; x₄² − 2 = 1/166464. The
     sequence is Cauchy (decreasing from n = 2, bounded below by 1) but its
     only possible limit L satisfies L² = 2, and no rational does.
     Teacher explains: Compute exactly:
        x₂ = (1 + 2)/2 = 3/2
        x₃ = (3/2 + 4/3)/2 = (17/6)/2 = 17/12
        x₄ = (17/12 + 24/17)/2 = (289 + 288)/(204·2) = 577/408
     The identity: xₙ₊₁ = (xₙ² + 2)/(2xₙ), so
        xₙ₊₁² − 2 = [(xₙ² + 2)² − 8xₙ²]/(4xₙ²) = (xₙ² − 2)²/(4xₙ²) ≥ 0
     Errors: x₂² − 2 = 1/4, x₃² − 2 = (1/4)²/(4·9/4) = 1/144,
     x₄² − 2 = (1/144)²/(4·289/144) = 1/166464: the error SQUARES each step.
     From n ≥ 2, xₙ² ≥ 2, so xₙ₊₁ − xₙ = (2 − xₙ²)/(2xₙ) ≤ 0: decreasing and
     ≥ 1. And |xₘ − xₙ| ≤ xₙ − √2 ≤ (xₙ² − 2)/2 → 0, so the sequence is Cauchy.
     If it converged to a rational L, then L = (L + 2/L)/2 gives L² = 2,
     impossible. In R, the class of (xₙ) IS √2. Common mistake: concluding
     'Cauchy, so convergent' inside Q. Now you try: the same recipe for √3 with
     x₁ = 2: find x₂, x₃. (7/4; 97/56, with (97/56)² − 3 = 1/3136)

D12. In the Cauchy-sequence construction, prove (a) that ~ (xₙ − yₙ → 0) is an
     equivalence relation, (b) that every Cauchy sequence is bounded, and use
     (b) to show termwise multiplication is well defined. (c) Show that
     (0.9, 0.99, 0.999, ...) ~ (1, 1, 1, ...), so 0.999... = 1.
     Answer: Proofs below.
     Teacher explains: (a) Reflexive: xₙ − xₙ = 0 → 0. Symmetric: |yₙ − xₙ| =
     |xₙ − yₙ|. Transitive: |xₙ − zₙ| ≤ |xₙ − yₙ| + |yₙ − zₙ| < ε/2 + ε/2 for
     large n (the triangle inequality with ε/2 each).
     (b) Take ε = 1: there is N with |xₙ − x_N| < 1 for n ≥ N, so
        |xₙ| ≤ max(|x₁|, |x₂|, ..., |x_{N−1}|, |x_N| + 1) = M
     (finitely many terms, then a band of width 2 around x_N).
     Product: if xₙ ~ x′ₙ and yₙ ~ y′ₙ, then
        xₙyₙ − x′ₙy′ₙ = xₙ(yₙ − y′ₙ) + y′ₙ(xₙ − x′ₙ)
     and |xₙ| ≤ M, |y′ₙ| ≤ M′ turn each piece into (bounded)·(→ 0) → 0 ✓. The
     product sequence is itself Cauchy by the same splitting with m and n.
     (c) 1 − 0.99...9 (n nines) = 10⁻ⁿ → 0 (Archimedean: 10ⁿ > n > 1/ε), so the
     two sequences are equivalent and name the same real. Common mistakes:
     thinking 0.999... is 'just less than 1' (there is no real strictly
     between them: their difference is smaller than every 10⁻ⁿ); forgetting
     boundedness in the product proof. Now you try: which real is the class of
     xₙ = ∑_{k=1}^{n} 3/10ᵏ, and of yₙ = 1/3 + (−1)ⁿ/n? (both 1/3; they are
     equivalent)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Peano arithmetic. Use a + 0 = a, a + S(b) = S(a + b), a·0 = 0,
    a·S(b) = a·b + a, a⁰ = 1, a^S(b) = a^b·a; 1 = S(0), 2 = S(1), and so on.
    a) write 3 using S and 0 only          b) write 5 using S and 0 only
    c) a + 1 in terms of S                 d) 1 + 1
    e) 3 + 2                               f) 0 + 4 (needs a lemma: which?)
    g) 2·1                                 h) 3·2
    i) 2·3                                 j) 1·a (by induction: result?)
    k) 2²                                  l) 2³
    m) 3⁰                                  n) 0⁰ (by the definition)
    o) S(S(0)) + S(S(S(0))) in S-form      p) the order 2 ≤ 5: which c?
    q) is 0 = S(n) possible? which axiom   r) S(a) = S(b) ⇒ ? which axiom
    s) the base case and step of 'every n ≠ 0 is a successor'
    t) which axiom lets us define + by recursion
    Answers:
      a) S(S(S(0)))    b) S(S(S(S(S(0)))))    c) a + 1 = S(a + 0) = S(a)
      d) S(1 + 0) = S(1) = 2    e) S(3 + 1) = S(S(3 + 0)) = S(S(3)) = 5
      f) 4; lemma 0 + b = b (induction on b)    g) 2·0 + 2 = 0 + 2 = 2
      h) 3·1 + 3 = 3 + 3 = 6    i) 2·2 + 2 = 4 + 2 = 6    j) 1·a = a
      k) 2¹·2 = (2⁰·2)·2 = (1·2)·2 = 4    l) 2²·2 = 8    m) 1    n) 1
      o) S(S(S(S(S(0)))))    p) c = 3 (5 = 2 + 3)
      q) no; axiom (ii): 0 is not a successor    r) a = b; S is one-one
      s) base: 0 is excluded (vacuous); step: S(n) is a successor; so by
         induction every element is 0 or a successor
      t) the induction axiom (recursion theorem)

M2. Integers as classes [(a, b)] = 'a − b'. Give the answer as a pair and as
    an integer.
    a) [(7, 3)]                 b) [(3, 7)]                 c) [(0, 5)]
    d) [(12, 12)]               e) [(9, 0)]                 f) [(2, 3)] + [(5, 1)]
    g) [(4, 9)] + [(6, 2)]      h) [(2, 3)]·[(5, 1)]        i) [(4, 9)]·[(6, 2)]
    j) [(0, 3)]·[(0, 4)]        k) −[(8, 3)]                l) [(3, 8)] + [(6, 2)]
    m) [(3, 8)]·[(6, 2)]        n) [(1, 4)]·[(1, 4)]        o) [(3, 1)]·[(1, 3)]
    p) [(0, 5)]·[(0, 3)]        q) [(9, 4)]·[(2, 6)]        r) [(10, 1)] + [(1, 10)]
    s) [(5, 0)]·[(0, 7)]        t) [(8, 3)]·[(8, 3)]        u) [(1, 6)]·[(4, 2)]
    v) representative of [(15, 22)] with a 0 entry
    w) representative of [(30, 11)] with a 0 entry
    x) x with (x, 5) ~ (9, 2)   y) y with (4, y) ~ (1, 7)
    Answers:
      a) 4   b) −4   c) −5   d) 0   e) 9   f) [(7, 4)] = 3
      g) [(10, 11)] = −1   h) [(13, 17)] = −4   i) [(42, 62)] = −20
      j) [(12, 0)] = 12   k) [(3, 8)] = −5   l) [(9, 10)] = −1
      m) [(34, 54)] = −20   n) [(17, 8)] = 9   o) [(6, 10)] = −4
      p) [(15, 0)] = 15   q) [(42, 62)] = −20   r) [(11, 11)] = 0
      s) [(0, 35)] = −35   t) [(73, 48)] = 25   u) [(16, 26)] = −10
      v) (0, 7)   w) (19, 0)   x) 12   y) 10

M3. Rationals as classes [(a, b)] = a/b, b ≠ 0.
    a) is (2, 3) ~ (8, 12)?            b) is (−3, 4) ~ (6, −8)?
    c) is (5, 7) ~ (7, 5)?             d) is (0, 3) ~ (0, −11)?
    e) is (6, 9) ~ (−4, −6)?           f) is (1, 3) ~ (33, 100)?
    g) [(1, 2)] + [(1, 3)]             h) [(2, 3)] + [(−3, 4)]
    i) [(1, 4)] + [(1, 4)]             j) [(2, 3)]·[(9, 4)]
    k) [(5, 6)] + [(−1, −3)]           l) [(−4, 7)]·[(7, −2)]
    m) [(1, −2)] + [(3, 5)]            n) inverse of [(−3, 5)]
    o) [(3, 4)] ÷ [(9, 8)]             p) [(1, 2)] − [(1, 3)]
    q) which is larger: [(5, −7)] or [(−2, 3)]?
    r) a rational strictly between 2/3 and 3/4 (mediant)
    s) the midpoint of 2/3 and 3/4
    t) is [(a, b)] > 0 when a = −3, b = −5?
    u) the mediant rule (a + c)/(b + d): does it define a function on Q?
    v) the embedding of −4 ∈ Z in Q
    Answers:
      a) yes   b) yes   c) no   d) yes   e) yes (6·(−6) = −36 = 9·(−4))
      f) no (100 ≠ 99)   g) [(5, 6)]   h) [(−1, 12)]   i) [(8, 16)] = 1/2
      j) [(18, 12)] = 3/2   k) [(−21, −18)] = 7/6   l) [(−28, −14)] = 2
      m) [(−1, −10)] = 1/10   n) [(5, −3)] = −5/3   o) [(24, 36)] = 2/3
      p) 1/6   q) [(−2, 3)] (−2/3 > −5/7)   r) 5/7   s) 17/24
      t) yes (3/5)   u) no: (1, 2) ~ (2, 4) but with (1, 3) the mediants 2/5
      and 3/7 differ; not well defined   v) [(−4, 1)]

M4. Which of N, Z, Q, R have the property? (List all that do.)
    a) closed under subtraction         b) every element has an additive inverse
    c) every nonzero element has a multiplicative inverse
    d) is a field                       e) is an ordered field
    f) Archimedean (∀x > 0, y ∃n : nx > y)
    g) every non-empty subset bounded above has a supremum in the set
    h) every Cauchy sequence of its elements converges in the set
    i) countable                        j) between any two elements lies another
    k) every non-empty subset has a least element
    l) contains a solution of x² = 2    m) contains a solution of x + 5 = 2
    n) contains a solution of 3x = 2    o) every element has a successor that is
       the next element
    Answers: a) Z, Q, R   b) Z, Q, R   c) Q, R   d) Q, R   e) Q, R
             f) N, Z, Q, R   g) N, Z, R (in N, Z a bounded set has a maximum)
             h) N, Z, R (Cauchy integer sequences are eventually constant)
             i) N, Z, Q   j) Q, R   k) N   l) R   m) Z, Q, R   n) Q, R
             o) N, Z

M5. Find sup and inf (write 'none' if they do not exist in R), and say
    whether each is attained (max/min). Here n, m ∈ {1, 2, 3, ...}.
    a) {1/n}                       b) {1 − 1/n}
    c) {(−1)ⁿ/n}                   d) {n/(n + 1)}
    e) {(n + 1)/n}                 f) {x ∈ Q : 0 < x ≤ 3}
    g) {x ∈ R : x² − 5x + 6 < 0}   h) {x ∈ R : |x − 1| ≤ 4}
    i) {x ∈ Q : x² < 2}            j) {x ∈ R : x³ < 8}
    k) N                           l) {(−1)ⁿ + 1/n}
    m) {2⁻ⁿ + 3⁻ᵐ}                 n) {1/n − 1/m}
    o) {n²/2ⁿ}                     p) {m/n : m < n}
    q) {x + y : x ∈ (0, 1), y ∈ [2, 3]}
    r) {x ∈ Q : x < √3}            s) {(−2)ⁿ}
    t) {1 + (−1)ⁿ/n²}
    Answers:
      a) sup 1 = max; inf 0, not attained
      b) sup 1, not attained; inf 0 = min
      c) sup 1/2 = max (n = 2); inf −1 = min (n = 1)
      d) sup 1, not attained; inf 1/2 = min
      e) sup 2 = max; inf 1, not attained
      f) sup 3 = max; inf 0, not attained
      g) the set is (2, 3): sup 3, inf 2, neither attained
      h) the set is [−3, 5]: max 5, min −3
      i) sup √2, inf −√2, neither attained (no sup in Q at all)
      j) sup 2, not attained; inf none (unbounded below)
      k) inf 1 = min; sup none
      l) sup 3/2 = max (n = 2); inf −1, not attained
      m) sup 5/6 = max (n = m = 1); inf 0, not attained
      n) sup 1, inf −1, neither attained
      o) sup 9/8 = max (n = 3); inf 0, not attained
      p) sup 1, inf 0, neither attained
      q) the set is (2, 4): sup 4, inf 2, neither attained
      r) sup √3, not attained; inf none
      s) sup none, inf none (terms −2, 4, −8, 16, ...)
      t) sup 5/4 = max (n = 2); inf 0 = min (n = 1)

M6. Is the subset of Q a Dedekind cut? If yes, which real number is it?
    a) {x : x < 5}                     b) {x : x ≤ 5}
    c) {x : x < 0 or x² < 3}           d) {x : x² < 3}
    e) ∅                               f) Q
    g) {x : x³ < 2}                    h) {x : x > 1}
    i) {x : x < 1} ∪ {2}               j) {x : x ≤ 0 or x² ≤ 2}
    k) {x : x < 1 − 1/n for some n}    l) {x : x < 1/n for all n}
    m) {x : x < 0 or x² < 4}           n) {x : x ≤ 0 or x² ≤ 4}
    o) {x : x·|x| < 5}                 p) {x : x < π}
    q) N (as a subset of Q)            r) {x : x < −2/3}
    s) {x : 2x + 1 < 0}                t) {x : x < 1} ∩ {x : x < √2}
    Answers:
      a) yes, 5   b) no (greatest element 5)   c) yes, √3
      d) no (not downward closed)   e) no   f) no   g) yes, ∛2
      h) no (not downward closed)   i) no (not downward closed: 1.5 missing)
      j) yes, √2 (x² = 2 has no rational root)   k) yes, 1
      l) no (it is {x ≤ 0}, greatest element 0)   m) yes, 2
      n) no (greatest element 2)   o) yes, √5   p) yes, π
      q) no   r) yes, −2/3   s) yes, −1/2   t) yes, 1

M7. Arithmetic and order of cuts (r* = {p ∈ Q : p < r}; √2-cut = {x : x < 0
    or x² < 2}).
    a) 2* + 3*                            b) (1/2)* + (1/3)*
    c) (−3)* + 3*                         d) −(3*)
    e) ∪ₙ (1 − 1/n)*                      f) ∩ₙ (1/n)*: a cut?
    g) ∪ₙ (−1/n)*                         h) ∪ₙ (2 − 1/n²)*
    i) is 1.41 in the √2-cut?             j) is 1.42 in the √2-cut?
    k) is 7/5 in the √2-cut?              l) is 17/12 in the √2-cut?
    m) is the √2-cut ⊆ (3/2)*?            n) is (7/5)* ⊆ the √2-cut?
    o) (2*)·(3*) (positive cuts)          p) (√2-cut)·(√2-cut)
    q) sup{(1 − 1/n)*}                    r) the cut of 0.999...
    s) 0* + α                             t) α + (−α)
    Answers:
      a) 5*   b) (5/6)*   c) 0*   d) (−3)*   e) 1*
      f) no: it is {x ≤ 0}; the infimum is 0*   g) 0*   h) 2*
      i) yes (1.9881 < 2)   j) no (2.0164 > 2)   k) yes (49/25 < 2)
      l) no (289/144 > 2)   m) yes   n) yes   o) 6*   p) 2*   q) 1*
      r) 1*   s) α   t) 0*

M8. Is the sequence of rationals Cauchy? Does it converge in Q? What is its
    limit in R?
    a) 1/n                            b) (−1)ⁿ
    c) 1, 1.4, 1.41, 1.414, ... (√2)  d) ∑_{k=0}^{n} 1/k!
    e) n                              f) (1 + 1/n)ⁿ
    g) ∑_{k=1}^{n} 1/k                h) ∑_{k=1}^{n} 1/2ᵏ
    i) F_{n+1}/F_n (Fibonacci)        j) ∑_{k=1}^{n} (−1)^{k+1}/k
    k) ∑_{k=0}^{n} (−1)ᵏ/(2k + 1)      l) ∑_{k=1}^{n} 9/10ᵏ
    m) ⌊10ⁿπ⌋/10ⁿ                     n) (1 + (−1)ⁿ)/n
    o) ∑_{k=1}^{n} 1/k²               p) Babylonian for √3, x₁ = 2
    q) (−1)ⁿ/n                        r) n/(n + 1)
    Answers:
      a) Cauchy; yes; 0   b) not Cauchy; no; none
      c) Cauchy; no; √2   d) Cauchy; no; e
      e) not Cauchy (unbounded); none   f) Cauchy; no; e
      g) not Cauchy (H_{2n} − H_n ≥ 1/2); diverges to ∞
      h) Cauchy; yes; 1   i) Cauchy; no; (1 + √5)/2
      j) Cauchy; no; ln 2   k) Cauchy; no; π/4   l) Cauchy; yes; 1
      m) Cauchy; no; π   n) Cauchy; yes; 0   o) Cauchy; no; π²/6
      p) Cauchy; no; √3   q) Cauchy; yes; 0   r) Cauchy; yes; 1

M9. Babylonian sequences xₙ₊₁ = (xₙ + a/xₙ)/2. Give x₂, x₃, x₄ as fractions
    (and x₄² − a where asked).
    a) a = 2, x₁ = 1                   b) a = 2, x₁ = 1: x₄² − 2
    c) a = 3, x₁ = 2                   d) a = 3, x₁ = 2: x₃² − 3
    e) a = 5, x₁ = 2                   f) a = 5, x₁ = 2: x₃² − 5
    g) a = 10, x₁ = 3                  h) a = 10, x₁ = 3: x₃² − 10
    i) a = 7, x₁ = 3                   j) a = 7, x₁ = 3: x₃² − 7
    k) a = 3, x₁ = 1                   l) decimal value of x₄ for a = 2
    m) decimal value of x₄ for a = 10  n) the identity for xₙ₊₁² − a
    Answers:
      a) 3/2, 17/12, 577/408   b) 1/166464
      c) 7/4, 97/56, 18817/10864   d) 1/3136
      e) 9/4, 161/72, 51841/23184   f) 1/5184
      g) 19/6, 721/228, 1039681/328776   h) 1/51984
      i) 8/3, 127/48, 32257/12192   j) 1/2304
      k) 2, 7/4, 97/56   l) 1.4142157 (√2 = 1.4142136)
      m) 3.1622777 (√10 = 3.1622777)
      n) xₙ₊₁² − a = (xₙ² − a)²/(4xₙ²)

M10. Write the real number defined by the repeating decimal (the class of its
     truncation sequence) as a fraction in lowest terms.
     a) 0.(3)        b) 0.(9)         c) 0.(142857)    d) 0.1(6)
     e) 0.(27)       f) 1.2(34)       g) 0.08(3)       h) 2.(5)
     i) 0.(123)      j) 0.4(9)        k) 3.1(4)        l) 0.0(45)
     m) 0.(076923)   n) 0.58(3)       o) 1.(6)         p) 0.2(142857)
     (the bracket repeats: 0.1(6) = 0.1666...)
     Answers: a) 1/3   b) 1   c) 1/7   d) 1/6   e) 3/11   f) 611/495
              g) 1/12   h) 23/9   i) 41/333   j) 1/2   k) 283/90   l) 1/22
              m) 1/13   n) 7/12   o) 5/3   p) 3/14

M11. Archimedean property and density. In h)-p) use the proof recipe: take
     the LEAST n ∈ N with 1/n < y − x, then m = ⌊nx⌋ + 1, so x < m/n < y.
     a) least n with 1/n < 0.003          b) least n with 1/n < 0.0007
     c) least n with 0.07n > 5            d) least n with 2⁻ⁿ < 10⁻⁶
     e) least n with 2ⁿ > 10⁹             f) least n with 1/n² < 10⁻⁶
     g) least n with (0.9)ⁿ < 0.01        h) x = 0.3, y = 0.31
     i) x = √2, y = 1.42                  j) x = 1/3, y = 0.34
     k) x = π, y = 3.15                   l) x = 0.9, y = 0.91
     m) x = e, y = 2.72                   n) x = √3, y = 1.74
     o) x = −0.51, y = −0.5               p) x = 2/7, y = 3/10
     q) an irrational number between 0 and 0.001
     r) an irrational number between 1/3 and 1/2
     Answers:
       a) 334   b) 1429   c) 72   d) 20   e) 30   f) 1001   g) 44
       h) n = 101, m = 31: 31/101 ≈ 0.30693
       i) n = 173, m = 245: 245/173 ≈ 1.41618
       j) n = 151, m = 51: 51/151 ≈ 0.33775
       k) n = 119, m = 374: 374/119 = 22/7 ≈ 3.14286
       l) n = 101, m = 91: 91/101 ≈ 0.90099
       m) n = 583, m = 1585: 1585/583 ≈ 2.71870
       n) n = 126, m = 219: 219/126 = 73/42 ≈ 1.73810
       o) n = 101, m = −51: −51/101 ≈ −0.50495
       p) n = 71, m = 21: 21/71 ≈ 0.29577
       q) for example √2/2000 ≈ 0.000707
       r) for example 1/3 + √2/10 ≈ 0.47475

M12. Give a concise proof (sketch).
     a) √3 is irrational
     b) √6 is irrational
     c) ∛2 is irrational
     d) √2 + √3 is irrational
     e) log₂ 3 is irrational
     f) if r ≠ 0 is rational and x irrational, then rx and r + x are irrational
     g) {x ∈ Q : x ≤ 0 or x² < 3} has no supremum in Q
     h) N is not bounded above in R (Archimedean property)
     i) between any two reals x < y there is a rational
     j) between any two reals x < y there is an irrational
     k) sup A is unique
     l) if A ⊆ B are non-empty and bounded above, sup A ≤ sup B
     m) sup(A + B) = sup A + sup B, where A + B = {a + b}
     n) the relation (a, b) ~ (c, d) ⇔ a + d = b + c respects addition on Z
     o) a Dedekind cut α contains every rational p < q for some q ∈ α, and
        every rational in α is less than every rational not in α
     p) a convergent sequence in Q is Cauchy
     Answers:
       a) 3n² = m² ⇒ 3 | m ⇒ m = 3k ⇒ n² = 3k² ⇒ 3 | n; contradicts lowest
          terms (3 is prime, so 3 | m² ⇒ 3 | m).
       b) m² = 6n² ⇒ 2 | m ⇒ m = 2k ⇒ 2k² = 3n² ⇒ 2 | n; contradiction.
       c) m³ = 2n³ ⇒ 2 | m ⇒ 8k³ = 2n³ ⇒ n³ = 4k³ ⇒ 2 | n; contradiction.
       d) if √2 + √3 = r ∈ Q then 3 = (r − √2)² = r² − 2r√2 + 2, so
          √2 = (r² − 1)/(2r) ∈ Q; contradiction.
       e) 2^(m/n) = 3 ⇒ 2ᵐ = 3ⁿ with m, n ≥ 1: even = odd; contradiction.
       f) if rx = s ∈ Q then x = s/r ∈ Q; if r + x = s then x = s − r ∈ Q.
       g) for p > 0 put q = (3p + 3)/(p + 3); then q² − 3 = 6(p² − 3)/(p + 3)²
          and q − p = (3 − p²)/(p + 3): if p² < 3, q is a larger element; if
          p² > 3, q is a smaller upper bound.
       h) if s = sup N existed, some n > s − 1, so n + 1 > s; contradiction.
       i) choose n with 1/n < y − x and m = ⌊nx⌋ + 1; then nx < m ≤ nx + 1 <
          ny, so x < m/n < y.
       j) apply i) to x/√2 < y/√2 to get a rational q ≠ 0 (shrink if needed)
          between them; then q√2 is irrational and x < q√2 < y.
       k) if s, t are both least upper bounds, s ≤ t and t ≤ s.
       l) sup B is an upper bound of B ⊇ A, so of A; hence sup A ≤ sup B.
       m) a + b ≤ sup A + sup B, so ≤ holds; given ε pick a > sup A − ε/2 and
          b > sup B − ε/2, so a + b > sup A + sup B − ε.
       n) (a, b) ~ (a′, b′) ⇒ (a + c) + (b′ + d) = (b + d) + (a′ + c), i.e.
          (a + c, b + d) ~ (a′ + c, b′ + d).
       o) first is condition (ii); for the second, if p ∈ α, r ∉ α and r ≤ p
          then r ∈ α by (ii) (or r = p); contradiction.
       p) xₙ → L: |xₘ − xₙ| ≤ |xₘ − L| + |L − xₙ| < ε/2 + ε/2 for m, n ≥ N.

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: build each system as a 'repair' of the previous one: Z repairs
  subtraction (pairs = debts), Q repairs division (pairs = fractions), R
  repairs gaps (cuts = left halves of the line, or Cauchy sequences = better
  and better approximations); always check an operation on classes with two
  different representatives before trusting it; draw the lattice of pairs
  (a, b) with the diagonals that form each integer; compute the Babylonian
  iterates by hand to feel a Cauchy sequence that has no rational limit.
  Harder: prove that any two complete ordered fields are isomorphic (map
  each rational to itself and extend by suprema); define multiplication of
  arbitrary cuts via signs and prove the distributive law; show the Cauchy and
  Dedekind constructions give isomorphic fields; prove that every ordered field
  with the least-upper-bound property is Archimedean, and give a non-Archimedean
  ordered field (rational functions R(x) ordered by leading coefficient, where
  x is 'infinitely large').


================================================================================
TOPIC 9.5 — SINGLE-VARIABLE CALCULUS (RIGOROUS REVISIT): EPSILON-DELTA LIMITS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - DEFINITION: lim_{x→a} f(x) = L means ∀ε > 0 ∃δ > 0 such that
    0 < |x − a| < δ ⇒ |f(x) − L| < ε. In words: f(x) can be kept within ε of L
    by keeping x within δ of a (x ≠ a). δ may depend on ε and on a, NEVER on
    x. The value f(a), or whether f is even defined at a, plays no part.
  - NEGATION: lim_{x→a} f(x) ≠ L means ∃ε > 0 such that ∀δ > 0 ∃x with
    0 < |x − a| < δ and |f(x) − L| ≥ ε. To show NO limit exists, show this for
    every L, or use two sequences xₙ → a, yₙ → a (≠ a) with f(xₙ), f(yₙ)
    tending to different values.
  - FINDING δ (scratch work, then a clean proof): LINEAR f = mx + c: δ = ε/|m|.
    Otherwise factor |f(x) − L| = |x − a|·|g(x)|, restrict δ ≤ 1 (or ≤ a
    convenient number) to get |g(x)| ≤ K, then take δ = min(1, ε/K). Examples:
    x² at 3: δ = min(1, ε/7); 1/x at 2: δ = min(1, 2ε); √x at 4:
    δ = min(4, 2ε). Any smaller δ also works; there is no 'the' δ.
  - ONE-SIDED limits (0 < x − a < δ or 0 < a − x < δ); lim exists ⇔ both
    one-sided limits exist and are equal. LIMITS AT INFINITY: lim_{x→∞} f = L
    ⇔ ∀ε ∃M : x > M ⇒ |f(x) − L| < ε. INFINITE limits: lim_{x→a} f = ∞ ⇔
    ∀M ∃δ : 0 < |x − a| < δ ⇒ f(x) > M.
  - LIMIT LAWS (proved from the definition): limits are UNIQUE; sums (ε/2 +
    ε/2), products (write fg − LM = f(g − M) + M(f − L), bound f near a),
    quotients (M ≠ 0; keep |g| > |M|/2 near a). ORDER: if f ≤ g near a then
    L ≤ M (strict inequalities become ≤). SQUEEZE: g ≤ f ≤ h, g, h → L ⇒ f → L.
    Key limits: sin x / x → 1 (squeeze cos x ≤ sin x/x ≤ 1), (1 − cos x)/x² →
    1/2.
  - SEQUENTIAL (Heine) CRITERION: lim_{x→a} f(x) = L ⇔ f(xₙ) → L for EVERY
    sequence xₙ → a with xₙ ≠ a. Used mostly to DISPROVE limits (sin(1/x),
    cos(1/x), Dirichlet's function 1_Q at 0).
  - CONTINUITY at a: ∀ε ∃δ : |x − a| < δ ⇒ |f(x) − f(a)| < ε, i.e.
    lim_{x→a} f(x) = f(a). Sums, products, quotients and compositions of
    continuous functions are continuous. Discontinuities: REMOVABLE (limit
    exists ≠ f(a)), JUMP (one-sided limits differ), INFINITE, ESSENTIAL
    (oscillating). 1_Q is continuous nowhere; x·1_Q(x) only at 0; Thomae's
    function (1/q at p/q, 0 at irrationals) exactly at the irrationals.
  - UNIFORM CONTINUITY on a set S: ∀ε ∃δ (independent of the point) with
    x, y ∈ S, |x − y| < δ ⇒ |f(x) − f(y)| < ε. Lipschitz (|f(x) − f(y)| ≤
    K|x − y|) ⇒ uniformly continuous with δ = ε/K. x² on R, 1/x on (0, 1),
    sin(1/x) on (0, 1) are NOT; √x on [0, ∞) is (δ = ε²). HEINE-CANTOR:
    continuous on a closed bounded [a, b] ⇒ uniformly continuous.
  - BIG THEOREMS on [a, b] (all need COMPLETENESS of R): INTERMEDIATE VALUE
    THEOREM (continuous f takes every value between f(a) and f(b); proof by
    sup or bisection), EXTREME VALUE THEOREM (bounded and attains max and min;
    proof by Bolzano-Weierstrass). DERIVATIVE: f′(a) = lim_{h→0} (f(a + h) −
    f(a))/h; differentiable ⇒ continuous, not conversely (|x| at 0).
  - TRAPS: quantifier order (∃δ ∀ε is a different, much stronger, statement);
    choosing δ in terms of x; forgetting 0 < |x − a|; 'f(a) = L so the limit is
    L' (true only for continuous f); 'strict inequality passes to the limit'
    (1/x > 0 but the limit is 0); IVT on a non-continuous function.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Prove from the definition that lim_{x→2} (3x − 1) = 5. Which δ works when
    ε = 0.01? Explain why ANY smaller δ also works, and what the quantifiers
    'for every ε there exists δ' mean in the picture.
    Answer: δ = ε/3; for ε = 0.01, δ = 1/300 (or anything smaller).
    Teacher explains: Scratch work: we want |f(x) − 5| < ε, so simplify it and
    look for |x − 2|:
       |(3x − 1) − 5| = |3x − 6| = 3|x − 2|
    This is < ε exactly when |x − 2| < ε/3. Clean proof: Let ε > 0. Put
    δ = ε/3. If 0 < |x − 2| < δ then
       |(3x − 1) − 5| = 3|x − 2| < 3δ = ε  ✓
    Picture: draw the horizontal band 5 ± ε around the limit; the claim is that
    some vertical band 2 ± δ is mapped entirely INTO it. The opponent chooses ε
    (any, however small); you must answer with a δ. If δ works, a smaller δ′
    gives a thinner band of x's, all of which were already fine. For ε = 0.01,
    δ = 0.01/3 ≈ 0.00333: every x in (1.99667, 2.00333) has 3x − 1 in
    (4.99, 5.01). Common mistakes: writing 'δ = ε/3 so |x − 2| < ε/3' and
    stopping (the proof must END with |f(x) − L| < ε); choosing δ depending on
    x. Now you try: prove lim_{x→−1} (5x + 2) = −3 and give δ for ε = 0.1.
    (|5x + 2 + 3| = 5|x + 1|, δ = ε/5 = 0.02)

D2. Prove that lim_{x→3} x² = 9. Explain the 'restrict δ ≤ 1' trick and find a
    suitable δ for ε = 0.1. What is the LARGEST δ that works for ε = 0.1?
    Answer: δ = min(1, ε/7) works; for ε = 0.1, δ = 1/70 ≈ 0.01429. The
    largest δ is √9.1 − 3 ≈ 0.016621.
    Teacher explains: Factor: |x² − 9| = |x − 3|·|x + 3|. The factor |x − 3| is
    what we control; |x + 3| must be BOUNDED. So first insist δ ≤ 1:
       |x − 3| < 1  ⇒  2 < x < 4  ⇒  5 < x + 3 < 7  ⇒  |x + 3| < 7
    Then |x² − 9| < 7|x − 3|, and this is < ε when |x − 3| < ε/7. Both
    conditions are guaranteed by δ = min(1, ε/7). Proof: if 0 < |x − 3| < δ,
       |x² − 9| = |x − 3||x + 3| < (ε/7)·7 = ε  ✓
    For ε = 0.1: δ = min(1, 1/70) = 1/70. The exact largest δ comes from
    solving 8.9 < x² < 9.1 near 3: x ∈ (√8.9, √9.1) = (2.98329, 3.01662); the
    nearer end is √9.1 − 3 ≈ 0.016621 (the parabola is steeper on the right).
    Our 1/70 is smaller, so it is fine: the proof never needs the best δ.
    Common mistake: 'δ = ε/|x + 3|', which depends on x and is not allowed.
    Now you try: prove lim_{x→2} (x² + x) = 6. (|x² + x − 6| = |x − 2||x + 3|,
    and |x − 2| < 1 ⇒ |x + 3| < 6, so δ = min(1, ε/6))

D3. Prove that lim_{x→2} 1/x = 1/2 and that lim_{x→1} x³ = 1. For 1/x, why do
    we need δ ≤ 1 (or at least δ < 2)?
    Answer: δ = min(1, 2ε) for 1/x; δ = min(1, ε/7) for x³. Without a
    restriction x could approach 0, where 1/x blows up.
    Teacher explains: For 1/x:
       |1/x − 1/2| = |2 − x|/(2|x|) = |x − 2|/(2|x|)
    The dangerous factor is 1/|x|: we need x kept AWAY from 0. With
    |x − 2| < 1 we get 1 < x < 3, so 1/(2|x|) < 1/2 and
       |1/x − 1/2| < |x − 2|/2 < ε   when   |x − 2| < 2ε
    So δ = min(1, 2ε). For x³ factor the difference of cubes:
       |x³ − 1| = |x − 1|·|x² + x + 1|
       |x − 1| < 1 ⇒ 0 < x < 2 ⇒ x² + x + 1 < 4 + 2 + 1 = 7
    so |x³ − 1| < 7|x − 1| and δ = min(1, ε/7). With larger δ (say δ = 3 in the
    1/x problem) x could be 0.001 and 1/x = 1000, nowhere near 1/2. Common
    mistake: bounding the bad factor from the WRONG side (we need an upper
    bound for 1/|x|, i.e. a LOWER bound for |x|). Now you try: prove
    lim_{x→3} 1/(x − 1) = 1/2. (|x − 3| < 1 ⇒ x − 1 > 1, so
    |1/(x − 1) − 1/2| = |x − 3|/(2|x − 1|) < |x − 3|/2; δ = min(1, 2ε))

D4. Prove lim_{x→4} √x = 2 and lim_{x→0⁺} √x = 0. Then prove that
    lim_{x→0} x sin(1/x) = 0 by giving an explicit δ.
    Answer: δ = min(4, 2ε); δ = ε² for the one-sided limit; δ = ε for x sin(1/x).
    Teacher explains: For square roots RATIONALISE:
       |√x − 2| = |x − 4|/(√x + 2) ≤ |x − 4|/2
    (valid for x ≥ 0, since √x + 2 ≥ 2). We need x ≥ 0 so that √x exists:
    |x − 4| < 4 guarantees it. Then |√x − 2| < ε when |x − 4| < 2ε, so
    δ = min(4, 2ε). At 0 from the right: for 0 < x < δ, √x < √δ, and √δ = ε
    when δ = ε²; so
       0 < x < ε²  ⇒  |√x − 0| < ε  ✓
    (Here δ is much SMALLER than ε for small ε: the graph is vertical at 0.)
    For x sin(1/x), use |sin| ≤ 1:
       |x sin(1/x) − 0| ≤ |x| < ε   when 0 < |x| < δ = ε
    The function is not defined at 0 at all, which does not matter. Common
    mistakes: trying to 'plug in' 1/0; writing sin(1/x) → 0 (it does NOT
    converge; only the product does, because |x| squeezes it). Now you try:
    prove lim_{x→9} √x = 3. (|√x − 3| ≤ |x − 9|/3, δ = min(9, 3ε))

D5. Write the negation of 'lim_{x→a} f(x) = L'. Use it to prove that the sign
    function sgn(x) (−1, 0, 1 for x < 0, = 0, > 0) has no limit at 0, and use
    sequences to prove that lim_{x→0} sin(1/x) does not exist.
    Answer: ∃ε > 0 ∀δ > 0 ∃x : 0 < |x − a| < δ and |f(x) − L| ≥ ε. For sgn take
    ε = 1/2; for sin(1/x) use xₙ = 1/(nπ) and yₙ = 1/(2nπ + π/2).
    Teacher explains: Negating flips each quantifier and negates the end:
       ∀ε ∃δ ∀x (0 < |x − a| < δ ⇒ |f(x) − L| < ε)
       ∃ε ∀δ ∃x (0 < |x − a| < δ  and  |f(x) − L| ≥ ε)
    sgn: suppose the limit were L. Take ε = 1/2. For any δ > 0, the points
    x = δ/2 and x = −δ/2 have sgn values 1 and −1, which are 2 apart, so they
    cannot BOTH lie within 1/2 of L (else |1 − (−1)| < 1/2 + 1/2 = 1). So one
    of them violates the definition; no L works. sin(1/x):
       xₙ = 1/(nπ) → 0,           sin(1/xₙ) = sin(nπ) = 0 → 0
       yₙ = 1/(2nπ + π/2) → 0,    sin(1/yₙ) = sin(π/2) = 1 → 1
    If the limit L existed, both sequences of values would tend to L, forcing
    0 = L = 1. The graph oscillates between −1 and 1 infinitely often in every
    interval (−δ, δ). Common mistake: showing only that f(0) is undefined;
    undefinedness at a never kills a limit. Now you try: show
    lim_{x→0} cos(1/x) does not exist. (xₙ = 1/(2nπ) gives 1, yₙ =
    1/((2n + 1)π) gives −1)

D6. Prove the limit laws: if f(x) → L and g(x) → M as x → a, then
    (a) f(x) + g(x) → L + M, (b) f(x)g(x) → LM. Why must we first show f is
    bounded near a in (b)?
    Answer: Proofs below: the ε/2 trick for sums; a splitting and a bound for
    products.
    Teacher explains: (a) Given ε > 0, use the hypotheses with ε/2: there are
    δ₁, δ₂ with |f(x) − L| < ε/2 when 0 < |x − a| < δ₁ and |g(x) − M| < ε/2
    when 0 < |x − a| < δ₂. Let δ = min(δ₁, δ₂). Then
       |(f + g) − (L + M)| ≤ |f − L| + |g − M| < ε/2 + ε/2 = ε
    (b) Add and subtract f(x)M:
       f g − L M = f (g − M) + M (f − L)
    The second term is fine: |M||f − L| is small. The first term is (something)
    × (small), and it is small only if the 'something' f(x) stays BOUNDED.
    With ε = 1: |f(x) − L| < 1 near a, so |f(x)| < |L| + 1. Then choose δ so
    that |g − M| < ε/(2(|L| + 1)) and |f − L| < ε/(2(|M| + 1)):
       |f g − L M| < (|L| + 1)·ε/(2(|L| + 1)) + |M|·ε/(2(|M| + 1)) < ε
    (The + 1 in |M| + 1 avoids division by zero if M = 0.) Common mistake:
    writing |f g − LM| ≤ |f − L||g − M|, which is FALSE. Now you try: prove
    that if f(x) → L then c·f(x) → cL. (if c = 0 trivial; else use ε/|c|)

D7. Use the squeeze theorem to find lim_{x→0} x² cos(1/x²), prove
    lim_{x→0} (sin x)/x = 1 geometrically, and deduce lim (1 − cos x)/x² and
    lim (sin 3x)/(sin 5x).
    Answer: 0; 1; 1/2; 3/5.
    Teacher explains: −x² ≤ x² cos(1/x²) ≤ x² and both bounds → 0, so the
    middle → 0. For (sin x)/x, draw the unit circle, angle x ∈ (0, π/2):
       area of triangle OAP ≤ area of sector OAP ≤ area of triangle OAT
       (1/2) sin x         ≤ (1/2) x            ≤ (1/2) tan x
    Divide by (1/2) sin x > 0 and take reciprocals:
       cos x ≤ (sin x)/x ≤ 1
    As x → 0⁺, cos x → 1, so (sin x)/x → 1; the function is even, so the same
    holds from the left. Then
       (1 − cos x)/x² = (1 − cos²x)/(x²(1 + cos x))
                      = (sin x / x)²·1/(1 + cos x) → 1·(1/2) = 1/2
       (sin 3x)/(sin 5x) = [(sin 3x)/(3x)]·[(5x)/(sin 5x)]·(3/5) → 3/5
    Common mistakes: using L'Hôpital's rule to prove (sin x)/x → 1 (circular:
    the derivative of sin needs this limit); writing sin 3x / sin 5x = 3/5
    'by cancelling sin'. Now you try: lim_{x→0} (tan x − sin x)/x³. (1/2:
    tan x (1 − cos x)/x³ = (tan x/x)·(1 − cos x)/x²)

D8. (a) Prove lim_{x→∞} (2x + 1)/(x + 3) = 2 and find M for ε = 0.01.
    (b) Prove lim_{x→1} 1/(x − 1)² = ∞ and find δ for M = 10⁶.
    (c) Find the one-sided limits of ⌊x⌋ at 2 and of |x|/x at 0.
    Answer: (a) any M ≥ 5/ε works; for ε = 0.01, x > 497 is enough. (b) δ =
    1/√M = 0.001. (c) 1 and 2; −1 and 1 (neither limit exists).
    Teacher explains: (a) |f(x) − 2| = |(2x + 1 − 2x − 6)/(x + 3)| = 5/(x + 3)
    for x > −3. We need 5/(x + 3) < ε ⇔ x > 5/ε − 3. With ε = 0.01:
       x > 500 − 3 = 497
    (Taking M = 5/ε is also fine and simpler.) (b) We need 1/(x − 1)² > M ⇔
    |x − 1| < 1/√M, so δ = 1/√M; for M = 10⁶, δ = 10⁻³. (c) Just left of 2
    (1 < x < 2) ⌊x⌋ = 1; just right (2 ≤ x < 3) it is 2:
       lim_{x→2⁻} ⌊x⌋ = 1,  lim_{x→2⁺} ⌊x⌋ = 2   (jump of size 1)
    |x|/x = −1 for x < 0 and +1 for x > 0. A two-sided limit exists exactly
    when both one-sided limits exist and AGREE. Common mistakes: writing
    lim_{x→∞} = 2 'because the leading coefficients are 2 and 1' without a
    proof (fine as a method, not as a proof); treating ∞ as a number (the
    limit '= ∞' is a statement about growth, not a real limit). Now you try:
    find M for lim_{x→∞} (3x − 2)/(x + 1) = 3 with ε = 0.05. (|diff| =
    5/(x + 1) < 0.05 ⇔ x > 99)

D9. Prove that f(x) = x² is continuous at every a ∈ R, and explain why δ must
    depend on a. Show that Dirichlet's function D(x) = 1_Q(x) is continuous
    nowhere, but g(x) = x·1_Q(x) is continuous at 0.
    Answer: δ = min(1, ε/(2|a| + 1)); D is discontinuous everywhere; g is
    continuous exactly at 0.
    Teacher explains: |x² − a²| = |x − a|·|x + a|. If |x − a| < 1 then
       |x + a| ≤ |x − a| + 2|a| < 1 + 2|a|
    so |x² − a²| < (2|a| + 1)|x − a| < ε when |x − a| < ε/(2|a| + 1). The
    parabola gets STEEPER as |a| grows, so a fixed ε needs a smaller δ far out:
    for ε = 0.1, δ = 0.1/3 at a = 1 but 0.1/201 at a = 100. (This is exactly
    why x² is not uniformly continuous on R; see D11.) Dirichlet: every
    interval contains rationals and irrationals, so for any a and any δ there
    are x within δ of a with |D(x) − D(a)| = 1; with ε = 1/2 the definition
    fails. For g at 0:
       |g(x) − g(0)| = |x·1_Q(x)| ≤ |x| < ε   when |x| < δ = ε
    At a ≠ 0, g jumps between 0 (irrationals) and ≈ a (rationals), so it is
    discontinuous. Common mistake: believing 'continuous = can be drawn without
    lifting the pen' is a proof; g cannot be drawn at all and is still
    continuous at 0. Now you try: at which points is h(x) = x² 1_Q(x) + x·(1 −
    1_Q(x)) continuous? (where x² = x: at 0 and 1)

D10. State and prove the sequential criterion: lim_{x→a} f(x) = L if and only
     if f(xₙ) → L for every sequence xₙ → a with xₙ ≠ a. Use it to show that
     if f is continuous at L′ and g(x) → L′, then f(g(x)) → f(L′).
     Answer: Proof below, both directions.
     Teacher explains: (⇒) Let ε > 0 and take the δ of the definition. Since
     xₙ → a, there is N with 0 < |xₙ − a| < δ for n ≥ N; then |f(xₙ) − L| < ε
     for n ≥ N. So f(xₙ) → L.
     (⇐) Contrapositive: suppose the limit is NOT L. Then (negation, D5)
        ∃ε > 0 ∀δ > 0 ∃x : 0 < |x − a| < δ, |f(x) − L| ≥ ε
     Use δ = 1/n for n = 1, 2, 3, ... to pick xₙ with 0 < |xₙ − a| < 1/n and
     |f(xₙ) − L| ≥ ε. Then xₙ → a, xₙ ≠ a, but f(xₙ) does not tend to L:
     a bad sequence exists. Composition: if xₙ → a (xₙ ≠ a) then
     g(xₙ) → L′ (criterion for g), so f(g(xₙ)) → f(L′) (continuity of f,
     which lets us drop the condition g(xₙ) ≠ L′). By the criterion,
     f(g(x)) → f(L′). Common mistake: in (⇐) 'checking some sequences';
     the criterion needs EVERY sequence, and it is mainly used the other way,
     to disprove limits with one or two bad sequences. Now you try: use the
     criterion to show lim_{x→0} 1_Q(x) does not exist. (xₙ = 1/n gives 1,
     yₙ = √2/n gives 0)

D11. Define uniform continuity. Prove that f(x) = x² is uniformly continuous on
     [0, 2] but not on R, that 1/x is not uniformly continuous on (0, 1), and
     that √x is uniformly continuous on [0, ∞).
     Answer: On [0, 2], δ = ε/4. On R, ε = 1 fails with x = n, y = n + 1/n.
     For 1/x, ε = 1 fails with x = 1/n, y = 1/(n + 1). For √x, δ = ε².
     Teacher explains: Uniform means ONE δ that works at every point:
        continuity:   ∀a ∀ε ∃δ(ε, a) ...
        uniform:      ∀ε ∃δ(ε) ∀a ...
     On [0, 2]: |x² − y²| = |x + y||x − y| ≤ 4|x − y| < ε when |x − y| < ε/4.
     On R: take ε = 1. Whatever δ is offered, choose n > 1/δ and
        x = n,  y = n + 1/n:  |x − y| = 1/n < δ,
        |x² − y²| = 2 + 1/n² > 1 = ε
     so no single δ works. For 1/x on (0, 1): x = 1/n, y = 1/(n + 1) are
     1/(n(n + 1)) apart (as close as we like) but |1/x − 1/y| = 1. For √x,
     assume y ≤ x: √x − √y ≤ √(x − y) (square both sides: x − 2√(xy) + y ≤
     x − y ⇔ y ≤ √(xy) ✓), so |x − y| < ε² gives |√x − √y| < ε: δ = ε² works
     everywhere even though the slope is infinite at 0. Heine-Cantor: every
     continuous function on a CLOSED BOUNDED interval is uniformly continuous;
     (0, 1) and R are not of that kind. Common mistake: 'x² is continuous,
     hence uniformly continuous'. Now you try: find δ for x³ on [−1, 1].
     (|x³ − y³| = |x − y||x² + xy + y²| ≤ 3|x − y|, δ = ε/3)

D12. Prove the Intermediate Value Theorem for continuous f on [a, b] with
     f(a) < 0 < f(b), using S = {x ∈ [a, b] : f(x) < 0} and c = sup S. Then
     use bisection to locate the real root of x³ − x − 1 = 0 in [1, 2] to
     within 1/32.
     Answer: f(c) = 0 (proof below). Bisection gives the root in
     [1.3125, 1.34375]; in fact x ≈ 1.324718.
     Teacher explains: S is non-empty (a ∈ S) and bounded above by b, so
     c = sup S exists by COMPLETENESS. Show f(c) is neither < 0 nor > 0:
        if f(c) < 0: continuity gives δ with f < 0 on (c − δ, c + δ), and
                     c < b, so points just right of c lie in S:
                     c is not an upper bound. Contradiction.
        if f(c) > 0: f > 0 on (c − δ, c + δ), so no point of S lies there,
                     and c − δ/2 is a smaller upper bound. Contradiction.
     Hence f(c) = 0. Bisection (f(x) = x³ − x − 1, f(1) = −1, f(2) = 5):
        m = 1.5      f = 0.875     > 0   root in [1, 1.5]
        m = 1.25     f = −0.2969   < 0   root in [1.25, 1.5]
        m = 1.375    f = 0.2246    > 0   root in [1.25, 1.375]
        m = 1.3125   f = −0.0515   < 0   root in [1.3125, 1.375]
        m = 1.34375  f = 0.0826    > 0   root in [1.3125, 1.34375]
     Five halvings shrink length 1 to 1/32 = 0.03125; the midpoint 1.328 is
     within 1/64 of the root 1.3247. The theorem FAILS in Q: x² − 2 on
     [1, 2] ∩ Q changes sign with no rational zero. Common mistakes: applying
     IVT to 1/x on [−1, 1] (not continuous at 0); forgetting that IVT gives
     existence, not uniqueness. Now you try: show x⁵ − 3x + 1 = 0 has a root in
     (0, 1). (f(0) = 1 > 0, f(1) = −1 < 0, f is a polynomial so continuous)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Linear limits: give the LARGEST δ that works for the given ε.
    a) lim_{x→1} (4x + 3) = 7, ε = 0.01       b) lim_{x→2} (3x − 1) = 5, ε = 0.03
    c) lim_{x→−1} (5x + 2) = −3, ε = 0.1      d) lim_{x→0} (7 − 2x) = 7, ε = 0.05
    e) lim_{x→4} (x/2 + 1) = 3, ε = 0.01      f) lim_{x→3} (7 − 4x) = −5, ε = 0.002
    g) lim_{x→10} 0.1x = 1, ε = 0.001         h) lim_{x→−2} (9x + 4) = −14, ε = 0.18
    i) lim_{x→1/2} (8x − 3) = 1, ε = 10⁻⁴     j) lim_{x→2} (2x − 1)/3 = 1, ε = 0.1
    k) lim_{x→π} πx = π², ε = 0.01            l) lim_{x→0} (3 − x)/5 = 3/5, ε = 0.02
    m) lim_{x→5} 6 = 6, ε = 0.001             n) lim_{x→a} (mx + c), m ≠ 0, any ε
    o) lim_{x→1} (100x − 99) = 1, ε = 0.5     p) lim_{x→0} (x/1000) = 0, ε = 10⁻⁶
    Answers: a) 0.0025   b) 0.01   c) 0.02   d) 0.025   e) 0.02   f) 0.0005
             g) 0.01   h) 0.02   i) 1.25 × 10⁻⁵   j) 0.15   k) 0.01/π ≈ 0.003183
             l) 0.1   m) every δ > 0 works   n) ε/|m|   o) 0.005   p) 0.001

M2. Give a δ (in terms of ε) that proves the limit. (One valid choice is
    shown; any smaller positive δ is also correct.)
    a) lim_{x→3} x² = 9                  b) lim_{x→1} x² = 1
    c) lim_{x→−2} x² = 4                 d) lim_{x→2} (x² + x) = 6
    e) lim_{x→1} (x² − 3x) = −2          f) lim_{x→1} x³ = 1
    g) lim_{x→2} x³ = 8                  h) lim_{x→2} 1/x = 1/2
    i) lim_{x→1} 1/x = 1                 j) lim_{x→1} 1/(x + 1) = 1/2
    k) lim_{x→4} √x = 2                  l) lim_{x→9} √x = 3
    m) lim_{x→0⁺} √x = 0                 n) lim_{x→a} |x| = |a|
    o) lim_{x→0} x sin(1/x) = 0          p) lim_{x→2} (x² − 4)/(x − 2) = 4
    q) lim_{x→1} (2x² − 1) = 1           r) lim_{x→1} 1/x² = 1
    s) lim_{x→0} ∛x = 0                  t) lim_{x→a} x² = a² (any a)
    u) lim_{x→3} 1/x = 1/3               v) lim_{x→0} x² cos(1/x) = 0
    Answers:
      a) min(1, ε/7)    b) min(1, ε/3)    c) min(1, ε/5)    d) min(1, ε/6)
      e) min(1, ε/2)  (x² − 3x + 2 = (x − 1)(x − 2), |x − 2| < 2)
      f) min(1, ε/7)    g) min(1, ε/19)   h) min(1, 2ε)
      i) min(1/2, ε/2)  (|x − 1| < 1/2 ⇒ x > 1/2)
      j) min(1, 2ε)     k) min(4, 2ε)     l) min(9, 3ε)     m) ε²
      n) ε              o) ε              p) ε              q) min(1, ε/6)
      r) min(1/2, ε/10)  (x ∈ (1/2, 3/2): |x + 1|/x² < (5/2)/(1/4) = 10)
      s) ε³             t) min(1, ε/(2|a| + 1))            u) min(1, 6ε)
      v) √ε  (|x² cos(1/x)| ≤ x²)

M4. Evaluate the limit (factor, rationalise or divide by the top power).
    a) lim_{x→3} (x² − 9)/(x − 3)              b) lim_{x→2} (x³ − 8)/(x − 2)
    c) lim_{x→0} (√(x + 4) − 2)/x              d) lim_{x→2} (x² − 5x + 6)/(x² − 4)
    e) lim_{x→3} (1/x − 1/3)/(x − 3)           f) lim_{x→9} (√x − 3)/(x − 9)
    g) lim_{x→1} (x⁴ − 1)/(x − 1)              h) lim_{x→0} ((1 + x)³ − 1)/x
    i) lim_{x→2} (x² + x − 6)/(x² − x − 2)     j) lim_{x→0} (√(1 + x) − √(1 − x))/x
    k) lim_{x→8} (x − 8)/(∛x − 2)              l) lim_{x→1} (2x² − 3x + 1)/(x − 1)
    m) lim_{x→0} (3x² + 2x)/(x² − 4x)          n) lim_{x→∞} (3x² − x)/(2x² + 5)

M5. Trigonometric limits and the squeeze theorem.
    a) lim_{x→0} (sin 3x)/x                    b) lim_{x→0} (sin 5x)/(sin 2x)

    k) lim_{x→π/3} (sin x − sin(π/3))/(x − π/3)

    n) lim_{x→0} (1 − cos x)/(x sin x)         o) lim_{x→0} x/(sin 3x)
