M6. One-sided limits: give lim_{x→a⁻} and lim_{x→a⁺}, and say whether the
    two-sided limit exists.
    a) ⌊x⌋, a = 2                              b) |x|/x, a = 0
    c) x² (x < 1), 2x − 1 (x ≥ 1), a = 1       d) x + 1 (x < 0), x² (x ≥ 0), a = 0
    e) 1/x, a = 0                              f) e^(1/x), a = 0
    g) 1/(1 + e^(1/x)), a = 0                  h) √(x − 2), a = 2
    i) x − ⌊x⌋, a = 3                          j) ⌊x²⌋, a = 1
    k) (x² − 1)/|x − 1|, a = 1                 l) (sin x)/|x|, a = 0
    m) arctan(1/x), a = 0                      n) ⌈x⌉, a = 0
    o) (|x| + x)/(2x), a = 0                   p) x⌊1/x⌋, a = 0 (right only)
    q) ⌊x⌋ + ⌊−x⌋, a = 1                       r) 2^(1/(x − 1)), a = 1
    Answers:
      a) 1, 2; no          b) −1, 1; no           c) 1, 1; yes, 1
      d) 1, 0; no          e) −∞, ∞; no           f) 0, ∞; no
      g) 1, 0; no          h) undefined, 0; only the right limit (0)
      i) 1, 0; no          j) 0, 1; no            k) −2, 2; no
      l) −1, 1; no         m) −π/2, π/2; no       n) 0, 1; no
      o) 0, 1; no          p) right limit 1 (1 − x < x⌊1/x⌋ ≤ 1)
      q) −1, −1; yes, −1 (although the value at 1 is 0)
      r) 0, ∞; no

M7. Limits at infinity and infinite limits: find M (or δ) as asked.
    a) (2x + 1)/(x + 3) → 2 as x → ∞: least M with x > M ⇒ error < 0.01
    b) 1/x → 0: M for ε = 0.001
    c) (3x − 2)/(x + 1) → 3: M for ε = 0.05
    d) 1/x² → 0: M for ε = 10⁻⁴
    e) (x² + 1)/x² → 1: M for ε = 10⁻⁴
    f) 1/(x − 1)² → ∞ as x → 1: δ for 'f > 1000'
    g) 1/|x| → ∞ as x → 0: δ for 'f > 10⁶'
    h) x² → ∞ as x → ∞: M for 'x² > 10⁶'
    i) −1/(x − 2)² → −∞ as x → 2: δ for 'f < −10⁴'
    j) e^(−x) → 0: M for ε = 0.001
    k) (sin x)/x → 0: an M for ε = 0.01
    l) (x + 1)/x² → 0: least M for ε = 0.01
    m) √x → ∞: M for '√x > 100'
    n) ln x → ∞: M for 'ln x > 10'
    o) √(x + 1) − √x → 0: an M for ε = 0.01
    p) 1/(x − 2) → ∞ as x → 2⁺: δ for 'f > M' (general M)
    Answers: a) 497   b) 1000   c) 99   d) 100   e) 100   f) 1/√1000 ≈ 0.03162
             g) 10⁻⁶   h) 1000   i) 0.01   j) ln 1000 ≈ 6.908   k) 100
             l) 50 + 10√26 ≈ 100.99   m) 10⁴   n) e¹⁰ ≈ 22026.5
             o) 2500 (error < 1/(2√x))   p) 1/M

M8. True (T) or false (F)? (f, g are real functions, a ∈ R.)
    a) '∃δ > 0 ∀ε > 0 : 0 < |x − a| < δ ⇒ |f(x) − L| < ε' means lim f = L
    b) the negation of lim_{x→a} f = L is: ∃ε > 0 ∀δ > 0 ∃x with
       0 < |x − a| < δ and |f(x) − L| ≥ ε
    c) if lim_{x→a} f(x) = L then f(a) = L
    d) changing f(a) can change lim_{x→a} f(x)
    e) if δ works for ε, every smaller positive δ works for ε
    f) if δ works for ε, it works for every larger ε
    g) a limit, if it exists, is unique
    h) if neither lim f nor lim g exists, lim (f + g) cannot exist
    i) if lim fg exists then lim f and lim g exist
    j) if f(x) < g(x) near a and both limits exist, then lim f < lim g
    k) if lim_{x→a} f(x) = L > 0 then f(x) > 0 for all x ≠ a near a
    l) uniformly continuous ⇒ continuous
    m) continuous on (0, 1) ⇒ bounded
    n) continuous on [a, b] ⇒ uniformly continuous
    o) continuous on [a, b] ⇒ attains a maximum
    p) if f(a)f(b) < 0 then f has a zero in (a, b)
    q) every continuous f : [0, 1] → [0, 1] has a fixed point
    r) lim_{x→a} f = L ⇔ f(xₙ) → L for every xₙ → a with xₙ ≠ a
    s) if lim_{x→a} |f(x)| = |L| then lim_{x→a} f(x) = L
    t) if lim_{x→a} |f(x)| = 0 then lim_{x→a} f(x) = 0
    u) differentiable at a ⇒ continuous at a
    v) continuous at a ⇒ differentiable at a
    Answers: a) F   b) T   c) F   d) F   e) T   f) T   g) T   h) F   i) F
             j) F   k) T   l) T   m) F   n) T   o) T   p) F   q) T   r) T
             s) F   t) T   u) T   v) F

M9. Continuity. In a)-h) find the constant(s) making f continuous everywhere;
    in i)-r) classify the behaviour at the given point (removable, jump,
    infinite, essential, or continuous).
    a) kx + 1 (x ≤ 2), x² (x > 2)
    b) (x² − 4)/(x − 2) (x ≠ 2), k (x = 2)
    c) sin(kx)/x (x ≠ 0), 3 (x = 0)
    d) kx² (x ≤ 1), 3x − k (x > 1)
    e) (√(x + 1) − 1)/x (x ≠ 0), k (x = 0)
    f) x sin(1/x) (x ≠ 0), k (x = 0)
    g) x² (x < 1), ax + b (1 ≤ x ≤ 2), 3x (x > 2)
    h) (1 − cos x)/x² (x ≠ 0), k (x = 0)
    i) (x² − 1)/(x − 1) at 1          j) ⌊x⌋ at 2
    k) 1/x at 0                       l) sin(1/x) at 0
    m) |x|/x at 0                     n) 1_Q(x) at 3
    o) x·1_Q(x) at 0                  p) x·1_Q(x) at 1
    q) Thomae's function at 1/2       r) Thomae's function at √2
    Answers:
      a) k = 3/2   b) k = 4   c) k = 3   d) k = 3/2   e) k = 1/2   f) k = 0
      g) a = 5, b = −4   h) k = 1/2
      i) removable   j) jump   k) infinite   l) essential   m) jump
      n) essential (discontinuous everywhere)   o) continuous
      p) essential (discontinuous)   q) removable (limit 0 ≠ 1/2)
      r) continuous

M10. Is f uniformly continuous on the set? If yes, give a δ(ε).
     a) 3x + 1 on R                     b) x² on [0, 2]
     c) x² on R                         d) 1/x on (0, 1)
     e) 1/x on [1, ∞)                   f) √x on [0, ∞)
     g) sin x on R                      h) sin(1/x) on (0, 1)
     i) x sin(1/x) on (0, 1)            j) eˣ on R
     k) eˣ on (−∞, 0]                   l) x³ on [−1, 1]
     m) ln x on (0, 1)                  n) ln x on [1, ∞)
     o) |x| on R                        p) x² on [−3, 3]
     q) 1/(1 + x²) on R                 r) cos(x²) on R
     s) x² on [n, n + 1]                t) any polynomial on [a, b]
     Answers:
       a) yes, ε/3   b) yes, ε/4   c) no   d) no   e) yes, ε   f) yes, ε²
       g) yes, ε   h) no   i) yes (extends continuously to [0, 1])   j) no
       k) yes, ε   l) yes, ε/3   m) no   n) yes, ε   o) yes, ε
       p) yes, ε/6   q) yes, ε (|f′| ≤ 3√3/8 < 1)   r) no
       s) yes, ε/(2n + 2)   t) yes (Heine-Cantor; δ = ε/max|f′|)

M11. Derivatives from the definition f′(a) = lim_{h→0} (f(a + h) − f(a))/h
     (write 'not differentiable' where appropriate).
     a) x² at 3                     b) x³ at 2
     c) 1/x at 2                    d) √x at 4
     e) |x| at 0                    f) x|x| at 0
     g) x² sin(1/x), 0 at 0; at 0   h) x sin(1/x), 0 at 0; at 0
     i) ∛x at 0                     j) sin x at 0
     k) 1/(x + 1) at 0              l) x² + 5x at −1
     m) x²·1_Q(x) at 0              n) x² (x ≤ 1), 2x − 1 (x > 1); at 1
     o) x² (x ≤ 1), x (x > 1); at 1 p) √(1 + x) at 0
     q) cos x at 0                  r) x⌊x⌋ at 1/2
     Answers: a) 6   b) 12   c) −1/4   d) 1/4   e) not differentiable
              (one-sided −1, 1)   f) 0   g) 0 (|h sin(1/h)| ≤ |h|)
              h) not differentiable (sin(1/h) has no limit)
              i) not differentiable (h^(−2/3) → ∞)   j) 1   k) −1   l) 3
              m) 0   n) 2   o) not differentiable (left 2, right 1)
              p) 1/2   q) 0   r) 0 (f = 0 on [0, 1))

M12. Give a concise proof (sketch).
     a) lim_{x→2} (5x − 3) = 7
     b) lim_{x→1} x² = 1
     c) lim_{x→3} 1/x = 1/3
     d) lim_{x→0} x² sin(1/x) = 0
     e) lim_{x→0} sgn(x) does not exist
     f) lim_{x→0} sin(1/x) does not exist
     g) the limit of a function at a point is unique
     h) x⁵ − 3x + 1 = 0 has a root in (0, 1)
     i) x = cos x has a solution in [0, π/2]
     j) every polynomial of odd degree has a real root
     k) a continuous f on [a, b] is bounded
     l) x² is not uniformly continuous on R
     m) f continuous at a, f(a) > 0 ⇒ f > 0 on some (a − δ, a + δ)
     n) a Lipschitz function is uniformly continuous
     o) the composition of continuous functions is continuous
     p) differentiable at a ⇒ continuous at a
     Answers:
       a) |5x − 3 − 7| = 5|x − 2|; δ = ε/5.
       b) |x² − 1| = |x − 1||x + 1| < 3|x − 1| when |x − 1| < 1; δ = min(1, ε/3).
       c) |x − 3| < 1 ⇒ x > 2 ⇒ |1/x − 1/3| = |x − 3|/(3x) < |x − 3|/6;
          δ = min(1, 6ε).
       d) |x² sin(1/x)| ≤ x² < ε when |x| < √ε; δ = √ε.
       e) ε = 1/2: for every δ, x = ±δ/2 give values 1 and −1, which cannot
          both be within 1/2 of any L.
       f) xₙ = 1/(nπ) → 0 gives 0; yₙ = 1/(2nπ + π/2) gives 1.
       g) if L ≠ L′, take ε = |L − L′|/2; for x close to a,
          |L − L′| ≤ |f(x) − L| + |f(x) − L′| < 2ε = |L − L′|; contradiction.
       h) f(0) = 1 > 0, f(1) = −1 < 0, f continuous; IVT.
       i) g(x) = x − cos x: g(0) = −1 < 0, g(π/2) = π/2 > 0; IVT.
       j) p(x)/(leading term) → 1 as x → ±∞, so p takes both signs; IVT.
       k) if not, pick xₙ with |f(xₙ)| > n; a subsequence converges to
          c ∈ [a, b] (Bolzano-Weierstrass); continuity gives f(xₙₖ) → f(c),
          bounded; contradiction.
       l) ε = 1: for any δ take n > 1/δ, x = n, y = n + 1/n: |x − y| < δ but
          |x² − y²| = 2 + 1/n² > 1.
       m) ε = f(a)/2: |f(x) − f(a)| < f(a)/2 ⇒ f(x) > f(a)/2 > 0.
       n) |f(x) − f(y)| ≤ K|x − y| < ε when |x − y| < δ = ε/K.
       o) given ε, choose η for g at f(a), then δ for f at a with
          |f(x) − f(a)| < η; then |g(f(x)) − g(f(a))| < ε.
       p) f(a + h) − f(a) = h·[(f(a + h) − f(a))/h] → 0·f′(a) = 0.

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: play the ε-δ 'game' aloud (the student names an ε, you answer
  with a δ, then swap roles); always do scratch work backwards from
  |f(x) − L| < ε and write the proof forwards; draw the ε-band and δ-band on
  the graph; practise factoring out |x − a| and bounding the other factor with
  'δ ≤ 1'; keep a list of the 'standard δs' (ε/|m|, min(1, ε/7), min(1, 2ε),
  ε²). Harder: prove Thomae's function is continuous exactly at the
  irrationals; prove the Extreme Value Theorem and Heine-Cantor from
  Bolzano-Weierstrass; show a monotone function has only jump
  discontinuities, and only countably many; prove that a continuous additive
  f (f(x + y) = f(x) + f(y)) must be f(x) = cx; construct a function
  continuous at exactly one point (x·1_Q(x)) and one differentiable at
  exactly one point (x²·1_Q(x)).


================================================================================
TOPIC 9.6 — SEQUENCES AND SERIES OF REAL NUMBERS; CONVERGENCE TESTS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - CONVERGENCE: aₙ → L means ∀ε > 0 ∃N such that n ≥ N ⇒ |aₙ − L| < ε. A
    convergent sequence has a UNIQUE limit and is BOUNDED. aₙ → ∞ means ∀M ∃N
    with aₙ > M for n ≥ N. Limit laws (sum, product, quotient), the ORDER law
    (aₙ ≤ bₙ ⇒ lim aₙ ≤ lim bₙ) and the SQUEEZE theorem hold as for functions.
  - STANDARD LIMITS: 1/nᵖ → 0 (p > 0); rⁿ → 0 for |r| < 1; n^(1/n) → 1;
    a^(1/n) → 1 (a > 0); nᵏ/aⁿ → 0 (a > 1); aⁿ/n! → 0; n!/nⁿ → 0;
    (ln n)ᵏ/nᵖ → 0; (1 + x/n)ⁿ → eˣ. Growth order: ln n ≪ nᵖ ≪ aⁿ ≪ n! ≪ nⁿ.
  - MONOTONE CONVERGENCE THEOREM: a monotone bounded sequence converges (to
    its sup if increasing). Method for recursions aₙ₊₁ = g(aₙ): prove bounded
    and monotone by induction, THEN solve L = g(L). Examples:
    aₙ₊₁ = √(2 + aₙ) → 2; (1 + 1/n)ⁿ increases to e < 3.
  - SUBSEQUENCES: if aₙ → L every subsequence → L (so two subsequences with
    different limits prove divergence). Every sequence has a MONOTONE
    subsequence; BOLZANO-WEIERSTRASS: every bounded sequence has a convergent
    subsequence. limsup aₙ (liminf aₙ) is the largest (smallest) subsequential
    limit; aₙ converges ⇔ limsup = liminf (finite).
  - CAUCHY CRITERION: (aₙ) converges in R ⇔ it is Cauchy (∀ε ∃N :
    m, n ≥ N ⇒ |aₘ − aₙ| < ε). Harmonic sums Hₙ are not Cauchy since
    H₂ₙ − Hₙ ≥ 1/2.
  - SERIES: ∑aₙ converges when the partial sums Sₙ = a₁ + ... + aₙ converge.
    GEOMETRIC ∑_{n≥0} arⁿ = a/(1 − r) for |r| < 1, diverges for |r| ≥ 1.
    TELESCOPING: ∑(bₙ − bₙ₊₁) = b₁ − lim bₙ (use partial fractions).
    nth-TERM TEST: ∑aₙ convergent ⇒ aₙ → 0; the converse is FALSE (∑1/n).
    p-SERIES ∑1/nᵖ converges ⇔ p > 1.
  - TESTS FOR aₙ ≥ 0: COMPARISON (0 ≤ aₙ ≤ bₙ); LIMIT COMPARISON (aₙ/bₙ → c,
    0 < c < ∞ ⇒ same behaviour); INTEGRAL TEST (f positive decreasing:
    ∑f(n) and ∫₁^∞ f behave alike, with ∫_{N+1}^∞ f ≤ R_N ≤ ∫_N^∞ f); CAUCHY
    CONDENSATION (∑aₙ ~ ∑2ᵏa_{2ᵏ} for decreasing aₙ).
  - RATIO TEST: |aₙ₊₁/aₙ| → L: L < 1 absolutely convergent, L > 1 divergent,
    L = 1 NO INFORMATION (∑1/n and ∑1/n² both give 1). ROOT TEST: |aₙ|^(1/n)
    → L, same conclusions. Ratio suits factorials and powers; root suits nth
    powers.
  - ALTERNATING SERIES (LEIBNIZ) TEST: if bₙ ↓ 0 then ∑(−1)ⁿ⁺¹bₙ converges,
    and |S − Sₙ| ≤ bₙ₊₁ (the error is at most the first omitted term).
  - ABSOLUTE vs CONDITIONAL: ∑|aₙ| convergent ⇒ ∑aₙ convergent (absolutely);
    ∑(−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n = ln 2 converges only CONDITIONALLY. Absolutely convergent
    series may be rearranged freely; a conditionally convergent one can be
    rearranged to any sum (RIEMANN REARRANGEMENT THEOREM).
  - TRAPS: 'aₙ → 0 so ∑aₙ converges' (false); using limit comparison or ratio
    tests on series with terms of both signs without absolute values;
    'ratio = 1 so it diverges'; confusing the SEQUENCE of terms with the
    SEQUENCE of partial sums; forgetting that the Leibniz test needs bₙ
    decreasing to 0.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Prove from the definition that (3n + 1)/(n + 2) → 3. For ε = 0.01 find
    the least N with |aₙ − 3| < ε for all n ≥ N. Prove also that
    (−1)ⁿ does not converge.
    Answer: |aₙ − 3| = 5/(n + 2); N = 499 for ε = 0.01. (−1)ⁿ diverges
    (ε = 1 argument below).
    Teacher explains: Simplify the distance first:
       |(3n + 1)/(n + 2) − 3| = |3n + 1 − 3n − 6|/(n + 2) = 5/(n + 2)
    Scratch: 5/(n + 2) < ε ⇔ n + 2 > 5/ε ⇔ n > 5/ε − 2. Proof: given ε > 0,
    by the Archimedean property choose N > 5/ε − 2; then for n ≥ N,
    |aₙ − 3| = 5/(n + 2) < ε ✓. For ε = 0.01:
       5/(n + 2) < 0.01 ⇔ n + 2 > 500 ⇔ n > 498,  so N = 499
    (n = 498 gives exactly 0.01, which is NOT < 0.01). For (−1)ⁿ: if it tended
    to L, take ε = 1; for large n both |1 − L| < 1 and |−1 − L| < 1, so
    2 = |1 − (−1)| ≤ |1 − L| + |L + 1| < 2, a contradiction. Common mistakes:
    choosing N depending on n; proving aₙ gets 'closer and closer' to 3 (it
    also gets closer to 4!); the definition needs ALL n ≥ N inside the ε-band.
    Now you try: prove (2n − 1)/(n + 1) → 2 and find N for ε = 0.001.
    (|aₙ − 2| = 3/(n + 1) < 0.001 ⇔ n > 2999; N = 3000)

D2. Prove that a convergent sequence is bounded and that its limit is unique.
    Then prove the squeeze theorem and use it to find lim (sin n)/n and
    lim (n! /nⁿ).
    Answer: Proofs below; both limits are 0.
    Teacher explains: Bounded: take ε = 1; there is N with |aₙ − L| < 1 for
    n ≥ N, so |aₙ| < |L| + 1 there. Only finitely many terms come before N:
       |aₙ| ≤ M = max(|a₁|, ..., |a_{N−1}|, |L| + 1)   for all n
    Unique: if aₙ → L and aₙ → L′ with L ≠ L′, use ε = |L − L′|/2; for large n
    both distances are < ε, so |L − L′| ≤ |aₙ − L| + |aₙ − L′| < |L − L′|,
    impossible. Squeeze: if bₙ ≤ aₙ ≤ cₙ and bₙ, cₙ → L, then for large n
       L − ε < bₙ ≤ aₙ ≤ cₙ < L + ε,   so |aₙ − L| < ε
    Applications:
       −1/n ≤ (sin n)/n ≤ 1/n                      → 0
       0 < n!/nⁿ = (1/n)·(2/n)···(n/n) ≤ 1/n      → 0
    (every factor after the first is ≤ 1). Common mistake: 'bounded ⇒
    convergent' (the sequence (−1)ⁿ is bounded but diverges). Now you try:
    find lim (2ⁿ/n!) by squeezing. (for n ≥ 3, 0 < 2ⁿ/n! = (2/1)(2/2)(2/3)···
    (2/n) ≤ 2·(2/n) = 4/n → 0)

D3. Find, with reasons, lim (n² + 3n)/(2n² − 1), lim (√(n² + n) − n) and
    lim ∛(n³ + n²) − n. Prove that n^(1/n) → 1 using the binomial theorem.
    Answer: 1/2, 1/2, 1/3, and n^(1/n) → 1 (proof below).
    Teacher explains: Divide by the TOP power, then use 1/n → 0:
       (n² + 3n)/(2n² − 1) = (1 + 3/n)/(2 − 1/n²) → 1/2
    For a difference of roots multiply by the CONJUGATE:
       √(n² + n) − n = n/(√(n² + n) + n) = 1/(√(1 + 1/n) + 1) → 1/2
    For cube roots use a³ − b³ = (a − b)(a² + ab + b²) with a = ∛(n³ + n²),
    b = n: the difference is n²/(a² + ab + b²), and a² + ab + b² ~ 3n², so the
    limit is 1/3. For n^(1/n): write n^(1/n) = 1 + hₙ with hₙ ≥ 0. Then for
    n ≥ 2, keeping one term of the binomial expansion,
       n = (1 + hₙ)ⁿ ≥ C(n, 2)hₙ² = n(n − 1)hₙ²/2
    so hₙ² ≤ 2/(n − 1) and 0 ≤ hₙ ≤ √(2/(n − 1)) → 0. Hence n^(1/n) → 1.
    Common mistakes: 'n^(1/n) → n⁰ = 1' (the exponent and the base both move;
    compare (1 + 1/n)ⁿ → e, not 1); '∞ − ∞ = 0' for √(n² + n) − n. Now you
    try: lim (2ⁿ + 3ⁿ)/(3ⁿ⁺¹ − 2ⁿ) and lim (4ⁿ + 5ⁿ)^(1/n). (divide by 3ⁿ:
    1/3; 5·(1 + (4/5)ⁿ)^(1/n) → 5)

D4. Let a₁ = √2 and aₙ₊₁ = √(2 + aₙ). Prove that (aₙ) is increasing and
    bounded above by 2, and find its limit. Compute a₂, a₃, a₄.
    Answer: The limit is 2; a₂ ≈ 1.84776, a₃ ≈ 1.96157, a₄ ≈ 1.99037.
    Teacher explains: Two inductions, then one equation.
       bounded:    a₁ = √2 < 2; if aₙ < 2 then aₙ₊₁ = √(2 + aₙ) < √4 = 2
       increasing: a₂ = √(2 + √2) > √2 = a₁; if aₙ₊₁ > aₙ then
                   aₙ₊₂ = √(2 + aₙ₊₁) > √(2 + aₙ) = aₙ₊₁
    By the MONOTONE CONVERGENCE THEOREM aₙ → L for some L ≤ 2. Let n → ∞ in
    aₙ₊₁² = 2 + aₙ (aₙ₊₁ → L too, being a subsequence):
       L² = 2 + L  ⇒  (L − 2)(L + 1) = 0  ⇒  L = 2   (L ≥ √2 > 0)
    Values: a₂ = √3.41421 = 1.84776, a₃ = √3.84776 = 1.96157,
    a₄ = √3.96157 = 1.99037: indeed creeping up to 2 (these are 2 cos(π/2ⁿ⁺¹)).
    Warning: solving L = g(L) BEFORE knowing the limit exists can mislead.
    For aₙ₊₁ = aₙ², a₁ = 2, the 'equation' L = L² gives L = 0 or 1, yet
    aₙ = 2^(2ⁿ⁻¹) → ∞. Existence must come first. Now you try: a₁ = 1,
    aₙ₊₁ = √(6 + aₙ). (increasing, bounded by 3; L² = 6 + L gives L = 3)

D5. Prove that eₙ = (1 + 1/n)ⁿ is increasing and bounded above by 3, so it
    converges (its limit is called e). Compute e₁, e₂, e₁₀, e₁₀₀, e₁₀₀₀.
    Answer: e₁ = 2, e₂ = 2.25, e₁₀ ≈ 2.59374, e₁₀₀ ≈ 2.70481,
    e₁₀₀₀ ≈ 2.71692 → e ≈ 2.71828.
    Teacher explains: Expand by the binomial theorem:
       eₙ = ∑_{k=0}^{n} C(n, k)/nᵏ
          = ∑_{k=0}^{n} (1/k!)(1 − 1/n)(1 − 2/n)···(1 − (k − 1)/n)
    Increasing: when n grows, each bracket (1 − j/n) grows and a new positive
    term appears, so eₙ < eₙ₊₁. Bounded: each bracket is ≤ 1 and k! ≥ 2^(k−1),
    so
       eₙ ≤ 1 + 1 + 1/2! + 1/3! + ... ≤ 1 + 1 + 1/2 + 1/4 + ... = 3
    By monotone convergence the limit exists, and 2 < e ≤ 3. The same
    comparison shows e = ∑1/k! = 2.718281828... The numbers show SLOW
    convergence: error ≈ e/(2n), so e₁₀₀₀ is still wrong in the 3rd decimal.
    Common mistakes: '(1 + 1/n)ⁿ → 1ⁿ = 1' or '→ ∞ because ⁿ → ∞' (the base
    tends to 1 while the exponent tends to ∞: an indeterminate 1^∞ form). Now
    you try: find lim (1 + 3/n)ⁿ and lim (1 − 1/n)ⁿ. (e³; 1/e)

D6. For aₙ = (−1)ⁿ(1 + 1/n) find the even and odd subsequences, limsup,
    liminf, sup and inf, and decide whether aₙ converges. State Bolzano-
    Weierstrass and prove that the harmonic sums Hₙ = 1 + 1/2 + ... + 1/n do
    not form a Cauchy sequence.
    Answer: a₂ₖ → 1, a₂ₖ₊₁ → −1; limsup 1, liminf −1, sup 3/2 (n = 2),
    inf −2 (n = 1); divergent. H₂ₙ − Hₙ ≥ 1/2.
    Teacher explains: Split by parity:
       n even: 1 + 1/n = 3/2, 5/4, 7/6, ...     → 1  (decreasing)
       n odd: −(1 + 1/n) = −2, −4/3, −6/5, ...   → −1 (increasing)
    The subsequential limits are exactly ±1, so limsup = 1, liminf = −1; they
    differ, so aₙ diverges (a convergent sequence has every subsequence
    tending to the same limit). Note sup and inf are different from limsup and
    liminf: sup = 3/2, inf = −2 are attained by early terms. BOLZANO-
    WEIERSTRASS: every bounded sequence has a convergent subsequence (here,
    two of them). Harmonic sums:
       H₂ₙ − Hₙ = 1/(n + 1) + ... + 1/(2n) ≥ n·(1/(2n)) = 1/2
    (n terms, each ≥ the smallest 1/(2n)). With ε = 1/2 no N can make all
    |Hₘ − Hₙ| < ε, so (Hₙ) is not Cauchy and cannot converge: Hₙ → ∞, though
    very slowly (H₁₀₀ ≈ 5.187 ≈ ln 100 + 0.5772). Common mistake: 'Hₙ₊₁ − Hₙ
    = 1/(n + 1) → 0, so Hₙ is Cauchy' (Cauchy needs ALL pairs m, n ≥ N, not
    just neighbours). Now you try: limsup and liminf of cos(2nπ/3). (values
    −1/2, −1/2, 1 repeating: limsup 1, liminf −1/2)

D7. Find the sums: (a) ∑_{n=0}^∞ (2/3)ⁿ, (b) ∑_{n=1}^∞ 3/4ⁿ, (c)
    ∑_{n=1}^∞ 1/(n(n + 1)), (d) ∑_{n=1}^∞ 1/(n(n + 2)), (e) ∑_{n=1}^∞ 1/(4n² − 1).
    Does ∑ n/(n + 1) converge?
    Answer: (a) 3, (b) 1, (c) 1, (d) 3/4, (e) 1/2. ∑ n/(n + 1) diverges (its
    terms tend to 1, not 0).
    Teacher explains: Geometric: Sₙ = a(1 − rⁿ)/(1 − r) → a/(1 − r) when
    |r| < 1.
       (a) first term 1, r = 2/3:  1/(1 − 2/3) = 3
       (b) first term 3/4, r = 1/4:  (3/4)/(3/4) = 1
    Telescoping: split with partial fractions and write Sₙ out:
       (c) 1/(n(n + 1)) = 1/n − 1/(n + 1);  Sₙ = 1 − 1/(n + 1) → 1
       (d) 1/(n(n + 2)) = (1/2)(1/n − 1/(n + 2));
           Sₙ = (1/2)(1 + 1/2 − 1/(n + 1) − 1/(n + 2)) → 3/4
       (e) 1/(4n² − 1) = (1/2)(1/(2n − 1) − 1/(2n + 1));
           Sₙ = (1/2)(1 − 1/(2n + 1)) → 1/2
    In (d) the gap is 2, so TWO leading terms survive (1 and 1/2). nth-term
    test: if ∑aₙ converges then aₙ = Sₙ − Sₙ₋₁ → L − L = 0; here n/(n + 1) → 1,
    so ∑ diverges. Common mistakes: using a/(1 − r) with the wrong first term
    (b); keeping only one surviving term in (d). Now you try: ∑_{n=1}^∞
    1/(n(n + 1)(n + 2)). (1/4, from 1/(n(n+1)(n+2)) = (1/2)[1/(n(n + 1)) −
    1/((n + 1)(n + 2))])

D8. Prove that ∑1/n diverges (by grouping) and that ∑1/n² converges. State
    the integral test and use it to show that 1.6406 < ∑_{n=1}^∞ 1/n² < 1.6498,
    given S₁₀ = ∑_{n=1}^{10} 1/n² ≈ 1.549768. For which p does ∑1/nᵖ converge?
    Answer: ∑1/n = ∞; ∑1/n² converges; S₁₀ + 1/11 ≈ 1.640677 < S <
    S₁₀ + 1/10 ≈ 1.649768 (true value π²/6 ≈ 1.644934). p > 1.
    Teacher explains: Group the harmonic series in blocks of length 2ᵏ:
       1 + 1/2 + (1/3 + 1/4) + (1/5 + ... + 1/8) + (1/9 + ... + 1/16) + ...
       ≥ 1 + 1/2 + 1/2 + 1/2 + 1/2 + ...  = ∞
    For 1/n², partial sums increase and are bounded, using 1/n² < 1/(n(n − 1))
    = 1/(n − 1) − 1/n for n ≥ 2:
       Sₙ < 1 + (1 − 1/n) < 2
    INTEGRAL TEST: if f is positive and decreasing, ∑f(n) converges ⇔
    ∫₁^∞ f(x) dx converges, and the tail R_N = ∑_{n>N} f(n) satisfies
       ∫_{N+1}^∞ f(x) dx ≤ R_N ≤ ∫_N^∞ f(x) dx
    (compare rectangles with the area under the curve). With f = 1/x²:
    ∫_N^∞ dx/x² = 1/N, so 1/11 ≤ R₁₀ ≤ 1/10, giving
       1.549768 + 0.090909 = 1.640677 < S < 1.549768 + 0.1 = 1.649768
    and indeed π²/6 = 1.644934. For 1/xᵖ, ∫₁^∞ x^(−p) dx is finite exactly when
    p > 1. Common mistake: taking ∫₁^∞ f as the SUM of the series; it only has
    the same convergence behaviour. Now you try: does ∑ 1/(n ln n) (n ≥ 2)
    converge? (no: ∫ dx/(x ln x) = ln ln x → ∞)

D9. Use comparison or limit comparison to decide: (a) ∑ 1/(n² + n + 1),
    (b) ∑ 1/(2n − 1), (c) ∑ (n + 1)/(n³ − 2) (n ≥ 2), (d) ∑ sin(1/n),
    (e) ∑ 1/√(n(n + 1)), (f) ∑ (2 + sin n)/n².
    Answer: (a) C, (b) D, (c) C, (d) D, (e) D, (f) C.
    Teacher explains: Find the DOMINANT behaviour of the term, then compare
    with a p-series:
       (a) 1/(n² + n + 1) < 1/n²                         C (direct)
       (b) [1/(2n − 1)]/[1/n] = n/(2n − 1) → 1/2         D (limit, 1/n)
       (c) [(n + 1)/(n³ − 2)]/[1/n²] → 1                 C (limit, 1/n²)
       (d) sin(1/n)/(1/n) → 1 (sin x/x → 1)              D (limit, 1/n)
       (e) √(n(n + 1)) < n + 1, so term > 1/(n + 1)      D (direct)
       (f) 0 < (2 + sin n)/n² ≤ 3/n²                     C (direct)
    LIMIT COMPARISON: if aₙ, bₙ > 0 and aₙ/bₙ → c with 0 < c < ∞, then both
    converge or both diverge (for large n, (c/2)bₙ < aₙ < 2c·bₙ). Direct
    comparison needs the inequality in the RIGHT direction: to prove
    divergence, the term must be ≥ a divergent one; to prove convergence,
    ≤ a convergent one. In (b), 1/(2n − 1) < 1/n is true but useless. Common
    mistake: comparing (c) with 1/n³ ('the top is small'): the top n + 1
    matters, the term behaves like 1/n². Now you try: ∑ (√n + 1)/(n² + 1) and
    ∑ ln(1 + 1/n). (C, ~ n^(−3/2); D, ln(1 + 1/n) ~ 1/n, or telescoping to
    ln(N + 1))

D10. Use the ratio or root test: (a) ∑ n²/2ⁿ, (b) ∑ n!/nⁿ, (c) ∑ 3ⁿ/n!,
     (d) ∑ (n/(2n + 1))ⁿ, (e) ∑ nⁿ/n!. Explain why neither test decides
     ∑ 1/nᵖ. Find the sums ∑_{n≥1} n²/2ⁿ and ∑_{n≥1} 3ⁿ/n!.
     Answer: (a) L = 1/2, C; (b) L = 1/e, C; (c) L = 0, C; (d) root L = 1/2,
     C; (e) L = e, D. For 1/nᵖ both give L = 1. Sums: 6 and e³ − 1 ≈ 19.0855.
     Teacher explains: Compute the ratio and simplify BEFORE taking the limit:
        (a) [(n + 1)²/2ⁿ⁺¹]·[2ⁿ/n²] = (1/2)(1 + 1/n)² → 1/2
        (b) [(n + 1)!/(n + 1)ⁿ⁺¹]·[nⁿ/n!] = nⁿ/(n + 1)ⁿ = 1/(1 + 1/n)ⁿ → 1/e
        (c) [3ⁿ⁺¹/(n + 1)!]·[n!/3ⁿ] = 3/(n + 1) → 0
        (d) (aₙ)^(1/n) = n/(2n + 1) → 1/2
        (e) the reciprocal of (b): (1 + 1/n)ⁿ → e > 1
     Why L = 1 tells nothing: for 1/nᵖ, (n/(n + 1))ᵖ → 1 and (1/nᵖ)^(1/n) =
     (n^(1/n))^(−p) → 1, for every p, yet the series converges for p > 1 and
     diverges for p ≤ 1. Sums: from ∑xⁿ = 1/(1 − x), differentiating and
     multiplying by x twice gives ∑n²xⁿ = x(1 + x)/(1 − x)³; at x = 1/2:
     (1/2)(3/2)/(1/8) = 6. And ∑_{n≥0} 3ⁿ/n! = e³, so from n = 1 it is e³ − 1.
     Common mistakes: 'L = 1 so the series diverges'; forgetting absolute
     values for series with negative terms. Now you try: ∑ 2ⁿn!/nⁿ and
     ∑ 3ⁿn!/nⁿ. (ratio 2/e < 1, C; 3/e > 1, D)

D11. Prove the alternating series test for ∑(−1)ⁿ⁺¹bₙ with bₙ ↓ 0, including
     the error bound |S − Sₙ| ≤ bₙ₊₁. How many terms of ∑(−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n² guarantee
     an error below 0.001? Classify ∑(−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n, ∑(−1)ⁿ/n², ∑(−1)ⁿn/(n + 1),
     and explain Riemann's rearrangement theorem with ∑(−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n = ln 2.
     Answer: 31 terms. Conditionally convergent; absolutely convergent;
     divergent. A suitable rearrangement of 1 − 1/2 + 1/3 − ... sums to
     (1/2) ln 2 (or to any real number).
     Teacher explains: Even partial sums increase and odd ones decrease:
        S₂ₙ₊₂ = S₂ₙ + (b₂ₙ₊₁ − b₂ₙ₊₂) ≥ S₂ₙ
        S₂ₙ₊₁ = S₂ₙ₋₁ − (b₂ₙ − b₂ₙ₊₁) ≤ S₂ₙ₋₁
     and S₂ₙ ≤ S₂ₙ₊₁ ≤ S₁, so both converge (monotone, bounded), and their
     difference b₂ₙ₊₁ → 0: same limit S. S always lies between consecutive
     partial sums, so |S − Sₙ| ≤ |Sₙ₊₁ − Sₙ| = bₙ₊₁. For 1/n²:
        1/(n + 1)² < 0.001 ⇔ n + 1 > √1000 ≈ 31.6 ⇔ n ≥ 31
     Classification: ∑1/n diverges but the alternating version converges:
     CONDITIONAL. ∑1/n² converges, so ∑(−1)ⁿ/n² is ABSOLUTELY convergent.
     n/(n + 1) → 1 ≠ 0: divergent by the nth-term test (Leibniz does not
     apply; bₙ does not tend to 0). Rearrangement: take one positive term then
     two negative ones:
        (1 − 1/2) − 1/4 + (1/3 − 1/6) − 1/8 + (1/5 − 1/10) − 1/12 + ...
        = 1/2 − 1/4 + 1/6 − 1/8 + 1/10 − ... = (1/2)(1 − 1/2 + 1/3 − ...)
        = (1/2) ln 2
     The same terms, a different order, HALF the sum: order matters for
     conditionally convergent series. Common mistake: applying the Leibniz test
     without checking bₙ is decreasing. Now you try: how many terms of
     ∑(−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n³ for error < 10⁻⁴? (1/(n + 1)³ < 10⁻⁴ ⇔ n + 1 ≥ 22; 21 terms)

D12. Decide convergence, naming the test: (a) ∑_{n≥2} 1/(n ln n),
     (b) ∑_{n≥2} 1/(n (ln n)²), (c) ∑ 1/n^(1 + 1/n), (d) ∑_{n≥2} (−1)ⁿ/ln n,
     (e) ∑ (1 − 1/n)^(n²), (f) ∑ n e^(−n²). State Cauchy's condensation test.
     Answer: (a) D, (b) C, (c) D, (d) conditionally C, (e) C, (f) C.
     Teacher explains: CONDENSATION: for aₙ ≥ 0 decreasing, ∑aₙ and ∑2ᵏa_{2ᵏ}
     converge together (group in blocks [2ᵏ, 2ᵏ⁺¹) as in D8).
        (a) 2ᵏ·1/(2ᵏ ln 2ᵏ) = 1/(k ln 2): harmonic                  D
        (b) 2ᵏ·1/(2ᵏ (k ln 2)²) = 1/(k² (ln 2)²): p = 2             C
        (c) [1/n^(1+1/n)]/[1/n] = 1/n^(1/n) → 1: limit comparison
            with ∑1/n (the terms are SMALLER than 1/n, yet it diverges)  D
        (d) 1/ln n decreases to 0: Leibniz gives convergence; but
            1/ln n > 1/n, so not absolutely: conditional
        (e) root test: ((1 − 1/n)^(n²))^(1/n) = (1 − 1/n)ⁿ → 1/e < 1  C
        (f) integral test: ∫₁^∞ x e^(−x²) dx = 1/(2e) finite (f decreasing
            for x ≥ 1/√2), or ratio test: ratio → 0                C
     (c) is the classic warning: 'the exponent is bigger than 1' is not
     enough; it must stay ≥ some p > 1. (a) and (b) show that even a log
     factor decides: ∑1/(n (ln n)ᵖ) converges exactly when p > 1. Common
     mistake: ratio test on (a)/(b)/(c) (it always gives 1 there). Now you
     try: ∑_{n≥2} 1/(ln n)ⁿ and ∑_{n≥2} 1/(ln n)². (root: 1/ln n → 0, C;
     1/(ln n)² > 1/n for large n, D)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find lim_{n→∞} aₙ (write 'diverges' or '∞' where appropriate).
    a) (3n + 1)/(n + 2)              b) (n² + 3n)/(2n² − 1)
    c) (5n³ − n)/(n⁴ + 1)            d) (n² + 1)/(n + 1)
    e) √(n² + n) − n                 f) √(n + 1) − √n
    g) n^(1/n)                       h) (2ⁿ + 3ⁿ)/(3ⁿ⁺¹ − 2ⁿ)

    m) 2ⁿ/n!                         n) n!/nⁿ
    o) (sin n)/n                     p) (−1)ⁿ n/(n + 1)
    q) (ln n)/n                      r) (ln n)²/√n
    s) (n² + 1)^(1/n)                t) ∛(n³ + n²) − n
    u) n(√(1 + 1/n) − 1)             v) n sin(1/n)
    w) (1 + 1/n²)ⁿ                   x) (4ⁿ + 5ⁿ)^(1/n)

             i) e²   j) e³   k) 1/e   l) 0   m) 0   n) 0   o) 0
             p) diverges (subsequences → ±1)   q) 0   r) 0   s) 1   t) 1/3
             u) 1/2   v) 1   w) 1   x) 5

M2. Find the LEAST N such that |aₙ − L| < ε for every n ≥ N.
    a) 1/n → 0, ε = 0.01                  b) 1/n² → 0, ε = 10⁻⁴
    c) (3n + 1)/(n + 2) → 3, ε = 0.01     d) (2n − 1)/(n + 1) → 2, ε = 0.001
    e) 1/√n → 0, ε = 0.01                 f) (−1)ⁿ/n → 0, ε = 0.05
    g) 2⁻ⁿ → 0, ε = 0.001                 h) (0.9)ⁿ → 0, ε = 0.01
    i) n/(n² + 1) → 0, ε = 0.01           j) (n + 1)/n → 1, ε = 0.003
    k) 2n²/(n² + 1) → 2, ε = 0.01         l) 1/n! → 0, ε = 10⁻⁶
    m) √(n + 1) − √n → 0, ε = 0.01        n) n^(1/n) → 1, ε = 0.1
    o) (1 + 1/n)ⁿ → e, ε = 0.01           p) (sin n)/n → 0, ε = 0.001: give
                                             an N that surely works

             h) 44   i) 100   j) 334   k) 15   l) 10   m) 2500   n) 39
             o) 135   p) N = 1001 (|aₙ| ≤ 1/n)

M3. Is the sequence bounded? monotone? convergent (limit)?
    a) 1/n                         b) (−1)ⁿ
    c) n²                          d) (−1)ⁿ/n
    e) n/(n + 1)                   f) (−2)ⁿ
    g) sin(nπ/2)                   h) Hₙ = 1 + 1/2 + ... + 1/n
    i) 2ⁿ/n!                       j) (1 + 1/n)ⁿ
    k) n^(1/n)                     l) cos n
    m) n + (−1)ⁿ                   n) n + 1/n
    o) (1 + (−1)ⁿ)/n               p) √n − √(n − 1)
    q) n for n even, 1/n for n odd r) (1 − 1/n)ⁿ
    Answers:
      a) bounded; decreasing; → 0     b) bounded; not monotone; diverges
      c) unbounded; increasing; → ∞   d) bounded; not monotone; → 0
      e) bounded; increasing; → 1     f) unbounded; not monotone; diverges
      g) bounded; not monotone; diverges (1, 0, −1, 0, ...)
      h) unbounded; increasing; → ∞
      i) bounded; non-increasing (2, 2, 4/3, 2/3, ...); → 0
      j) bounded; increasing; → e
      k) bounded; decreasing from n = 3 (1, 1.414, 1.442, 1.414, ...); → 1
      l) bounded; not monotone; diverges
      m) unbounded; not monotone (0, 3, 2, 5, 4, ...); → ∞
      n) unbounded; increasing (2, 2.5, 3.33, ...); → ∞
      o) bounded; not monotone (0, 1, 0, 1/2, ...); → 0
      p) bounded; decreasing; → 0
      q) unbounded; not monotone; diverges (no limit, not → ∞)
      r) bounded; increasing (0, 1/4, 8/27, ...); → 1/e

M4. Recursive sequences: find the limit (after checking it exists).
    a) a₁ = √2, aₙ₊₁ = √(2 + aₙ)          b) a₁ = 1, aₙ₊₁ = √(6 + aₙ)
    c) a₁ = 1, aₙ₊₁ = (aₙ + 2/aₙ)/2       d) a₁ = 0, aₙ₊₁ = (aₙ + 1)/2
    e) a₁ = 1, aₙ₊₁ = 1 + 1/aₙ            f) a₁ = 0, aₙ₊₁ = 1/(2 − aₙ)
    g) a₁ = 1, aₙ₊₁ = aₙ/2 + 3            h) a₁ = 1, aₙ₊₁ = √(3aₙ)
    i) a₁ = 5, aₙ₊₁ = √(aₙ + 12)          j) a₁ = 1/2, aₙ₊₁ = aₙ²
    k) a₁ = 2, aₙ₊₁ = aₙ²                 l) a₁ = 1, aₙ₊₁ = cos aₙ
    m) a₁ = 3, aₙ₊₁ = (aₙ + 4/aₙ)/2       n) a₁ = 1, aₙ₊₁ = aₙ/(1 + aₙ)
    o) a₁ = 3, aₙ₊₁ = 3 − 2/aₙ            p) F₁ = F₂ = 1, Fₙ₊₂ = Fₙ₊₁ + Fₙ:
                                             lim Fₙ₊₁/Fₙ

      l) ≈ 0.739085 (the root of x = cos x)   m) 2   n) 0 (aₙ = 1/n)

      the limit)   p) (1 + √5)/2

M7. Quick decisions: convergent (C) or divergent (D)? Use the nth-term test,

M8. Comparison / limit comparison (all terms positive): C or D, and the
    series you compare with.
    a) ∑ 1/(n² + n + 1)           b) ∑ 1/(2n − 1)
    c) ∑ (n + 1)/(n³ + 2)         d) ∑ 1/√(n(n + 1))
    e) ∑ sin(1/n)                 f) ∑ sin²(1/n)
    g) ∑ (1 − cos(1/n))           h) ∑ ln(1 + 1/n²)

    k) ∑ (2 + sin n)/n            l) ∑ (2 + sin n)/n²
    m) ∑ √n/(n² + 1)              n) ∑ (ln n)/n
    o) ∑ (ln n)/n²                p) ∑_{n≥2} (n² + 2n)/(n⁴ − 3)

M9. Ratio or root test: give the limit L and the conclusion (C, D, or

M10. Alternating and signed series: absolutely convergent (A), conditionally
     convergent (CC) or divergent (D). In o)-r) find the least number of terms
     n guaranteeing error < the given bound by the Leibniz estimate.
     a) ∑ (−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n                b) ∑ (−1)ⁿ/n²
     c) ∑ (−1)ⁿ/√n                 d) ∑ (−1)ⁿ n/(n + 1)
     e) ∑_{n≥2} (−1)ⁿ/ln n         f) ∑ (−1)ⁿ/2ⁿ
     g) ∑ (−1)ⁿ n/(n² + 1)         h) ∑ (−1)ⁿ sin(1/n)
     i) ∑ cos(nπ)/n^(3/2)          j) ∑ (−1)ⁿ(1 + 1/n)ⁿ
     k) ∑ (sin n)/n²               l) ∑_{n≥2} (−1)ⁿ/(n ln n)
     m) ∑ (−1)ⁿ n!/nⁿ              n) ∑ (−1)ⁿ(√(n + 1) − √n)
     o) ∑ (−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n², error < 0.001
     p) ∑ (−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n, error < 0.01
     q) ∑_{k≥0} (−1)ᵏ/k!, error < 10⁻⁶ (give the last index k used)
     r) ∑ (−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n³, error < 10⁻⁴
     Answers: a) CC   b) A   c) CC   d) D   e) CC   f) A   g) CC   h) CC
              i) A   j) D   k) A   l) CC   m) A   n) CC   o) 31   p) 100
              q) k = 9 (1/10! < 10⁻⁶)   r) 21

M11. Mixed: C or D (all n large enough for the terms to be defined).
     a) ∑ 1/(n ln n)               b) ∑ 1/(n (ln n)²)
     c) ∑ n e^(−n²)                d) ∑ (ln n)/n^(3/2)
     e) ∑ 1/(ln n)^(ln n)          f) ∑ 1/(ln n)ⁿ
     g) ∑ 1/(ln n)²                h) ∑ (n^(1/n) − 1)
     i) ∑ (n^(1/n) − 1)²           j) ∑ 1/2^(√n)
     k) ∑ 1/n!                     l) ∑ (1 − 1/n)ⁿ
     m) ∑ (n + 1)/(n 2ⁿ)           n) ∑ 1/(n² − ln n)
     o) ∑ 1/√(n³ + 1)              p) ∑ (−1)ⁿ/∛n
     q) ∑ arctan(1/n)              r) ∑ n²/(n³ + 1)
     s) ∑ (1 + 1/n)^(−n²)          t) ∑ n!/(1·3·5···(2n − 1))
     u) ∑ 1/(n ln n ln ln n)       v) ∑ e^(−√n)
     Answers: a) D   b) C   c) C   d) C
              e) C ((ln n)^(ln n) = n^(ln ln n) ≥ n² for large n)
              f) C (root)   g) D   h) D (~ (ln n)/n)   i) C (~ (ln n)²/n²)
              j) C   k) C   l) D (terms → 1/e)   m) C   n) C   o) C
              p) CC (Leibniz)   q) D   r) D   s) C (root → 1/e)
              t) C (ratio → 1/2)   u) D (condensation twice)
              v) C (e^(−√n) ≤ 1/n² for large n)

M12. Give a concise proof (sketch).
     a) if ∑aₙ converges then aₙ → 0
     b) the harmonic series diverges
     c) a monotone increasing sequence bounded above converges
     d) every sequence has a monotone subsequence
     e) Bolzano-Weierstrass
     f) every Cauchy sequence of reals converges
     g) absolute convergence implies convergence
     h) the ratio test for L < 1
     i) ∑_{n≥1} 1/n² < 2
     j) if ∑|aₙ| converges then ∑aₙ² converges
     k) n^(1/n) → 1
     l) ∑1/nᵖ converges for p > 1 (condensation)
     m) the alternating series test
     n) if aₙ → L then (a₁ + ... + aₙ)/n → L (Cesàro)
     o) if aₙ → L and aₙ ≥ 0 then √aₙ → √L
     p) a subsequence of a convergent sequence has the same limit
     Answers:
       a) aₙ = Sₙ − Sₙ₋₁ → S − S = 0.
       b) S_{2ᵏ} ≥ 1 + k/2 by grouping in blocks of 2^(j−1) terms each ≥ 1/2.
       c) L = sup aₙ; given ε, some a_N > L − ε, then L − ε < a_N ≤ aₙ ≤ L
          for n ≥ N.
       d) call n a PEAK if aₙ ≥ aₘ for all m > n; infinitely many peaks give
          a decreasing subsequence; finitely many let us build an increasing
          one starting after the last peak.
       e) bounded sequence → monotone subsequence (d) → converges (c).
       f) Cauchy ⇒ bounded ⇒ convergent subsequence aₙₖ → L (e);
          |aₙ − L| ≤ |aₙ − aₙₖ| + |aₙₖ − L| < ε.
       g) 0 ≤ aₙ + |aₙ| ≤ 2|aₙ| converges by comparison; subtract ∑|aₙ|. (Or:
          partial sums of ∑aₙ are Cauchy since |∑_{m}^{n} aₖ| ≤ ∑_{m}^{n} |aₖ|.)
       h) pick r with L < r < 1; for n ≥ N, |aₙ₊₁| ≤ r|aₙ|, so
          |aₙ| ≤ |a_N| r^(n−N): compare with a geometric series.
       i) 1 + ∑_{n≥2} 1/(n(n − 1)) = 1 + ∑ (1/(n − 1) − 1/n) = 2, and
          1/n² < 1/(n(n − 1)) for n ≥ 2.
       j) aₙ → 0, so |aₙ| ≤ 1 for large n and aₙ² ≤ |aₙ|: comparison.
       k) n = (1 + hₙ)ⁿ ≥ n(n − 1)hₙ²/2, so 0 ≤ hₙ ≤ √(2/(n − 1)) → 0.
       l) ∑2ᵏ(2ᵏ)^(−p) = ∑(2^(1−p))ᵏ is geometric with ratio < 1.
       m) S₂ₙ increases, S₂ₙ₊₁ decreases, S₂ₙ₊₁ − S₂ₙ = b₂ₙ₊₁ → 0: common limit.
       n) split the average at N: the first N terms contribute
          (fixed)/n → 0, the rest lie within ε of L.
       o) |√aₙ − √L| = |aₙ − L|/(√aₙ + √L) ≤ |aₙ − L|/√L (L > 0); if L = 0,
          √aₙ < ε when aₙ < ε².
       p) nₖ ≥ k, so k ≥ N ⇒ nₖ ≥ N ⇒ |aₙₖ − L| < ε.

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: always write out the first five terms (of the sequence AND of the
  partial sums) before choosing a method; keep the test checklist in order:
  (1) nth-term test, (2) geometric / p-series / telescoping, (3) comparison or
  limit comparison with the dominant term, (4) ratio for factorials and
  powers, root for nth powers, (5) integral test or condensation for logs,
  (6) alternating series test, then check absolute convergence; say aloud
  'terms → 0 is necessary, never sufficient'. Harder: prove Riemann's
  rearrangement theorem; show ∑ 1/p over primes diverges; prove that
  Hₙ − ln n decreases to Euler's constant γ ≈ 0.5772; prove that
  liminf |aₙ₊₁/aₙ| ≤ liminf |aₙ|^(1/n) ≤ limsup |aₙ|^(1/n) ≤ limsup |aₙ₊₁/aₙ|
  (so the root test is stronger); show e is irrational using
  0 < e − ∑_{k≤n} 1/k! < 1/(n!·n).


================================================================================
TOPIC 9.7 — TAYLOR AND MACLAURIN SERIES; POWER SERIES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A POWER SERIES about a is ∑ aₙ(x − a)ⁿ (n = 0, 1, 2, ...). There is a
    RADIUS OF CONVERGENCE R, 0 ≤ R ≤ ∞, such that the series converges
    ABSOLUTELY for |x − a| < R and DIVERGES for |x − a| > R. At the two end
    points x = a ± R anything can happen, and each end point must be tested
    separately (p-series, alternating series test, nth-term test).
  - Finding R: RATIO test, 1/R = lim |aₙ₊₁/aₙ| when the limit exists; ROOT
    test / CAUCHY-HADAMARD, 1/R = limsup |aₙ|^(1/n), which always works. For
    series in x² or x³ (gaps), apply the ratio test to the TERMS, not to the
    coefficients. Examples: ∑ xⁿ/n! has R = ∞, ∑ n! xⁿ has R = 0, ∑ xⁿ has R = 1.
  - ABEL'S LEMMA: if ∑ aₙx₀ⁿ converges, then ∑ aₙxⁿ converges absolutely for
    every |x| < |x₀| (compare with a geometric series). This is why the set of
    convergence is an interval centred at a.
  - Inside (a − R, a + R) a power series can be DIFFERENTIATED and INTEGRATED
    term by term, and the new series have the SAME radius R (end-point
    behaviour may change). The sum is infinitely differentiable there and
    aₙ = f⁽ⁿ⁾(a)/n!: a power series is its own Taylor series (UNIQUENESS).
  - TAYLOR POLYNOMIAL of degree n about a: Pₙ(x) = ∑ₖ₌₀ⁿ f⁽ᵏ⁾(a)(x − a)ᵏ/k!;
    MACLAURIN means a = 0. TAYLOR'S THEOREM (Lagrange remainder):
    f(x) = Pₙ(x) + Rₙ(x), Rₙ(x) = f⁽ⁿ⁺¹⁾(c)(x − a)ⁿ⁺¹/(n + 1)! for some c
    between a and x. Error bound: |Rₙ(x)| ≤ M|x − a|ⁿ⁺¹/(n + 1)! where
    M ≥ |f⁽ⁿ⁺¹⁾| between a and x. For alternating series, the error is at most
    the first omitted term.
  - The TAYLOR SERIES of f converges to f(x) exactly when Rₙ(x) → 0. A smooth
    function need NOT equal its Taylor series: f(x) = e^(−1/x²) (f(0) = 0) has
    every derivative 0 at 0, so its Maclaurin series is 0. Functions equal to
    their Taylor series near each point are called ANALYTIC.
  - STANDARD SERIES (learn them): eˣ = ∑ xⁿ/n! (all x);
    sin x = ∑ (−1)ⁿx²ⁿ⁺¹/(2n + 1)!, cos x = ∑ (−1)ⁿx²ⁿ/(2n)! (all x);
    1/(1 − x) = ∑ xⁿ (|x| < 1); ln(1 + x) = x − x²/2 + x³/3 − ... (−1 < x ≤ 1);
    arctan x = x − x³/3 + x⁵/5 − ... (|x| ≤ 1); sinh x, cosh x (odd and even
    parts of eˣ); BINOMIAL (1 + x)^α = ∑ C(α, n)xⁿ with
    C(α, n) = α(α − 1)...(α − n + 1)/n!, valid for |x| < 1.
  - BUILD new series from old ones by substitution (e^(−x²)), multiplication
    (eˣ sin x), division (tan x = sin x/cos x), differentiation and
    integration; never by computing 10 derivatives by hand if avoidable.
  - USES: limits of 0/0 forms ((x − sin x)/x³ → 1/6), approximations with a
    guaranteed error, integrals with no elementary antiderivative
    (∫ e^(−x²) dx), sums of numerical series (∑ n/2ⁿ = 2), higher derivatives
    at a point (f⁽ⁿ⁾(0) = n! aₙ).
  - TRAPS: forgetting to test end points; using the ratio test on a series
    with missing powers; writing a Taylor series about a in powers of x
    instead of (x − a); assuming the Taylor series converges to f; treating
    the Lagrange c as a known number (it is only known to lie between a
    and x).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Derive the Maclaurin series of eˣ, sin x and cos x from the formula
    aₙ = f⁽ⁿ⁾(0)/n!. Use them to write down the Maclaurin series of e^(2x) and
    of x cos x, and check formally that e^(ix) = cos x + i sin x.
    Answer: eˣ = 1 + x + x²/2! + x³/3! + ...; sin x = x − x³/3! + x⁵/5! − ...;
    cos x = 1 − x²/2! + x⁴/4! − ...; e^(2x) = ∑ 2ⁿxⁿ/n!;
    x cos x = x − x³/2! + x⁵/4! − ...; e^(ix) = cos x + i sin x.
    Teacher explains: A Maclaurin series needs only the values of the
    derivatives at 0. Make a table:
       f       f′      f″      f‴      f⁽⁴⁾   ...   value at 0
       eˣ      eˣ      eˣ      eˣ      eˣ           1, 1, 1, 1, 1, ...
       sin x   cos x   −sin x  −cos x  sin x        0, 1, 0, −1, 0, ...
       cos x   −sin x  −cos x  sin x   cos x        1, 0, −1, 0, 1, ...
    So aₙ = 1/n! for eˣ; for sin x only ODD powers survive with signs
    +, −, +, ...; for cos x only EVEN powers. Once we have a series we get
    others by SUBSTITUTION and MULTIPLICATION, not by differentiating again:
       e^(2x) = 1 + 2x + (2x)²/2! + (2x)³/3! + ... = 1 + 2x + 2x² + 4x³/3 + ...
       x cos x = x(1 − x²/2 + x⁴/24 − ...) = x − x³/2 + x⁵/24 − ...
    Put x → ix in eˣ and group the real and imaginary terms (i² = −1, i³ = −i,
    i⁴ = 1):
       e^(ix) = 1 + ix − x²/2! − ix³/3! + x⁴/4! + ix⁵/5! − ...
              = (1 − x²/2! + x⁴/4! − ...) + i(x − x³/3! + x⁵/5! − ...)
    which is cos x + i sin x (Euler). Note sin is odd and cos is even, and the
    series show it: only odd, or only even, powers. Common mistakes: writing
    x³/3 instead of x³/3! = x³/6; forgetting the alternating signs. Now you try:
    derive the Maclaurin series of cosh x = (eˣ + e^(−x))/2.
    (1 + x²/2! + x⁴/4! + ... = ∑ x²ⁿ/(2n)!)

D2. Find the Taylor polynomial of degree 4 of ln x about a = 1, and use it to
    estimate ln 1.1. Compare with the true value 0.0953102.
    Answer: P₄(x) = (x − 1) − (x − 1)²/2 + (x − 1)³/3 − (x − 1)⁴/4;
    ln 1.1 ≈ 0.0953083.
    Teacher explains: Differentiate and evaluate at a = 1:
       f(x) = ln x        f(1) = 0
       f′(x) = 1/x        f′(1) = 1
       f″(x) = −1/x²      f″(1) = −1        ÷ 2!  →  −1/2
       f‴(x) = 2/x³       f‴(1) = 2         ÷ 3!  →   1/3
       f⁽⁴⁾(x) = −6/x⁴    f⁽⁴⁾(1) = −6      ÷ 4!  →  −1/4
    In general f⁽ᵏ⁾(1)/k! = (−1)^(k+1)(k − 1)!/k! = (−1)^(k+1)/k. The
    polynomial is in powers of (x − 1), NOT of x. At x = 1.1, x − 1 = 0.1:
       P₄(1.1) = 0.1 − 0.005 + 0.000333 − 0.000025 = 0.0953083
    The true value is 0.0953102, an error of about 2 × 10⁻⁶, which is less
    than the next term 0.1⁵/5 = 2 × 10⁻⁶ (alternating series). The expansion
    is the familiar ln(1 + h) = h − h²/2 + h³/3 − ... with h = x − 1. Common
    mistakes: expanding about 0 (ln 0 does not exist); writing the terms as
    xᵏ; dividing by k instead of k! when the derivative is not simplified.
    Now you try: the Taylor polynomial of degree 2 of √x about 4, and the
    estimate of √4.1. (2 + (x − 4)/4 − (x − 4)²/64; 2.0248438)

D3. State Taylor's theorem with the Lagrange remainder. Use it (a) to show
    that |sin x − (x − x³/6)| ≤ |x|⁵/120 for all x, and (b) to decide how many
    terms of ∑ 1/n! are needed to compute e with error less than 10⁻⁶.
    Answer: (a) since the x⁴ term of sin x is 0, P₃ = P₄ and |R₄| ≤ |x|⁵/5!.
    (b) Terms up to 1/9!, that is 10 terms; e ≈ 2.7182815.
    Teacher explains: TAYLOR'S THEOREM: if f has n + 1 derivatives on an
    interval containing a and x, then
       f(x) = f(a) + f′(a)(x − a) + ... + f⁽ⁿ⁾(a)(x − a)ⁿ/n! + Rₙ(x),
       Rₙ(x) = f⁽ⁿ⁺¹⁾(c)(x − a)ⁿ⁺¹/(n + 1)!    for some c between a and x.
    It is the mean value theorem (n = 0) grown up. We never know c, we only
    BOUND the derivative.
    (a) For sin x about 0, x − x³/6 is P₃ and also P₄ (the x⁴ coefficient is
    sin 0/4! = 0). Using n = 4: R₄(x) = f⁽⁵⁾(c)x⁵/5! = cos(c)x⁵/120, and
    |cos c| ≤ 1, so |R₄| ≤ |x|⁵/120. For x = 0.1 the error is below
    8.4 × 10⁻⁸. Using P₄ instead of P₃ gave a better bound for free.
    (b) For eˣ at x = 1: Rₙ(1) = e^c/(n + 1)! with 0 < c < 1, so
       0 < Rₙ(1) < e/(n + 1)! < 3/(n + 1)!
    We need 3/(n + 1)! < 10⁻⁶, that is (n + 1)! > 3 000 000:
       9! = 362 880 (too small),   10! = 3 628 800 (enough)
    so n + 1 = 10, n = 9, and we add 1 + 1 + 1/2! + ... + 1/9! = 2.7182815...
    (true e = 2.7182818...). Common mistakes: putting c = x or c = a in the
    remainder; using e < 2 in the bound (it is e < 3). Now you try: bound the
    error of cos x ≈ 1 − x²/2 for |x| ≤ 0.2. (x⁴/24 ≤ 0.0016/24 < 6.7 × 10⁻⁵)

D4. Find the radius and interval of convergence of (a) ∑ₙ₌₁ n xⁿ/3ⁿ and
    (b) ∑ₙ₌₁ xⁿ/n.
    Answer: (a) R = 3, interval (−3, 3). (b) R = 1, interval [−1, 1).
    Teacher explains: The RATIO TEST on the absolute value of the terms:
       (a) |uₙ₊₁/uₙ| = (n + 1)|x|ⁿ⁺¹/3ⁿ⁺¹ · 3ⁿ/(n|x|ⁿ)
                     = ((n + 1)/n) · |x|/3  →  |x|/3
    The series converges absolutely when |x|/3 < 1, i.e. |x| < 3, and
    diverges when |x| > 3. So R = 3. END POINTS (the ratio test says nothing
    there, the limit is 1):
       x = 3:   ∑ n·3ⁿ/3ⁿ = ∑ n          terms do not → 0: diverges
       x = −3:  ∑ n(−1)ⁿ                 terms do not → 0: diverges
    Interval (−3, 3).
       (b) |uₙ₊₁/uₙ| = n|x|/(n + 1) → |x|, so R = 1.
       x = 1:   ∑ 1/n        harmonic series: diverges
       x = −1:  ∑ (−1)ⁿ/n    alternating, 1/n decreases to 0: converges
    Interval [−1, 1). Common mistakes: stopping at R and forgetting the end
    points; saying 'the ratio is 1 so it converges'; testing x = −3 by
    comparing with ∑ 1/n. Now you try: interval of convergence of
    ∑ₙ₌₁ (x − 2)ⁿ/(n·2ⁿ). (R = 2; [0, 4))

D5. State the Cauchy-Hadamard formula. Find R for (a) ∑ (2 + (−1)ⁿ)ⁿ xⁿ,
    (b) ∑ n! xⁿ, (c) ∑ xⁿ/n!, (d) ∑ x²ⁿ/4ⁿ, and explain why the ratio test
    fails for (a) and must be used carefully in (d).
    Answer: (a) R = 1/3. (b) R = 0. (c) R = ∞. (d) R = 2.
    Teacher explains: CAUCHY-HADAMARD: for ∑ aₙ(x − a)ⁿ,
       1/R = limsup |aₙ|^(1/n)      (1/0 = ∞, 1/∞ = 0)
    It always works because the root test uses limsup.
    (a) aₙ = (2 + (−1)ⁿ)ⁿ is 3ⁿ for even n and 1 for odd n. So |aₙ|^(1/n) is
    3, 1, 3, 1, ...; its limsup is 3, so R = 1/3. The RATIO aₙ₊₁/aₙ is 3ⁿ⁺¹
    for odd n and 3⁻ⁿ for even n: it has no limit, so the ratio test gives
    no answer.
    (b) |aₙ₊₁/aₙ| = n + 1 → ∞, so R = 0: the series converges only at x = 0.
    (c) |aₙ₊₁/aₙ| = 1/(n + 1) → 0, so R = ∞: converges for every x.
    (d) The coefficients of odd powers are 0, so aₙ₊₁/aₙ is undefined. Apply
    the ratio test to the TERMS uₙ = x²ⁿ/4ⁿ:
       |uₙ₊₁/uₙ| = x²/4 < 1   ⇔   |x| < 2,     so R = 2
    (or substitute y = x²: ∑ yⁿ/4ⁿ has radius 4 in y, so |x|² < 4). At x = ±2
    the terms are all 1: diverges; interval (−2, 2). Common mistake in (d):
    writing R = 4 by treating x² as x. Now you try: R for ∑ (−1)ⁿx³ⁿ/8ⁿ and
    for ∑ nⁿxⁿ. (2; 0)

D6. Prove ABEL'S LEMMA: if ∑ aₙx₀ⁿ converges and |x| < |x₀|, then ∑ aₙxⁿ
    converges absolutely. Deduce that the set where a power series converges
    is an interval centred at 0 (possibly a single point or all of R).
    Answer: Proof below; define R = sup{|x| : the series converges at x}.
    Teacher explains: Since ∑ aₙx₀ⁿ converges, its terms tend to 0, so they
    are BOUNDED: |aₙx₀ⁿ| ≤ M for all n. Put r = |x|/|x₀| < 1. Then
       |aₙxⁿ| = |aₙx₀ⁿ| · |x/x₀|ⁿ ≤ M rⁿ
    and ∑ M rⁿ is a convergent geometric series. By the comparison test,
    ∑ |aₙxⁿ| converges. So: convergence at one point x₀ forces absolute
    convergence at every point nearer to 0. Contrapositive: divergence at x₁
    forces divergence at every |x| > |x₁| (if it converged at such x, it would
    converge at x₁).
    Now let R = sup{|x| : ∑ aₙxⁿ converges} (R = ∞ if unbounded). If
    |x| < R, there is a point x₀ of convergence with |x| < |x₀| ≤ R, so the
    series converges absolutely at x. If |x| > R, it diverges by the
    definition of sup. Hence the convergence set is (−R, R) plus possibly one
    or both end points. Common mistake: thinking conditional convergence at
    x₀ gives only conditional convergence inside; inside it is always
    ABSOLUTE. Now you try: if ∑ aₙxⁿ converges at x = −4 and diverges at
    x = 6, what can you say about R, and about convergence at x = 3, x = −7?
    (4 ≤ R ≤ 6; converges absolutely at 3; diverges at −7)

D7. Starting from 1/(1 − x) = ∑ xⁿ (|x| < 1), use term-by-term
    differentiation and integration to find (a) ∑ₙ₌₁ n xⁿ in closed form and
    the value of ∑ₙ₌₁ n/2ⁿ, (b) the Maclaurin series of ln(1 + x), (c) the
    Maclaurin series of arctan x, and hence Leibniz's series for π/4.
    Answer: (a) x/(1 − x)²; ∑ n/2ⁿ = 2. (b) ln(1 + x) = x − x²/2 + x³/3 − ...
    (c) arctan x = x − x³/3 + x⁵/5 − ...; π/4 = 1 − 1/3 + 1/5 − 1/7 + ...
    Teacher explains: THEOREM: inside the interval of convergence a power
    series may be differentiated or integrated term by term, and the new
    series has the same R.
    (a) Differentiate the geometric series and multiply by x:
       1/(1 − x)² = 1 + 2x + 3x² + ... = ∑ₙ₌₁ n xⁿ⁻¹
       ∑ₙ₌₁ n xⁿ = x/(1 − x)²            (|x| < 1)
    At x = 1/2:  ∑ n/2ⁿ = (1/2)/(1/2)² = 2. (Check: 1/2 + 2/4 + 3/8 + 4/16
    + 5/32 = 1.78, and the tail makes up the rest.)
    (b) Replace x by −t: 1/(1 + t) = 1 − t + t² − t³ + ...; integrate from 0
    to x (|x| < 1):
       ln(1 + x) = ∫₀ˣ dt/(1 + t) = x − x²/2 + x³/3 − x⁴/4 + ...
    (c) Replace x by −t²: 1/(1 + t²) = 1 − t² + t⁴ − ...; integrate:
       arctan x = x − x³/3 + x⁵/5 − x⁷/7 + ...      (|x| < 1)
    At x = 1 the series still converges (alternating series test), and
    ABEL'S THEOREM (a power series converging at an end point is continuous
    there) gives arctan 1 = π/4 = 1 − 1/3 + 1/5 − ...; similarly ln 2 = 1 −
    1/2 + 1/3 − .... Do not forget the constant of integration: here both
    sides vanish at 0, so it is 0. Common mistakes: differentiating the
    constant term into n = 0 of the new sum (the sum now starts at n = 1);
    claiming the end-point behaviour is unchanged (∑ xⁿ⁺¹/(n + 1) converges
    at x = −1, ∑ xⁿ does not). Now you try: find ∑ₙ₌₁ n²/2ⁿ.
    (differentiate x/(1 − x)² and multiply by x: x(1 + x)/(1 − x)³; at x = 1/2
    the sum is 6)

D8. Without computing derivatives, find Maclaurin series up to the stated
    power: (a) e^(−x²) to x⁶; (b) sin²x to x⁶; (c) eˣ sin x to x⁵;
    (d) tan x to x⁵.
    Answer: (a) 1 − x² + x⁴/2 − x⁶/6. (b) x² − x⁴/3 + 2x⁶/45.
    (c) x + x² + x³/3 − x⁵/30. (d) x + x³/3 + 2x⁵/15.
    Teacher explains: By UNIQUENESS of power series, any correct algebra with
    series gives THE Taylor series.
    (a) Substitute u = −x² into eᵘ = 1 + u + u²/2 + u³/6:
       e^(−x²) = 1 − x² + x⁴/2 − x⁶/6 + ...
    (b) Use sin²x = (1 − cos 2x)/2 and cos 2x = 1 − 2x² + 2x⁴/3 − 4x⁶/45 + ...
    (the terms are (2x)²/2, (2x)⁴/24 = 16x⁴/24, (2x)⁶/720 = 64x⁶/720):
       sin²x = x² − x⁴/3 + 2x⁶/45 − ...
    (c) Multiply like polynomials, collecting one power at a time:
       (1 + x + x²/2 + x³/6 + x⁴/24)(x − x³/6 + x⁵/120)
       x¹:  1
       x²:  1
       x³:  1/2 − 1/6 = 1/3
       x⁴:  1/6 − 1/6 = 0
       x⁵:  1/24 − 1/12 + 1/120 = (5 − 10 + 1)/120 = −1/30
    (d) tan x is odd, so tan x = x + a₃x³ + a₅x⁵ + ...; from
    tan x · cos x = sin x:
       (x + a₃x³ + a₅x⁵)(1 − x²/2 + x⁴/24) = x − x³/6 + x⁵/120
       x³:  a₃ − 1/2 = −1/6                  ⇒  a₃ = 1/3
       x⁵:  a₅ − a₃/2 + 1/24 = 1/120         ⇒  a₅ = 1/120 + 1/6 − 1/24 = 2/15
    Common mistakes: in (b) squaring the series of sin x and dropping the
    cross terms; in (c) stopping the product too early, so that a term such as
    x·(x⁴/24) is lost. Now you try: eˣ cos x to x⁴. (1 + x − x³/3 − x⁴/6)

D9. Use series to evaluate (a) lim (x − sin x)/x³, (b) lim (1/sin²x − 1/x²),
    (c) lim (eˣ − e^(−x) − 2x)/(x − sin x), all as x → 0.
    Answer: (a) 1/6. (b) 1/3. (c) 2.
    Teacher explains: Replace each function by enough terms of its series
    that the LOWEST surviving power is visible; the limit is the ratio of the
    leading coefficients.
    (a) x − sin x = x − (x − x³/6 + x⁵/120 − ...) = x³/6 − x⁵/120 + ...
       (x − sin x)/x³ = 1/6 − x²/120 + ...  →  1/6
    (b) Combine first: 1/sin²x − 1/x² = (x² − sin²x)/(x² sin²x). With
    sin²x = x² − x⁴/3 + ...:
       numerator    = x⁴/3 − ...
       denominator  = x²(x² − ...) = x⁴ + ...
       ratio        →  1/3
    (c) eˣ − e^(−x) = 2 sinh x = 2x + x³/3 + ...; so the numerator is
    x³/3 + ..., the denominator x³/6 + ..., the limit is (1/3)/(1/6) = 2.
    L'Hôpital would need three rounds of differentiation for (a) and (c);
    series do it in one line and also give the next term. Common mistake:
    keeping too few terms, e.g. sin x ≈ x in (a) gives 0/x³ and a wrong
    answer 0. Now you try: lim (ln(1 + x) − x + x²/2)/x³ as x → 0. (1/3)

D10. State the binomial series for (1 + x)^α. Write the first four terms for
     α = 1/2, use them to estimate √26, and derive the series of arcsin x to
     the x⁵ term from (1 − t²)^(−1/2).
     Answer: (1 + x)^α = ∑ C(α, n)xⁿ, |x| < 1; √(1 + x) = 1 + x/2 − x²/8 +
     x³/16 − ...; √26 ≈ 5.09902; arcsin x = x + x³/6 + 3x⁵/40 + ...
     Teacher explains: The generalised binomial coefficient is
        C(α, n) = α(α − 1)(α − 2)...(α − n + 1)/n!,   C(α, 0) = 1
     (it is f⁽ⁿ⁾(0)/n! for f = (1 + x)^α). For α = 1/2:
        C(1/2, 1) = 1/2
        C(1/2, 2) = (1/2)(−1/2)/2 = −1/8
        C(1/2, 3) = (1/2)(−1/2)(−3/2)/6 = 1/16
     If α is a whole number the series stops (ordinary binomial theorem);
     otherwise R = 1. To use it, make the small quantity small:
        √26 = √(25(1 + 1/25)) = 5(1 + 0.04)^(1/2)
            ≈ 5(1 + 0.02 − 0.0002 + 0.000004) = 5.09902
     (true value 5.0990195). For arcsin, take α = −1/2 and x = −t²:
        (1 − t²)^(−1/2) = 1 + t²/2 + 3t⁴/8 + ...
     (C(−1/2, 2) = (−1/2)(−3/2)/2 = 3/8), and integrate from 0 to x:
        arcsin x = x + x³/6 + 3x⁵/40 + ...
     Common mistakes: √26 = √(1 + 25) with x = 25 (outside |x| < 1, the series
     diverges); sign errors in C(α, n) when α is negative. Now you try:
     estimate ∛1.03 with three terms after 1. (1 + 0.01 − 0.0001 + 0.0000017
     = 1.0099017)

D11. (a) Prove that the Maclaurin series of eˣ converges to eˣ for EVERY
     real x. (b) Let f(x) = e^(−1/x²) for x ≠ 0 and f(0) = 0. Show that
     f⁽ⁿ⁾(0) = 0 for all n, and conclude that a smooth function need not equal
     its Taylor series.
     Answer: (a) |Rₙ(x)| ≤ e^|x| |x|ⁿ⁺¹/(n + 1)! → 0. (b) Proof below; the
     series is identically 0 but f(x) > 0 for x ≠ 0.
     Teacher explains: (a) By Taylor's theorem Rₙ(x) = e^c xⁿ⁺¹/(n + 1)! with c
     between 0 and x, so e^c ≤ e^|x| (a fixed number for fixed x) and
        |Rₙ(x)| ≤ e^|x| · |x|ⁿ⁺¹/(n + 1)!
     For fixed x, |x|ⁿ/n! → 0 (the ratio of consecutive terms is |x|/(n + 1)
     → 0, so eventually each term is less than half the previous one). So
     Rₙ(x) → 0 and eˣ = ∑ xⁿ/n! for all x. Convergence of the SERIES alone
     (ratio test, R = ∞) does not prove this; we must show the remainder
     tends to 0.
     (b) For x ≠ 0, by induction f⁽ⁿ⁾(x) = Pₙ(1/x) e^(−1/x²) with Pₙ a
     polynomial (differentiating e^(−1/x²) brings down 2/x³). At 0 use the
     definition:
        f′(0) = lim (e^(−1/h²) − 0)/h,   put t = 1/h:   = lim t e^(−t²) = 0
     since e^(t²) beats every power of t. The same argument with
     t^k e^(−t²) → 0 shows each f⁽ⁿ⁺¹⁾(0) = lim f⁽ⁿ⁾(h)/h = 0. So every
     Maclaurin coefficient is 0 and the Taylor series is 0 for all x, which
     equals f only at x = 0. Here Rₙ(x) = f(x) for every n and does NOT tend
     to 0. Common mistake: 'all derivatives exist, therefore f is analytic'.
     Now you try: show sin x equals its Maclaurin series for every x.
     (|f⁽ⁿ⁺¹⁾(c)| ≤ 1, so |Rₙ(x)| ≤ |x|ⁿ⁺¹/(n + 1)! → 0)

D12. (a) Evaluate ∫₀¹ e^(−x²) dx correct to 3 decimal places using series.
     (b) Find the Taylor series of f(x) = 1/x about a = 2 and its interval of
     convergence; explain why R = 2.
     Answer: (a) 0.747. (b) 1/x = ∑ (−1)ⁿ(x − 2)ⁿ/2ⁿ⁺¹, valid for 0 < x < 4.
     Teacher explains: (a) e^(−x²) has no elementary antiderivative, but its
     series integrates term by term (R = ∞):
        ∫₀¹ e^(−x²) dx = ∫₀¹ (1 − x² + x⁴/2! − x⁶/3! + ...) dx
                       = ∑ (−1)ⁿ/((2n + 1) n!)
                       = 1 − 1/3 + 1/10 − 1/42 + 1/216 − 1/1320 + 1/9360 − ...
     The series alternates with decreasing terms, so the error is less than
     the first omitted term. Adding up to −1/1320:
        1 − 0.333333 + 0.1 − 0.023810 + 0.004630 − 0.000758 = 0.746729
     with error less than 1/9360 < 0.00011, so the integral lies between
     0.74673 and 0.74684, and to 3 decimal places it is 0.747 (true value
     0.746824).
     (b) Write x = 2 + (x − 2) and use the geometric series:
        1/x = 1/(2 + (x − 2)) = (1/2) · 1/(1 + (x − 2)/2)
            = (1/2) ∑ (−1)ⁿ ((x − 2)/2)ⁿ = ∑ (−1)ⁿ(x − 2)ⁿ/2ⁿ⁺¹
     valid for |x − 2| < 2, i.e. 0 < x < 4; at x = 0 and x = 4 the terms have
     modulus 1/2 and do not tend to 0. The radius 2 is the DISTANCE FROM THE
     CENTRE 2 TO THE SINGULARITY at 0: a power series cannot converge across
     the point where f blows up. (Likewise 1/(1 + x²) has R = 1 about 0
     because of the complex singularities ±i, even though it is smooth on R.)
     Common mistakes in (a): rounding each term to 3 places before adding;
     stopping when the terms 'look small' without an error bound. Now you
     try: ∫₀¹ sin(x²) dx to 4 decimal places. (1/3 − 1/42 + 1/1320 − 1/75600
     = 0.31027 to 5 places, so 0.3103)

--------------------------

M8. Error bounds (use the Lagrange remainder or the alternating series
