       o) yes (P² = P = Pᵀ)
       p) I − P

M11. Least squares.
     a) line y = a + bx through (0, 1), (1, 3), (2, 4), (3, 4)
     b) line through (0, 0), (1, 1), (2, 1)
     c) line through (1, 2), (2, 3), (3, 5), (4, 6)
     d) line through (−1, 0), (0, 1), (1, 3)
     e) line through (1, 1), (2, 2), (3, 2)
     f) line y = bx (through O) for (1, 2), (2, 3), (3, 7)
     g) constant y = c for the data values 2, 3, 7
     h) the least-squares solution of x = 1, x = 3, x = 5
     i) the least-squares solution of x + y = 2, x − y = 0, x = 2
     j) the projection Ax̂ of b = (2, 0, 2) in i)
     k) parabola y = a + bx + cx² through (−1, 1), (0, 0), (1, 1), (2, 5)
     l) the sum of squared errors in a)
     Answers:
       a) y = 3/2 + x                 b) y = 1/6 + x/2
       c) y = 1/2 + 7x/5              d) y = 4/3 + 3x/2
       e) y = 2/3 + x/2               f) y = (29/14)x
       g) c = 4 (the mean)            h) x = 3
       i) x = 4/3, y = 1              j) (7/3, 1/3, 4/3)
       k) y = −3/20 + x/20 + 5x²/4    l) 1

M12. Prove (concise proof sketches).
     a) ⟨0, v⟩ = 0 for every v.
     b) Equality holds in Cauchy-Schwarz iff u, v are dependent.
     c) The triangle inequality ‖u + v‖ ≤ ‖u‖ + ‖v‖.
     d) Pythagoras: u ⊥ v ⇒ ‖u + v‖² = ‖u‖² + ‖v‖²; is the converse true in
        a real space?
     e) W^⊥ is a subspace, and W ∩ W^⊥ = {0}.
     f) If W₁ ⊆ W₂ then W₂^⊥ ⊆ W₁^⊥.
     g) BESSEL: for an orthonormal set e₁, ..., eₖ, Σ ⟨v, eᵢ⟩² ≤ ‖v‖².
     h) null(A) = (row A)^⊥ for a real matrix A.
     i) AᵀA is invertible iff the columns of A are independent.
     j) Q with orthonormal columns satisfies ⟨Qx, Qy⟩ = ⟨x, y⟩ and ‖Qx‖ = ‖x‖.
     k) An n × n real matrix with orthonormal columns also has orthonormal
        rows.
     l) Gram-Schmidt: span(u₁, ..., uₖ) = span(v₁, ..., vₖ) and uₖ ≠ 0.
     m) The reflection R = 2P − I (P an orthogonal projection) satisfies
        R² = I and Rᵀ = R.
     n) If A is real symmetric, eigenvectors for distinct eigenvalues are
        orthogonal.
     Answers:
       a) ⟨0, v⟩ = ⟨0 + 0, v⟩ = 2⟨0, v⟩, so ⟨0, v⟩ = 0.
       b) Equality ⇔ discriminant 0 ⇔ ‖u − tv‖ = 0 for some t ⇔ u = tv (or
          v = 0).
       c) ‖u + v‖² = ‖u‖² + 2⟨u, v⟩ + ‖v‖² ≤ ‖u‖² + 2‖u‖‖v‖ + ‖v‖²
          = (‖u‖ + ‖v‖)² by Cauchy-Schwarz.
       d) Expand; the cross term 2⟨u, v⟩ is 0. Converse: yes in a real
          space, since equality forces 2⟨u, v⟩ = 0 (false over C: only
          Re⟨u, v⟩ = 0).
       e) Linearity of ⟨·, w⟩ gives closure; v ∈ W ∩ W^⊥ ⇒ ⟨v, v⟩ = 0 ⇒
          v = 0.
       f) v ⊥ everything in W₂ ⇒ v ⊥ everything in the smaller W₁.
       g) w = Σ ⟨v, eᵢ⟩eᵢ; v − w ⊥ w, so ‖v‖² = ‖w‖² + ‖v − w‖² ≥ ‖w‖²
          = Σ ⟨v, eᵢ⟩².
       h) Ax = 0 ⇔ (row i)·x = 0 for every i ⇔ x ⊥ every row ⇔ x ⊥ row A.
       i) AᵀAx = 0 ⇒ xᵀAᵀAx = ‖Ax‖² = 0 ⇒ Ax = 0; so null(AᵀA) = null(A),
          which is {0} iff the columns are independent.
       j) ⟨Qx, Qy⟩ = xᵀQᵀQy = xᵀy since QᵀQ = I; put y = x.
       k) QᵀQ = I with Q square ⇒ Qᵀ = Q⁻¹ ⇒ QQᵀ = I: the rows are
          orthonormal.
       l) Induction: uₖ = vₖ − (a combination of u₁, ..., u_{k−1}) ∈
          span(v₁, ..., vₖ) and vice versa; uₖ = 0 would put vₖ in
          span(v₁, ..., v_{k−1}), contradicting independence.
       m) R² = 4P² − 4P + I = I (as P² = P); Rᵀ = 2Pᵀ − I = R.
       n) λ⟨u, v⟩ = ⟨Au, v⟩ = ⟨u, Av⟩ = μ⟨u, v⟩ and λ ≠ μ ⇒ ⟨u, v⟩ = 0.

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw every projection in R² first (the 'shadow' of v on a line,
  with the right-angle mark on v − proj v), and insist on the check
  ⟨v − proj v, w⟩ = 0 after every projection or Gram-Schmidt step; in
  Gram-Schmidt clear fractions by rescaling each uₖ before moving on; for
  function spaces make a small table of ∫ xⁿ over the interval before
  starting; for least squares always write A, then AᵀA and Aᵀb, then solve, and
  sanity-check that the residuals add to 0 when a constant term is present.
  Harder: prove that the Legendre polynomials (d/dx)ⁿ(x² − 1)ⁿ are orthogonal
  on [−1, 1]; show that among all polynomials of degree ≤ n the projection is
  the best L² approximation but NOT the best maximum-error approximation
  (Chebyshev); derive the QR least-squares method Rx̂ = Qᵀb; prove Bessel's
  inequality for the infinite system {sin nx} and deduce Σ 1/n² ≤ π²/6 from
  f(x) = x (in fact equality: Parseval gives Σ 1/n² = π²/6); show that every
  inner product on Rⁿ is xᵀGy for a unique symmetric positive definite G.


================================================================================
TOPIC 9.19 — ORDINARY DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS: FIRST ORDER, SECOND-ORDER LINEAR,
             SYSTEMS, LAPLACE TRANSFORM METHODS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - An ORDINARY DIFFERENTIAL EQUATION (ODE) links an unknown function y(x) and
    its derivatives. ORDER = highest derivative present. It is LINEAR if y and
    its derivatives appear only to the first power with coefficients depending
    on x alone: aₙ(x)y⁽ⁿ⁾ + ... + a₁(x)y′ + a₀(x)y = f(x); HOMOGENEOUS when
    f = 0. A SOLUTION must satisfy the equation on an INTERVAL; an INITIAL
    VALUE PROBLEM (IVP) adds y(x₀) = y₀ (and y′(x₀) = y₁ for order 2).
  - FIRST ORDER METHODS. SEPARABLE y′ = g(x)h(y): ∫ dy/h(y) = ∫ g(x) dx (and
    check the constant solutions h(y) = 0, which division loses). LINEAR
    y′ + P(x)y = Q(x): multiply by the INTEGRATING FACTOR μ = e^(∫P dx), then
    (μy)′ = μQ. EXACT M dx + N dy = 0 with ∂M/∂y = ∂N/∂x: solution F(x, y) = C
    where F_x = M, F_y = N; if not exact try μ(x) when (M_y − N_x)/N depends on
    x only, μ(y) when (N_x − M_y)/M depends on y only.
  - SUBSTITUTIONS. HOMOGENEOUS y′ = F(y/x): put y = vx, y′ = v + xv′, which
    separates. BERNOULLI y′ + P y = Q yⁿ: put v = y^(1−n), which is linear.
    y′ = F(ax + by + c): put u = ax + by + c.
  - EXISTENCE AND UNIQUENESS (Picard–Lindelöf): if f and ∂f/∂y are continuous
    near (x₀, y₀), the IVP y′ = f(x, y), y(x₀) = y₀ has exactly one solution on
    some interval |x − x₀| < h, obtained as the limit of PICARD ITERATES
    φₖ₊₁(x) = y₀ + ∫ from x₀ to x of f(t, φₖ(t)) dt. Without the Lipschitz
    condition uniqueness can fail (y′ = 3y^(2/3), y(0) = 0). Solutions of
    nonlinear equations may blow up in finite time (y′ = y², y(0) = 1).
  - SECOND-ORDER LINEAR THEORY: solutions of y″ + p y′ + q y = 0 form a
    2-dimensional vector space. y₁, y₂ are a FUNDAMENTAL SET iff the WRONSKIAN
    W = y₁y₂′ − y₁′y₂ ≠ 0; ABEL: W(x) = C e^(−∫p dx), so W is never zero or
    always zero. General solution of the nonhomogeneous equation =
    y_h (complementary) + y_p (any particular solution).
  - CONSTANT COEFFICIENTS a y″ + b y′ + c y = 0: try e^(rx), characteristic
    equation a r² + b r + c = 0. Distinct real r₁, r₂: c₁e^(r₁x) + c₂e^(r₂x);
    repeated r: (c₁ + c₂x)e^(rx); complex α ± iβ: e^(αx)(c₁ cos βx + c₂ sin βx).
    Higher order works the same way (a root of multiplicity m gives
    e^(rx), xe^(rx), ..., x^(m−1)e^(rx)). CAUCHY–EULER x²y″ + axy′ + by = 0:
    try xʳ, r(r − 1) + ar + b = 0; repeated root gives xʳ ln x.
  - PARTICULAR SOLUTIONS. UNDETERMINED COEFFICIENTS for f = polynomial ×
    exponential × sine/cosine: guess the same form with unknown constants; if
    the guess solves the homogeneous equation (RESONANCE) multiply by x (or x²
    for a double root). VARIATION OF PARAMETERS works for any f (leading
    coefficient 1): y_p = −y₁ ∫ y₂f/W dx + y₂ ∫ y₁f/W dx. REDUCTION OF ORDER:
    if y₁ is known, put y = v y₁.
  - SYSTEMS x′ = Ax (A a constant n × n matrix): if Av = λv then e^(λt)v is a
    solution; with n independent eigenvectors x = ∑ cᵢ e^(λᵢt)vᵢ. Complex
    λ = α ± iβ: take real and imaginary parts of e^(λt)v. In general
    x(t) = e^(At)x(0). A 2 × 2 phase portrait is classified by the eigenvalues:
    real same sign = NODE, opposite signs = SADDLE, complex = SPIRAL (FOCUS),
    purely imaginary = CENTRE; stable when all real parts < 0. Any n-th order
    equation becomes a first-order system with x₁ = y, x₂ = y′, ...
  - LAPLACE TRANSFORM F(s) = L{f}(s) = ∫ from 0 to ∞ of e^(−st) f(t) dt.
    TABLE: L{1} = 1/s, L{tⁿ} = n!/sⁿ⁺¹, L{e^(at)} = 1/(s − a),
    L{sin bt} = b/(s² + b²), L{cos bt} = s/(s² + b²). RULES: linearity;
    L{y′} = sY − y(0), L{y″} = s²Y − s y(0) − y′(0); s-SHIFT
    L{e^(at)f} = F(s − a); t-SHIFT L{u(t − a)f(t − a)} = e^(−as)F(s);
    L{t f(t)} = −F′(s); L{δ(t − a)} = e^(−as); CONVOLUTION L{f * g} = F G.
    Method for an IVP: transform, solve the ALGEBRAIC equation for Y, use
    partial fractions, invert with the table.
  - COMMON TRAPS: forgetting the constant solutions in separable equations;
    forgetting to divide by the leading coefficient before using μ or the
    variation-of-parameters formula; applying initial conditions to y_h
    instead of y_h + y_p; missing resonance; sign errors in L{y″}; inverting
    e^(−as)F(s) without the step function.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Solve the IVP y′ = xy², y(0) = 1. On what interval does the solution
    exist? What happens with y(0) = 0? Also solve y′ = y cos x generally.
    Answer: y = 2/(2 − x²), valid on −√2 < x < √2. With y(0) = 0 the solution
    is y ≡ 0. y′ = y cos x has general solution y = C e^(sin x) (C = 0 gives
    y ≡ 0).
    Teacher explains: The right side is (function of x) × (function of y), so
    the equation is SEPARABLE. Divide by y² (allowed while y ≠ 0) and
    integrate each side:
       dy/y² = x dx
       −1/y = x²/2 + C
       y(0) = 1:   −1 = 0 + C,  C = −1
       −1/y = x²/2 − 1 = (x² − 2)/2   ⇒   y = 2/(2 − x²)
    Check: y′ = 4x/(2 − x²)² = x · y² ✓. A solution lives on an INTERVAL
    containing x₀ = 0 on which it is defined and differentiable: the formula
    blows up at x = ±√2, so the solution is y = 2/(2 − x²) on (−√2, √2) only,
    even though the equation itself is fine everywhere. When we divided by y²
    we lost y ≡ 0, which also solves y′ = xy²; it is the solution of the IVP
    with y(0) = 0 (by uniqueness, D5, no other solution passes through (0, 0)).
    For y′ = y cos x: dy/y = cos x dx, ln|y| = sin x + K, |y| = e^K e^(sin x),
    and removing the modulus y = C e^(sin x) with C = ±e^K; C = 0 restores the
    lost solution y ≡ 0, so C is any real number. Common mistakes: writing
    '+ C' only at the end after exponentiating (e^(sin x) + C is WRONG);
    forgetting the constant solution; claiming the solution exists for all x.
    Now you try: solve y′ = y², y(0) = 1, and say where it blows up.
    (y = 1/(1 − x), blows up as x → 1⁻)

D2. Solve x y′ + 2y = x³, y(1) = 1 for x > 0, and derive the integrating
    factor formula for y′ + P(x)y = Q(x).
    Answer: y = x³/5 + 4/(5x²). In general μ = e^(∫P dx) and
    y = (1/μ)(∫ μQ dx + C).
    Teacher explains: First put the equation in STANDARD form (coefficient of
    y′ equal to 1) by dividing by x:
       y′ + (2/x) y = x²,          P = 2/x,  Q = x²
    We want a μ(x) with μ(y′ + Py) = (μy)′ = μy′ + μ′y. This needs μ′ = Pμ,
    so μ = e^(∫P dx). Here ∫P dx = 2 ln x, so μ = e^(2 ln x) = x². Multiply:
       x²y′ + 2xy = x⁴     that is   (x²y)′ = x⁴
       x²y = x⁵/5 + C      ⇒   y = x³/5 + C/x²
       y(1) = 1:   1/5 + C = 1,   C = 4/5
    so y = x³/5 + 4/(5x²). Check: xy′ + 2y = (3x³/5 − 8/(5x²)) +
    (2x³/5 + 8/(5x²)) = x³ ✓. The structure is always y = y_p + C y_h with
    y_h = 1/μ solving the homogeneous equation. Common mistakes: using P = 2
    (forgetting to divide by x); dropping the constant before dividing by μ,
    which loses the whole C/x² family. Now you try: y′ + y = eˣ, y(0) = 1.
    (y = (eˣ + e^(−x))/2 = cosh x)

D3. Show that (2xy + 3) dx + (x² + 4y) dy = 0 is exact and solve it. Then show
    that (y² + 2x) dx + xy dy = 0 is not exact, find an integrating factor and
    solve.
    Answer: x²y + 3x + 2y² = C. The second is not exact; μ = x gives
    3x²y² + 4x³ = C.
    Teacher explains: M dx + N dy = 0 is EXACT if it is dF for some F, and
    then F(x, y) = C. By equality of mixed partials, a necessary (and on a
    rectangle sufficient) test is M_y = N_x:
       M = 2xy + 3,  M_y = 2x;    N = x² + 4y,  N_x = 2x    ✓ exact
    Build F: integrate M in x, then fix the 'constant' g(y):
       F = ∫(2xy + 3) dx = x²y + 3x + g(y)
       F_y = x² + g′(y) = N = x² + 4y   ⇒   g′ = 4y,  g = 2y²
    Solution: x²y + 3x + 2y² = C. Second equation: M_y = 2y, N_x = y, not
    exact. Look for μ(x): μ exists when (M_y − N_x)/N is a function of x alone:
       (2y − y)/(xy) = 1/x     ⇒   μ′/μ = 1/x,   μ = x
    Multiply: (xy² + 2x²) dx + x²y dy = 0, now M_y = 2xy = N_x ✓. Then
    F = x²y²/2 + 2x³/3, i.e. 3x²y² + 4x³ = C. Common mistakes: integrating N
    with respect to x; adding the y-terms of ∫N dy twice when they already
    appeared in ∫M dx. Now you try: solve (y cos x + 2x) dx + (sin x − 1) dy = 0.
    (y sin x + x² − y = C)

D4. (a) Solve the homogeneous equation y′ = (x² + y²)/(xy), y(1) = 2, x > 0.
    (b) Solve the Bernoulli (logistic) equation y′ = y − y², y(0) = 1/2.
    Answer: (a) y = x√(2 ln x + 4). (b) y = 1/(1 + e^(−x)).
    Teacher explains: (a) The right side is a function of y/x only:
    (x² + y²)/(xy) = 1/v + v with v = y/x. Put y = vx, y′ = v + xv′:
       v + x v′ = 1/v + v      ⇒   x v′ = 1/v
       v dv = dx/x             ⇒   v²/2 = ln x + C
       y(1) = 2 ⇒ v(1) = 2:   2 = C
       y²/x² = 2 ln x + 4      ⇒   y = x√(2 ln x + 4)
    (positive root since y(1) > 0). (b) Bernoulli form y′ − y = −y² with n = 2.
    Put v = y^(1−n) = 1/y, so v′ = −y′/y²:
       v′ = −(y − y²)/y² = −1/y + 1 = −v + 1
       v′ + v = 1   ⇒   v = 1 + C e^(−x)
       v(0) = 2   ⇒   C = 1,    y = 1/(1 + e^(−x))
    This is the logistic curve: it rises from 1/2 towards the equilibrium
    y = 1, never crossing it. Common mistakes: substituting y = vx but
    forgetting the v term in y′ = v + xv′; for Bernoulli, dividing only part
    of the equation by yⁿ. Now you try: y′ + y = y², general solution.
    (y = 1/(1 + C eˣ), plus y ≡ 0)

D5. (a) Tea at 90 °C is left in a room at 20 °C and cools to 60 °C in
    10 minutes. Using Newton's law of cooling, when does it reach 30 °C?
    (b) A tank holds 100 L of brine containing 20 kg of salt. Pure water flows
    in at 5 L/min and the well-mixed solution flows out at 5 L/min. When is
    the salt halved? If instead brine with 0.2 kg/L flows in (tank starting
    with fresh water), how much salt is present after 20 minutes?
    Answer: (a) t = 10 ln 7/ln(7/4) ≈ 34.8 min. (b) 20 ln 2 ≈ 13.9 min;
    20(1 − e^(−1)) ≈ 12.6 kg.
    Teacher explains: (a) Newton: dT/dt = −k(T − 20). Separable in T − 20:
       T − 20 = 70 e^(−kt)
       t = 10:  40 = 70 e^(−10k)   ⇒   k = ln(7/4)/10 ≈ 0.05596
       T = 30:  10 = 70 e^(−kt)    ⇒   t = ln 7 / k = 10 ln 7/ln(7/4) ≈ 34.8
    (b) Let Q(t) = kg of salt. RATE IN − RATE OUT, where rate out =
    (concentration Q/100 kg/L) × (5 L/min):
       Q′ = 0 − 5Q/100 = −Q/20,  Q(0) = 20   ⇒   Q = 20 e^(−t/20)
       Q = 10  ⇒  t = 20 ln 2 ≈ 13.86 min
    With brine coming in: rate in = 0.2 × 5 = 1 kg/min, so
       Q′ = 1 − Q/20,  Q(0) = 0   ⇒   Q = 20(1 − e^(−t/20))
       Q(20) = 20(1 − e^(−1)) ≈ 12.64 kg
    The long-run amount 20 kg = 0.2 kg/L × 100 L, as common sense demands.
    Common mistakes: using concentration Q instead of Q/volume; when inflow
    and outflow rates differ the volume V(t) changes and must appear as Q/V(t).
    Now you try: 20% of a radioactive sample decays in 5 years. Find the
    half-life. (5 ln 2/ln(5/4) ≈ 15.5 years)

D6. State the Picard–Lindelöf theorem. Compute the Picard iterates for
    y′ = y, y(0) = 1 and identify the limit. Show that y′ = 3y^(2/3), y(0) = 0
    has infinitely many solutions, and explain which hypothesis fails.
    Answer: φₖ(x) = 1 + x + x²/2! + ... + xᵏ/k! → eˣ. For y′ = 3y^(2/3):
    y ≡ 0, y = x³ and, for each c ≥ 0, y = 0 (x ≤ c), (x − c)³ (x > c) are all
    solutions; ∂f/∂y = 2y^(−1/3) is unbounded near y = 0 (f is not Lipschitz).
    Teacher explains: PICARD–LINDELÖF: if f(x, y) is continuous on the
    rectangle R = {|x − x₀| ≤ a, |y − y₀| ≤ b}, |f| ≤ M there, and f is
    LIPSCHITZ in y (|f(x, y) − f(x, z)| ≤ L|y − z|, true if ∂f/∂y is
    continuous on R), then the IVP has exactly one solution on
    |x − x₀| ≤ h = min(a, b/M). The proof rewrites the IVP as the integral
    equation y(x) = y₀ + ∫ f(t, y(t)) dt and shows the iteration is a
    contraction. For y′ = y, y(0) = 1:
       φ₀ = 1
       φ₁ = 1 + ∫₀ˣ 1 dt = 1 + x
       φ₂ = 1 + ∫₀ˣ (1 + t) dt = 1 + x + x²/2
       φ₃ = 1 + x + x²/2 + x³/6,   ...   φₖ = ∑ from j = 0 to k of xʲ/j!
    These are the Taylor polynomials of eˣ, and eˣ is the unique solution. For
    y′ = 3y^(2/3): y = x³ gives y′ = 3x² = 3(x³)^(2/3) ✓, and y ≡ 0 works too;
    gluing 0 to (x − c)³ at x = c is still differentiable, so there are
    infinitely many solutions through (0, 0). The derivative
    ∂f/∂y = 2y^(−1/3) blows up at y = 0, so no Lipschitz constant exists there.
    Common mistake: believing every IVP has a unique global solution; the
    theorem is LOCAL (compare D1). Now you try: Picard iterates φ₁, φ₂ for
    y′ = 2x(1 + y), y(0) = 0, and guess the solution. (φ₁ = x²,
    φ₂ = x² + x⁴/2; y = e^(x²) − 1)

D7. Solve (a) y″ − 5y′ + 6y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 0; (b) y″ + 4y′ + 4y = 0,
    y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 0; (c) y″ + 2y′ + 5y = 0, y(0) = 0, y′(0) = 2. Explain
    where the three forms of solution come from.
    Answer: (a) y = 3e^(2x) − 2e^(3x); (b) y = (1 + 2x)e^(−2x);
    (c) y = e^(−x) sin 2x.
    Teacher explains: Try y = e^(rx): then y′ = re^(rx), y″ = r²e^(rx) and the
    equation becomes (r² + br + c)e^(rx) = 0, so r must be a root of the
    CHARACTERISTIC equation.
       (a) r² − 5r + 6 = 0,  r = 2, 3:   y = Ae^(2x) + Be^(3x)
           y(0):  A + B = 1;   y′(0):  2A + 3B = 0   ⇒   A = 3, B = −2
       (b) r² + 4r + 4 = (r + 2)² = 0,  r = −2 (double):  y = (A + Bx)e^(−2x)
           y(0):  A = 1;   y′(0):  −2A + B = 0   ⇒   B = 2
       (c) r² + 2r + 5 = 0,  r = −1 ± 2i:  y = e^(−x)(A cos 2x + B sin 2x)
           y(0):  A = 0;   y′(0):  −A + 2B = 2   ⇒   B = 1
    Why xe^(rx) for a double root: writing D = d/dx, the equation is
    (D + 2)²y = 0 and (D + 2)(xe^(−2x)) = e^(−2x), then (D + 2)e^(−2x) = 0. Why
    sines and cosines: e^((−1 + 2i)x) = e^(−x)(cos 2x + i sin 2x) is a complex
    solution; its real and imaginary parts are real solutions because the
    coefficients are real. In each case the two functions have non-zero
    Wronskian, so they span the 2-dimensional solution space (D8). Physically
    (c) is a DAMPED oscillation: the envelope e^(−x) decays, the frequency is
    2. Common mistakes: writing Ae^(−2x) + Be^(−2x) for a double root (only
    one constant really); differentiating e^(−x) sin 2x wrongly when applying
    y′(0). Now you try: y″ + 9y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 3. (cos 3x + sin 3x)

D8. (a) Prove Abel's formula W(x) = C e^(−∫p dx) for two solutions of
    y″ + p(x)y′ + q(x)y = 0 and deduce that W is either never zero or always
    zero. (b) Given y₁ = x solves x²y″ − xy′ + y = 0 (x > 0), find a second
    solution by reduction of order and write the general solution.
    Answer: (a) W′ = −pW (proof below). (b) y₂ = x ln x; y = x(c₁ + c₂ ln x).
    Teacher explains: (a) W = y₁y₂′ − y₁′y₂. Differentiate (the y₁′y₂′ terms
    cancel) and use y″ = −py′ − qy:
       W′ = y₁y₂″ − y₁″y₂
          = y₁(−py₂′ − qy₂) − (−py₁′ − qy₁)y₂
          = −p(y₁y₂′ − y₁′y₂) = −pW
    This first-order linear equation gives W = C e^(−∫p dx). An exponential is
    never zero, so W ≡ 0 (C = 0) or W is never zero (C ≠ 0). Consequently
    two solutions form a FUNDAMENTAL SET as soon as W ≠ 0 at a single point,
    and then every solution is c₁y₁ + c₂y₂ (choose c₁, c₂ to match y(x₀),
    y′(x₀); the linear system has determinant W(x₀) ≠ 0; uniqueness does the
    rest). (b) Put y = v x: y′ = v′x + v, y″ = v″x + 2v′.
       x²(v″x + 2v′) − x(v′x + v) + vx = x³v″ + x²v′ = 0
       v″/v′ = −1/x   ⇒   v′ = 1/x   ⇒   v = ln x
    So y₂ = x ln x, and W(x, x ln x) = x ≠ 0 for x > 0. This is a
    CAUCHY–EULER equation: trying y = xʳ gives r(r − 1) − r + 1 = (r − 1)² = 0,
    a double root r = 1, and the rule 'double root gives xʳ ln x' matches.
    Common mistake: taking W = 0 at one point for functions that are NOT
    solutions of a common equation (x² and x|x| have W ≡ 0 yet are
    independent on R). Now you try: solve x²y″ − 2y = 0 for x > 0.
    (r² − r − 2 = 0, r = 2, −1: y = Ax² + B/x)

D9. Find the general solution of y″ − 3y′ + 2y = 4x + e^(3x). Then find a
    particular solution of y″ − 3y′ + 2y = eˣ and of y″ + y = cos x, and
    explain resonance.
    Answer: y = c₁eˣ + c₂e^(2x) + 2x + 3 + e^(3x)/2. y_p = −xeˣ;
    y_p = (x/2) sin x.
    Teacher explains: Homogeneous part: r² − 3r + 2 = (r − 1)(r − 2), so
    y_h = c₁eˣ + c₂e^(2x). Treat the two forcing terms separately
    (SUPERPOSITION):
       for 4x try Ax + B:     0 − 3A + 2(Ax + B) = 4x
                              2A = 4,  −3A + 2B = 0   ⇒   A = 2, B = 3
       for e^(3x) try Ce^(3x):  (9 − 9 + 2)C = 1   ⇒   C = 1/2
    For eˣ the guess Ceˣ fails, because eˣ already solves the homogeneous
    equation (r = 1 is a root): the left side gives 0 · C eˣ. Multiply by x:
       y = Cxeˣ,  y′ = C(x + 1)eˣ,  y″ = C(x + 2)eˣ
       C[(x + 2) − 3(x + 1) + 2x]eˣ = −Ceˣ = eˣ   ⇒   C = −1
    For y″ + y = cos x, ±i are characteristic roots, so try
    x(A cos x + B sin x): substituting gives −2A sin x + 2B cos x = cos x,
    A = 0, B = 1/2. This is RESONANCE: forcing at the natural frequency makes
    the response grow like x. Rule: multiply the naive guess by xᵐ, where m is
    the multiplicity of the forcing exponent as a root. Common mistakes:
    guessing only A sin x for a cos x forcing (you need both); applying the
    initial conditions to y_h before adding y_p. Now you try: a particular
    solution of y″ − 2y′ + y = eˣ. (x²eˣ/2: r = 1 is a double root)

D10. (a) Use variation of parameters to solve y″ + y = sec x on
     −π/2 < x < π/2. (b) An undamped spring satisfies x″ + 4x = cos 2t,
     x(0) = x′(0) = 0. Find x(t) and describe the motion.
     Answer: (a) y = c₁cos x + c₂sin x + x sin x + cos x ln(cos x).
     (b) x = (t/4) sin 2t: oscillation with amplitude growing linearly
     (resonance).
     Teacher explains: (a) Undetermined coefficients cannot handle sec x (its
     derivatives never close up). With y₁ = cos x, y₂ = sin x, W = 1, and
     f = sec x (leading coefficient already 1), look for
     y = u₁y₁ + u₂y₂ with u₁′y₁ + u₂′y₂ = 0 and u₁′y₁′ + u₂′y₂′ = f. Solving
     by Cramer's rule:
        u₁′ = −y₂f/W = −sin x sec x = −tan x     ⇒   u₁ = ln(cos x)
        u₂′ =  y₁f/W =  cos x sec x = 1          ⇒   u₂ = x
        y_p = cos x ln(cos x) + x sin x
     Check: y_p′ = −sin x ln(cos x) − sin x + sin x + x cos x
     = −sin x ln(cos x) + x cos x, and y_p″ + y_p reduces to sec x ✓.
     (b) Natural frequency √4 = 2 equals the forcing frequency, so try
     t(A cos 2t + B sin 2t); substitution gives A = 0, B = 1/4, and
     y_h = c₁cos 2t + c₂sin 2t with x(0) = 0 ⇒ c₁ = 0 and x′(0) = 0 ⇒ c₂ = 0:
        x(t) = (t/4) sin 2t
     The mass swings with period π while the amplitude t/4 grows without bound
     (in reality the spring breaks or damping takes over). With damping c > 0
     the response stays bounded and the forced amplitude peaks near the natural
     frequency. Common mistakes: using the variation-of-parameters formula
     without first dividing by the leading coefficient; forgetting absolute
     values (ln|cos x|) outside the given interval. Now you try:
     y″ − 2y′ + y = eˣ/x, x > 0, a particular solution. (xeˣ ln x)

D11. Solve the system x′ = x + 2y, y′ = 2x + y with x(0) = 3, y(0) = 1.
     Classify the equilibrium at the origin. Then write y″ + 3y′ + 2y = 0 as a
     system and classify it.
     Answer: x = 2e^(3t) + e^(−t), y = 2e^(3t) − e^(−t); SADDLE. The system
     (y, y′)′ = [[0, 1], [−2, −3]](y, y′) has eigenvalues −1, −2: STABLE
     NODE.
     Teacher explains: Write X′ = AX with A = [[1, 2], [2, 1]]. Look for
     solutions e^(λt)v: substituting gives Av = λv, an EIGENVALUE problem.
        det(A − λI) = (1 − λ)² − 4 = 0   ⇒   λ = 3 or λ = −1
        λ = 3:   (A − 3I)v = 0,   v = (1, 1)
        λ = −1:  (A + I)v = 0,    v = (1, −1)
        X = c₁e^(3t)(1, 1) + c₂e^(−t)(1, −1)
        X(0) = (3, 1):   c₁ + c₂ = 3,  c₁ − c₂ = 1   ⇒   c₁ = 2, c₂ = 1
     One eigenvalue positive and one negative: trajectories come in along the
     line y = −x and go out along y = x, a SADDLE (unstable). Equivalently
     X(t) = e^(At)X(0) with e^(At) = P e^(Dt) P⁻¹ when A = PDP⁻¹. For the second
     part put x₁ = y, x₂ = y′: x₁′ = x₂, x₂′ = −2x₁ − 3x₂. The characteristic
     polynomial of the matrix, λ² + 3λ + 2, is exactly the characteristic
     equation of the ODE: λ = −1, −2, both negative, so every trajectory
     tends to the origin: a stable node. Quick 2 × 2 test with τ = trace,
     Δ = det: Δ < 0 saddle; Δ > 0 and τ² − 4Δ ≥ 0 node; τ² − 4Δ < 0 spiral;
     τ = 0 < Δ centre; stable if τ < 0. Common mistake: solving each equation
     separately as if x′ = x + 2y did not involve y. Now you try:
     x′ = 3x + y, y′ = 2y, x(0) = y(0) = 1. (x = 2e^(3t) − e^(2t), y = e^(2t))

D12. (a) From the definition find L{e^(at)} and prove L{y′} = sY(s) − y(0).
     (b) Solve y″ + 3y′ + 2y = e^(−3t), y(0) = 0, y′(0) = 1 by Laplace
     transforms. (c) Solve y′ + y = u(t − 1), y(0) = 0, where u is the unit
     step.
     Answer: (a) 1/(s − a) for s > a. (b) y = (3e^(−t) − 4e^(−2t) + e^(−3t))/2.
     (c) y = u(t − 1)(1 − e^(−(t − 1))).
     Teacher explains: (a) ∫₀^∞ e^(−st)e^(at) dt = [e^(−(s − a)t)/(−(s − a))]
     from 0 to ∞ = 1/(s − a), provided s > a so the upper limit vanishes. By
     parts: ∫₀^∞ e^(−st)y′ dt = [e^(−st)y]₀^∞ + s∫₀^∞ e^(−st)y dt = −y(0) + sY
     (for y of exponential order). Applying it twice gives
     L{y″} = s²Y − sy(0) − y′(0). (b) Transform every term:
        (s²Y − 0 − 1) + 3(sY − 0) + 2Y = 1/(s + 3)
        (s + 1)(s + 2)Y = 1 + 1/(s + 3) = (s + 4)/(s + 3)
        Y = (s + 4)/((s + 1)(s + 2)(s + 3))
     Partial fractions by the cover-up rule:
        s = −1:  3/((1)(2)) = 3/2;   s = −2:  2/((−1)(1)) = −2;
        s = −3:  1/((−2)(−1)) = 1/2
        Y = (3/2)/(s + 1) − 2/(s + 2) + (1/2)/(s + 3)
     so y = (3/2)e^(−t) − 2e^(−2t) + (1/2)e^(−3t). Check y(0) = 0,
     y′(0) = −3/2 + 4 − 3/2 = 1 ✓. The initial conditions are built in: no
     constants to find at the end. (c) L{u(t − 1)} = e^(−s)/s:
        (s + 1)Y = e^(−s)/s   ⇒   Y = e^(−s)[1/s − 1/(s + 1)]
     Without the e^(−s), 1/s − 1/(s + 1) inverts to 1 − e^(−t); the factor
     e^(−s) SHIFTS it to start at t = 1 (t-shift rule). So y = 0 for t < 1 and
     y = 1 − e^(−(t − 1)) for t ≥ 1. Common mistakes: L{y″} = s²Y − y′(0)
     (missing the s y(0) term); inverting e^(−s)/s as e^(−t); forgetting the
     u(t − 1) factor in the answer. Now you try: y″ − y = 1, y(0) = 0,
     y′(0) = 1. (Y = 1/(s(s − 1)), y = eᵗ − 1)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Solve the separable IVP; give y explicitly.
    a) y′ = 2x, y(0) = 1                 b) y′ = 3y, y(0) = 2
    c) y′ = −2xy, y(0) = 1               d) y′ = xy², y(0) = 1
    e) y′ = y², y(0) = 1                 f) y′ = x/y, y(0) = 2
    g) y′ = −x/y, y(0) = 3               h) y′ = e^(x − y), y(0) = ln 2
    i) y′ = 1 + y², y(0) = 0             j) y′ = (1 + y²)/(1 + x²), y(0) = 1
    k) y′ = y cos x, y(0) = 2            l) y′ = y(1 − y), y(0) = 1/2
    m) xy′ = 2y, y(1) = 3                n) y′ = 2x(y + 1), y(0) = 0
    o) y′ = 3x²y², y(0) = −1             p) y′ = xe^(−y), y(0) = 0
    q) y′ = (y − 1)/x, y(1) = 3          r) y′ = √y, y(0) = 1 (x > −2)
    s) yy′ = xe^(x²), y(0) = 2           t) y′ = 2y/(x + 1), y(0) = 1
    Answers:
      a) x² + 1              b) 2e^(3x)             c) e^(−x²)
      d) 2/(2 − x²)          e) 1/(1 − x)           f) √(x² + 4)
      g) √(9 − x²)           h) ln(eˣ + 1)          i) tan x
      j) (1 + x)/(1 − x)     k) 2e^(sin x)          l) 1/(1 + e^(−x))
      m) 3x²                 n) e^(x²) − 1          o) −1/(x³ + 1)
      p) ln(1 + x²/2)        q) 1 + 2x              r) (x/2 + 1)²
      s) √(e^(x²) + 3)       t) (x + 1)²

M2. Solve the linear equation (general solution, or the IVP).
    a) y′ + y = 0                        b) y′ + 2y = 4, y(0) = 0
    c) y′ − y = e^(2x), y(0) = 0         d) y′ + y = eˣ, y(0) = 1
    e) y′ + y = x                        f) y′ + 2xy = x
    g) y′ + y/x = 1 (x > 0)              h) xy′ + 2y = x², y(1) = 1
    i) y′ − 2y/x = x³, y(1) = 0          j) y′ + y tan x = cos x
    k) y′ + y = sin x                    l) y′ − 3y = e^(3x), y(0) = 1
    m) y′ + 3x²y = x²                    n) y′ + y cot x = 2 cos x
    o) (1 + x²)y′ + 2xy = 1              p) y′ + 4y = 8, y(0) = 5
    q) y′ − y = −x, y(0) = 2             r) xy′ − y = x² sin x
    s) y′ + 2y = e^(−2x), y(0) = 3       t) L i′ + R i = E (constants), i(0) = 0
    Answers:
      a) Ce^(−x)                         b) 2 − 2e^(−2x)
      c) e^(2x) − eˣ                     d) cosh x = (eˣ + e^(−x))/2
      e) x − 1 + Ce^(−x)                 f) 1/2 + Ce^(−x²)
      g) x/2 + C/x                       h) x²/4 + 3/(4x²)
      i) x⁴/2 − x²/2                     j) (x + C) cos x
      k) (sin x − cos x)/2 + Ce^(−x)     l) (x + 1)e^(3x)
      m) 1/3 + Ce^(−x³)                  n) sin x + C/sin x
      o) (x + C)/(1 + x²)                p) 2 + 3e^(−4x)
      q) x + 1 + eˣ                      r) x(C − cos x)
      s) (x + 3)e^(−2x)                  t) (E/R)(1 − e^(−Rt/L))

M3. Test for exactness; solve (use the integrating factor given or found).
    a) (2x + y) dx + (x + 2y) dy = 0
    b) (2xy + 3) dx + (x² + 4y) dy = 0
    c) (y cos x + 2x) dx + (sin x − 1) dy = 0
    d) (3x² + y) dx + (x − 2y) dy = 0
    e) (e^y + 1) dx + xe^y dy = 0
    f) ye^(xy) dx + (xe^(xy) + 2y) dy = 0
    g) 2xy³ dx + 3x²y² dy = 0
    h) (x + y) dx + (x − y) dy = 0
    i) (y − x) dx + (x + y) dy = 0
    j) (sin y + y cos x) dx + (x cos y + sin x) dy = 0
    k) (1 + ln x + y/x) dx − (1 − ln x) dy = 0   (x > 0)
    l) (3x²y + 2) dx + (x³ + 2y) dy = 0
    m) (x² + y²) dx + 2xy dy = 0
    n) 2xy dx + (x² + 1) dy = 0, y(0) = 3
    o) (y² + 2x) dx + xy dy = 0
    p) y dx − x dy = 0
    q) xy dx + x² dy = 0
    Answers:
      a) exact; x² + xy + y² = C
      b) exact; x²y + 3x + 2y² = C
      c) exact; y sin x + x² − y = C
      d) exact; x³ + xy − y² = C
      e) exact; xe^y + x = C
      f) exact; e^(xy) + y² = C
      g) exact; x²y³ = C
      h) exact; x² + 2xy − y² = C
      i) exact; y² + 2xy − x² = C
      j) exact; x sin y + y sin x = C
      k) exact; x ln x + y ln x − y = C
      l) exact; x³y + 2x + y² = C
      m) exact; x³ + 3xy² = C
      n) exact; x²y + y = 3, y = 3/(1 + x²)
      o) not exact; μ = x; 3x²y² + 4x³ = C
      p) not exact; μ = 1/y²; x/y = C (or y = Cx)
      q) not exact; μ = y; x²y² = C (xy = C)

M4. Solve by substitution (homogeneous, Bernoulli, or u = ax + by + c).
    a) y′ = (x + y)/x                   b) y′ = y/x + x/y
    c) y′ = (x² + y²)/(xy), y(1) = 2    d) y′ = y/x + e^(y/x)
    e) y′ = y/x + tan(y/x)              f) y′ + y = y²
    g) y′ = y − y², y(0) = 1/2          h) y′ + y/x = y² (x > 0)
    i) y′ = y + xy³                     j) y′ = 2y − y³
    k) y′ = (x + y)²                    l) y′ = x + y + 1
    m) xy′ + y = x³y³
    Answers:
      a) y = x ln|x| + Cx               b) y² = 2x² ln|x| + Cx²
      c) y = x√(2 ln x + 4)             d) y = −x ln(C − ln|x|)
      e) sin(y/x) = Cx                  f) y = 1/(1 + Ceˣ) (and y ≡ 0)
      g) y = 1/(1 + e^(−x))             h) y = 1/(x(C − ln x))
      i) y^(−2) = 1/2 − x + Ce^(−2x)    j) y^(−2) = 1/2 + Ce^(−4x)
      k) y = tan(x + C) − x             l) y = Ceˣ − x − 2
      m) y^(−2) = Cx² − 2x³

M5. Write the general solution (constant coefficients).
    a) y″ − y = 0                  b) y″ + y = 0
    c) y″ − 4y′ + 3y = 0           d) y″ + 5y′ + 6y = 0
    e) y″ − 6y′ + 9y = 0           f) y″ + 4y′ + 4y = 0
    g) y″ + 9y = 0                 h) y″ + 2y′ + 5y = 0
    i) y″ − 2y′ + 10y = 0          j) y″ + y′ = 0
    k) 2y″ − 5y′ + 2y = 0          l) y″ + y′ + y = 0
    m) 4y″ + 4y′ + y = 0           n) y″ − 2y′ − y = 0
    o) y‴ − y′ = 0                 p) y‴ − 3y″ + 3y′ − y = 0
    q) y⁗ − y = 0                  r) y⁗ + 2y″ + y = 0
    s) y‴ + y = 0                  t) y⁗ + 4y = 0
    Answers:
      a) c₁eˣ + c₂e^(−x)                 b) c₁cos x + c₂sin x
      c) c₁eˣ + c₂e^(3x)                 d) c₁e^(−2x) + c₂e^(−3x)
      e) (c₁ + c₂x)e^(3x)                f) (c₁ + c₂x)e^(−2x)
      g) c₁cos 3x + c₂sin 3x             h) e^(−x)(c₁cos 2x + c₂sin 2x)
      i) eˣ(c₁cos 3x + c₂sin 3x)         j) c₁ + c₂e^(−x)
      k) c₁e^(2x) + c₂e^(x/2)
      l) e^(−x/2)(c₁cos(√3x/2) + c₂sin(√3x/2))
      m) (c₁ + c₂x)e^(−x/2)              n) c₁e^((1 + √2)x) + c₂e^((1 − √2)x)
      o) c₁ + c₂eˣ + c₃e^(−x)            p) (c₁ + c₂x + c₃x²)eˣ
      q) c₁eˣ + c₂e^(−x) + c₃cos x + c₄sin x
      r) (c₁ + c₂x)cos x + (c₃ + c₄x)sin x
      s) c₁e^(−x) + e^(x/2)(c₂cos(√3x/2) + c₃sin(√3x/2))
      t) eˣ(c₁cos x + c₂sin x) + e^(−x)(c₃cos x + c₄sin x)

M6. Solve the initial (or boundary) value problem.
    a) y″ − y = 0, y(0) = 2, y′(0) = 0
    b) y″ + 4y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 4
    c) y″ − 5y′ + 6y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 0
    d) y″ + 4y′ + 4y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 0
    e) y″ + 2y′ + 5y = 0, y(0) = 0, y′(0) = 2
    f) y″ − 2y′ + y = 0, y(0) = 2, y′(0) = 3
    g) y″ + y′ − 2y = 0, y(0) = 0, y′(0) = 3
    h) y″ + 9y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 3
    i) y″ − 4y′ + 13y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 2
    j) y″ + 6y′ + 9y = 0, y(0) = 0, y′(0) = 1
    k) y″ − y′ − 6y = 0, y(0) = 5, y′(0) = 0
    l) y″ + 2y′ + 2y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = −1
    m) y″ − 4y′ + 4y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 0
    n) y″ = 6x, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 0
    o) y″ + y = 0, y(0) = 0, y(π/2) = 2
    p) y″ + y = 0, y(0) = 0, y(π) = 0
    q) y″ + y = 0, y(0) = 0, y(π) = 1
    Answers:
      a) eˣ + e^(−x) = 2 cosh x          b) cos 2x + 2 sin 2x
      c) 3e^(2x) − 2e^(3x)               d) (1 + 2x)e^(−2x)
      e) e^(−x) sin 2x                   f) (2 + x)eˣ
      g) eˣ − e^(−2x)                    h) cos 3x + sin 3x
      i) e^(2x) cos 3x                   j) xe^(−3x)
      k) 2e^(3x) + 3e^(−2x)              l) e^(−x) cos x
      m) (1 − 2x)e^(2x)                  n) x³ + 1
      o) 2 sin x                         p) C sin x, any C (infinitely many)
      q) no solution

M7. Find a particular solution (undetermined coefficients); in s) solve the IVP;
    in u)–x) write only the correct trial form.
    a) y″ + y = 3                      b) y″ − y = x
    c) y″ + 4y = 8x²                   d) y″ − 3y′ + 2y = 4x
    e) y″ − 3y′ + 2y = e^(3x)          f) y″ − 3y′ + 2y = eˣ
    g) y″ + y = cos x                  h) y″ + y = eˣ
    i) y″ + 4y = sin x                 j) y″ + 4y = sin 2x
    k) y″ − 2y′ + y = eˣ               l) y″ + 2y′ = 4
    m) y″ − y = eˣ                     n) y″ + y′ − 6y = 10e^(2x)
    o) y″ + 2y′ + 5y = 5x + 2          p) y″ + y = xeˣ
    q) y″ − 4y = e^(2x) + 4            r) y″ + 9y = cos 3x + x
    s) y″ + y = 2 sin x, y(0) = y′(0) = 0
    t) y″ + y′ = 6x
    u) y″ − 2y′ + y = xeˣ              v) y″ + 4y = x cos 2x
    w) y″ + 2y′ + 2y = e^(−x) sin x    x) y‴ − y″ = x
    Answers:
      a) 3                   b) −x                  c) 2x² − 1
      d) 2x + 3              e) e^(3x)/2            f) −xeˣ
      g) (x/2) sin x         h) eˣ/2                i) (sin x)/3
      j) −(x/4) cos 2x       k) x²eˣ/2              l) 2x
      m) (x/2)eˣ             n) 2xe^(2x)            o) x
      p) (x − 1)eˣ/2         q) (x/4)e^(2x) − 1     r) (x/6) sin 3x + x/9
      s) sin x − x cos x     t) 3x² − 6x
      u) x²(Ax + B)eˣ        v) x[(Ax + B)cos 2x + (Cx + D)sin 2x]
      w) xe^(−x)(A cos x + B sin x)          x) x²(Ax + B)

M8. Variation of parameters, Cauchy–Euler equations, reduction of order.
    a) y″ + y = sec x                  b) y″ + y = tan x
    c) y″ − 2y′ + y = eˣ/x (x > 0)     d) y″ + y = csc x
    e) y″ + 4y = sec 2x                f) x²y″ − 2y = 0
    g) x²y″ + xy′ − y = 0              h) x²y″ − xy′ + y = 0
    i) x²y″ + xy′ + y = 0              j) x²y″ − 3xy′ + 4y = 0
    k) x²y″ + 3xy′ + 5y = 0            l) x²y″ − 2xy′ + 2y = x³
    m) x²y″ − 4xy′ + 6y = 0, y(1) = 0, y′(1) = 1
    n) y″ − 2y′ + y = 0, y₁ = eˣ: find y₂
    o) x²y″ − xy′ + y = 0, y₁ = x: find y₂
    p) (1 − x²)y″ − 2xy′ + 2y = 0, y₁ = x: find y₂ (|x| < 1)
    Answers (a)–e) give y_p; add c₁y₁ + c₂y₂; x > 0 in f)–m)):
      a) x sin x + cos x ln|cos x|
      b) −cos x ln|sec x + tan x|
      c) xeˣ ln x
      d) −x cos x + sin x ln|sin x|
      e) (x/2) sin 2x + (1/4) cos 2x ln|cos 2x|
      f) c₁x² + c₂/x                     g) c₁x + c₂/x
      h) x(c₁ + c₂ ln x)                 i) c₁cos(ln x) + c₂sin(ln x)
      j) x²(c₁ + c₂ ln x)
      k) (1/x)(c₁cos(2 ln x) + c₂sin(2 ln x))
      l) c₁x + c₂x² + x³/2               m) x³ − x²
      n) xeˣ                             o) x ln x
      p) (x/2) ln((1 + x)/(1 − x)) − 1

M9. Wronskians, independence and Abel's formula.
    a) W(eˣ, e^(2x))                   b) W(cos x, sin x)
    c) W(e^(ax), e^(bx))               d) W(x, x²)
    e) W(eˣ, xeˣ)                      f) W(1, x, x²)
    g) W(eˣcos x, eˣsin x)             h) W(x, x ln x)
    i) W(cos 2x, sin 2x)               j) W(1, eˣ, e^(2x))
    k) W(x², x³)                       l) W(x, eˣ)
    m) W(cos x, sin x, eˣ)             n) W(sin²x, cos²x, 1): dependent?
    o) Abel: W for y″ + 2xy′ + y = 0
    p) Abel: W for x²y″ + xy′ + (x² − 1)y = 0
    q) Abel: W for y″ − 3y′ + ky = 0 (any k)
    r) y₁ = eˣ, W(y₁, y₂) = e^(3x): find y₂ (up to adding a multiple of y₁)
    Answers:
      a) e^(3x)        b) 1               c) (b − a)e^((a + b)x)
      d) x²            e) e^(2x)          f) 2
      g) e^(2x)        h) x               i) 2
      j) 2e^(3x)       k) x⁴              l) (x − 1)eˣ
      m) 2eˣ           n) W = 0; dependent (sin²x + cos²x − 1 = 0)
      o) Ce^(−x²)      p) C/x             q) Ce^(3x)
      r) e^(2x) ((y₂/y₁)′ = W/y₁² = eˣ)

M10. Linear systems X′ = AX. For a)–k) give the eigenvalues and the type of
     the origin; solve l)–n); compute e^(At) in o)–q); r) is nonlinear.
     a) A = [[2, 0], [0, −3]]          b) A = [[1, 2], [2, 1]]
     c) A = [[−2, 1], [1, −2]]         d) A = [[0, 1], [−1, 0]]
     e) A = [[−1, −2], [2, −1]]        f) A = [[3, −2], [2, −2]]
     g) A = [[1, 1], [−1, 1]]          h) A = [[−3, 1], [0, −3]]
     i) A = [[4, −3], [2, −1]]         j) A = [[0, 1], [−2, −3]]
     k) A = [[1, 4], [−1, 1]]
     l) A = [[1, 2], [2, 1]], X(0) = (2, 0)
     m) A = [[3, 1], [0, 2]], X(0) = (1, 1)
     n) A = [[−2, 1], [1, −2]], X(0) = (1, 3)
     o) A = [[1, 1], [0, 1]]           p) A = [[0, 1], [−1, 0]]
     q) A = [[2, 1], [0, 2]]
     r) x′ = x(3 − y), y′ = y(x − 2): equilibria and linear type at each
     Answers:
       a) 2, −3; saddle               b) 3, −1; saddle
       c) −1, −3; stable node         d) ±i; centre
       e) −1 ± 2i; stable spiral      f) 2, −1; saddle
       g) 1 ± i; unstable spiral      h) −3 (double); stable degenerate node
       i) 1, 2; unstable node         j) −1, −2; stable node
       k) 1 ± 2i; unstable spiral
       l) X = e^(3t)(1, 1) + e^(−t)(1, −1)
       m) X = (2e^(3t) − e^(2t), e^(2t))
       n) X = 2e^(−t)(1, 1) + e^(−3t)(−1, 1)
       o) eᵗ[[1, t], [0, 1]]
       p) [[cos t, sin t], [−sin t, cos t]]
       q) e^(2t)[[1, t], [0, 1]]
       r) (0, 0): Jacobian diag(3, −2), saddle; (2, 3): Jacobian
          [[0, −2], [3, 0]], eigenvalues ±i√6, centre (linearisation)

M11. Find the Laplace transform F(s).
     a) 1                    b) t                   c) t³
     d) e^(2t)               e) sin 3t              f) cos 3t
     g) te^(−t)              h) e^(2t)cos t         i) sinh 2t
     j) t sin t              k) 3 − 2t + t²         l) cos²t
     m) u(t − 2)             n) (t − 1)u(t − 1)     o) δ(t − 3)
     p) t²e^(3t)             q) sin t cos t         r) e^(−t)sin 2t
     s) ∫₀ᵗ sin τ dτ         t) 1 for 0 < t < 2, 0 for t > 2
     u) t cos 2t
     Answers:
       a) 1/s                  b) 1/s²                 c) 6/s⁴
       d) 1/(s − 2)            e) 3/(s² + 9)           f) s/(s² + 9)
       g) 1/(s + 1)²           h) (s − 2)/((s − 2)² + 1)
       i) 2/(s² − 4)           j) 2s/(s² + 1)²
       k) 3/s − 2/s² + 2/s³    l) (s² + 2)/(s(s² + 4))
       m) e^(−2s)/s            n) e^(−s)/s²            o) e^(−3s)
       p) 2/(s − 3)³           q) 1/(s² + 4)           r) 2/((s + 1)² + 4)
       s) 1/(s(s² + 1))        t) (1 − e^(−2s))/s      u) (s² − 4)/(s² + 4)²

M12. Invert, or solve by Laplace transforms.
     a) L⁻¹{1/(s − 3)}                 b) L⁻¹{4/s³}
     c) L⁻¹{1/(s² + 4)}                d) L⁻¹{(s + 1)/(s² + 2s + 5)}
     e) L⁻¹{1/(s(s + 1))}              f) L⁻¹{1/((s − 1)(s − 2))}
     g) L⁻¹{(2s + 3)/(s² + 9)}         h) L⁻¹{1/(s + 2)²}
     i) L⁻¹{e^(−2s)/s²}                j) L⁻¹{1/(s²(s + 1))}
     k) L⁻¹{s/(s² + 1)²}               l) L⁻¹{1/(s²(s² + 1))} (convolution)
     m) L⁻¹{(3s + 1)/((s − 1)(s² + 1))}
     n) y′ + 2y = 0, y(0) = 3
     o) y′ − y = e^(2t), y(0) = 0
     p) y″ + y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 2
     q) y″ − 3y′ + 2y = e^(3t), y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 0
     r) y″ + 4y = sin t, y(0) = y′(0) = 0
     s) y″ + 2y′ + y = e^(−t), y(0) = y′(0) = 0
     t) y″ + 2y′ + 5y = 0, y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 1
     u) y′ + y = u(t − 1), y(0) = 0
     v) y″ + y = δ(t − π), y(0) = y′(0) = 0
     w) y(t) = t + ∫₀ᵗ sin(t − τ) y(τ) dτ
     Answers:
       a) e^(3t)                b) 2t²                 c) (sin 2t)/2
       d) e^(−t)cos 2t          e) 1 − e^(−t)          f) e^(2t) − eᵗ
       g) 2 cos 3t + sin 3t     h) te^(−2t)            i) (t − 2)u(t − 2)
       j) t − 1 + e^(−t)        k) (t sin t)/2         l) t − sin t
       m) 2eᵗ − 2 cos t + sin t
       n) 3e^(−2t)              o) e^(2t) − eᵗ         p) cos t + 2 sin t
       q) e^(3t)/2 − 2e^(2t) + 5eᵗ/2
       r) (2 sin t − sin 2t)/6
       s) t²e^(−t)/2            t) e^(−t)(cos 2t + sin 2t)
       u) u(t − 1)(1 − e^(−(t − 1)))
       v) −u(t − π) sin t       w) t + t³/6

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: before solving, CLASSIFY the equation (order, linear or not,
  separable, exact, constant coefficients) and write the name at the top; always
  check the answer by substituting back (it takes one minute and catches most
  sign errors); keep a one-page table: characteristic roots → solution forms,
  forcing term → trial form, and the ten basic Laplace pairs; draw the direction
  field of y′ = f(x, y) for simple f to see solutions before computing them.
  Harder: prove the Picard–Lindelöf theorem with the contraction mapping
  principle; show that e^(At) = P e^(Dt) P⁻¹ and that det e^(At) = e^(t·tr A);
  solve Airy's equation y″ = xy by power series; prove L{f * g} = FG and use it
  to solve an integral equation; analyse the damped forced oscillator
  x″ + 2ζωx′ + ω²x = cos Ωt and find the resonance peak
  (Ω = ω√(1 − 2ζ²)); sketch the full phase portrait of the Lotka–Volterra
  system and prove that its orbits are closed using the conserved quantity
  x − 2 ln x + y − 3 ln y.


================================================================================
TOPIC 9.20 — DISCRETE MATHEMATICS: LOGIC, COUNTING, RECURRENCE RELATIONS,
             GENERATING FUNCTIONS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - PROPOSITIONAL LOGIC: connectives ¬ (not), ∧ (and), ∨ (inclusive or),
    ⊕ (exclusive or), → (implies), ↔ (iff). p → q is FALSE only when p is true
    and q false; p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q ≡ ¬q → ¬p (CONTRAPOSITIVE), but the CONVERSE
    q → p and the INVERSE ¬p → ¬q are not equivalent to it. A TAUTOLOGY is
    true in every row of the truth table, a CONTRADICTION in none, otherwise a
    CONTINGENCY; A ≡ B means A ↔ B is a tautology.
  - LAWS: De Morgan ¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q, ¬(p ∨ q) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q; distributive,
    absorption p ∨ (p ∧ q) ≡ p, double negation, ¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q,
    (p ∧ q) → r ≡ p → (q → r). Every Boolean function has a DNF (OR of the
    true rows) and a CNF (AND of clauses killing the false rows); {¬, ∧},
    {¬, ∨} and {NAND} are FUNCTIONALLY COMPLETE. There are 2^(2ⁿ) Boolean
    functions of n variables.
  - PREDICATE LOGIC: ∀x P(x), ∃x P(x); the DOMAIN matters. Negation swaps
    the quantifiers and negates the inside: ¬∀x P(x) ≡ ∃x ¬P(x),
    ¬∃x P(x) ≡ ∀x ¬P(x). The ORDER of different quantifiers matters:
    ∀x ∃y (x + y = 0) is true in Z, ∃y ∀x (x + y = 0) is false. An argument
    is VALID when the conclusion is true in every row (or model) where all the
    premises are true: modus ponens, modus tollens, hypothetical syllogism,
    disjunctive syllogism, resolution.
  - COUNTING PRINCIPLES: SUM rule (disjoint choices add), PRODUCT rule
    (successive choices multiply), COMPLEMENT (count what you do not want and
    subtract), DIVISION rule (each outcome counted k times: divide by k).
    Arrangements: n! of n objects; P(n, r) = n!/(n − r)! ordered selections;
    C(n, r) = n!/(r!(n − r)!) unordered; words with repeated letters
    n!/(n₁! n₂! ... nₖ!); circular arrangements (n − 1)!.
  - SELECTIONS WITH REPETITION (stars and bars): the number of solutions of
    x₁ + ... + xₖ = n in integers xᵢ ≥ 0 is C(n + k − 1, k − 1); with xᵢ ≥ 1 it
    is C(n − 1, k − 1). Lower bounds: subtract them first; upper bounds: use
    inclusion–exclusion or generating functions. Distributions: distinct
    objects into distinct boxes kⁿ; onto kⁿ − C(k, 1)(k − 1)ⁿ + ...; distinct
    objects into identical nonempty boxes S(n, k) (Stirling numbers of the
    second kind); identical into identical boxes = PARTITIONS of n.
  - BINOMIAL THEOREM (x + y)ⁿ = ∑ C(n, k)xᵏyⁿ⁻ᵏ; Pascal C(n, k) = C(n − 1, k)
    + C(n − 1, k − 1); ∑ C(n, k) = 2ⁿ; ∑ kC(n, k) = n2ⁿ⁻¹; Vandermonde
    ∑ C(m, j)C(n, k − j) = C(m + n, k); hockey stick. Prove identities by
    COMBINATORIAL (double counting) arguments as well as algebra. Multinomial
    coefficient n!/(a! b! c!) is the coefficient of xᵃyᵇzᶜ in (x + y + z)ⁿ.
  - INCLUSION–EXCLUSION: |A₁ ∪ ... ∪ Aₙ| = ∑|Aᵢ| − ∑|Aᵢ ∩ Aⱼ| + ... +
    (−1)ⁿ⁺¹|A₁ ∩ ... ∩ Aₙ|. DERANGEMENTS Dₙ = n! ∑ (−1)ᵏ/k! (D₄ = 9, D₅ = 44),
    Dₙ/n! → 1/e; Dₙ = (n − 1)(Dₙ₋₁ + Dₙ₋₂). Euler's φ(n) = n ∏(1 − 1/p).
  - PIGEONHOLE PRINCIPLE: n + 1 objects in n boxes put two in one box; N
    objects in k boxes put at least ⌈N/k⌉ in some box. The art is choosing
    the boxes (remainders, pairs {1, 2n}, subsquares, parity classes).
  - RECURRENCE RELATIONS: set up by conditioning on the first or last step.
    LINEAR HOMOGENEOUS with constant coefficients aₙ = c₁aₙ₋₁ + ... + cₖaₙ₋ₖ:
    characteristic equation rᵏ = c₁rᵏ⁻¹ + ... + cₖ; distinct roots give
    aₙ = ∑ Aᵢrᵢⁿ, a root of multiplicity m gives (A + Bn + ... + Cnᵐ⁻¹)rⁿ.
    NONHOMOGENEOUS: aₙ = (homogeneous) + (particular); for f(n) = polynomial ×
    sⁿ guess the same form, multiplied by nᵐ if s is a root of multiplicity m.
    DIVIDE AND CONQUER T(n) = aT(n/b) + f(n): unroll or use the MASTER
    THEOREM (compare f(n) with n^(log_b a)).
  - GENERATING FUNCTIONS: A(x) = ∑ aₙxⁿ (ordinary). Key series 1/(1 − x) =
    ∑ xⁿ, 1/(1 − ax) = ∑ aⁿxⁿ, 1/(1 − x)ᵏ = ∑ C(n + k − 1, k − 1)xⁿ,
    (1 + x)ⁿ, and generalised binomial (1 + x)^α. PRODUCTS of GFs count
    combined choices (coefficient of xⁿ in ∏ (allowed powers)); a recurrence
    becomes an algebraic equation for A(x), solved by partial fractions.
    EXPONENTIAL GFs ∑ aₙxⁿ/n! count arrangements (labelled objects). Catalan
    numbers Cₙ = C(2n, n)/(n + 1) = 1, 1, 2, 5, 14, 42, 132 with
    C(x) = (1 − √(1 − 4x))/(2x).
  - COMMON TRAPS: using C when order matters (or P when it does not);
    double counting 'at least one' by choosing one first and then the rest;
    forgetting that p → q is true when p is false; negating 'for all ... there
    exists' without swapping BOTH quantifiers; fitting initial conditions to
    the homogeneous part before adding the particular solution.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Using truth tables, show that ((p → q) ∧ (q → r)) → (p → r) is a
    tautology, that p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p, and that p → q is NOT equivalent to its
    converse q → p.
    Answer: The first formula is true in all 8 rows; p → q and ¬q → ¬p have the
    same column (T, F, T, T); the converse has column (T, T, F, T).
    Teacher explains: Build the table with a column for each piece:
       p  q │ p → q │ ¬q → ¬p │ q → p │ ¬p ∨ q
       T  T │   T   │    T    │   T   │   T
       T  F │   F   │    F    │   T   │   F
       F  T │   T   │    T    │   F   │   T
       F  F │   T   │    T    │   T   │   T
    Columns 1, 2 and 4 agree, so p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p ≡ ¬p ∨ q; the converse differs
    in rows 2 and 3. For the first formula (HYPOTHETICAL SYLLOGISM) a quicker
    method than 8 rows: an implication is false only if its hypothesis is true
    and its conclusion false. Suppose p → r is false: then p = T, r = F. For
    p → q to be true we need q = T, but then q → r = T → F is false. So the
    hypothesis cannot be true while the conclusion is false: tautology. The
    rows with p false make p → q 'vacuously' true; this is the convention that
    lets 'if n > 2 then n² > 4' be true for every n. Common mistakes: treating
    p → q as false when p is false; believing a statement and its converse
    are the same (every square is a rectangle, not conversely). Now you try:
    show (p ∧ q) → r ≡ p → (q → r). (Both are false only for p = q = T, r = F)

D2. (a) Simplify ¬(p ∨ (¬p ∧ q)) with the laws of logic. (b) Find a DNF and a
    CNF for the MAJORITY function m(p, q, r), which is true when at least two
    of p, q, r are true. (c) Show that NAND (p ↑ q = ¬(p ∧ q)) is
    functionally complete.
    Answer: (a) ¬p ∧ ¬q. (b) DNF (p ∧ q) ∨ (q ∧ r) ∨ (p ∧ r); CNF
    (p ∨ q) ∧ (q ∨ r) ∧ (p ∨ r). (c) ¬p ≡ p ↑ p, p ∧ q ≡ (p ↑ q) ↑ (p ↑ q).
    Teacher explains: (a) Work from the inside out, naming each law:
       ¬(p ∨ (¬p ∧ q)) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬(¬p ∧ q)        De Morgan
                       ≡ ¬p ∧ (p ∨ ¬q)         De Morgan, double negation
                       ≡ (¬p ∧ p) ∨ (¬p ∧ ¬q)  distributive
                       ≡ F ∨ (¬p ∧ ¬q)         ¬p ∧ p ≡ F
                       ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q
    (b) DNF: one term for each TRUE row (TTT, TTF, TFT, FTT), then simplify;
    the four minterms collapse to the three pairs because
    (p ∧ q ∧ r) ∨ (p ∧ q ∧ ¬r) ≡ p ∧ q, and so on. CNF: majority is false
    exactly when at least two variables are false, i.e. when some pair is
    both false, so we need every pair to contain a truth: (p ∨ q) ∧ (q ∨ r)
    ∧ (p ∨ r). (c) Since every function has a DNF, {¬, ∧, ∨} is complete; by
    De Morgan p ∨ q ≡ ¬(¬p ∧ ¬q), so {¬, ∧} is complete; and both ¬ and ∧ can
    be written with ↑ alone as shown. Common mistake: in a DNF writing a term
    for each FALSE row. Now you try: write p ∨ q using ↑ only.
    ((p ↑ p) ↑ (q ↑ q))

D3. (a) Negate, with no ¬ in front of a quantifier: 'for every ε > 0 there is
    a δ > 0 such that for all x, |x − a| < δ implies |f(x) − L| < ε'.
    (b) Decide the truth of ∀x ∃y (x + y = 0) and ∃y ∀x (x + y = 0) over Z.
    (c) Is this argument valid? 'If it rains, the match is off. If the match is
    off, we go to the cinema. We did not go to the cinema. Therefore it did not
    rain.' (d) Is 'All mathematicians like puzzles; some puzzle-lovers play
    chess; so some mathematicians play chess' valid?
    Answer: (a) ∃ε > 0 ∀δ > 0 ∃x (|x − a| < δ ∧ |f(x) − L| ≥ ε). (b) True;
    false. (c) Valid. (d) Invalid.
    Teacher explains: (a) Push ¬ inwards one symbol at a time: each ∀ becomes
    ∃ and each ∃ becomes ∀, and at the end ¬(A → B) ≡ A ∧ ¬B:
       ¬∀ε ∃δ ∀x (A → B) ≡ ∃ε ¬∃δ ∀x (A → B) ≡ ∃ε ∀δ ¬∀x (A → B)
                         ≡ ∃ε ∀δ ∃x ¬(A → B) ≡ ∃ε ∀δ ∃x (A ∧ ¬B)
    This is exactly what you prove to show f(x) does NOT tend to L. (b) For
    each x choose y = −x: true. The second says one y works for ALL x; x = 0
    forces y = 0 and x = 1 then fails: false. (c) Symbolise r → m, m → c, ¬c.
    Then r → c (hypothetical syllogism) and ¬c give ¬r (modus tollens): valid.
    (d) A model with all premises true and the conclusion false: mathematicians
    = {Ann}, puzzle-lovers = {Ann, Bob}, chess players = {Bob}. So the
    argument is invalid, however plausible its conclusion. Common mistakes:
    negating to '∀ε ∃δ ...' (only the inside negated); calling an argument
    invalid because a premise is false (validity is about FORM). Now you try:
    negate 'every student has a friend who knows every teacher'. (Some student
    has, for every friend, a teacher that friend does not know)

D4. A password has 6, 7 or 8 characters, each an upper-case letter (26) or a
    digit (10), and must contain at least one digit. How many passwords are
    there? Also: how many 4-digit numbers have distinct digits, and in how
    many ways can 6 people sit in a row if A and B must not sit together?
    Answer: 2,684,483,063,360 passwords; 4,536 numbers; 480 seatings.
    Teacher explains: 'At least one' is a signal for the COMPLEMENT: all
    strings minus those with no digit. For length k that is 36ᵏ − 26ᵏ, and the
    three lengths are disjoint, so by the SUM rule:
       k = 6:  36⁶ − 26⁶ = 2,176,782,336 − 308,915,776 = 1,867,866,560
       k = 7:  36⁷ − 26⁷ = 78,364,164,096 − 8,031,810,176 = 70,332,353,920
       k = 8:  36⁸ − 26⁸ = 2,821,109,907,456 − 208,827,064,576
                                                    = 2,612,282,842,880
       total = 2,684,483,063,360
    4-digit numbers with distinct digits, by the PRODUCT rule filling the
    thousands digit first (it cannot be 0): 9 × 9 × 8 × 7 = 4,536. Seating: all
    arrangements 6! = 720; glue A and B into one block (5 blocks, 5! orders,
    2 orders inside the block): 2 × 120 = 240 together, so 720 − 240 = 480
    apart. Common mistake: counting 'at least one digit' as 10 × 36⁵ × 6
    (choose the position of 'the' digit, then anything): strings with two
    digits are counted twice. Now you try: how many 5-letter words (26 letters)
    have at least one repeated letter? (26⁵ − 26·25·24·23·22 = 11,881,376 −
    7,893,600 = 3,987,776)

D5. (a) A committee of 5 is chosen from 6 men and 7 women with at least 2
    women. How many committees? (b) How many arrangements of the letters of
    MISSISSIPPI are there, and in how many are the four S's together?
    (c) In how many ways can 7 people sit at a round table?
    Answer: (a) 1,176. (b) 34,650; 840. (c) 720.
    Teacher explains: (a) Order does not matter: combinations. Complement:
       all committees                  C(13, 5)         = 1,287
       no woman                        C(6, 5)          =     6
       exactly one woman               7 × C(6, 4)      =   105
       at least two women              1,287 − 6 − 105  = 1,176
    (Direct check: C(7,2)C(6,3) + C(7,3)C(6,2) + C(7,4)C(6,1) + C(7,5)
    = 420 + 525 + 210 + 21 = 1,176 ✓.) (b) 11 letters with M × 1, I × 4, S × 4,
    P × 2: 11!/(1! 4! 4! 2!) = 34,650 (divide because swapping identical
    letters gives the same word: the DIVISION rule). With the S's glued into
    one block SSSS there are 8 objects M, IIII, PP, [SSSS]:
    8!/(4! 2!) = 840. (c) Around a table only relative positions count: fix
    one person's seat and arrange the other 6 in a row: 6! = 720 (equivalently
    7!/7, dividing by the 7 rotations). Common mistake: in (a) choosing '2
    women first, then any 3 of the remaining 11': C(7,2)C(11,3) = 3,465
    overcounts committees with 3 or more women. Now you try: arrangements of
    COMMITTEE. (9!/(2!2!2!) = 45,360)

D6. (a) Prove ∑ from k = 0 to n of C(n, k) = 2ⁿ and kC(n, k) = nC(n − 1, k − 1)
    combinatorially, and deduce ∑ kC(n, k) = n2ⁿ⁻¹. (b) Prove Vandermonde's
    identity. (c) Find the coefficient of x⁴ in (2x − 1/x)⁸ and the constant
    term in (x² − 2/x)⁶.
    Answer: (a), (b) proofs below. (c) 1,792; 240.
    Teacher explains: (a) Count subsets of an n-set in two ways: by size,
    ∑ C(n, k); by deciding in or out for each element, 2ⁿ. For the second,
    count pairs (committee of k, chair): choose the committee then the chair,
    kC(n, k); or the chair first (n ways) then the other k − 1 members,
    nC(n − 1, k − 1). Summing over k gives ∑ kC(n, k) = n∑ C(n − 1, k − 1)
    = n2ⁿ⁻¹. (Algebraic check: differentiate (1 + x)ⁿ and put x = 1.)
    (b) From m men and n women choose k people: C(m + n, k). Split by the
    number j of men: C(m, j)C(n, k − j); sum over j. (c) General term of
    (2x − 1/x)⁸:
       C(8, k)(2x)⁸⁻ᵏ(−1/x)ᵏ = C(8, k)2⁸⁻ᵏ(−1)ᵏ x⁸⁻²ᵏ
       8 − 2k = 4  ⇒  k = 2:   C(8, 2) · 2⁶ · (+1) = 28 × 64 = 1,792
    For (x² − 2/x)⁶: C(6, k)x¹²⁻²ᵏ(−2)ᵏx⁻ᵏ = C(6, k)(−2)ᵏx¹²⁻³ᵏ, so k = 4:
    15 × 16 = 240. Common mistakes: forgetting the power of the coefficient 2
    or the sign (−1)ᵏ. Now you try: prove ∑ C(n, k)² = C(2n, n). (Vandermonde
    with m = n, k = n, and C(n, n − j) = C(n, j))

D7. (a) How many solutions does x₁ + x₂ + x₃ + x₄ = 20 have in nonnegative
    integers? In positive integers? In nonnegative integers with x₁ ≤ 3?
    (b) Explain stars and bars and the four basic distribution problems.
    Answer: (a) 1,771; 969; 802. (b) See below.
    Teacher explains: STARS AND BARS: a solution is a row of 20 stars cut
    into 4 groups by 3 bars, e.g. ★★★|★★★★★★★★||★★★★★★★★★ means (3, 8, 0, 9).
    Any arrangement of 20 stars and 3 bars gives one solution, so
       nonnegative:   C(20 + 3, 3) = C(23, 3) = 1,771
    Positive: give each variable 1 first (yᵢ = xᵢ − 1 ≥ 0, ∑ yᵢ = 16):
       C(16 + 3, 3) = C(19, 3) = 969
    Upper bound x₁ ≤ 3: count the complement x₁ ≥ 4 (put y₁ = x₁ − 4, sum
    16): C(19, 3) = 969, so 1,771 − 969 = 802. Distributing n objects into k
    boxes:
       objects     boxes       any                   none empty
       identical   distinct    C(n + k − 1, k − 1)   C(n − 1, k − 1)
       distinct    distinct    kⁿ                    k! S(n, k) (onto maps)
       distinct    identical   S(n,1) + ... + S(n,k) S(n, k)
       identical   identical   partitions of n into at most k / exactly k parts
    Common mistakes: C(20, 4) or C(24, 4) (count the SYMBOLS: n stars plus
    k − 1 bars, choose the bar positions); applying stars and bars to
    distinct objects. Now you try: how many ways can 12 doughnuts be chosen
    from 5 kinds? (C(16, 4) = 1,820)

D8. (a) How many integers from 1 to 1000 are divisible by none of 2, 3, 5, 7?
    (b) Derive the formula for the number Dₙ of derangements (permutations
    with no fixed point) and find D₅. (c) Five letters are put at random into
    their five envelopes. What is the probability that none is correct?
    Answer: (a) 228. (b) Dₙ = n!(1 − 1/1! + 1/2! − ... + (−1)ⁿ/n!), D₅ = 44.
    (c) 44/120 = 11/30 ≈ 0.367.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let A₂ be the multiples of 2, etc. |Aₚ| = ⌊1000/p⌋
    and |Aₚ ∩ A_q| = ⌊1000/(pq)⌋:
       singles: 500 + 333 + 200 + 142                              = 1175
       pairs:   166 + 100 + 71 + 66 + 47 + 28                      =  478
       triples: 33 + 23 + 14 + 9                                   =   79
       all four: ⌊1000/210⌋                                        =    4
       |union| = 1175 − 478 + 79 − 4 = 772;   none = 1000 − 772 = 228
    (b) Let Aᵢ = permutations fixing i. Any k chosen points are fixed by
    (n − k)! permutations, and there are C(n, k) ways to choose them:
       Dₙ = n! − C(n,1)(n − 1)! + C(n,2)(n − 2)! − ... = n! ∑ (−1)ᵏ/k!
       D₅ = 120(1 − 1 + 1/2 − 1/6 + 1/24 − 1/120) = 60 − 20 + 5 − 1 = 44
    (c) 44/120 = 11/30. As n grows, Dₙ/n! → e⁻¹ ≈ 0.3679: surprisingly the
    chance hardly depends on n. Common mistakes: stopping after the pairs
    (inclusion–exclusion must alternate to the end); using 1000/p without the
    floor. Now you try: how many permutations of 1, ..., 5 fix exactly two
    points? (C(5, 2)D₃ = 10 × 2 = 20)

D9. Prove: (a) any n + 1 numbers chosen from {1, 2, ..., 2n} include two that
    are coprime; (b) any n + 1 numbers chosen from {1, 2, ..., 2n} include one
    that divides another; (c) among any 6 people there are 3 mutual
    acquaintances or 3 mutual strangers.
    Answer: Proofs below; the bound n + 1 is sharp in (a) and (b) (take the
    n even numbers, or n + 1, ..., 2n).
    Teacher explains: The pigeonhole principle is easy; choosing the HOLES is
    the whole art. (a) Holes: the n pairs {1, 2}, {3, 4}, ..., {2n − 1, 2n}.
    n + 1 numbers in n pairs: two lie in one pair, so they are consecutive, and
    consecutive integers are coprime (a common divisor divides their
    difference 1). (b) Write each number as 2ᵃ × m with m odd. There are only n
    odd numbers below 2n, so two chosen numbers share the same odd part m:
    2ᵃm and 2ᵇm with a < b, and the first divides the second. (c) Fix a person
    P. The other 5 fall into 2 holes (know P / do not know P), so by the
    generalised principle at least ⌈5/2⌉ = 3 are in one hole, say A, B, C all
    know P. If any two of A, B, C know each other, they form a triangle with
    P; if none do, A, B, C are 3 mutual strangers. (With 5 people this can
    fail: a pentagon of friendships.) So the Ramsey number R(3, 3) = 6.
    Common mistake: 'proving' a pigeonhole statement by examples. Now you try:
    show that among any 5 points with integer coordinates two have a midpoint
    with integer coordinates. (4 parity classes (even/odd, even/odd); two
    points in the same class have even coordinate sums)

D10. (a) Let aₙ be the number of bit strings of length n with no two
     consecutive 0s. Find a recurrence and a₁₀. (b) Solve aₙ = 5aₙ₋₁ − 6aₙ₋₂,
     a₀ = 1, a₁ = 0 and aₙ = 4aₙ₋₁ − 4aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 1, a₁ = 4. (c) Explain why
     rⁿ is the right guess.
     Answer: (a) aₙ = aₙ₋₁ + aₙ₋₂, a₁ = 2, a₂ = 3, a₁₀ = 144. (b) aₙ = 3·2ⁿ −
     2·3ⁿ; aₙ = (n + 1)2ⁿ.
     Teacher explains: (a) Condition on the LAST bit. If it is 1, the first
     n − 1 bits are any good string: aₙ₋₁ ways. If it is 0, the bit before
     must be 1 and the first n − 2 bits are any good string: aₙ₋₂ ways.
        a₁ = 2 (0, 1),  a₂ = 3 (01, 10, 11)
        3, 5, 8, 13, 21, 34, 55, 89, 144   ⇒   a₁₀ = 144
     (the Fibonacci numbers, aₙ = Fₙ₊₂). (b) Put aₙ = rⁿ: rⁿ = 5rⁿ⁻¹ − 6rⁿ⁻²,
     divide by rⁿ⁻²: r² − 5r + 6 = 0, r = 2, 3. The recurrence is LINEAR, so
     any combination A2ⁿ + B3ⁿ works, and two initial values fix A, B:
        n = 0:  A + B = 1;   n = 1:  2A + 3B = 0   ⇒   A = 3, B = −2
     Check: a₂ = 5·0 − 6·1 = −6 and 3·4 − 2·9 = −6 ✓. Second one: r² − 4r + 4
     = (r − 2)², double root, so aₙ = (A + Bn)2ⁿ; A = 1, 2(A + B) = 4 gives
     B = 1. (c) The solutions of a k-th order linear recurrence form a
     k-dimensional vector space (a solution is fixed by k initial values),
     exactly as for linear ODEs (Topic 9.19): the characteristic roots give a
     basis. Common mistake: in the double-root case writing A2ⁿ + B2ⁿ, which
     cannot fit both initial values. Now you try: aₙ = aₙ₋₁ + 2aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 2,
     a₁ = 1. (aₙ = 2ⁿ + (−1)ⁿ)

D11. (a) Solve aₙ = 3aₙ₋₁ + 2n, a₀ = 1. (b) The Tower of Hanoi needs Hₙ moves
     with Hₙ = 2Hₙ₋₁ + 1, H₁ = 1: solve. (c) Merge sort takes T(n) = 2T(n/2) +
     n comparisons with T(1) = 1 (n a power of 2): solve, and state the master
     theorem.
     Answer: (a) aₙ = (5·3ⁿ − 2n − 3)/2. (b) Hₙ = 2ⁿ − 1. (c) T(n) =
     n log₂ n + n.
     Teacher explains: (a) Homogeneous part: C·3ⁿ. The forcing 2n is a
     polynomial of degree 1 (and 1 is not a characteristic root), so guess
     pₙ = An + B:
        An + B = 3(A(n − 1) + B) + 2n = (3A + 2)n + (3B − 3A)
        A = 3A + 2 ⇒ A = −1;   B = 3B − 3A ⇒ 2B = 3A ⇒ B = −3/2
        aₙ = C·3ⁿ − n − 3/2;   a₀ = 1 ⇒ C = 5/2
     Check: a₁ = 3 + 2 = 5 and (15 − 2 − 3)/2 = 5 ✓. Apply initial values to
     the WHOLE solution, never to C·3ⁿ alone. (b) To move n discs: move n − 1
     to the spare peg, the big disc across, then n − 1 on top. Particular
     solution a constant c = 2c + 1, c = −1; so Hₙ = A2ⁿ − 1 with H₁ = 1,
     A = 1: Hₙ = 2ⁿ − 1 (a neat trick: Hₙ + 1 doubles each time). (c) Put
     n = 2ᵏ and tₖ = T(2ᵏ)/2ᵏ: dividing T(2ᵏ) = 2T(2ᵏ⁻¹) + 2ᵏ by 2ᵏ gives
     tₖ = tₖ₋₁ + 1, t₀ = 1, so tₖ = k + 1 and T(n) = n(log₂ n + 1). MASTER
     THEOREM for T(n) = aT(n/b) + Θ(nᵈ): Θ(nᵈ) if d > log_b a; Θ(nᵈ log n) if
     d = log_b a; Θ(n^(log_b a)) if d < log_b a. Merge sort: a = b = 2, d = 1 =
     log₂ 2, so Θ(n log n) ✓. Common mistake: guessing pₙ = An for a
     polynomial forcing (the constant term is needed). Now you try:
     aₙ = 2aₙ₋₁ + 2ⁿ, a₀ = 1. (2 is a root, so guess Bn2ⁿ: aₙ = (n + 1)2ⁿ)

D12. (a) In how many ways can a bag of n fruits be filled if the number of
     apples is even, the number of bananas a multiple of 5, there are at most
     4 oranges and at most 1 pear? (b) Use a generating function to derive the
     closed form of the Fibonacci numbers F₀ = 0, F₁ = 1, Fₙ = Fₙ₋₁ + Fₙ₋₂.
     Answer: (a) n + 1 ways. (b) Fₙ = (φⁿ − ψⁿ)/√5 with φ = (1 + √5)/2,
     ψ = (1 − √5)/2.
     Teacher explains: (a) Each fruit type contributes a factor listing the
     ALLOWED numbers as powers of x; multiplying the factors adds the numbers:
        apples  1 + x² + x⁴ + ...   = 1/(1 − x²)
        bananas 1 + x⁵ + x¹⁰ + ...  = 1/(1 − x⁵)
        oranges 1 + x + ... + x⁴    = (1 − x⁵)/(1 − x)
        pears   1 + x               = (1 − x²)/(1 − x)
     The product is 1/(1 − x)² = ∑ (n + 1)xⁿ: everything cancels! So there are
     n + 1 bags of n fruits (13 bags of 12). (b) Let F(x) = ∑ Fₙxⁿ. Multiply the
     recurrence by xⁿ and sum over n ≥ 2:
        F(x) − x = x F(x) + x² F(x)   ⇒   F(x) = x/(1 − x − x²)
     Factor 1 − x − x² = (1 − φx)(1 − ψx) with φ + ψ = 1, φψ = −1. Partial
     fractions:
        x/((1 − φx)(1 − ψx)) = (1/√5)[1/(1 − φx) − 1/(1 − ψx)]
     and 1/(1 − φx) = ∑ φⁿxⁿ, giving BINET's formula Fₙ = (φⁿ − ψⁿ)/√5. Since
     |ψ| < 1, Fₙ is the nearest integer to φⁿ/√5. The same method with a
     quadratic equation C(x) = 1 + xC(x)² gives the Catalan numbers. Common
     mistakes: mishandling the first terms when shifting the sum (the '− x'
     comes from F₁); forgetting that factors for 'at most k' are finite
     sums. Now you try: solve aₙ = 2aₙ₋₁ + 1, a₀ = 0 with a generating
     function. (A(x) = x/((1 − x)(1 − 2x)) = 1/(1 − 2x) − 1/(1 − x),
     aₙ = 2ⁿ − 1)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Tautology (T), contradiction (C) or contingency (N)?
    a) p ∨ ¬p                              b) p ∧ ¬p
    c) p → p                               d) p → q
    e) (p ∧ q) → p                         f) p → (p ∨ q)
    g) ((p → q) ∧ p) → q                   h) ((p → q) ∧ ¬q) → ¬p
    i) (p → q) → (q → p)                   j) ((p → q) ∧ (q → r)) → (p → r)
    k) ((p ∨ q) ∧ ¬p) → q                  l) (p → q) ↔ (¬q → ¬p)
    m) (p → q) ↔ (¬p → ¬q)                 n) ¬(p → q) ∧ q
    o) (p ↔ q) ↔ ((p → q) ∧ (q → p))       p) (p ∧ (p → q)) ∧ ¬q
    q) ((p → r) ∧ (q → r)) → ((p ∨ q) → r) r) (p → (q → r)) ↔ ((p ∧ q) → r)
    s) (p ⊕ q) ↔ (p ↔ ¬q)                  t) (p ∨ q) → (p ∧ q)
    u) ((p → q) → p) → p                   v) (p → q) ∨ (q → p)
    Answers: a) T   b) C   c) T   d) N   e) T   f) T   g) T   h) T   i) N   j) T
             k) T   l) T   m) N   n) C   o) T   p) C   q) T   r) T   s) T   t) N
             u) T   v) T

M2. Simplify, negate, or decide the truth value.
    a) Simplify ¬(p ∨ (¬p ∧ q))          b) Simplify (p ∧ q) ∨ (p ∧ ¬q)
    c) Simplify (p → q) ∧ (p → ¬q)       d) Simplify (p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ q)
    e) Simplify p ∨ (p ∧ q)              f) Simplify p → (q → p)
    g) Negate p → q                      h) Negate p ↔ q
    i) Negate ∀x (P(x) → Q(x))           j) Negate ∃x ∀y (x < y)
    k) Negate ∀ε > 0 ∃N ∀n ≥ N |aₙ − L| < ε
    l) Converse and contrapositive of 'if n² is even then n is even'
    m) DNF and CNF of p ⊕ q              n) DNF of the majority m(p, q, r)
    o) Express p → q using only ¬ and ∧  p) Number of Boolean functions of 3
                                            variables
    Truth values:
    q) ∀x ∈ Z ∃y ∈ Z (x + y = 0)         r) ∃y ∈ Z ∀x ∈ Z (x + y = x)
    s) ∀x ∈ R ∃y ∈ R (xy = 1)            t) ∃x ∈ R ∀y ∈ R (xy = 0)
    u) ∀x ∈ N ∃y ∈ N (y < x)  (N = {1, 2, ...})
    v) ∀x ∈ R ∃y ∈ R (y² = x)            w) ∃x ∈ Z (x² = 2)
    x) ∀x ∈ R ∀y ∈ R ∃z ∈ R (x < z < y ∨ y ≤ x)
    Answers:
      a) ¬p ∧ ¬q        b) p               c) ¬p
      d) q              e) p               f) T (tautology)
      g) p ∧ ¬q         h) p ↔ ¬q (equivalently p ⊕ q)
      i) ∃x (P(x) ∧ ¬Q(x))                j) ∀x ∃y (y ≤ x)
      k) ∃ε > 0 ∀N ∃n ≥ N |aₙ − L| ≥ ε
      l) converse: if n is even then n² is even; contrapositive: if n is odd
         then n² is odd (both true here)
      m) (p ∧ ¬q) ∨ (¬p ∧ q);  (p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ ¬q)
      n) (p ∧ q) ∨ (q ∧ r) ∨ (p ∧ r)      o) ¬(p ∧ ¬q)
      p) 2⁸ = 256
      q) T   r) T (y = 0)   s) F (x = 0)   t) T (x = 0)   u) F (x = 1)
      v) F (x = −1)   w) F   x) T (density of R)

M7. Pigeonhole principle: give the least number, or a proof sketch.
    a) people needed to be sure two share a birth month
    b) people needed to be sure three share a birth month
    c) cards drawn from a deck to be sure of 3 of one suit
    d) socks drawn from a drawer of 5 colours to be sure of a matching pair
    e) integers needed to be sure two leave the same remainder mod 7
    f) numbers chosen from {1, ..., 20} to be sure two sum to 21
    g) numbers chosen from {1, ..., 20} to be sure one divides another
    h) students needed to be sure 5 get the same grade (grades A–E)
    i) 100 objects in 7 boxes: the least possible size of the fullest box
    j) people needed to be sure two have the same two initials
    k) sequence length forcing a monotone subsequence of length 4
       (distinct reals)
    l) Prove: among any n + 1 integers two differ by a multiple of n.
    m) Prove: 5 points in an equilateral triangle of side 2 include two at
       distance ≤ 1.
    n) Prove: 51 points in a unit square include 3 inside some disc of radius
       1/7.
    o) Prove: in any group of people two have the same number of friends
       in the group.
    Answers: a) 13   b) 25   c) 9   d) 6   e) 8   f) 11   g) 11   h) 21
             i) 15   j) 677   k) 10
      l) n possible remainders mod n, n + 1 integers: two share a remainder, so
         their difference is divisible by n.
      m) Joining midpoints cuts the triangle into 4 triangles of side 1; two
         points lie in one small triangle, at distance ≤ 1.
      n) Cut into 25 squares of side 1/5; one square has ⌈51/25⌉ = 3 points;
         its circumscribed disc has radius √2/10 ≈ 0.1414 < 1/7 ≈ 0.1429.
      o) With n people degrees lie in {0, ..., n − 1}, but 0 and n − 1 cannot
         both occur; so n people take at most n − 1 values.

M8. Solve the linear homogeneous recurrence.
    a) aₙ = 3aₙ₋₁, a₀ = 2
    b) aₙ = 5aₙ₋₁ − 6aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 1, a₁ = 0
    c) aₙ = aₙ₋₁ + 2aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 2, a₁ = 1
    d) aₙ = 4aₙ₋₁ − 4aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 1, a₁ = 4
    e) aₙ = 6aₙ₋₁ − 9aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 1, a₁ = 6
    f) aₙ = 7aₙ₋₁ − 10aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 2, a₁ = 7
    g) aₙ = −aₙ₋₁ + 6aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 0, a₁ = 5
    h) aₙ = aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 1, a₁ = 0
    i) aₙ = 2aₙ₋₁ − 2aₙ₋₂, a₀ = 1, a₁ = 1
    j) Fₙ = Fₙ₋₁ + Fₙ₋₂, F₀ = 0, F₁ = 1
    k) Lₙ = Lₙ₋₁ + Lₙ₋₂, L₀ = 2, L₁ = 1
    l) aₙ = 2aₙ₋₁ + aₙ₋₂ − 2aₙ₋₃, a₀ = 3, a₁ = 2, a₂ = 6
    m) aₙ = 6aₙ₋₁ − 12aₙ₋₂ + 8aₙ₋₃, a₀ = 1, a₁ = 4, a₂ = 16
    Answers:
      a) 2·3ⁿ                   b) 3·2ⁿ − 2·3ⁿ          c) 2ⁿ + (−1)ⁿ
      d) (n + 1)2ⁿ              e) (n + 1)3ⁿ            f) 2ⁿ + 5ⁿ
      g) 2ⁿ − (−3)ⁿ             h) (1 + (−1)ⁿ)/2        i) 2^(n/2) cos(nπ/4)
      j) (φⁿ − ψⁿ)/√5, φ, ψ = (1 ± √5)/2               k) φⁿ + ψⁿ
      l) 1 + (−1)ⁿ + 2ⁿ         m) (n² + n + 2)2ⁿ⁻¹

M9. Nonhomogeneous and divide-and-conquer recurrences (n = 2ᵏ in k)–o)).
    a) aₙ = aₙ₋₁ + 3, a₀ = 2              b) aₙ = aₙ₋₁ + n, a₀ = 0
    c) aₙ = aₙ₋₁ + 2n − 1, a₀ = 0         d) aₙ = aₙ₋₁ + n², a₀ = 0
    e) aₙ = 2aₙ₋₁ + 1, a₀ = 0             f) aₙ = 3aₙ₋₁ + 2n, a₀ = 1
    g) aₙ = 2aₙ₋₁ + 2ⁿ, a₀ = 1            h) aₙ = 2aₙ₋₁ + n, a₀ = 0
    i) aₙ = 5aₙ₋₁ − 6aₙ₋₂ + 2, a₀ = 1, a₁ = 2
    j) aₙ = naₙ₋₁, a₀ = 1
    k) T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n, T(1) = 1       l) T(n) = T(n/2) + 1, T(1) = 1
    m) T(n) = 2T(n/2) + 1, T(1) = 1       n) T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n, T(1) = 1
    o) T(n) = 3T(n/2) + n, T(1) = 1
    Order of growth by the master theorem:
    p) T(n) = 8T(n/2) + n²                q) T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n²
    r) T(n) = 7T(n/2) + n²                s) T(n) = T(n/2) + n
    t) T(n) = 9T(n/3) + n²
    Answers:
      a) 3n + 2                b) n(n + 1)/2           c) n²
      d) n(n + 1)(2n + 1)/6    e) 2ⁿ − 1               f) (5·3ⁿ − 2n − 3)/2
      g) (n + 1)2ⁿ             h) 2ⁿ⁺¹ − n − 2         i) 3ⁿ − 2ⁿ + 1
      j) n!                    k) n log₂ n + n         l) log₂ n + 1
      m) 2n − 1                n) 2n² − n              o) 3n^(log₂ 3) − 2n
      p) Θ(n³)                 q) Θ(n²)                r) Θ(n^(log₂ 7))
      s) Θ(n)                  t) Θ(n² log n)

M10. Set up a recurrence and compute the value asked for.
     a) bit strings of length 10 with no two consecutive 0s
     b) bit strings of length 10 containing 00
     c) tilings of a 2 × 10 board by dominoes
     d) ways to climb 8 stairs taking 1 or 2 steps at a time
     e) subsets of {1, ..., 10} with no two consecutive numbers
     f) Tower of Hanoi moves for 8 discs

     h) ternary strings of length 5 with no two consecutive 0s
